The Author's Apology for His Book

	When I first took my pen in hand to write
	like this, I did not for a moment think
	that I should ever make a little book
	in such a style. In fact I had begun
	to make another; and when that was nearly done,
	before I knew it, I had started this.
	And this is how it was. I, writing of the way
	and race of saints, in this our gospel day,
	fell suddenly into an allegory
	about their journey, and the road to glory,
	in more than twenty things that I set down.
	That done, I had another twenty in my head;
	and then they started to multiply again,
	like sparks that fly up off the coals of a fire.
	Well then, I thought, if you breed as fast as that,
	I'll put you by yourselves, in case at last
	you go on for ever, and eat out the book
	that I am already busy with.
	So that is what I did. But even then
	I did not mean to show the world my pen and ink
	in such a style. I only meant to make
	I knew not what — nor did I set out
	to please my neighbour by it. No, not I.
	I did it to satisfy my own self.
	Nor did I spend on this scribble of mine
	anything but odd spare hours; nor did I mean
	anything by it but to divert myself
	from worse thoughts that make me go wrong.
	So I set pen to paper with delight,
	and quickly had my thoughts in black and white.
	Because, having got hold of my method by the end,
	it came as fast as I pulled; and so I wrote
	it down, until at last it came to be,
	for length and breadth, the size that you see here.
	Well, when I had put my ends together like this,
	I showed them to some others, to find out whether
	they would condemn them or approve of them.
	And some said, Let them live; and some, Let them die.
	Some said, John, print it. Others said, Not so.
	Some said, It might do good. Others said, No.
	So now I was in a difficulty, and could not see
	which was the best thing for me to do.
	At last I thought: since you are so divided,
	I will print it — and so the matter is settled.
	Because, I thought, some clearly want it done,
	although some others do not run that way.
	So to find out which side advised the best,
	I thought it right to put it to the test.
	I thought further that if I now refused
	the ones who wanted it, and would not oblige them,
	I did not know but I might be keeping them
	from something that would be a great delight.
	And to those who were against its coming out
	I said: I am unwilling to offend you;
	but since your brothers are pleased with it,
	hold off from judging till you have seen more.
	If you will not read it, then leave it alone.
	Some love the meat, and some love picking the bone.
	Yes, and to smooth them down a little further,
	I reasoned it out with them like this:
	May I not write in a style like this?
	And in this method too, and yet not miss
	my end, which is your good? Why should it not be done?
	Dark clouds bring water when the bright bring none.
	Yes — dark or bright, if they let their silver drops
	come down, the earth, by yielding crops,
	gives praise to both, and finds fault with neither,
	but stores up the fruit they yield together.
	Yes, it mixes them so thoroughly that in her fruit
	nobody can tell this from that. They suit
	her well when she is hungry; but if she is full,
	she spews them both out and makes their blessing nothing.
	You see the ways a fisherman will take
	to catch a fish; what devices he will make.
	See how he sets his whole wit to the job,
	and all his snares, his lines, his rods, his hooks and nets.
	And yet there are fish that neither hook nor line
	nor snare nor net nor device can make yours:
	they must be groped for, and tickled too,
	or they will not be caught, whatever you do.
	How the fowler goes about catching his game
	by all sorts of means, more than a man could name!
	His guns, his nets, his limed twigs, light and bell;
	he creeps, he walks, he stands; and who could tell
	the whole list of his postures? And yet not one of these
	will make him master of whatever birds he please.
	No, for this one he must pipe and whistle —
	and if he does, that other bird he'll miss.
	If a pearl can live inside a toad's head,
	and can be found inside an oyster shell too;
	if things that promise nothing hold inside them
	what is better than gold — then who will scorn,
	having some inkling of it, to look there,
	so that he may find it? Now, my little book,
	although it lacks all those decorations
	that would make it take with this man or that,
	is not without those things that far excel
	what lives in fine but empty notions.
	"Well, I am still not fully satisfied
	that this book of yours will stand up when it is properly tested."
	Why, what is the matter? "It is dark."
	And what of that? "But it is made up." What of that, I ask?
	Some men, with made-up words as dark as mine,
	make truth throw sparks and set its rays to shine.
	"But they lack solidity." Speak your mind, man.
	"They drown the weak; metaphors make us blind."
	Solidity is certainly what suits the pen
	of a man who writes of divine things to men.
	But must I necessarily lack solidity because
	I speak by metaphors? Were not God's laws,
	his gospel laws, in the old days set out
	by types and shadows and metaphors? And yet
	no sober man will be quick to find fault
	with them, in case he should be found assaulting
	the highest wisdom. No — he stoops instead,
	and tries to find out what, by pins and loops,
	by calves and sheep, by heifers and by rams,
	by birds and herbs, and by the blood of lambs,
	God is saying to him. And happy is the man
	who finds the light and grace that are in them.
	So do not be too quick to conclude
	that I lack solidity, or that I am crude.
	Not everything solid-looking is solid;
	nor should we despise everything in parables,
	in case we take up lightly what is most harmful
	and rob our souls of the things that are good.
	My dark and cloudy words only hold
	the truth, as cabinets enclose the gold.
	The prophets used metaphors a great deal
	to set out truth; and anybody who considers
	Christ and his apostles too will plainly see
	that truths are wrapped in such cloaks to this day.
	Am I afraid to say that holy scripture,
	which for its style and phrasing beats all wit,
	is everywhere so full of all these things —
	dark figures, allegories? And yet there springs
	from that same book that brightness, and those rays
	of light that turn our darkest nights to days.
	Come — let my critic look now at his own life
	and find darker lines there than any he finds
	in my book; yes, and let him know as well
	that in his best things there are worse lines too.
	If we may only stand before impartial men,
	I will lay ten against his one
	that they will take my meaning in these lines
	far better than they take his lies in silver shrines.
	Come — truth, even in swaddling clothes, I find,
	informs the judgment and puts right the mind;
	pleases the understanding, makes the will
	submit; and fills the memory as well
	with what our imaginations like;
	and it tends to quiet our troubles too.
	Sound words, I know, are what Timothy is to use,
	and old wives' fables are what he is to refuse;
	but nowhere did grave Paul forbid him
	the use of parables — in which lay hidden
	that gold, those pearls, those precious stones that were
	worth digging for, and digging with great care.
	Let me add one word more. O man of God,
	are you offended? Do you wish I had
	put my matter out in another dress?
	Or that I had been more direct in things?
	Let me put three things; then I submit
	to my betters, as is fitting.
	First. I do not find that I am denied the use
	of this method of mine, so long as I put no abuse
	on the words, the things or the readers, and am not crude
	in my handling of figure or comparison
	or in applying them; but rather seek in every way I can
	to advance the truth, one way or another.
	Denied, did I say? No — I have leave for it,
	and example too, and that from men who have
	pleased God better by their words or ways
	than any man alive today —
	leave to express my mind like this, to declare
	things to you that are the most excellent there are.
	Second. I find that men as high as trees will write
	in dialogue, and nobody slights them
	for writing so. Indeed, if they abuse
	the truth, then cursed be they and the craft they use
	to that end. But still, let truth go free
	to make her sallies upon you and me
	whichever way it pleases God. Because who knows better
	than the one who first taught us to plough
	how to guide our minds and pens for his design?
	And he makes lowly things usher in the divine.
	Third. I find that holy scripture in many places
	has a likeness to this method, where the case
	calls for one thing to be used to set out another.
	So I may use it, and yet smother
	none of truth's golden beams. In fact by this method I may
	make it throw out its rays as bright as day.
	And now, before I put my pen away,
	I will show what good my book may do; and then
	commit both you and it into that Hand
	which pulls the strong down and makes the weak stand.
	This book chalks out in front of your eyes
	the man who is looking for the everlasting prize.
	It shows you where he comes from, where he goes,
	what he leaves undone, and also what he does.
	It shows you too how he runs, and runs,
	until he comes to the gate of glory.
	It shows as well who set out for life at full speed,
	as though they meant to win the lasting crown;
	and here you may see the reason too
	why they lose their labour, and die like fools.
	This book will make a traveller of you,
	if you will be governed by its advice;
	it will direct you to the Holy Land,
	if you will understand its directions.
	Yes — it will make the lazy man active,
	and make the blind see delightful things as well.
	Are you after something rare and profitable?
	Would you like to see a truth inside a fable?
	Are you forgetful? Would you like to remember
	from New Year's Day to the last of December?
	Then read my fancies; they will stick like burrs,
	and to the helpless may be comforters.
	This book is written in the kind of language
	that may take hold of listless people's minds.
	It seems a novelty, and yet contains
	nothing but sound and honest gospel strains.
	Would you like to divert yourself from melancholy?
	Would you be cheerful, and yet be far from folly?
	Would you read riddles, and their explanation?
	Or else be drowned in your own contemplation?
	Do you love food you have to pick at? Or would you see
	a man up in the clouds, and hear him speak to you?
	Would you be in a dream, and yet not sleep?
	Or would you in a moment laugh and weep?
	Would you like to lose yourself and take no harm,
	and find yourself again without a charm?
	Would you read yourself, and read you know not what,
	and yet know whether you are blessed or not,
	by reading the very same lines? Oh, then come here,
	and lay my book, your head, and your heart together.

John Bunyan

Part One: Christian

The City of Destruction

As I walked through the wilderness of this world, I came upon a certain place where there was a den, and I lay down in that place to sleep. And as I slept, I dreamed a dream. I dreamed, and look — "I saw a man dressed in rags, standing in a certain place, his face turned away from his own house, a book in his hand, and a great burden on his back." I looked, and I saw him open the book and read in it; and as he read, he wept and shook; and at last, unable to hold it in any longer, he broke out in a miserable cry: "What shall I do?"

[Note: the den is Bedford jail, where Bunyan wrote this book during the twelve years he spent in prison for preaching without a licence.]

In this condition he went home, and held himself in as long as he could, so that his wife and children would not see how distressed he was. But he could not stay silent for long, because his trouble kept growing. So at last he opened his mind to his wife and children, and began to talk to them like this. "My dear wife," he said, "and you, my own children — I am a ruined man, because of a burden that lies heavy on me. And more: I have it on certain word that this city of ours is going to be burned with fire out of heaven. In that terrible overthrow I myself, and you, my wife, and you, my sweet children, will all come to a wretched end — unless some way of escape can be found, which as yet I cannot see."

At this his family were badly shaken — not because they believed a word of what he had told them, but because they thought some kind of madness had got into his head. So, since it was getting on towards night and they hoped a night's sleep might settle his brain, they hurried him off to bed. But the night was as bad for him as the day had been, and instead of sleeping he spent it sighing and weeping. When morning came, they wanted to know how he was. Worse, he told them; and he set about talking to them again — but by now they were starting to harden against him. They decided to drive his madness out of him by being rough and short with him. Sometimes they laughed at him, sometimes they told him off, and sometimes they simply took no notice of him at all. So he began to go off to his room by himself, to pray for them and pity them, and to grieve over his own misery. He also took to walking alone in the fields, sometimes reading and sometimes praying; and he spent his days that way for some time.

Now I saw one day, as he was walking in the fields, that he was reading in his book as he usually did, and in great distress of mind; and as he read he burst out, as he had before, crying: "What shall I do to be saved?"

I saw too that he looked this way and that, as if he meant to run — and yet he stood still, because, as far as I could see, he could not tell which way to go. Then I looked, and I saw a man named Evangelist coming towards him, who asked, "Why are you crying out?"

He answered: Sir, I can see from the book in my hand that I am condemned to die, and after that to come to judgment; and I find that I am not willing to do the first, nor able to face the second.

Then Evangelist said: Why not willing to die, when this life comes with so many troubles in it?

The man answered: Because I am afraid that this burden on my back will sink me lower than the grave, and I shall fall into Tophet, the fire-pit itself. And Sir, if I am not fit to go to prison, then I am certainly not fit to go to judgment, and from there to execution. Thinking about all this is what makes me cry out.

Then Evangelist said: If that is your condition, why are you standing still?

He answered: Because I do not know where to go.

Then Evangelist gave him a roll of parchment, and written inside it was: "Flee from the wrath to come."

So the man read it, and looking very closely at Evangelist, said: Where must I flee to?

Then Evangelist pointed with his finger across a very wide field and said: Do you see that little gate over there?

The man said: No.

Then the other said: Do you see that shining light?

He said: I think I do.

Then Evangelist said: Keep that light in your eye and go straight towards it, and you will see the gate; and when you knock at it, you will be told what you are to do.

So I saw in my dream that the man began to run. He had not got far from his own door when his wife and children saw him going and began calling after him to come back. But the man put his fingers in his ears and ran on, crying, "Life! Life! Eternal life!" He never looked behind him, but fled towards the middle of the plain.

The neighbours came out too, to watch him run; and as he ran, some jeered, some threatened, and some shouted after him to come back. Among these last were two who were determined to fetch him back by force. One of them was called Obstinate, and the other Pliable. By this time the man had got a good distance ahead of them, but they were set on chasing him all the same — and they did, and before long they caught up with him.

Then the man said: Neighbours, why have you come?

They said: To persuade you to come back with us.

But he said: That is out of the question. You live in the City of Destruction — the place where I was born too. I can see that it is so. And dying there, sooner or later, you will sink lower than the grave, into a place that burns with fire and brimstone. Be sensible, good neighbours, and come along with me.

What, said Obstinate, and leave our friends and our comforts behind us?

Yes, said Christian — for that was his name — because everything "that you would leave behind" is not worth comparing with the smallest part of what I am going to find. And if you will come along with me, and hold to it, you will fare as well as I do; because where I am going there is enough and to spare. Come on, and put my words to the test.

Obstinate. What are these things you are after, that you would leave the whole world to find them?

Christian. I am after "an inheritance that cannot spoil or be spoiled or fade away." It is kept safe in heaven, to be handed over at the appointed time to those who look for it in earnest. Read it for yourself, if you like, in my book.

Obstinate. Nonsense, said Obstinate. Away with your book. Are you coming back with us or not?

Christian. Not I, said the other, because I have put my hand to the plough.

Obstinate. Come on, then, neighbour Pliable, let us turn round and go home without him. There is a whole tribe of these crackbrained fools who, once they get a notion by the end of it, think themselves wiser than seven men who can give you a reason.

Pliable. Don't be so hard on him, said Pliable. If what the good man Christian says is true, then the things he is after are better than ours. My heart rather inclines to go with my neighbour.

Obstinate. What, more fools yet? Take my advice and come back. Who knows where a man with a fever in his head will lead you? Go back, go back, and be sensible.

Christian. No — come with your neighbour Pliable. There really are such things to be had as I told you of, and far greater glories besides. If you will not believe me, read here in this book; and as for whether what is written in it is true, look — every word of it is guaranteed by the blood of the one who made it.

Pliable. Well, neighbour Obstinate, said Pliable, I am coming to a decision. I mean to go along with this good man and throw in my lot with him. But, my good companion, do you know the way to this place you want so much?

Christian. I have been directed by a man named Evangelist to hurry to a little gate that lies ahead of us, where we will be given instructions about the road.

Pliable. Come on, then, good neighbour, let us be going.

So the two of them went off together.

Obstinate. And I am going back where I came from, said Obstinate. I will be no companion to such deluded, fanciful fellows.

Now I saw in my dream that when Obstinate had gone back, Christian and Pliable went on across the plain, talking; and this is how their conversation began.

Christian. Come, neighbour Pliable, how are you? I am glad you were persuaded to come with me. If Obstinate himself had felt what I have felt of the power and the terror of what is still unseen, he would not have turned his back on us so lightly.

Pliable. Come, neighbour Christian, since there is nobody here but the two of us, tell me more. What are these things we are going to, and how are they to be enjoyed?

Christian. I can picture them in my mind better than I can speak them with my tongue. But since you want to know, I will read about them out of my book.

Pliable. And do you really think the words of your book are certainly true?

Christian. Yes, truly — because it was made by one who cannot lie.

Pliable. Well said. What things are they?

Christian. There is a kingdom without end to live in, and everlasting life to be given us, so that we may live in that kingdom forever.

Pliable. Well said. And what else?

Christian. There are crowns of glory to be given us, and clothes that will make us shine like the sun in the sky.

Pliable. That sounds very fine. And what else?

Christian. There will be no more crying, and no more sorrow; because the one who owns the place will wipe every tear from our eyes.

Pliable. And what company will we have there?

Christian. There we will be with seraphim and cherubim — creatures so bright they will dazzle your eyes to look at them. There you will meet thousands upon thousands who have gone on ahead of us to that place. Not one of them will do you any harm; they are all loving and holy, every one of them walking in the sight of God and standing accepted in his presence forever. In a word: there we will see the elders with their golden crowns; there we will see the holy virgins with their golden harps; there we will see men who were cut to pieces by this world, burned in the fire, eaten by wild beasts, drowned in the sea, all for the love they had for the Lord of that place — and every one of them well, and dressed in immortality as if it were a coat.

Pliable. Just hearing it is enough to carry a man's heart away. But are these things really to be had? How are we to get a share in them?

Christian. The Lord, the Governor of that country, has set it down in this book; and the sum of it is that if we are truly willing to have it, he will give it to us free.

Pliable. Well, my good companion, I am glad to hear it. Come on, let us pick up the pace.

Christian. I cannot go as fast as I would like, because of this burden on my back.

The Slough of Despond

Now I saw in my dream that, just as they had finished this talk, they came up to a very muddy bog in the middle of the plain; and being careless, they both fell suddenly into it. The name of the bog was Despond. So there they floundered for a while, plastered all over with the filth of it; and Christian, because of the burden on his back, began to sink.

Pliable. Then Pliable said: Well, neighbour Christian — where are you now?

Christian. Truly, said Christian, I do not know.

Pliable. At that Pliable began to take offence, and said angrily to his companion: Is this the happiness you have been telling me about all this while? If we come off this badly at the very start, what can we expect between here and the end of the journey? If I get out of this alive, you can have your fine country to yourself. And with that he gave a couple of desperate heaves and got out of the mud on the side of the bog nearest his own house — and off he went, and Christian never saw him again.

So Christian was left floundering in the Slough of Despond alone. Still he kept struggling towards the side of the bog furthest from his own house and nearest the gate; and he reached it, but he could not climb out, because of the burden on his back. But I saw in my dream that a man came to him, whose name was Help, and asked him what he was doing there.

Christian. Sir, said Christian, I was told to come this way by a man called Evangelist, who also directed me to that gate over there, so that I might escape the wrath to come. And as I was going towards it, I fell in here.

Help. But why did you not look for the steps?

Christian. Fear was after me so hard that I ran the nearest way, and fell in.

Help. Then he said: Give me your hand. So Christian gave him his hand, and he pulled him out, and set him on solid ground, and told him to go on his way.

Then I went over to the man who had pulled him out and said: Sir — since the road from the City of Destruction to that gate runs across this place, why has this patch never been repaired, so that poor travellers might get across more safely?

And he said to me: This muddy bog is the kind of place that cannot be repaired. It is the low ground into which all the scum and filth that comes with the conviction of sin drains continually, and that is why it is called the Slough of Despond. For as often as a sinner wakes up to the state he is in, there rise up in him a great many fears and doubts and discouraging thoughts, and all of them run together and settle here. That is the reason this ground is so bad.

It is not the King's wish that the place should stay this bad. His workmen, following the instructions of his Majesty's surveyors, have been at work on this patch of ground for more than sixteen hundred years now, to see whether it could be mended. To my own knowledge, said he, there have been at least twenty thousand cartloads swallowed up here — yes, and millions of sound and healthy instructions besides, brought in every season from every corner of the King's territory. And those who know say they are the best material there is for making good ground of the place, if it could be made good at all. But it is the Slough of Despond still, and it will still be the Slough of Despond when they have done everything they can.

It is true that the Lawgiver has had a set of good, solid stepping stones laid right across the middle of the bog. But at the times when the place is throwing up its filth worst — as it does when the weather is on the turn — the steps can hardly be seen; and when they can, men get dizzy in the head and put a foot down beside them, and then they go in properly, steps or no steps. But the ground is good once they have got in at the gate.

Now I saw in my dream that by this time Pliable had got home again, and his neighbours came round to see him. Some of them called him a wise man for turning back, and some called him a fool for risking himself with Christian at all; and others jeered at him for being a coward, saying, "Well, if I had once set out, I would not have been so feeble as to give up over a few difficulties." So Pliable sat there among them, sheepish. But eventually he got his confidence back, and then they all changed their tune, and started sneering at poor Christian behind his back.

And that is all there is to say about Pliable.

Mr. Worldly-Wiseman

Now as Christian was walking along by himself, he caught sight of somebody a good way off, coming across the field towards him; and it happened that they met just where their paths crossed. The gentleman's name was Mr. Worldly-Wiseman, and he lived in the town of Carnal Policy — a very large town, and close to the one Christian had come from. This man, meeting Christian, had some idea who he was, because Christian's setting out from the City of Destruction had been much talked about, not only in the town where he lived but in a few other places as well, where it was becoming the talk of the town. So Master Worldly-Wiseman, having a fair guess about him from the labouring way he walked, and from his sighs and groans and the like, opened a conversation with him.

Worldly-Wiseman. Well now, my good fellow — where are you off to, loaded up like that?

Christian. Loaded up indeed, as heavily as any poor creature ever was, I think. And since you ask me where I am off to: I am going to that little gate ahead of me, sir, because there, so I am told, I shall be set on a road that will rid me of this heavy burden.

Worldly-Wiseman. Have you a wife and children?

Christian. Yes. But I am so weighed down with this burden that I cannot take the pleasure in them that I used to. It feels as though I had none.

Worldly-Wiseman. Will you listen to me, if I give you some advice?

Christian. If it is good advice, I will. I am badly in need of good advice.

Worldly-Wiseman. Then my advice is this: get rid of that burden as fast as you possibly can. You will never have any peace of mind until you do, and until you do you cannot enjoy any of the good things God has given you.

Christian. But that is exactly what I am after — to be rid of this heavy burden. Only I cannot get it off myself, and there is nobody in our part of the country who can lift it off my shoulders. That is why I am going this way, as I told you: so that I can be rid of my burden.

Worldly-Wiseman. And who told you to go this way to be rid of it?

Christian. A man who seemed to me a very great and honourable person. His name, as I recall, was Evangelist.

Worldly-Wiseman. Curse him for the advice! There is not a more dangerous or troublesome road in the world than the one he has pointed you down, as you will find out if you go on doing what he tells you. You have run into something already, I see — that is the mud of the Slough of Despond on you. And that bog is only the beginning of the sorrows that wait for people who keep on down that road. Listen to me; I am older than you. On the road you are taking you can expect exhaustion, pain, hunger, danger, nakedness, the sword, lions, dragons, darkness — and in a word, death, and heaven knows what else. All this is perfectly true; there is plenty of testimony to confirm it. So why should a man throw himself away so carelessly, just because he listened to a stranger?

Christian. Why, sir, this burden on my back is more terrible to me than everything you have just listed. In fact I hardly care what I meet on the road, so long as I also meet with deliverance from my burden.

Worldly-Wiseman. How did you come by the burden in the first place?

Christian. By reading this book in my hand.

Worldly-Wiseman. I thought as much. The same thing has happened to you as happens to other weak men who go meddling with matters too far above them, and fall suddenly into just this sort of derangement. And that derangement does not only unman a man, as I can see it has unmanned you — it drives him off on desperate ventures to get hold of he knows not what.

Christian. I know exactly what I want to get hold of. Relief from this heavy burden.

Worldly-Wiseman. But why look for relief along this road, with so many dangers waiting on it? — especially when, if you would only be patient and hear me out, I could direct you to exactly what you want without any of the dangers you are running into this way. And the remedy is close at hand. I will add this, too: instead of those dangers you will meet with a great deal of safety, and friendship, and contentment.

Christian. Please, sir, let me in on this secret.

Worldly-Wiseman. Well then. In that village over there — the village is called Morality — there lives a gentleman named Legality: a very shrewd man, and one with an excellent reputation, who has the skill to get burdens like yours off men's shoulders. To my own knowledge he has done a great deal of good that way. And what is more, he also has the skill to cure people whose wits have gone a little astray under the weight of their burdens. You may go to him, as I say, and be helped at once. His house is not quite a mile from here; and if he should not be at home himself, he has a fine young son named Civility who can do the job, to be honest, quite as well as the old gentleman. There, I tell you, you may be relieved of your burden. And if you would rather not go back to your old house — as indeed I would not advise you to — you can send for your wife and children to join you in this village. There are houses standing empty there now, and you could have one of them at a reasonable rent. Food is cheap there too, and good. And what will make your life happier still is that you will be living among honest neighbours, respectable and well thought of.

Now Christian was brought up short for a moment; but before long he decided that if what this gentleman had said was true, the wisest thing he could do was take his advice. So he spoke again.

Christian. Sir, which is my road to this honest man's house?

Worldly-Wiseman. Do you see that hill over there?

Christian. Yes, very clearly.

Worldly-Wiseman. You must go past that hill, and the first house you come to is his.

So Christian turned off his road to go to Mr. Legality's house for help. But when he had got close up to the hill, it seemed so high, and the side of it nearest the road hung out over him so far, that Christian was afraid to go any further in case the hill should come down on his head. So he stood still, and had no idea what to do. And his burden now felt heavier to him than it had while he was on his proper road. There were flashes of fire coming out of the hill, too, that made Christian afraid he would be burned. So he stood there sweating and shaking with fear, and he began to be sorry that he had ever taken Mr. Worldly-Wiseman's advice.

And then he saw Evangelist coming to meet him — and at the sight of him he began to go red with shame. Evangelist came nearer and nearer; and coming up to him, he looked at him with a hard and terrible expression, and began to reason with him.

Evangelist. What are you doing here, Christian?

At these words Christian had no idea what to answer, and for the moment stood there without a word. Then Evangelist went on: Are you not the man I found crying outside the walls of the City of Destruction?

Christian. Yes, dear sir, I am the man.

Evangelist. Did I not point you the way to the little gate?

Christian. Yes, dear sir, said Christian.

Evangelist. How is it, then, that you have turned aside so quickly? Because you are off the road now.

Christian. I met a gentleman as soon as I had got across the Slough of Despond, and he persuaded me that in the village ahead I could find a man who would take my burden off.

Evangelist. What sort of man was he?

Christian. He looked like a gentleman, and he talked to me at length, and in the end he got me to give in. So I came here. But when I saw this hill, and the way it hangs out over the road, I stopped short, in case it should fall on my head.

Evangelist. What did this gentleman say to you?

Christian. Why, he asked me where I was going, and I told him.

Evangelist. And what did he say then?

Christian. He asked me whether I had a family, and I told him. But, I said, I am so loaded down by this burden on my back that I cannot take pleasure in them as I used to.

Evangelist. And what did he say then?

Christian. He told me to get rid of my burden quickly. And I told him that relief was exactly what I was looking for — and that I was therefore going to that gate over there, to get further directions about how to reach the place where I could be delivered. So he said that he would show me a better way, a shorter one, without so many difficulties on it as the road you had set me in, sir; and that this way would bring me to a gentleman's house, a man with the skill to take these burdens off. So I believed him, and turned off that road onto this one, hoping I might be quickly relieved of my burden. But when I got to this place and saw how things really were, I stopped for fear of the danger, as I said — and now I do not know what to do.

Evangelist. Then Evangelist said: Stand still a moment, and let me show you the words of God.

So he stood there trembling. Then Evangelist said: "See that you do not refuse him who speaks. For if they did not escape who refused him that spoke on earth, much less shall we escape, if we turn away from him that speaks from heaven." And he said further: "Now the just shall live by faith; but if any man draw back, my soul shall have no pleasure in him."

And he applied the words like this: You are the man who is running headlong into this misery. You have begun to reject the counsel of the Most High, and to draw your foot back out of the way of peace — very nearly to the point of risking your own damnation.

Then Christian fell down at his feet like a dead man, crying, "I am finished — there is no hope for me!" At the sight of that, Evangelist caught him by the right hand and said, "Every kind of sin and blasphemy shall be forgiven to men," and "Do not be faithless, but believing." Then Christian revived a little and stood up, still shaking, as he had at first, in front of Evangelist.

Then Evangelist went on: Pay close attention now to what I am going to tell you. I am going to show you who it was that fooled you, and who it was he sent you to.

The man who met you is one Worldly-Wiseman, and he is rightly named. Partly it is because he has a taste for nothing but the teaching of this world — which is why he always goes to church in the town of Morality — and partly because it is that teaching he likes best, since it is the one that best saves him from the cross. And because his temper is of that earthbound kind, he sets himself to head off my ways, right though they are.

Now there are three things in this man's advice that you must utterly detest.

First, that he turned you off the road. Second, that he worked to make the cross look hateful to you. And third, that he set your feet on the road that leads to the ministry of death.

First, you must detest his turning you off the road — and your own agreeing to it — because it means rejecting the counsel of God in favour of the counsel of a Worldly-Wiseman. The Lord says, "Strive to enter in at the narrow gate" — the gate I am sending you to; for "narrow is the gate that leads to life, and there are few that find it." It is from this little gate, and from the road to it, that this wicked man has turned you, and very nearly to your destruction. So hate him for turning you off the road, and hate yourself for listening to him.

Second, you must detest his working to make the cross look hateful to you; because you are to value the cross "above the treasures of Egypt." Besides, the King of glory has told you that whoever "will save his life shall lose it," and that "he who comes after me, and does not hate his father and mother and wife and children and brothers and sisters, and his own life as well, cannot be my disciple." So when a man works to persuade you that a thing will be the death of you, when the Truth himself has said that without it you cannot have eternal life — that teaching you must detest.

Third, you must hate his setting your feet on the road that leads to the ministry of death. And for this, consider who it was he sent you to, and how completely unable that person was to deliver you from your burden.

The man you were sent to for relief, whose name is Legality, is the son of the slave woman who exists now, and who is in slavery along with her children; and in the hidden sense of the thing, she is this Mount Sinai that you have been afraid would fall on your head. Now if she and her children are slaves, how can you expect to be made free by them? So this Legality is not able to set you free from your burden. No man has ever yet been rid of his burden by him, and no man ever will be. You cannot be made right with God by the works of the law; by keeping the law's rules, no living man can get rid of his burden. Therefore Mr. Worldly-Wiseman is a stranger to the truth, and Mr. Legality is a fraud; and as for his son Civility, for all his simpering looks, he is nothing but a hypocrite and cannot help you. Believe me, there is nothing in all this racket you have heard from these stupid men but a scheme to cheat you out of your salvation by turning you off the road I had set you on.

After this, Evangelist called aloud to the heavens to confirm what he had said; and with that there came words and fire out of the mountain that poor Christian was standing under, which made the hair stand up on his body. The words were these: "As many as are of the works of the law are under the curse; for it is written, Cursed is everyone that does not continue in all the things which are written in the book of the law, to do them."

Now Christian expected nothing but death, and began to cry out miserably, cursing the hour when he ever met Mr. Worldly-Wiseman, and calling himself a thousand kinds of fool for listening to his advice. He was deeply ashamed, too, to think that this gentleman's arguments — which came from nowhere but human self-interest — should have had enough weight with him to make him abandon the right road. Then he turned to Evangelist again, and this is what he said.

Christian. Sir, what do you think? Is there hope? May I go back now, and go up to the gate? Am I going to be cast off for this, and sent back from there in disgrace? I am sorry I listened to that man's advice. But can my sin be forgiven?

Evangelist. Then Evangelist said to him: Your sin is very great, because by it you have done two wrong things — you have abandoned the good road in order to walk in forbidden paths. And yet the man at the gate will still receive you, because he has goodwill towards people. Only, said he, take care that you do not turn aside again, "in case you perish from the way, when his anger is kindled but a little."

Then Christian set himself to go back; and Evangelist, after he had kissed him, gave him one smile and wished him godspeed. So he hurried on, and did not speak to anybody on the road; nor, if anyone asked him a question, would he give them so much as an answer. He walked like a man who was treading the whole way on forbidden ground, and who could not think himself safe by any means until he had got back onto the road he had left to follow Mr. Worldly-Wiseman's advice.

So in the course of time Christian came up to the gate. And over the gate was written: "Knock, and it shall be opened to you."

The Wicket Gate

So he knocked, more than once or twice, saying:

	May I come in? Will the one inside
	open to sorry me, though I have been
	a rebel with no claim on him at all?
	Then I will sing his praise for ever after.

At last a solemn person came to the gate, whose name was Goodwill, and asked who was there, and where he had come from, and what he wanted.

Christian. Here is a poor burdened sinner. I have come from the City of Destruction, but I am going to Mount Zion, so that I may be delivered from the wrath to come. And since I am told that the road there runs through this gate, I would like to know, sir, whether you are willing to let me in.

Goodwill. I am willing with all my heart, he said — and with that he opened the gate.

But as Christian was stepping through, the other man gave him a sharp pull. Christian said: What was that for? And the other told him: a little way off from this gate there stands a strong castle, and Beelzebub is its captain. From there, he and the ones with him shoot arrows at people coming up to this gate, in the hope that they may die before they can get in.

Then Christian said: I am glad and I am shaking, both at once.

So when he was inside, the man at the gate asked him who had directed him there.

Christian. Evangelist told me to come here and knock, which I did; and he said that you, sir, would tell me what I had to do.

Goodwill. An open door is set in front of you, and no one can shut it.

Christian. Now I am beginning to get some good out of the risks I have run.

Goodwill. But how is it that you came alone?

Christian. Because none of my neighbours saw the danger they were in, the way I saw mine.

Goodwill. Did any of them know you were coming?

Christian. Yes. My wife and children saw me at the start, and called after me to turn back; and some of my neighbours stood shouting and calling after me to come back too. But I put my fingers in my ears, and came on my way.

Goodwill. But did none of them follow you, to persuade you to go back?

Christian. Yes — both Obstinate and Pliable. But when they saw they could not get anywhere with me, Obstinate went back cursing, and Pliable came a little way with me.

Goodwill. But why did he not come the whole way?

Christian. We did come along together, until we reached the Slough of Despond, which we both suddenly fell into. And then my neighbour Pliable lost heart and would not risk going any further. So he got out again on the side nearest his own house, and told me I could have the fine country to myself for all he cared. Off he went his way and I came mine — he after Obstinate, and I to this gate.

Goodwill. Then Goodwill said: The poor man! Does he think so little of the glory of heaven that he does not reckon it worth the risk of a few difficulties to get it?

Christian. Truly, said Christian, I have told you the truth about Pliable; and if I told you the whole truth about myself, it would turn out there is nothing to choose between us. It is true he went back to his own house — but I turned aside onto the road of death, talked into it by the worldly arguments of a certain Mr. Worldly-Wiseman.

Goodwill. Oh — did he get hold of you? What, and he would have had you go looking for relief from Mr. Legality! The pair of them are a complete fraud. But did you take his advice?

Christian. Yes, as far as I dared. I went off to find Mr. Legality, until I thought the mountain that stands beside his house was going to come down on my head — and there I had to stop.

Goodwill. That mountain has been the death of many, and it will be the death of many more. You did well to escape being smashed to pieces by it.

Christian. Truly, I do not know what would have become of me there if Evangelist had not happened to meet me again, while I was standing brooding in the depths of my misery. It was God's mercy that he came to me a second time; otherwise I should never have got here at all. And now here I am, such as I am — fitter, really, to have died under that mountain than to be standing here talking to my lord. But what a kindness it is to me, that even so I am let in.

Goodwill. We turn nobody away, whatever they have done before they get here. They are "by no means cast out." So, good Christian, come a little way with me and I will teach you about the road you have to take. Look ahead of you. Do you see this narrow road? That is the way you must go. It was laid out by the patriarchs and the prophets, by Christ and by his apostles, and it is as straight as a ruler can make it. That is the road you must go.

Christian. But, said Christian, are there no turnings or bends where a stranger might lose his way?

Goodwill. Yes, there are many roads that join onto this one, and they are crooked and wide. This is how you can tell the right from the wrong: only the right one is straight and narrow.

Then I saw in my dream that Christian asked him further whether he could not help him off with the burden on his back — because he still had not been rid of it, and there was no way he could get it off without help.

Goodwill told him: as for your burden, be content to carry it until you come to the place of deliverance; for there it will fall off your back by itself.

Then Christian began to tighten his belt and get himself ready for the road. So the other told him that when he had gone some distance on from the gate, he would come to the house of the Interpreter; and that he should knock at the door there, and the Interpreter would show him excellent things. Then Christian said goodbye to his friend, and his friend once more wished him godspeed.

The House of the Interpreter

Then he went on until he came to the house of the Interpreter, where he knocked over and over. At last somebody came to the door and asked who was there.

Christian. Sir, here is a traveller, who was told by a friend of the master of this house to call in here for his own good. I would like to speak with the master of the house.

So he called the master of the house, who after a little while came to Christian and asked him what he wanted.

Christian. Sir, said Christian, I am a man who has come from the City of Destruction, and I am going to Mount Zion. I was told by the man who stands at the gate at the head of this road that if I called here you would show me excellent things — things that would help me on my journey.

Interpreter. Then the Interpreter said: Come in. I will show you what will do you good.

So he told his servant to light a candle, and told Christian to follow him. He took him into a private room and told his servant to open a door; and when he had done it, Christian saw hanging on the wall the picture of a very solemn person. This was how it looked. Its eyes were lifted up to heaven, it had the best of books in its hand, the law of truth was written on its lips, and the world was behind its back. It stood as though it were pleading with people, and a crown of gold hung over its head.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

Interpreter. The man in this picture is one in a thousand. He can father children, go through the pains of labour with them, and nurse them himself once they are born. As for seeing him with his eyes lifted to heaven, the best of books in his hand, and the law of truth written on his lips — that is to show you that his work is to know dark and difficult things and open them up for sinners; which is why you also see him standing as though he were pleading with people. And as for seeing the world thrown down behind him and a crown hanging over his head, that is to show you that because he thinks nothing of the things that are here and now, out of love for his Master's service, he is certain to have glory for his reward in the world that comes next.

Now, said the Interpreter, I have shown you this picture first because the man in it is the only man the Lord of the place you are going to has authorized to be your guide in all the hard places you may meet on the road. So take good notice of what I have shown you, and keep firmly in mind what you have seen — in case on your journey you run into people who claim to be leading you right, when their road goes down to death.

Then he took him by the hand and led him into a very large sitting room that was full of dust, because it had never been swept. After they had looked at it for a while, the Interpreter called for a man to sweep it. And when the man began to sweep, the dust flew about so thickly that Christian was nearly choked by it. Then the Interpreter said to a young woman standing by: Fetch the water and sprinkle the room. And when she had done it, the room was swept and cleaned easily and well.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

Interpreter. The Interpreter answered: This room is the heart of a man who has never been made holy by the sweet grace of the Gospel. The dust is his original sin and the corruption inside him, which has spoiled the whole man. The one who started sweeping is the Law; and the one who brought the water and sprinkled it is the Gospel.

Now, as for what you saw — that as soon as the first began to sweep, the dust flew about so much that he could not get the room clean, and you were nearly choked by it — that is to show you that the Law, instead of cleaning sin out of the heart by its own working, actually stirs sin up, gives it strength and makes it grow in the soul, at the very moment it exposes sin and forbids it. Because the Law does not give the power to master it.

And again, as you saw the young woman sprinkle the room with water, after which it was cleaned easily and well — that is to show you that when the Gospel comes into the heart with its sweet and precious influence, then, I say, just as you saw her lay the dust by sprinkling the floor with water, so is sin beaten and mastered, and the soul made clean through faith in the Gospel, and so made fit for the King of glory to live in.

I saw further, in my dream, that the Interpreter took him by the hand and brought him into a little room where two small children were sitting, each in his own chair. The elder was called Passion, and the other Patience. Passion seemed very dissatisfied, but Patience was perfectly calm. So Christian asked what the reason was for Passion's dissatisfaction. The Interpreter answered: The one who looks after them wants him to wait for his best things until the beginning of next year — but he wants everything now. Patience, on the other hand, is willing to wait.

Then I saw somebody come to Passion and bring him a bag of treasure, and pour it out at his feet; and he picked it up and gloated over it, and laughed Patience to scorn into the bargain. But I watched only a little while, and he had squandered the lot, and had nothing left but rags.

Christian. Then Christian said to the Interpreter: Explain this to me more fully.

Interpreter. So he said: These two boys stand for something. Passion stands for the people of this world, and Patience for the people of the world that is coming. Because, as you see here, Passion wants everything now, this year — that is to say, in this world. So it is with the people of this world: they must have all their good things now, and cannot wait until next year, that is, until the next world, for what is coming to them. The proverb "a bird in the hand is worth two in the bush" carries more weight with them than everything God has said about the good of the world to come. But as you saw that Passion very quickly squandered it all away and had nothing left but rags, so it will be with all such people at the end of this world.

Christian. Then Christian said: Now I see that Patience has the better wisdom, and for a number of reasons. First, because he waits for the best things. Second, because he will still have his glory when the other has nothing but rags.

Interpreter. No — you may add another. The glory of the next world will never wear out, but these things are gone in a moment. So Passion had far less reason to laugh at Patience for having his good things first than Patience will have to laugh at Passion for having his best things last. Because first has to give way to last, since last must still have its turn coming; but last gives way to nothing, because there is nothing after it to follow on. So the man who gets his share first is bound to have a time when he spends it; but the man who gets his share last has it lastingly. That is why it is said to the rich man in the story, "In your lifetime you received your good things, and Lazarus likewise received evil things; but now he is comforted, and you are tormented."

Christian. Then I see it is not best to be greedy for the things that are here now, but to wait for the things to come.

Interpreter. You are quite right. "For the things which are seen are temporary; but the things which are not seen are eternal." But even so — because present things and our bodily appetites are such close neighbours to one another, and because things to come and our bodily senses are such total strangers to one another — that is why the first two make friends so quickly, and why the distance between the second two keeps up.

Then I saw in my dream that the Interpreter took Christian by the hand and led him to a place where there was a fire burning against a wall, and somebody standing beside it constantly throwing a great deal of water on it to put it out; and yet the fire burned higher and hotter.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

The Interpreter answered: This fire is the work of grace going on in the heart. The one throwing water on it, to douse it and put it out, is the Devil. But as for why you see the fire burning higher and hotter in spite of him — you shall see the reason for that too.

So he took him round to the back of the wall, where he saw a man with a jar of oil in his hand, which he was also constantly pouring — but secretly — into the fire.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

The Interpreter answered: This is Christ, who continually keeps up, with the oil of his grace, the work already begun in the heart; and by that means, whatever the Devil can do, the souls of his people keep their grace still. And as for your seeing that the man stood behind the wall to keep the fire going, that is to teach you that it is hard for someone under temptation to see how this work of grace is being kept up in the soul.

I saw too that the Interpreter took him by the hand again and led him to a pleasant place where a magnificent palace had been built, beautiful to look at; and Christian was delighted at the sight of it. He also saw certain people walking about on the top of it, dressed all in gold.

Then Christian said: May we go in there?

Then the Interpreter took him and led him up towards the door of the palace; and there at the door stood a great crowd of men who clearly wanted to go in but did not dare. There was also a man sitting a little way off from the door, at a table, with a book and his inkpot in front of him, to take down the name of anyone who went in. And Christian saw that in the doorway there stood a number of men in armour to guard it, who were determined to do as much harm and damage as they could to anybody who tried to enter.

Now Christian was rather taken aback. At last, when every man had shrunk back for fear of the armed guards, Christian saw a man with a very determined look on his face come up to the man sitting there to write, and say, "Put my name down, sir." And when he had done it, Christian saw the man draw his sword, put a helmet on his head, and rush at the door and the armed men, who fell on him with murderous force. But the man was not discouraged in the least, and set about cutting and hacking most fiercely. So after he had taken many wounds and given many to those who were trying to keep him out, he cut his way straight through the lot of them and pushed forward into the palace — at which a delighted shout was heard from those inside, from the very people who were walking on the top of the palace, saying:

	Come in, come in;
	eternal glory you shall win.

So he went in, and was dressed in the same clothes as they were. Then Christian smiled and said: I really think I know what this means.

Now, said Christian, let me go on from here.

No, wait, said the Interpreter, until I have shown you a little more; and after that you shall go on your way.

So he took him by the hand again and led him into a very dark room, where a man sat in an iron cage.

Now the man, to look at, seemed extremely sad. He sat with his eyes on the ground and his hands folded together, and he sighed as though his heart would break. Then Christian said: What does this mean? At which the Interpreter told him to talk to the man himself.

So Christian said to the man: What are you?

The man answered: I am what I once was not.

Christian. What were you once?

The man. I was once a fine and flourishing believer, so I looked in my own eyes and in other people's too. I was once, as I thought, well set for the Celestial City, and I used to feel real joy at the thought of getting there.

Christian. Well — but what are you now?

The man. I am now a man of despair, and I am shut up inside it as I am shut up in this iron cage. I cannot get out. Oh, now I cannot get out!

Christian. But how did you come to be in this state?

The man. I stopped keeping watch and staying sober. I gave my appetites their head. I sinned against the light of the Word and against the goodness of God. I have grieved the Spirit, and he is gone. I taunted the Devil, and he has come to me. I have provoked God to anger, and he has left me. I have hardened my own heart so far that I cannot repent.

Then Christian said to the Interpreter: But is there no hope for a man like this? Ask him, said the Interpreter. No, said Christian, please, sir — you ask him.

Interpreter. Then the Interpreter said: Is there no hope? Must you be kept in this iron cage of despair?

The man. No. None at all.

Interpreter. Why, the Son of the Blessed One is full of pity.

The man. I have crucified him all over again, for myself. I have despised his person. I have despised his righteousness. I have "counted his blood an unholy thing." I have "insulted the Spirit of grace." So I have shut myself out of every promise, and there is nothing left for me now but threats — terrible threats, terrifying threats of certain judgment and burning fury, which will devour me as an enemy.

Interpreter. And what did you bring yourself into this state for?

The man. For the appetites, the pleasures and the profits of this world, in the enjoyment of which I promised myself a great deal of delight at the time. But now every one of those same things bites me and gnaws me like a burning worm.

Interpreter. But can you not repent now, and turn back?

The man. God has refused me repentance. His Word gives me no encouragement to believe. He himself has shut me up in this iron cage, and all the men in the world could not let me out. Oh, eternity, eternity! How am I going to face the misery I must meet with in eternity?

Interpreter. Then the Interpreter said to Christian: Let this man's misery stay in your memory, and be a permanent warning to you.

Christian. Well, said Christian, this is terrifying. God help me to keep watch and stay sober, and to pray that I may keep clear of whatever brought this man to such misery. Sir, is it not time for me to go on my way now?

Interpreter. Wait until I have shown you one more thing, and then you shall go on your way.

So he took Christian by the hand again and led him into a bedroom where somebody was getting out of bed; and as he put his clothes on, he was shaking and trembling. Then Christian said: Why is this man trembling like that? The Interpreter told the man to give Christian the reason for it himself.

So he began, and said: Last night, while I was asleep, I dreamed — and the sky went utterly black, and there was thunder and lightning in the most frightening way, so that it threw me into an agony. Then I looked up in my dream and saw the clouds driving past at an extraordinary speed, and I heard a great blast of a trumpet, and I also saw a man sitting on a cloud, attended by the thousands of heaven. They were all in flames of fire, and the sky itself was burning.

Then I heard a voice saying, "Arise, you dead, and come to judgment." And with that the rocks split, the graves opened, and the dead inside them came out. Some of them were tremendously glad and looked upward; and some tried to hide themselves under the mountains.

Then I saw the man who sat on the cloud open the book and tell the world to come near. But because of a fierce flame that came out from in front of him, there was a good distance kept between him and them, as there is between the judge and the prisoners in the dock. And I heard it announced to those attending the man who sat on the cloud, "Gather together the weeds, the chaff and the stubble, and throw them into the burning lake." And with that, the bottomless pit opened, just about where I was standing, and out of its mouth came thick smoke and coals of fire, with hideous noises.

It was also said to the same people, "Gather my wheat into the barn." And with that I saw many of them snatched up and carried away into the clouds — but I was left behind. I tried to hide myself too, but I could not, because the man who sat on the cloud kept his eye on me the whole time. My sins came into my mind as well, and my conscience accused me from every side. And at that I woke up.

Christian. But what was it about the sight that frightened you so much?

The man. Why, I thought that the day of judgment had come, and that I was not ready for it. But this frightened me most: that the angels gathered up a number of people and left me behind — and that the pit of hell opened its mouth just where I was standing. My conscience tormented me too; and, as I thought, the Judge kept his eye on me the whole time, with anger showing in his face.

Then the Interpreter said to Christian: Have you thought about all these things?

Christian. Yes — and they leave me hoping and afraid at once.

Interpreter. Well, keep all of them in your mind, so that they may be like a spur in your side to drive you forward on the road you have to travel.

Then Christian began to tighten his belt and get himself ready for the journey. And the Interpreter said: May the Comforter be with you always, good Christian, to guide you on the road that leads to the City.

So Christian went on his way, saying:

	Here I have seen things rare and worth the knowing,
	things pleasant, things to dread, things to keep me going
	steady in what I have set my hand to do.
	So let me think them over, and think them through,
	and understand what they were shown me for —
	and thank you, good Interpreter, once more.

The Cross and the Sepulchre

Now I saw in my dream that the road Christian had to go up was fenced on both sides with a wall, and that wall was called Salvation. So burdened Christian ran up this road — but not without great difficulty, because of the load on his back.

He ran on like that until he came to a place where the ground rose a little; and on that rise stood a cross, and a little below it, at the bottom, a tomb. So I saw in my dream that just as Christian came level with the cross, his burden came loose from his shoulders and fell off his back, and began to roll, and went on rolling until it came to the mouth of the tomb, where it fell in; and I never saw it again.

Then Christian was glad and light, and said with a happy heart, "He has given me rest by his suffering, and life by his death." Then he stood still a while to look and to wonder, because it was a great surprise to him that the sight of the cross should relieve him of his burden like that. So he looked, and looked again, until the springs in his head sent the water running down his cheeks.

Now as he stood there looking and crying, three Shining Ones came to him and greeted him with "Peace be to you." The first said to him, "Your sins are forgiven you." The second stripped off his rags and dressed him "in a change of clothing." The third set a mark on his forehead and gave him a scroll with a seal on it, which he told him to look at as he ran, and to hand in at the Celestial Gate. Then they went on their way.

And Christian gave three leaps for joy, and went on singing:

	This far I came, and all the way I bore
	my sin, and nothing eased the grief I wore
	till I came here. What kind of place is this!
	Must here be the beginning of my bliss?
	Must here the burden fall from off my back?
	Must here the cords that bound it to me crack?
	Blessed the cross, and blessed the tomb — but he
	who was put to shame there, blessed most, for me.

Then I saw in my dream that he went on like this until he came to a low place, where he saw three men a little off the road, fast asleep, with irons on their heels. One of them was called Simple, another Sloth, and the third Presumption.

Christian, seeing them lying there in that state, went over on the chance that he might wake them, and called out: You are like men asleep at the top of a mast, with the Dead Sea underneath you — a gulf with no bottom to it. So wake up and come away; and if you are willing, I will help you get those irons off. He also told them that if the one who "goes about like a roaring lion" came past, they would certainly end up between his teeth.

At that they looked at him and began to answer him, each in his own way. Simple said, "I don't see any danger." Sloth said, "Just a little more sleep." And Presumption said, "Every tub has to stand on its own bottom — what other answer do you expect me to give you?" And so they lay down to sleep again, and Christian went on his way.

Still, it troubled him to think that men in that much danger should think so little of the kindness of somebody who had freely offered to help them — waking them, warning them, and offering to get their irons off. And while he was still troubled about it, he caught sight of two men come tumbling over the wall on the left-hand side of the narrow road, and they came up to him at a good pace. One of them was called Formalist and the other Hypocrisy. So, as I say, they came up alongside him, and he got into conversation with them.

Christian. Gentlemen, where have you come from, and where are you going?

Formalist and Hypocrisy. We were born in the land of Vainglory, and we are going to Mount Zion for the credit of it.

Christian. Why did you not come in at the gate that stands at the beginning of the road? Do you not know it is written that anyone who does not come in by the door, "but climbs up some other way, is a thief and a robber"?

Formalist and Hypocrisy. They said that going round to the gate to get in was reckoned much too long a way about by everybody in their country; and that their usual practice was therefore to take a short cut and climb over the wall, as they had just done.

Christian. But will it not be counted an offence against the Lord of the city we are all bound for, to break his declared will like that?

Formalist and Hypocrisy. They told him that as far as that went, he need not trouble his head about it; because what they did they did by custom, and they could produce evidence, if it came to that, to prove the custom went back more than a thousand years.

Christian. But, said Christian, would your practice stand up in court?

Formalist and Hypocrisy. They told him that a custom of such long standing — over a thousand years — would surely be allowed as legal now by any impartial judge. And besides, they said, if we get onto the road, what does it matter how we got on? If we are on it, we are on it. You are on the road, and came in at the gate as far as we can see; and we are on the road too, and came tumbling over the wall. So how is your position any better than ours?

Christian. I walk by my Master's rule; you walk by the crude workings of your own imaginations. You are already counted thieves by the Lord of this road, so I doubt you will be found honest men at the end of it. You came in by yourselves, without his direction; and you will go out by yourselves, without his mercy.

To this they gave him very little answer; they only told him to look to himself. Then I saw that they all went on, each man his own way, without much conversation between them — except that these two men told Christian that as for laws and observances, they had no doubt they kept them quite as conscientiously as he did. So, they said, we cannot see any difference between you and us, except for that coat on your back, which we suppose some of your neighbours gave you to cover up the shame of being naked.

Christian. Laws and observances will not save you, since you did not come in by the door. And as for this coat on my back, it was given me by the Lord of the place I am going to — and given me, as you say, to cover my nakedness. I take it as a sign of his kindness to me, because I had nothing but rags before. And besides, I comfort myself with this as I go along: surely, I think, when I come to the gate of the city, the Lord of it will recognize me as one of his own, since I have his coat on my back — a coat he gave me the day he stripped me of my rags. What is more, I have a mark on my forehead, which perhaps you have not noticed, put there by one of my Lord's closest companions on the day my burden fell off my shoulders. And I will tell you something else: I was given a sealed scroll that day, to comfort me by reading it as I go along the road, and I was told to hand it in at the Celestial Gate as proof that I am certainly to be let in.

All of which, I suspect, you are without — and without because you did not come in at the gate.

To all this they gave him no answer at all; they only looked at each other and laughed. Then I saw that they all went on, except that Christian kept ahead, and had nobody to talk to but himself — sometimes with a sigh and sometimes cheerfully. He would also often read in the scroll that one of the Shining Ones had given him, and it refreshed him.

The Hill Difficulty

Then I watched them all go on until they came to the foot of the Hill Difficulty, at the bottom of which there was a spring. There were also two other roads in that same place, besides the one that came straight from the gate: one turned off to the left and the other to the right at the bottom of the hill. But the narrow road went straight up the hill, and the name of the climb up the hillside is Difficulty. Christian now went to the spring and drank from it to refresh himself, and then began to go up the hill, saying:

	The hill is high, and still I want to climb;
	the difficulty will not put me off,
	because I see the road to life runs here.
	Come, take heart — let us neither faint nor fear.
	Better the right road, hard as it may be,
	than the wrong easy one that ends in misery.

The other two came to the foot of the hill as well. But when they saw how steep and high it was, and that there were two other roads to take — and supposing that these two roads might join up again with the one Christian was climbing, on the other side of the hill — they decided to go those ways instead. Now the name of one of those roads was Danger and the name of the other was Destruction. So one of them took the road called Danger, which led him into a great wood; and the other went straight up the road to Destruction, which led him into a wide field full of dark mountains, where he stumbled and fell, and never got up again.

Then I looked after Christian, to watch him go up the hill; and I saw him drop from running to walking, and from walking to scrambling on his hands and knees, because the slope was so steep. Now about halfway up the hill there was a pleasant shelter, built by the Lord of the hill for the refreshment of weary travellers. Christian reached it, and sat down there to rest. Then he pulled his scroll out of his coat and read in it for comfort; and he also began looking over again the coat that had been given him as he stood by the cross. And pleasing himself like this for a while, he at last drifted into a doze, and from that into a deep sleep, which kept him there until it was almost night; and while he slept, his scroll fell out of his hand.

Now while he was sleeping, somebody came to him and woke him, saying, "Go to the ant, you sluggard; look at how she goes about things, and be wise." And at that Christian jumped up and hurried off on his way, and went at a good pace until he came to the top of the hill.

Now when he had got to the top, two men came running to meet him at full speed. One was called Timorous and the other Mistrust. Christian said to them: Gentlemen, what is the matter? You are running the wrong way.

Timorous answered that they had been going to the City of Zion and had got up that difficult stretch; but, he said, the further we go the more danger we run into, so we turned round and are going back.

Yes, said Mistrust — because just ahead of us there are a couple of lions in the road. Whether they were asleep or awake we could not tell, but we could not see how, if we came within reach of them, they would fail to tear us to pieces on the spot.

Christian. Then Christian said: You are frightening me — but where am I to run to, to be safe? If I go back to my own country, which is set aside for fire and brimstone, I shall certainly die there. If I can get to the Celestial City, I am certain to be safe there. I have to take the risk. Going back is nothing but death; going forward is the fear of death, and everlasting life beyond it. I am going on.

So Mistrust and Timorous ran off down the hill, and Christian went on his way. But thinking again about what he had heard from the two men, he felt inside his coat for his scroll, so that he could read in it and take comfort — and he felt, and could not find it.

Then Christian was badly shaken and had no idea what to do; because he had lost the thing that used to relieve him, and the thing that was meant to be his pass into the Celestial City. So there he stood, thoroughly confused, not knowing what to do. At last it occurred to him that he had fallen asleep in the shelter on the hillside; and going down on his knees, he asked God's forgiveness for that foolish act, and then went back to look for his scroll.

But who could possibly describe the misery in Christian's heart all the way back! Sometimes he sighed, sometimes he cried, and over and over again he told himself off for being fool enough to fall asleep in a place that had been put up only for a little refreshment when he was tired. So back he went, searching carefully to this side and that the whole way, hoping he might find the scroll that had comforted him so many times on his journey.

He went on like that until he came in sight of the shelter where he had sat down and slept — and the sight of it only renewed his misery, by bringing the wrong he had done in sleeping freshly back into his mind. So he came on, lamenting his sinful sleep, and saying: What a wretched man I am! To sleep in the daytime! To sleep in the middle of a hard climb! To indulge myself so far as to use for the ease of my own body a rest that the Lord of the hill put up only for the relief of pilgrims' spirits!

How many steps I have taken for nothing! It is what happened to Israel for their sin: they were sent back again by the way of the Red Sea. And I am made to walk in sorrow over ground I could have walked over with delight, if it had not been for this sinful sleep. How far along the road I could have been by now! I am made to walk the same stretch three times over when I need only have walked it once. And now night is going to catch me, too, because the day is nearly gone. Oh, if only I had not slept!

Now by this time he had come back to the shelter, where he sat down for a while and wept. But at last, as Christian's luck would have it, looking sorrowfully down under the bench, he spotted his scroll; and he snatched it up, trembling and hurried, and put it inside his coat.

But who can say how glad this man was to have his scroll again! Because this scroll was his assurance of life, and of being accepted when he reached the harbour he was making for. So he tucked it inside his coat, thanked God for turning his eye to the place where it lay, and with joy and tears set off on his journey again.

But how nimbly he went up the rest of that hill now! And yet, before he had got to the top, the sun went down on Christian; and that made him remember all over again how pointless his sleeping had been, and he began to feel sorry for himself once more.

Oh, you sinful sleep! Because of you I am going to be caught by the dark on my journey. I have to walk without the sun; darkness will cover the path under my feet; and I shall have to listen to the noise of the dismal creatures — all because of my sinful sleep.

And now he remembered too the story Mistrust and Timorous had told him, and how frightened they had been by the sight of the lions. So Christian said to himself again: These animals hunt at night. And if they were to come across me in the dark, how would I get away from them? How would I escape being torn to pieces by them?

So he went on his way. But while he was still lamenting his unhappy mistake, he lifted up his eyes — and there in front of him was a very grand palace, whose name was Beautiful, standing right beside the road.

The House Beautiful

So I saw in my dream that he hurried forward, hoping he might be able to get a bed there. Now before he had gone far he came into a very narrow passage, about a couple of hundred yards short of the porter's lodge; and looking sharply ahead as he went, he spotted two lions in the road. Now, he thought, I see the dangers that drove Mistrust and Timorous back. (The lions were chained, but he could not see the chains.) Then he was afraid, and he thought of turning back after them himself, because it seemed to him there was nothing but death ahead.

But the porter at the lodge, whose name is Watchful, seeing that Christian had stopped as though he meant to go back, called out to him: Is your strength as small as that? Do not be afraid of the lions — they are chained, and they are put there to test faith where there is any, and to expose those who have none. Keep to the middle of the path, and no harm will come to you.

Then I saw that he went on, shaking with fear of the lions but keeping careful hold of the porter's instructions. He heard them roar, but they did him no harm. Then he clapped his hands, and went on until he came and stood in front of the gate where the porter was.

Then Christian said to the porter: Sir, what house is this? And may I stay here tonight?
