The porter answered: This house was built by the Lord of the hill, and he built it for the relief and protection of pilgrims. And the porter also asked him where he was from, and where he was going.

Christian. I have come from the City of Destruction, and I am going to Mount Zion. But since the sun has now gone down, I would like to stay here tonight, if I may.

Porter. What is your name?

Christian. My name now is Christian, but at first my name was Graceless. I come of the line of Japheth, whom God will persuade to live in the tents of Shem.

Porter. But how does it happen that you come so late? The sun has set.

Christian. I would have been here sooner, except that — wretched man that I am — I fell asleep in the shelter that stands on the hillside. And even so I would have been here a good deal sooner, except that while I slept I lost my evidence, and came to the brow of the hill without it; and then, feeling for it and not finding it, I was forced to go back, sick at heart, to the place where I had slept my sleep. I found it there, and here I am.

Porter. Well, I will call out one of the young women of this house, who will, if she likes what you say, bring you in to meet the rest of the household, according to the rules here.

So Watchful the porter rang a bell, and at the sound of it there came out of the door of the house a serious and beautiful young woman named Discretion, who asked why she had been called.

The porter answered: This man is on a journey from the City of Destruction to Mount Zion; but being tired, and caught by the dark, he asked me if he might stay here tonight. So I told him I would call you, and that after you had talked with him you could do as you thought best, according to the rule of the house.

Then she asked him where he was from, and where he was going, and he told her. She also asked him how he had got onto the road, and he told her. Then she asked him what he had seen and met with along the way, and he told her. And last of all she asked him his name; and he said: It is Christian — and I want all the more to stay here tonight because, from what I understand, this place was built by the Lord of the hill for the relief and protection of pilgrims.

So she smiled, but there were tears in her eyes; and after a short pause she said: I will call out two or three more of the household. So she ran to the door and called out Prudence, Piety and Charity; and after a little more conversation with him, they brought him in to the household. Many of them met him on the doorstep, saying, "Come in, you who are blessed of the Lord" — this house was built by the Lord of the hill on purpose to take in pilgrims like you. Then he bowed his head and followed them into the house.

So when he had come in and sat down, they gave him something to drink, and agreed among themselves that until supper was ready some of them should have a proper conversation with Christian, to make the best use of the time. They appointed Piety, Prudence and Charity to talk with him, and this is how they began.

Piety. Come, good Christian, since we have been kind enough to take you into our house tonight, let us talk with you about everything that has happened to you on your pilgrimage — perhaps we may be the better for it ourselves.

Christian. Very willingly; and I am glad you are so well disposed.

Piety. What first moved you to take up a pilgrim's life?

Christian. I was driven out of my own country by a terrible sound in my ears — that unavoidable destruction was waiting for me if I stayed in the place where I was.

Piety. But how did it happen that you came out of your country this way?

Christian. It was as God would have it. When I was under the fear of destruction I did not know where to go; but as it happened a man came right up to me, while I stood there trembling and crying, whose name is Evangelist. He directed me to the little gate, which otherwise I should never have found, and so set me on the road that has brought me straight to this house.

Piety. But did you not come past the house of the Interpreter?

Christian. Yes, and I saw such things there that the memory of them will stay with me as long as I live — three of them especially: how Christ keeps up his work of grace in the heart in spite of Satan; how the man had sinned himself right out of any hope of God's mercy; and the dream of the man who thought in his sleep that the day of judgment had come.

Piety. Why — did you hear him tell his dream?

Christian. Yes, and a terrible one it was. I thought it made my heart ache to hear him telling it; and yet I am glad I heard it.

Piety. Was that all you saw at the house of the Interpreter?

Christian. No. He took me and showed me a magnificent palace, and the people inside it dressed in gold; and how a daring man came and cut his way through the armed men standing in the door to keep him out; and how he was called to come in and win eternal glory. I thought those things carried my heart away. I could have stayed at that good man's house a whole year, except that I knew I had further to go.

Piety. And what else did you see on the way?

Christian. See! Why, I went only a little further and I saw one — as I thought in my own mind — hanging bleeding on the tree. And the very sight of him made my burden fall off my back; because I was groaning under a very heavy burden, and then it dropped clean off me. It was a strange thing to me, because I had never seen anything like it before. And while I stood there looking up — for I could not stop looking — three Shining Ones came to me. One of them declared that my sins were forgiven; another stripped off my rags and gave me this embroidered coat that you can see; and the third set the mark you can see on my forehead, and gave me this sealed scroll. (And with that he pulled it out of his coat.)

Piety. But you saw more than that, did you not?

Christian. The things I have told you were the best of it. Still, I did see some other things. I saw three men — Simple, Sloth and Presumption — lying asleep a little off the road as I came, with irons on their heels; but do you think I could wake them? I also saw Formalist and Hypocrisy come tumbling over the wall, meaning to go to Zion, so they claimed; but they were soon lost, exactly as I told them they would be, though they would not believe it. But above all, I found it hard work getting up this hill, and just as hard getting past the lions' mouths. And truly, if it had not been for the good man, the porter who stands at the gate, I do not know but that after everything I might have turned back again. But now, thank God, I am here — and I thank you for taking me in.

Then Prudence thought it good to ask him a few questions, and asked him to answer them.

Prudence. Do you not sometimes think about the country you came from?

Christian. Yes — but with a great deal of shame and disgust. "Truly, if I had kept my mind on the country I came out of, I might have had the chance to go back; but as it is I want a better country, that is, a heavenly one."

Prudence. Do you still carry about with you some of the things you used to be caught up in back then?

Christian. Yes, but very much against my will — my inward and bodily thoughts above all, the ones that all my countrymen delighted in, and I along with them. But now every one of those things is a grief to me; and if I could have things my own way I would choose never to think about them again. And yet when I want to be doing what is best, what is worst is right there with me.

Prudence. Do you not find sometimes that those things seem beaten, though at other times they are your torment?

Christian. Yes, but only rarely. Still, they are golden hours to me, when it happens.

Prudence. Can you remember by what means you find your troubles beaten, at those times?

Christian. Yes. When I think of what I saw at the cross, that will do it. When I look at my embroidered coat, that will do it. When I look into the scroll I carry in my coat, that will do it. And when my thoughts grow warm about where I am going, that will do it.

Prudence. And what is it that makes you so eager to get to Mount Zion?

Christian. Why, there I hope to see alive the one who hung dead on the cross; and there I hope to be rid of all those things that are a torment to me even now. There, they say, there is no death; and there I shall live with exactly the company I like best. Because, to tell you the truth, I love him, since it was by him I was relieved of my burden; and I am sick of my own inward sickness. I would dearly like to be where I shall die no more, and with the company that goes on crying, "Holy, holy, holy."

Then Charity said to Christian: Have you a family? Are you a married man?

Christian. I have a wife and four small children.

Charity. And why did you not bring them along with you?

Christian. Then Christian wept, and said: Oh, how gladly I would have done it! But every one of them was dead set against my going on pilgrimage.

Charity. But you should have talked to them, and tried to show them the danger of staying behind.

Christian. So I did — and I told them too what God had shown me about the destruction of our city. "But I seemed to them like a man making a joke," and they did not believe me.

Charity. And did you pray that God would make your advice do them good?

Christian. Yes, and with a great deal of feeling; because you must understand that my wife and my poor children were very dear to me.

Charity. But did you tell them about your own misery, and your fear of destruction? Because I suppose the destruction was plain enough to you.

Christian. Yes, over and over and over. They could see my fears in my face too, and in my tears, and in the way I shook under the weight of the judgment hanging over our heads. But none of it was enough to persuade them to come with me.

Charity. But what could they say for themselves, as to why they would not come?

Christian. Why — my wife was afraid of losing this world, and my children were given up to the silly pleasures of being young. So between one thing and another, they left me to go wandering off like this on my own.

Charity. But did you not undercut everything you said to persuade them, by the emptiness of your own life?

Christian. I certainly cannot recommend my life; I am well aware of a great many failings in it. And I know too that a man's own conduct can quickly knock down what he is labouring by argument and persuasion to fix in other people's minds for their good. Still, I can say this much: I was very careful not to give them any excuse, by anything unfitting in my behaviour, to be put off going on pilgrimage. In fact it was that very thing they used to hold against me — that I was too strict, and that for their sakes I denied myself things they could see no harm in at all. No, I think I may say that if what they saw in me held them back, it was how careful I was about sinning against God, or doing any wrong to my neighbour.

Charity. Indeed, Cain hated his brother "because his own works were evil and his brother's were righteous"; and if your wife and children have been offended with you for this, they show by that they are set against what is good, and "you have delivered your own soul from their blood."

Now I saw in my dream that they sat there talking like this until supper was ready. So when it was prepared, they sat down to eat. Now the table was laid "with rich food, and with well-refined wine"; and all their talk at the table was about the Lord of the hill — what he had done, and why he had done what he did, and why he had built this house.

And from what they said, I gathered that he had been a great warrior, and had fought and killed "the one who had the power of death," but not without great danger to himself, which made me love him the more.

Because, as they said, and as I believe it too (said Christian), he did it at the cost of a great deal of blood. But what put the glory of grace into everything he did was that he did it out of pure love for his country. And besides, there were some of the household who said they had been and spoken with him since he died on the cross; and they testified they had it from his own lips that he is such a lover of poor pilgrims as you would not find from one end of the earth to the other.

They gave an example, too, of what they were saying: that he had stripped himself of his own glory so that he could do this for the poor; and that they had heard him say and insist "that he would not live on the mountain of Zion alone." They said, further, that he had made many pilgrims into princes, though they were born beggars by nature and had come off the rubbish heap.

So they talked together until late at night; and after they had committed themselves to their Lord for protection, they went to their rest. They put the Pilgrim in a large upstairs room whose window opened towards the sunrise; and the name of that room was Peace, where he slept until daybreak. Then he woke, and sang:

	Where am I now? Is this the love and care
	of Jesus for the men that pilgrims are?
	To be provided for like this — forgiven —
	and lodged already next door to heaven!

So in the morning they all got up; and after some more conversation they told him he was not to leave until they had shown him the treasures of the place.

First they took him into the study, where they showed him records of the greatest antiquity. In these, as I remember my dream, they showed him first the pedigree of the Lord of the hill: that he was the Son of the Ancient of Days, and came by that eternal descent. Here too were recorded more fully the things he had done, and the names of many hundreds he had taken into his service, and how he had settled them in dwellings that could not be pulled down either by length of years or by the wearing out of nature.

Then they read him some of the worthy deeds that his servants had done: how they had "conquered kingdoms, worked righteousness, obtained promises, stopped the mouths of lions, quenched the violence of fire, escaped the edge of the sword, out of weakness were made strong, grew brave in battle, and put foreign armies to flight."

Then they read again in another part of the records of the house, where it was shown how willing their Lord was to receive into his favour anybody at all — anybody — even those who in times past had offered great insults to him and to what he was doing. Here too were a number of other accounts of famous things, all of which Christian was given sight of, both ancient and modern, along with prophecies and predictions of things that are certain to come about, to the terror and astonishment of enemies and to the comfort and consolation of pilgrims.

The next day they took him into the armoury, where they showed him every kind of equipment their Lord had provided for pilgrims: sword, shield, helmet, breastplate, the weapon of all-prayer, and shoes that would never wear out. And there was enough of it here to equip as many men for their Lord's service as there are stars in the sky for number.

They also showed him some of the instruments with which some of his servants had done extraordinary things. They showed him Moses' staff; the hammer and nail with which Jael killed Sisera; the pitchers, trumpets and lamps too, with which Gideon put the armies of Midian to flight. Then they showed him the ox-goad with which Shamgar killed six hundred men. They showed him as well the jawbone with which Samson did such mighty deeds. They showed him, further, the sling and the stone with which David killed Goliath of Gath; and also the sword with which their Lord will kill the Man of Sin, on the day he rises up to take his prey. And they showed him a great many other excellent things besides, which delighted Christian very much.

When this was done, they went to their rest again.

The Valley of Humiliation and Apollyon

Then I saw in my dream that the next morning he got up to go on; but they asked him to stay another day as well. And then, they said, if the day is clear we will show you the Delectable Mountains — which, they said, would add still further to his comfort, because they were nearer the harbour he was making for than the place where he was now. So he agreed, and stayed.

When the morning came they took him to the top of the house and told him to look south. So he did — and there, a great way off, he saw a most beautiful mountainous country, made lovely with woods and vineyards and fruit of every kind, and flowers too, with springs and fountains, all delightful to look at. Then he asked the name of the country. They said it was Immanuel's Land; and it is common ground, they said, just as this hill is, to all pilgrims. And when you get there, they said, from there you will be able to see as far as the gate of the Celestial City, as the shepherds who live there will show you.

Now he thought about setting off, and they were willing that he should. But first, they said, let us go into the armoury again. So they did; and when they got there, they fitted him out from head to foot in tested armour, in case he should run into any attacks on the road. So being equipped like this, he walked out with his friends to the gate, and there he asked the porter whether he had seen any pilgrims go past.

The porter answered: Yes.

Christian. Please — did you know him? said he.

Porter. I asked his name, and he told me it was Faithful.

Christian. Oh, said Christian, I know him. He is from my own town, a close neighbour of mine; he comes from the place where I was born. How far ahead do you think he might be?

Porter. By this time he will have got below the hill.

Christian. Well, said Christian, good Porter, the Lord be with you, and add a great increase to all your blessings, for the kindness you have shown me.

Then he set off; but Discretion, Piety, Charity and Prudence would come with him down to the foot of the hill. So they went on together, going back over the things they had talked about before, until they came to the descent. Then Christian said: It was hard coming up, and as far as I can see it is dangerous going down.

Yes, said Prudence, so it is; because it is a hard business for a man to go down into the Valley of Humiliation, as you are doing now, and not slip somewhere along the way. That, they said, is why we have come out to see you down the hill.

So he began to go down, very carefully — and even so he slipped once or twice. Then I saw in my dream that when Christian had reached the bottom of the hill, these good companions gave him a loaf of bread, a bottle of wine and a bunch of raisins; and then he went on his way.

But now, in this Valley of Humiliation, poor Christian was in real trouble; because he had gone only a little way when he spotted a foul fiend coming across the field to meet him, whose name is Apollyon. Then Christian began to be afraid, and to turn over in his mind whether to go back or stand his ground. But then he considered that he had no armour for his back, and so he thought that turning his back might only give the fiend the greater advantage, and let him drive his darts in easily. So he decided to risk it and stand his ground. Because, he thought, even if I had nothing in view but saving my own life, standing would still be the best way.

So he went on, and Apollyon met him. Now the monster was hideous to look at. He was covered in scales like a fish — and they are his pride. He had wings like a dragon, feet like a bear, and out of his belly came fire and smoke, and his mouth was like the mouth of a lion. When he had come up to Christian, he looked him over with contempt and began to question him.

Apollyon. Where have you come from, and where are you bound?

Christian. I have come from the City of Destruction, which is the home of everything evil, and I am going to the City of Zion.

Apollyon. By this I gather you are one of my subjects, because all that country is mine, and I am its prince and god. How is it, then, that you have run away from your king? If I did not hope you might do me more service yet, I would strike you to the ground now, with a single blow.

Christian. I was born in your territory, it is true; but your service was hard, and your wages were not enough for a man to live on — "for the wages of sin is death." So when I came of age I did what other thoughtful people do, and looked about to see whether I could better myself.

Apollyon. There is no prince who will lose his subjects as lightly as that, and I do not intend to lose you yet. But since you complain of your service and your wages, be content to come back; and I promise you here whatever our country can provide.

Christian. But I have hired myself out to another — to the King of princes; and how can I go back with you in any honesty?

Apollyon. In this you have done what the proverb says — swapped a bad master for a worse. But it is quite ordinary for people who have declared themselves his servants to give him the slip after a while and come back to me. You do the same, and all will be well.

Christian. I have given him my word and sworn my allegiance to him. How, then, can I go back on it and not be hanged as a traitor?

Apollyon. You did just the same to me — and yet I am willing to let it all go, if you will only turn round now and come back.

Christian. What I promised you, I promised when I was under age. And besides, I reckon the Prince under whose flag I now stand is able to release me from it — yes, and to pardon what I did in going along with you as well. And besides that, you destroying Apollyon: to tell you the truth, I like his service, his wages, his servants, his government, his company and his country better than yours. So stop trying to persuade me. I am his servant, and I mean to follow him.

Apollyon. Think again, once your blood has cooled, about what you are likely to meet on the road you are taking. You know that for the most part his servants come to a bad end, because they are offenders against me and against my ways. How many of them have been put to shameful deaths! And besides, you reckon his service better than mine, when he has never yet come out from the place where he is to deliver anybody who served him out of our hands — whereas I, as all the world knows perfectly well, have delivered any number of those who have faithfully served me, either by force or by trickery, out of his hands and his people's, even after they were captured. And I will deliver you too.

Christian. If he holds off from delivering them for the present, it is on purpose to test their love, and see whether they will hold to him to the end. And as for the bad end you say they come to, in their own reckoning that is the most glorious thing of all. Because as for being delivered here and now, they do not much expect it: they are waiting for their glory, and then they will have it, when their Prince comes in his own glory and the glory of the angels.

Apollyon. You have already been unfaithful in your service to him. How do you expect to get wages out of him?

Christian. In what way, Apollyon, have I been unfaithful to him?

Apollyon. You lost your nerve at the very start, when you were nearly choked in the Slough of Despond. You tried wrong roads to be rid of your burden, when you should have waited until your Prince took it off. You sinfully fell asleep and lost your most precious possession. You were very nearly talked into turning back at the sight of the lions. And when you talk about your journey and about what you have seen and heard, there is a hunger for glory in you, in everything you say and do.

Christian. All this is true, and a good deal more that you have left out. But the Prince I serve and honour is merciful and ready to forgive. And besides — these weaknesses took hold of me in your country. That is where I drank them in. And I have groaned under them, and been sorry for them, and have been pardoned by my Prince.

Apollyon. Then Apollyon broke out into a terrible rage, saying: I am an enemy to this Prince. I hate his person, his laws and his people. I have come out on purpose to stop you.

Christian. Apollyon, be careful what you do. I am on the king's highway, the way of holiness; so look to yourself.

Apollyon. Then Apollyon straddled right across the whole width of the road and said: I have no fear at all in this matter. Prepare to die; because I swear by my den in hell that you shall go no further. Here is where I spill your soul.

And with that he threw a flaming dart at his chest. But Christian had a shield in his hand, and caught it with that, and so escaped the danger.

Then Christian drew his sword, because he saw it was time to move. And Apollyon came at him just as fast, throwing darts as thick as hail; and by them, in spite of everything Christian could do to dodge, Apollyon wounded him in the head, the hand and the foot. This made Christian give a little ground; so Apollyon pressed his attack with full force, and Christian took heart again and fought back as hard as he could.

This bitter fight lasted more than half a day, until Christian was nearly spent — because you have to understand that Christian, with his wounds, was bound to grow weaker and weaker.

Then Apollyon, seeing his chance, closed right in on Christian, and wrestling with him, threw him down hard; and with that, Christian's sword flew out of his hand. Then Apollyon said: I have you now. And with that he almost crushed him to death, so that Christian began to give up hope of living.

But as God would have it, while Apollyon was drawing back for his last blow, meant to finish this good man off altogether, Christian nimbly stretched out his hand for his sword and got hold of it, saying, "Do not gloat over me, my enemy; when I fall, I shall get up again." And with that he gave him a deadly thrust, which drove him back like a creature that has taken its death wound. Christian, seeing that, went at him again, saying, "No — in all these things we are more than conquerors, through him who loved us." And with that Apollyon spread out his dragon's wings and sped away, so that Christian saw him no more for a while.

Nobody can imagine, unless he had seen and heard it as I did, what yelling and hideous roaring Apollyon made all through this fight. He spoke like a dragon. And on the other side, what sighs and groans burst out of Christian's heart. I never once saw him give so much as a single cheerful look the whole time, until he realized that he had wounded Apollyon with his two-edged sword; and then, indeed, he did smile and look upward. But it was the most terrible sight I ever saw.

So when the battle was over, Christian said, "Here I will give thanks to the one who delivered me out of the mouth of the lion — to the one who helped me against Apollyon." And so he did, saying:

	Great Beelzebub, the captain of this fiend,
	planned my destruction, and to that same end
	sent him out armed; and he, with such a rage
	as only hell can give, joined me in battle.
	But blessed Michael helped me, and I,
	with a stroke of the sword, soon made him fly.
	So let me give him praise that has no end,
	and bless his holy name for evermore.

Then there came to him a hand, holding some of the leaves of the tree of life. Christian took them and put them on the wounds he had taken in the battle, and was healed immediately. He also sat down in that place to eat some bread and drink from the bottle that had been given him a little earlier. And so, refreshed, he set himself to his journey with his sword drawn in his hand; because he said, I do not know but that some other enemy may be close by. But he had no further trouble from Apollyon all the way through this valley.

Now at the end of this valley there was another, the Valley of the Shadow of Death; and Christian had no choice but to go through it, because the road to the Celestial City lay right through the middle of it. Now this valley is a very lonely place. The prophet Jeremiah describes it like this: "A wilderness, a land of deserts and of pits, a land of drought and of the shadow of death, a land that nobody" — nobody but a Christian — "passed through, and where nobody lived."

And here Christian was in worse trouble than he had been in his fight with Apollyon, as you will see from what followed.

The Valley of the Shadow of Death

Then I saw in my dream that when Christian had got to the edge of the Shadow of Death, two men met him — sons of the men who brought back a bad report of the good land — hurrying to go back the way they had come. Christian spoke to them like this.

Christian. Where are you going?

The men. They said: Back! Back! And we would advise you to do the same, if you set any value on either your life or your peace.

Christian. Why? What is the matter? said Christian.

The men. The matter! they said. We were going that way, the way you are going, and we went as far as we dared; and in fact we were nearly past the point of coming back at all. If we had gone a little further, we would not be here to bring you the news.

Christian. But what did you run into? said Christian.

The men. Why, we were nearly into the Valley of the Shadow of Death; only, luckily, we looked ahead of us and saw the danger before we got to it.

Christian. But what did you see? said Christian.

The men. See! Why, the valley itself, which is as black as pitch. We also saw the hobgoblins there, and satyrs, and dragons of the pit. And we heard in that valley a constant howling and yelling, like people in misery beyond words, sitting there bound in irons and affliction. And over that valley hang the discouraging clouds of confusion. Death is always spreading his wings over it too. In a word, the whole of it is terrible, and it has no order in it at all.

Christian. Then Christian said: From everything you have told me, I still see nothing to show that this is not my road to the harbour I want.

The men. Let it be your road; we will not choose it for ours.

So they parted, and Christian went on his way — but still with his sword drawn in his hand, for fear of being attacked.

Then I saw in my dream that for the whole length of this valley, there was on the right-hand side a very deep ditch. That ditch is the one into which the blind have led the blind in every age, and both of them have died miserably in it. And on the left-hand side there was a very dangerous bog, into which, if even a good man falls, he can find no bottom for his foot to stand on. King David once fell into that bog, and would no doubt have been smothered in it, if the one who is able had not pulled him out.

The path here was also extremely narrow, and so good Christian was all the harder pressed. Because when he tried in the dark to keep clear of the ditch on the one side, he was on the point of tipping over into the mud on the other; and when he tried to get away from the mud, without the greatest care he was ready to fall into the ditch. So he went on; and I heard him sighing bitterly here, because besides the dangers I have described, the path was so dark that time and again, when he lifted his foot to step forward, he had no idea where or on what he would be setting it down.

About the middle of this valley I made out the mouth of hell, and it stood right beside the road as well. Now, thought Christian, what am I going to do? And every now and then the flame and the smoke would come pouring out, with sparks and hideous noises — things that had no fear of Christian's sword the way Apollyon had — so that he was forced to put his sword away and take up another weapon, called All-prayer. So he cried out, in my hearing: "Lord, I beg you, deliver my soul!"

He went on like this for a long while, and still the flames kept reaching towards him. He heard mournful voices too, and rushing to and fro, so that sometimes he thought he was going to be torn to pieces, or trodden down like mud in the street. This terrifying sight was in front of him, and these dreadful noises in his ears, for several miles together.

And coming to a place where he thought he heard a company of fiends coming forward to meet him, he stopped and began to think out what he had best do. Sometimes he half thought of going back; then again he thought he might be halfway through the valley by now. He remembered too how many dangers he had already beaten, and that the danger of going back might be a good deal greater than going on. So he decided to keep going. And yet the fiends seemed to come nearer and nearer; but when they had got almost on top of him, he cried out in a tremendous voice, "I will walk in the strength of the Lord God!" — and at that they fell back, and came no further.

There is one thing I would not want to let slip past. I noticed that poor Christian was now so bewildered that he did not know his own voice; and this is how I could tell. Just as he came opposite the mouth of the burning pit, one of the wicked ones got in behind him and stepped up softly to him, and whispered a great many terrible blasphemies at him — which he genuinely believed had come out of his own mind. This put Christian to harder straits than anything he had met with before: to think that he was now blaspheming the one he had loved so much until then. And yet if he could have stopped it he would not have done it for anything; only he did not have the presence of mind either to stop his ears or to work out where these blasphemies were coming from.

When Christian had travelled in this wretched state for a considerable time, he thought he heard the voice of a man ahead of him on the road, saying, "Though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil; for you are with me."

Then he was glad, and for these reasons.

First, because he gathered from it that there were others in this valley who feared God, besides himself.

Second, because he understood that God was with them, even in that dark and dismal place. And why not with me, he thought — even though, because of what this place puts in the way, I cannot make it out.

Third, because he hoped that if he could catch up with them he would have company before long.

So he went on, and called out to the man ahead of him. But the man did not know what to answer, because he thought he was alone too. And before long, day broke. Then Christian said: He has turned "the shadow of death into the morning."

Now that morning had come, he looked back — not out of any wish to go back, but to see by daylight what dangers he had come through in the dark. So he saw more clearly the ditch on the one hand and the bog on the other, and how narrow the road was that ran between the two of them. And now he saw the hobgoblins, and the satyrs, and the dragons of the pit as well — but all a long way off, because after daybreak they did not come near. Still, they were shown to him, as it is written: "He uncovers deep things out of darkness, and brings the shadow of death out into the light."

Now Christian was deeply moved at having been delivered from all the dangers of his lonely road. He had feared them more before, and yet he saw them more clearly now, because the daylight made them plain to him.

And about this time the sun was rising, and this was another mercy to Christian. Because you have to notice that although the first part of the Valley of the Shadow of Death was dangerous, this second part, which he still had to go, was if anything far more dangerous. From the place where he now stood right to the end of the valley, the road was set so thick with snares, traps, springs and nets on the one hand, and so full of pits, pitfalls, deep holes and sudden drops on the other, that if it had been dark now, as it was when he came through the first part, then had he a thousand souls, by any reasonable reckoning they would all have been thrown away. But, as I said, the sun was just now rising. So he said: "His lamp shines on my head, and by his light I walk through the darkness."

So he came to the end of the valley in this light. Now I saw in my dream that at the end of this valley there lay blood, bones, ashes and the mangled bodies of men — pilgrims, in fact, who had come this way before. And while I was wondering what the reason for it might be, I spotted a little ahead of me a cave, where two giants, Pope and Pagan, had lived in the old days; and it was by their power and tyranny that the men whose bones and blood and ashes lay there had been cruelly put to death.

But Christian went past this place without much danger, which surprised me somewhat. Since then, though, I have learned that Pagan has been dead many a long day; and as for the other, although he is still alive, he has grown so decrepit and so stiff in the joints — partly from age, and partly from the many sharp maulings he took in his younger days — that he can now do little more than sit at the mouth of his cave, grinning at pilgrims as they go past and biting his nails because he cannot get at them.

Faithful

So I saw that Christian went on his way. Yet at the sight of the Old Man sitting in the mouth of the cave he did not know what to think — especially because the giant spoke to him, though he could not come after him, saying, "You will never learn until more of you are burned." But Christian held his tongue and put a good face on it, and so went past and took no harm. Then Christian sang:

	What a world of wonders — I can say no less —
	that I should have been kept safe through the distress
	I met with here. Blessed for ever be
	the hand that has delivered me from it.
	Dangers in darkness, devils, hell and sin
	closed round me while I was that valley in;
	snares, pits and traps and nets lay all about
	my path, that worthless, silly I might be
	caught, and entangled, and thrown down for good.
	But since I live — let Jesus wear the crown.

Now as Christian went on his way, he came to a slight rise that had been thrown up on purpose so that pilgrims could see ahead of them. So Christian went up it; and looking forward, he saw Faithful ahead of him on the road. Then Christian called out: Hey! Hello there! Wait, and I will be your companion.

At that, Faithful looked behind him; and Christian called out again: Wait, wait, until I catch up with you.

But Faithful answered: No — I am running for my life, and the avenger of blood is behind me.

At this Christian was rather stung, and putting on all his strength he quickly came up with Faithful, and in fact overtook him; so the last was first. Then Christian smiled a rather self-satisfied smile at having got the better of his brother — and then, not watching his feet properly, he suddenly tripped and fell, and could not get up again until Faithful came along to help him.

Then I saw in my dream that they went on together very affectionately, and had a good long talk about everything that had happened to them on their pilgrimage; and Christian began.

Christian. My honoured and much-loved brother Faithful, I am glad I have caught up with you, and that God has matched our tempers so well that we can walk as companions along such a pleasant stretch of road.

Faithful. I had hoped, dear friend, to have had your company all the way from our town; but you got a start on me, and so I have been forced to come this much of the road alone.

Christian. How long did you stay in the City of Destruction before you set out after me on your pilgrimage?

Faithful. Until I could not stay any longer. There was a great deal of talk, right after you had gone out, that our city would shortly be burned to the ground with fire from heaven.

Christian. What — did your neighbours talk like that?

Faithful. Yes, for a while it was in everybody's mouth.

Christian. What! And did nobody but you come out to escape the danger?

Faithful. Although there was a great deal of talk about it, as I say, I do not think they really believed it. Because in the heat of the discussion I heard some of them speak sneeringly about you, and about your desperate journey — which is what they called this pilgrimage of yours. But I did believe it, and I believe still, that the end of our city will come with fire and brimstone from above; and so I made my escape.

Christian. Did you hear any talk about neighbour Pliable?

Faithful. Yes, Christian. I heard he followed you until he came to the Slough of Despond, where, so some said, he fell in — though he would not have it known that he had. But I am quite sure he was thoroughly spattered with that particular kind of mud.

Christian. And what did the neighbours say to him?

Faithful. Ever since he went back he has been an object of ridicule, and among every sort of person. Some mock him and look down on him, and hardly anybody will give him work. He is now seven times worse off than if he had never left the city at all.

Christian. But why should they be so set against him, when they despise the road he abandoned too?

Faithful. Oh — they say, hang him, he is a turncoat; he was not true to what he claimed to believe. I think God has stirred up even his enemies to hiss at him and make him a byword, because he abandoned the road.

Christian. Did you have any conversation with him before you came out?

Faithful. I met him once in the street, but he slunk off to the other side, like a man ashamed of what he had done; so I did not speak to him.

Christian. Well, when I first set out I had hopes of that man. But now I am afraid he will die in the destruction of the city. Because what has happened to him is exactly what the true proverb says: "The dog has gone back to its own vomit, and the sow that was washed to her wallowing in the mud."

Faithful. Those are my fears about him too. But who can stop what is going to be?

Christian. Well, neighbour Faithful, said Christian, let us leave him and talk about things that concern us more directly. Tell me now what you have met with on the road as you came; because I know you must have met with something, or else it would be a wonder worth writing down.

Faithful. I got clear of the Slough that I gather you fell into, and got up to the gate without that danger. Only I met with one whose name was Wanton, who came near to doing me real harm.

Christian. It was well for you that you got out of her net. Joseph was hard put to it by her, and he escaped her as you did — but it nearly cost him his life. What did she do to you?

Faithful. You cannot imagine — well, you know something of it — what a flattering tongue she had. She worked at me hard to turn aside with her, promising me every kind of satisfaction.

Christian. No — she did not promise you the satisfaction of a good conscience.

Faithful. You know what I mean. Every satisfaction of the body.

Christian. Thank God you escaped her. "The man the Lord detests shall fall into her ditch."

Faithful. Well — I am not sure whether I escaped her altogether or not.

Christian. Why, I trust you did not give in to what she wanted?

Faithful. No — not so far as defiling myself. Because I remembered an old piece of writing I had seen, which said, "Her steps take hold on hell." So I shut my eyes, because I did not want to be bewitched by her looks. Then she screamed abuse at me, and I went on my way.

Christian. Did you meet with no other attack as you came?

Faithful. When I came to the foot of the hill called Difficulty, I met a very old man who asked me what I was and where I was bound. I told him I was a pilgrim going to the Celestial City. Then the old man said: You look like an honest fellow. Would you be content to live with me for the wages I will give you? Then I asked his name and where he lived. He said his name was Adam the First, and that he lived in the town of Deceit.

Then I asked him what his work was, and what the wages would be. He told me his work was any number of delights, and that his wages were that I should be his heir at the end. I asked him further what kind of house he kept and what other servants he had. So he told me his house was kept up with all the delicacies in the world, and that his servants were of his own fathering. Then I asked whether he had any children. He said he had only three daughters — the Lust of the Flesh, the Lust of the Eyes and the Pride of Life — and that I could marry all three of them if I liked. Then I asked how long he would want me to live with him; and he told me, as long as he lived himself.

Christian. Well — and what did the old man and you settle on in the end?

Faithful. Why, at first I found myself rather inclined to go with him, because I thought he talked very fair. But looking at his forehead while I was talking with him, I saw written there: "Put off the old man with his deeds."

Christian. And what then?

Faithful. Then it came into my mind, burning hot, that whatever he said and however he flattered, once he had got me home to his house he would sell me for a slave. So I told him to stop talking, because I would not go near the door of his house. Then he abused me, and told me he would send somebody after me who would make my road bitter to my soul.

So I turned to go away from him. But just as I turned to leave, I felt him take hold of my flesh and give me such a deadly wrench backwards that I thought he had pulled part of me away with him. It made me cry out, "What a wretched man I am!" So I went on my way up the hill.

Now when I had got about halfway up, I looked behind me and saw somebody coming after me, swift as the wind; and he caught up with me just about where the bench stands.

Christian. Just there, said Christian, is where I sat down to rest; and being overcome with sleep, that is where I lost this scroll out of my coat.

Faithful. But hear me out, good brother. As soon as the man caught up with me, it was a word and a blow — down he knocked me and left me for dead. And when I had come round a little, I asked him why he had treated me so. He said, because of my secret leaning towards Adam the First; and with that he struck me another deadly blow on the chest and knocked me over backwards, so that I lay at his feet as dead as before.

So when I came round again I begged him for mercy; but he said, I do not know how to show mercy — and with that knocked me down again. He would certainly have finished me off, except that One came by and told him to stop.

Christian. Who was it that told him to stop?

Faithful. I did not know him at first; but as he went past I saw the holes in his hands and in his side, and then I concluded he was our Lord. So I went on up the hill.

Christian. The man who caught up with you was Moses. He spares nobody, and he does not know how to show mercy to those who break his law.

Faithful. I know it very well. It was not the first time he has met with me. He was the one who came to me when I was living comfortably at home, and told me he would burn my house down over my head if I stayed there.

Christian. But did you not see the house that stands there at the top of the hill, on the side where Moses met you?

Faithful. Yes, and the lions too, before I got to it. But as for the lions, I think they were asleep — it was about midday. And since I had so much of the day still ahead of me, I went past the porter and came on down the hill.

Christian. He did tell me that he saw you go by. But I wish you had called in at the house, because they would have shown you so many remarkable things that you would hardly have forgotten them to your dying day. But tell me, please — did you meet nobody in the Valley of Humiliation?

Faithful. Yes. I met one Discontent, who would gladly have talked me into going back with him. His argument was that there was no honour to be had in that valley at all. He told me, further, that going down there was a way of disobeying all my friends — Pride, Arrogance, Self-conceit, Worldly-glory and the rest, who, he said he knew, would be very much offended if I made such a fool of myself as to wade through this valley.

Christian. Well — and how did you answer him?

Faithful. I told him that although everybody he named might claim to be related to me, and rightly, since they really were my relations by blood, still, since I became a pilgrim they have disowned me, just as I have rejected them; and so they were no more to me now than if they had never been of my family at all.

I told him too that as far as this valley went, he had thoroughly misrepresented the thing; "for humility comes before honour, and a haughty spirit before a fall." So I said, I would rather go through this valley to the honour that the wisest people reckon as honour, than choose the thing he thought most worth our affection.

Christian. Did you meet with nothing else in that valley?

Faithful. Yes — I met with Shame. But of all the men I met on my pilgrimage, he is the one I think is wrongly named. The others would take no for an answer after a little argument and a bit besides; but this brazen-faced Shame would never be finished.

Christian. Why — what did he say to you?

Faithful. What! Why, he attacked religion itself. He said it was a pitiful, low, sneaking business for a man to give his mind to religion. He said a tender conscience was an unmanly thing, and that for a man to watch his words and his behaviour, and tie himself up from the swaggering freedom that the fine spirits of the age go in for, would make him the laughing stock of the times. He objected too that hardly any of the powerful, the rich or the clever had ever been of my opinion — nor any of them at all, until they had been talked into being fools, and into a deliberate foolishness that risks losing everything for nobody knows what. He also objected to the low and humble station of most of the pilgrims of their day, and to their ignorance and their lack of any education in the sciences.

Yes, and he pressed me at that rate about a great many more things than I am telling you here: that it was a shame to sit whining and mourning under a sermon, and a shame to come home sighing and groaning; that it was a shame to ask my neighbour's forgiveness for small faults, or to make things good where I had taken something from anyone. He said too that religion made a man stand off from the great, on account of a few vices, which he called by finer names; and made him acknowledge and respect the low, because they belonged to the same religious brotherhood. And is that not a shame? he said.

Christian. And what did you say to him?

Faithful. Say! At first I could not tell what to say. He put me to it so hard that the blood came up into my face — this Shame fetched it up, and very nearly beat me off altogether. But at last I began to consider that "what is highly valued among men is detestable in the sight of God." And I thought again: this Shame tells me what men are, but it tells me nothing about what God is, or what the Word of God is. And I thought, further, that on the day of judgment we shall not be sentenced to death or to life according to the swaggering spirits of this world, but according to the wisdom and the law of the Highest.

So, I thought, what God says is best really is best, even if every man in the world is against it. Seeing, then, that God prefers his own religion; seeing that God prefers a tender conscience; seeing that those who make fools of themselves for the kingdom of heaven are the wisest of all, and that a poor man who loves Christ is richer than the greatest man in the world who hates him — Shame, be off with you; you are an enemy to my salvation. Am I to take you in, against my sovereign Lord? How would I look him in the face when he comes? If I am ashamed of his ways and his servants now, how can I expect his blessing?

But this Shame really was a bold villain. I could hardly shake him out of my company; he would come haunting me, whispering in my ear about one or another of the weaknesses that go along with religion. But at last I told him it was no use trying any further with me, because the very things he despised were the ones in which I saw the most glory. And so at last I got past this pestering creature. And when I had shaken him off, I began to sing:

	The trials that those men meet with on the way
	who are obedient to the heavenly call
	are many, and are fitted to the flesh,
	and come, and come, and come again afresh,
	so that now, or at some other time, we may
	be taken by them, beaten, thrown away.
	So let the pilgrims, let the pilgrims then
	be on their guard, and quit themselves like men.

Christian. I am glad, my brother, that you stood up to this villain so bravely; because of all of them, as you say, I think he is the one with the wrong name. He is so bold that he will follow us in the street and try to shame us in front of everybody — that is, to make us ashamed of what is good. And if he were not so brazen himself, he would never attempt what he does. But let us keep on resisting him; because for all his swagger, he advances nobody but the fool. "The wise shall inherit glory," Solomon said, "but shame shall be the promotion of fools."

Faithful. I think we shall have to cry out for help against Shame to the one who wants us to be valiant for the truth on the earth.

Christian. You are right. But did you meet nobody else in that valley?

Faithful. No, nobody — because I had sunshine the whole of the rest of the way through it, and through the Valley of the Shadow of Death as well.

Christian. It was well for you. I am sure it went very differently with me. Almost as soon as I entered that valley, I had a long and terrible fight with that foul fiend Apollyon. In fact I genuinely thought he would have killed me, especially when he got me down and crushed me under him as though he meant to crush me to pieces; because as he threw me, my sword flew out of my hand. He even told me he had me for certain. But I cried out to God, and he heard me and delivered me out of all my troubles. Then I went into the Valley of the Shadow of Death, and had no light for almost half the way through it. I thought over and over that I was going to be killed there. But at last day broke and the sun rose, and I went through what was left of it with far more ease and quiet.

Talkative

Moreover, I saw in my dream that as they went on, Faithful happened to look to one side and saw a man whose name is Talkative walking a little way off beside them; because in this place there was room enough for them all to walk. He was a tall man, and rather better-looking at a distance than close to. Faithful spoke to him like this.

Faithful. Friend, where are you off to? Are you going to the heavenly country?

Talkative. I am going to the same place.

Faithful. That is good; then I hope we may have the pleasure of your company.

Talkative. I shall be very glad indeed to be your companion.

Faithful. Come along, then, and let us go together, and spend our time discussing things that will do us good.

Talkative. Talking about good things is very agreeable to me, whether with you or with anybody else; and I am glad to have met people who are inclined to such a good occupation. Because, to tell you the truth, there are very few who care to spend their time that way as they travel — most would far rather be talking about things that do nobody any good. And that has been a grief to me.

Faithful. That is indeed something to be sorry about. Because what things on earth are so worthy of a man's tongue and mouth as the things of the God of heaven?

Talkative. I like you enormously, because what you say carries real conviction. And I will add: what is so pleasant, and what so profitable, as talking about the things of God? What things are so pleasant — that is, if a man takes any delight in things that are wonderful? For instance, if a man delights in talking about the history of things, or the mystery of them; or if a man loves to talk of miracles, wonders and signs — where will he find things recorded so delightfully, and so sweetly written, as in the Holy Scriptures?

Faithful. That is true. But being made better by such things in our talk should be what we are aiming at.

Talkative. That is exactly what I said. Because talking about such things is most profitable; a man may get knowledge of a great many things that way — of the emptiness of earthly things, for instance, and the value of the things above. That is speaking generally; but more particularly, by this a man may learn the necessity of the new birth, the insufficiency of our own works, the need of Christ's righteousness, and so on. Besides that, by talk a man may learn what it is to repent, to believe, to pray, to suffer, and the like. By this too a man may learn what the great promises and consolations of the Gospel are, to his own comfort. And further, by this a man may learn to refute false opinions, to defend the truth, and to instruct the ignorant.

Faithful. All this is true, and I am glad to hear these things from you.

Talkative. Alas — it is the lack of it that is the reason so few understand the need of faith, and the necessity of a work of grace in their souls if they are to have eternal life, but live on in ignorance under the works of the law, by which no man can possibly obtain the kingdom of heaven.

Faithful. But, if you will allow me — a heavenly knowledge of these things is the gift of God. No man arrives at it by his own effort, or merely by talking about them.

Talkative. All this I know very well. Because a man can receive nothing unless it is given him from heaven; it is all of grace, and not of works. I could give you a hundred scriptures to confirm it.

Faithful. Well then, said Faithful, what is the one subject we shall build our conversation on this time?

Talkative. Whatever you like. I will talk of things heavenly or things earthly; things moral or things evangelical; things sacred or things profane; things past or things to come; things foreign or things at home; things more essential or things merely circumstantial — so long as it is all done to our profit.

Faithful. Now Faithful began to wonder; and stepping over to Christian, who had been walking by himself all this while, he said to him quietly: What a splendid companion we have got! Surely this man will make an excellent pilgrim.

Christian. At this Christian smiled modestly, and said: This man you are so taken with will fool twenty people who do not know him, with that tongue of his.

Faithful. Do you know him, then?

Christian. Know him! Yes — better than he knows himself.

Faithful. Tell me — what is he?

Christian. His name is Talkative. He lives in our town. I am surprised you should be a stranger to him — though I suppose our town is a large one.

Faithful. Whose son is he? And whereabouts does he live?

Christian. He is the son of a man called Say-well, and he lived in Prating Row; and everybody who knows him knows him as Talkative of Prating Row. And for all his fine tongue, he is a poor specimen.

Faithful. Well, he seems a very fine man.

Christian. That is to people who do not know him thoroughly. He is at his best away from home; near home he is ugly enough. Your saying he is a fine man puts me in mind of something I have noticed in painters' work: some pictures look their best at a distance, and are a good deal less pleasant close up.

Faithful. But I am inclined to think you are only joking, because you smiled.

Christian. God forbid that I should joke about this — although I did smile — or that I should accuse anybody falsely. Let me tell you more about him. This man is for any company and any kind of talk. Just as he is talking with you now, so he will talk on the alehouse bench; and the more drink he has in his head, the more of these things he has in his mouth. Religion has no place in his heart, or his house, or his way of life. Everything he has is in his tongue, and his religion consists of making a noise with it.

Faithful. Do you say so? Then I have been badly taken in by this man.

Christian. Taken in! You may be sure of it. Remember the proverb, "They say, and do not." But "the kingdom of God is not in word, but in power." He talks about prayer, about repentance, about faith and about the new birth — but talking about them is all he can do. I have been in his family, and I have watched him both at home and away from it, and I know that what I am telling you about him is the truth. His house is as empty of religion as the white of an egg is of flavour. There is no prayer there, and no sign of any repentance for sin. In fact the animals, according to their kind, serve God far better than he does. He is the stain, the reproach and the shame of religion to everybody who knows him; religion can hardly get a good word said for it in that whole end of town, because of him. This is what the ordinary people who know him say: a saint abroad and a devil at home.

His poor family find it so. He is such a boor, such an abuser of his servants and so unreasonable with them, that they neither know how to do anything for him nor how to speak to him. People who have any business dealings with him say they would rather deal with a Turk than with him, because they would get fairer treatment. This Talkative will go one better than any of them, if he possibly can — cheat them, trick them and get the better of them. Besides that, he brings his sons up to follow in his footsteps; and if he finds in any of them what he calls a foolish timidity — which is his name for the first stirrings of a tender conscience — he calls them fools and blockheads, and will not employ them in anything much, or speak well of them in front of others. For my part, I believe that by his wicked life he has caused many people to stumble and fall, and that unless God prevents it he will be the ruin of many more.

Faithful. Well, my brother, I am bound to believe you — not only because you say you know him, but because you give your account of people like a Christian. I cannot think you say these things out of ill-will, but because it really is as you say.

Christian. If I had known him no better than you do, I might perhaps have thought of him as you did at first. And what is more, if this report had come only from people who are enemies of religion, I should have taken it for slander — the sort of thing that often falls from bad men's mouths onto good men's names and reputations. But all these things, and a great many more just as bad, I can prove him guilty of from my own knowledge. Besides, good men are ashamed of him. They can call him neither brother nor friend; the very mention of his name among them makes them blush, if they know him.

Faithful. Well, I see that saying and doing are two different things, and in future I shall keep a better eye on that distinction.

Christian. They are two different things indeed, and as different as the soul and the body. Because just as the body without the soul is nothing but a dead carcass, so saying, if it stands alone, is a dead carcass too. The soul of religion is the practical part: "Pure and undefiled religion before God the Father is this: to visit the fatherless and the widows in their affliction, and to keep oneself unstained by the world." Talkative has no idea of this. He thinks that hearing and saying will make a good Christian, and so he deceives his own soul.

Hearing is only the sowing of the seed; and talking is not enough to prove that there is any fruit in the heart and the life. And let us be quite sure of it: on the day of judgment men will be judged according to their fruit. It will not be asked then, did you believe? — but, were you doers, or talkers only? And they will be judged accordingly. The end of the world is compared to our harvest; and you know that at harvest men take notice of nothing but the fruit. Not that anything can be accepted that does not come from faith; but I say this to show you how worthless Talkative's profession will be on that day.

Faithful. That brings to my mind what Moses says, when he describes the clean animal. It is one that both divides the hoof and chews the cud — not one that only divides the hoof, or only chews the cud. The hare chews the cud, and yet it is unclean, because it does not divide the hoof. And that really is a picture of Talkative. He chews the cud: he goes looking for knowledge, he chews over the word. But he does not divide the hoof; he does not part company with the way of sinners. Like the hare, he keeps the foot of a dog or a bear, and so he is unclean.

Christian. You have given, as far as I know, the true Gospel sense of those texts. And I will add another thing. Paul calls some men — and great talkers too — "sounding brass and a clanging cymbal"; which is, as he explains it elsewhere, "lifeless things giving sound." Lifeless things, that is, without the true faith and grace of the Gospel; and so, things that will never be given a place in the kingdom of heaven among the children of life, even though the sound they make in their talk might be the tongue or the voice of an angel.

Faithful. Well, I was not so very fond of his company at first, and I am thoroughly sick of it now. What are we to do to be rid of him?

Christian. Take my advice and do as I tell you, and you will find that he will soon be sick of your company too — unless God touches his heart and turns it.

Faithful. What would you have me do?

Christian. Why, go to him, and start a serious conversation about the power of religion. And when he has approved of it — which he will — ask him plainly whether this thing is set up in his heart, his house and his way of life.

Faithful. Then Faithful stepped forward again and said to Talkative: Well, how are things? How goes it now?

Talkative. Thank you, well. I thought we should have had a great deal of talk by this time.

Faithful. Well, if you like, we can set about it now. And since you left it to me to name the question, let it be this: how does the saving grace of God show itself, when it is in a man's heart?

Talkative. I see, then, that our talk is to be about the power of things. Well, it is a very good question, and I shall be glad to answer you. And take my answer briefly, like this. First, where the grace and work of God is in the heart, it produces a great outcry against sin. Secondly —

Faithful. No, wait — let us take one at a time. I think you should rather say that it shows itself by inclining the soul to hate its sin.

Talkative. Why, what difference is there between crying out against sin and hating it?

Faithful. Oh, a great deal. A man may cry out against sin as a matter of policy; but he cannot hate it except by a real godly loathing of it. I have heard many men cry out against sin in the pulpit who can put up with it perfectly well in their hearts, their houses and their way of living. Joseph's mistress cried out at the top of her voice as though she were the holiest of women — and would gladly, for all that, have committed the sin with him. Some cry out against sin the way a mother cries out at the child in her lap, calling it a filthy, naughty girl, and then falls to hugging and kissing it.

Talkative. You are trying to catch me out, I see.

Faithful. No, not I. I only want to get things straight. But what is the second thing by which you would prove there is a work of grace in the heart?

Talkative. Great knowledge of Gospel mysteries.

Faithful. This sign should have come first; but first or last, it is false as well. Because knowledge, and great knowledge, can be got in the mysteries of the Gospel with no work of grace in the soul at all. In fact, a man may have all knowledge and still be nothing, and so be no child of God.

When Christ said, "Do you know all these things?" and the disciples answered yes, he added, "You are blessed if you do them." He does not put the blessing in knowing them, but in doing them. Because there is a kind of knowledge that is not accompanied by doing: "He that knows his master's will, and does not do it." A man may know like an angel and still be no Christian; so your sign is not a true one. Knowing is a thing that pleases talkers and boasters, certainly; but doing is what pleases God. Not that the heart can be good without knowledge — without that the heart is worthless. So there is knowledge and there is knowledge: knowledge that rests in the bare theory of things, and knowledge that comes along with the grace of faith and love, which sets a man to doing the will of God from the heart. The first will do well enough for the talker; but without the second the true Christian is not satisfied. "Give me understanding, and I shall keep your law; yes, I shall observe it with my whole heart."

Talkative. You are trying to catch me out again. This is not building anybody up.

Faithful. Well, if you would rather, put forward another sign of how this work of grace shows itself where it exists.

Talkative. Not I — because I can see we are not going to agree.

Faithful. Well, if you will not, will you allow me to do it?

Talkative. You may do as you please.

Faithful. A work of grace in the soul shows itself either to the person who has it, or to those standing by.

To the person who has it, in this way. It gives him a conviction of sin — especially of the corruption of his own nature and the sin of unbelief, on account of which he is certain to be damned if he does not find mercy at God's hand through faith in Jesus Christ. Seeing and feeling this works sorrow and shame for sin in him. He finds, further, the Saviour of the world revealed in him, and the absolute necessity of closing with him for life; at which he finds himself hungering and thirsting after him — and it is to that hunger and thirst that the promise is made. Now, according to how strong or weak his faith in his Saviour is, so is his joy and his peace, so is his love of holiness, so is his desire to know him better and to serve him in this world.

But although I say it shows itself to him like this, it is still only rarely that he is able to conclude that this is a work of grace; because the corruption still in him, and the way his reason has been damaged, make his mind misjudge in this matter. So in the man who has this work, a very sound judgment is needed before he can steadily conclude that this is a work of grace.

To other people, it shows itself in this way.

First, by a confession of his faith in Christ that comes out of experience.

Second, by a life that matches that confession: a life of holiness — holiness in the heart; holiness in the family, if he has one; and holiness in his way of life out in the world. Which in general teaches him inwardly to hate his sin, and to hate himself for it, in private; to put it down in his family; and to promote holiness out in the world — not by talk only, as a hypocrite or a talkative person may do, but by a practical submission, in faith and love, to the power of the Word.

And now, sir, as to this brief description of the work of grace and of how it shows itself: if you have anything to object, object. If not, then allow me to put a second question to you.

Talkative. No — my part now is not to object but to listen. So let me have your second question.

Faithful. It is this. Do you know this first part of the description in your own experience? And does your life and your conduct bear the same witness? Or does your religion stand in word and tongue, and not in deed and truth? Please, if you are inclined to answer me, say nothing more than you know the God above will say amen to, and nothing but what your own conscience can back you up in. "Because it is not the man who commends himself who is approved, but the man the Lord commends." Besides, to say I am this and that, when my own conduct and all my neighbours tell me I am lying, is great wickedness.

Talkative. Then Talkative began to go red; but recovering himself, he answered like this. You are coming now to experience, and conscience, and God, and appealing to him to justify what is said. This kind of conversation is not what I expected, and I am not disposed to answer questions like these, because I do not consider myself under any obligation to — unless you are setting yourself up as my catechist. And even if you did, I might still refuse to make you my judge. But tell me, please: why do you ask me such questions?

Faithful. Because I saw you were forward to talk, and because I did not know that you had anything but theory. Besides — to tell you the whole truth — I have heard of you, that you are a man whose religion is in his talk, and that your way of living gives the lie to everything your mouth professes. They say you are a stain among Christians; that religion is the worse off for your ungodly conduct; that some have already stumbled over your wicked ways, and that more are in danger of being destroyed by them. Your religion, and an alehouse, and greed, and impurity, and swearing, and lying, and keeping empty company, all stand together quite comfortably. The proverb that is said of a whore is true of you — that she is a shame to all women; so you are a shame to all who profess this faith.

Talkative. Since you are so ready to take up rumours and to judge as rashly as you do, I cannot help concluding that you are some peevish or melancholy man, not fit to be talked with. And so, goodbye.

Christian. Then Christian came up and said to his brother: I told you how it would turn out. Your words and his appetites could not agree. He would rather leave your company than reform his life. But he is gone, as I said. Let him go; the loss is nobody's but his own. He has saved us the trouble of leaving him — because carrying on as he is, which I suppose he will, he would only have been a blot on our company. Besides, the apostle says, "From such people turn away."

Faithful. But I am glad we had this short conversation with him. It may be he will think about it again. However that may be, I have dealt plainly with him, and so I am clear of his blood if he is lost.

Christian. You did well to talk to him as plainly as you did. There is very little of this honest dealing with people nowadays, and that is what makes religion stink in so many people's nostrils. It is these talkative fools, whose religion is all words while their conduct is corrupt and empty, who — being allowed so far into the fellowship of the godly — baffle the world, disgrace Christianity and grieve the sincere. I wish everybody would deal with such people as you have done. Then they would either be brought more into line with religion, or the company of the saints would get too hot for them.

Then Faithful said:

	How Talkative at first lifts up his plumes!
	How splendidly he speaks! How he presumes
	to sweep down all before him! But as soon
	as Faithful talks of heart-work, like the moon
	that's past the full, he dwindles as he goes.
	And so will all — except who heart-work knows.

So they went on talking about what they had seen along the way, and made easy going of a stretch that would otherwise, no doubt, have been tedious to them; because now they were going through a wilderness.

Now when they had got almost right out of this wilderness, Faithful happened to glance behind him and spotted somebody coming after them — and he knew him. Oh, said Faithful to his brother, who is that coming there? Then Christian looked, and said: It is my good friend Evangelist. Yes, and my good friend too, said Faithful, because it was he who set me on the road to the gate.

Now Evangelist came up to them, and greeted them like this.

Evangelist. Peace be with you, dearly beloved; and peace to those who help you.

Christian. Welcome, welcome, my good Evangelist. The sight of your face brings back to me your old kindness and your tireless labour for my eternal good.

Faithful. And a thousand times welcome, said good Faithful. Your company, dear Evangelist — how welcome it is to us poor pilgrims!

Evangelist. Then Evangelist said: How has it gone with you, my friends, since we last parted? What have you met with, and how have you conducted yourselves?

Then Christian and Faithful told him everything that had happened to them on the road, and how, and with what difficulty, they had got as far as that place.

Evangelist. I am very glad, said Evangelist — not that you have met with trials, but that you have come through them as victors, and that in spite of many weaknesses you have kept to the road to this very day.

I say I am very glad of it, both for my own sake and for yours. I have sown and you have reaped; and the day is coming when both the one who sowed and those who reaped shall rejoice together — that is, if you hold out, "for in due season you shall reap, if you do not faint." The crown is ahead of you, and it is one that cannot spoil; "so run, that you may obtain it." There are some who set out for this crown, and after they have gone a long way for it, somebody else comes in and takes it from them. So hold fast to what you have; let nobody take your crown.

You are not out of the devil's gunshot yet. You have not resisted to the point of blood in your struggle against sin. Keep the kingdom always in front of you, and believe steadily in the things that cannot be seen. Let nothing on this side of the other world get inside you. And above everything, keep good watch on your own hearts and on the appetites in them, "because they are deceitful above all things, and desperately wicked." Set your faces like flint; you have all the power in heaven and earth on your side.

Christian. Then Christian thanked him for his encouragement, but told him at the same time that they would like him to say more to them, to help them through the rest of the road — and all the more because they knew perfectly well that he was a prophet and could tell them about things that might happen to them, and how they might resist and overcome them. Faithful agreed with the request. So Evangelist began, as follows.

Evangelist. My sons, you have heard in the words of the truth of the Gospel that you must go through many troubles to enter the kingdom of heaven. And again, that in every city chains and afflictions are waiting for you. So you cannot expect to go long on your pilgrimage without them, in one form or another. You have already found something of the truth of these testimonies for yourselves, and more will follow immediately. Because now, as you see, you are almost out of this wilderness, and so you will soon come to a town that you will shortly see ahead of you; and in that town you will be set upon hard by enemies who will strain every nerve to kill you. And you may be certain that one or both of you must seal the testimony you hold with your blood. But be faithful to the point of death, and the King will give you a crown of life.

The one who dies there — although his death will be a violent one, and his pain perhaps great — will still have the better of his companion. Not only because he will arrive at the Celestial City soonest, but because he will escape a great many miseries that the other will meet with in the rest of his journey.

But when you have come to the town and find fulfilled what I have described to you here, then remember your friend, and quit yourselves like men, and commit the keeping of your souls to your God in well-doing, as to a faithful Creator.

Vanity Fair

Then I saw in my dream that when they had got out of the wilderness, they soon saw a town ahead of them, and the name of that town is Vanity. And at the town there is a fair kept, called Vanity Fair. It is kept all the year round. It is called Vanity Fair because the town where it is held is emptier than emptiness itself, and also because everything sold there, and everything that comes there, is vanity. As the wise man says, "All that comes is vanity."
