This fair is no newly set-up business, but a thing of long standing; and I will show you how it began.

Almost five thousand years ago there were pilgrims walking to the Celestial City, just as these two honest men are. And Beelzebub, Apollyon and Legion, with their companions, seeing from the path the pilgrims made that their road to the city lay through this town of Vanity, arranged to set up a fair here — a fair where every kind of vanity should be sold, and one that should last all the year round.

So at this fair all such goods are sold as houses, land, trades, positions, honours, promotions, titles, countries, kingdoms, appetites, pleasures and delights of every sort: whores, pimps, wives, husbands, children, masters, servants, lives, blood, bodies, souls, silver, gold, pearls, precious stones, and anything else you like. And what is more, at this fair there is at all hours to be seen conjuring, swindles, games, plays, fools, apes, cheats and rogues, and of every kind there is.

Here too are to be seen, and for nothing, thefts, murders, adulteries and perjuries — and those of a blood-red colour.

And just as at other, smaller fairs there are the various rows and streets with their own names, where such and such goods are sold, so here likewise there are the proper places, rows and streets — that is to say, countries and kingdoms — where the goods of this fair are most quickly to be found. Here is the Britain Row, the French Row, the Italian Row, the Spanish Row, the German Row, where several sorts of vanities are for sale. But as at other fairs one particular commodity is the leading article of the whole fair, so here the goods of Rome and her merchandise are greatly promoted — only our English nation, along with some others, has taken a dislike to them.

Now as I said, the road to the Celestial City runs right through this town where this lusty fair is kept; and anyone who means to go to the City and yet not go through this town would have to "go out of the world" altogether. The Prince of princes himself, when he was here, went through this town on his way to his own country, and on a fair day at that. Yes, and I think it was Beelzebub, the chief lord of this fair, who invited him to buy some of his vanities — and would have made him lord of the fair, if only he would have paid him homage as he went through the town. Indeed, because he was such a person of honour, Beelzebub took him from street to street and showed him all the kingdoms of the world in a very short time, so that he might, if it were possible, tempt the Blessed One to haggle for some of his goods and buy them. But he had no interest in the merchandise, and so he left the town without laying out so much as a farthing on these vanities.

So this fair is an ancient thing of long standing, and a very great fair. Now these pilgrims, as I said, had no choice but to go through it. Well, so they did. But as soon as they entered the fair, all the people in it were stirred up, and the whole town was in an uproar about them; and that for several reasons.

First, the pilgrims were dressed in a kind of clothing quite different from the clothing of anybody who traded at that fair. So the people of the fair stood and stared at them: some said they were fools, some that they were madmen, and some that they were foreigners.

Second, as they wondered at their clothes, so they wondered at their speech; because hardly anybody could understand what they said. They naturally spoke the language of Canaan, while the people who kept the fair were the men of this world — so that from one end of the fair to the other, each seemed to the other to be talking gibberish.

Third, what bewildered the traders more than a little was that these pilgrims thought nothing at all of their goods. They did not even care to look at them. And when the traders called on them to buy, they would put their fingers in their ears and cry, "Turn my eyes away from looking at vanity," and look upwards, to show that their trade and their business was in heaven.

One man, watching how they behaved, happened to say to them mockingly: What will you buy? But they looked at him gravely and answered, "We buy the truth."

At that, the chance was taken to despise them still more. Some mocked, some jeered, some spoke abusively, and some called on others to hit them. At last things came to an uproar and a great commotion in the fair, so much so that all order broke down. Word was quickly taken to the great man of the fair, who came down at once and appointed some of his most trusted friends to take these men in for examination, since the fair had nearly been overturned on their account.

So the men were brought in for questioning; and those who sat in judgment on them asked where they came from, where they were going, and what they were doing there in such unusual clothes. The men told them that they were pilgrims and strangers in the world, and that they were going to their own country, which was the heavenly Jerusalem; and that they had given the men of the town and the traders no reason at all to abuse them like this and hinder them on their journey — unless it was that when somebody asked them what they would buy, they had said they would buy the truth.

But the men appointed to examine them did not believe they were anything but madmen and lunatics, or else people who had come to throw the whole fair into confusion. So they took them and beat them, and smeared them with filth, and then put them in the cage, so that they might be made a public spectacle to everybody at the fair.

So there they lay for some time, and were made the target of anybody's amusement or spite or revenge, while the great man of the fair went on laughing at everything that happened to them. But the men were patient, and did not give back abuse for abuse but the very opposite — blessing, and giving good words for bad and kindness for injuries done. And some men in the fair who were more observant and less prejudiced than the rest began to check the baser sort and blame them for the constant abuse they were handing out. So those men rounded on them angrily in their turn, reckoning them as bad as the men in the cage, and telling them that they seemed to be in league with them and should be made to share their misfortunes.

The others replied that as far as they could see the men were quiet and sober and meant nobody any harm, and that there were plenty trading in their own fair who deserved the cage a great deal more — yes, and the pillory too — than the men they had been abusing.

So after various words had passed on both sides, with the two men behaving themselves the whole time very wisely and soberly in front of them, they fell to blows among themselves and did each other real harm. Then these two poor men were brought before their examiners again, and charged with being responsible for the recent uproar in the fair.

So they beat them pitilessly, and hung irons on them, and led them up and down the fair in chains, as an example and a warning to everyone else, so that nobody would speak up for them or join them. But Christian and Faithful behaved more wisely still, and took the disgrace and shame that was thrown at them with so much meekness and patience that it won several of the men in the fair over to their side — though only a few, compared with the rest.

This threw the other party into a still greater rage, so that they settled on putting these two men to death. So they announced that the cage and the irons would not do for them after all, but that they should die for the wrong they had done and for leading the men of the fair astray.

Then they were sent back to the cage again until further orders should be given about them. So they put them in, and fastened their feet in the stocks.

And here they called to mind again what they had heard from their faithful friend Evangelist, and were all the more settled in their road and in their sufferings by what he had told them would happen. They also comforted each other now with the thought that whichever of them it fell to to suffer, that one would have the better of it. So each man privately wished that he might be the one given that privilege — but committing themselves to the all-wise ordering of the one who rules everything, they stayed contentedly in the state they were in, until something else should be settled about them.

The Trial of Faithful

Then a suitable time was appointed, and they brought them out for trial with a view to condemning them. When the time came they were brought before their enemies and formally charged. The Judge's name was Lord Hate-good. Their indictment was one and the same in substance, though it varied a little in wording, and its contents were these:

"That they were enemies to their trade and disturbers of it; that they had stirred up disorder and division in the town, and had won a following over to their own most dangerous opinions, in contempt of the law of their prince."

Then Faithful began to answer, and said that he had only set himself against whatever had set itself against the one who is higher than the highest. And, said he, as for disorder, I make none, being a man of peace myself. Those who were won over to us were won by seeing our truth and innocence, and they have only turned from the worse to the better. And as for this king you speak of, since he is Beelzebub, the enemy of our Lord, I defy him and all his angels.

Then it was announced that anybody who had something to say on behalf of their lord the king against the prisoner at the bar should come forward at once and give their evidence. So three witnesses came in — Envy, Superstition and Pickthank. They were then asked whether they knew the prisoner at the bar, and what they had to say for their lord the king against him.

Then Envy stood forward and said, in effect: My Lord, I have known this man a long time, and I will testify on oath before this honourable bench that he is —

Judge. Stop. Swear him in first. (So they swore him in.) Then he said:

Envy. My Lord, this man, for all his respectable-sounding name, is one of the vilest men in our country. He has no regard for prince or people, law or custom, but does everything he can to fill everyone's head with certain disloyal notions of his, which he generally calls principles of faith and holiness. And in particular, I heard him say once myself that Christianity and the customs of our town of Vanity were flatly opposed to one another and could not be reconciled. By which statement, my Lord, he condemns at a single stroke not only everything we do that is worthy of praise, but us as well, for doing it.

Judge. Then the Judge said to him: Have you anything more to say?

Envy. My Lord, I could say a great deal more, only I would not want to weary the court. Still, if it is needed, once the other gentlemen have given their evidence — rather than let anything be missing that will finish him off — I will extend my testimony against him.

So he was told to stand aside.

Then they called Superstition and told him to look at the prisoner. They also asked him what he could say for their lord the king against him. Then they swore him in, and he began.

Superstition. My Lord, I have no great acquaintance with this man, nor do I want to know any more of him. However, this much I do know: that he is a thoroughly pestilent fellow, from a conversation I had with him in this town the other day. Because talking with him then, I heard him say that our religion was worthless, and of a kind by which a man could not possibly please God. And from these statements of his, my Lord, your Lordship knows perfectly well what necessarily follows: namely, that we still worship for nothing, that we are still in our sins, and that we shall be damned in the end. And that is what I have to say.

Then Pickthank was sworn in and told to say what he knew, on behalf of their lord the king, against the prisoner at the bar.

Pickthank. My Lord, and all you gentlemen — I have known this fellow a long time, and have heard him say things that ought not to be said. Because he has abused our noble prince Beelzebub, and has spoken contemptuously of his honourable friends, whose names are the Lord Old Man, the Lord Carnal Delight, the Lord Luxurious, the Lord Desire of Vain Glory, my old Lord Lechery, Sir Having Greedy, and all the rest of our nobility. And he has said, further, that if everybody were of his mind, then if it were possible not one of these noblemen would go on existing in this town any longer. Besides that, he has not been afraid to abuse you, my Lord, who are now appointed to be his judge, calling you an ungodly villain, along with a great many other such insulting terms, with which he has spattered most of the gentry of our town.

When Pickthank had told his story, the Judge turned his speech to the prisoner at the bar, saying: You renegade, heretic and traitor — have you heard what these honest gentlemen have testified against you?

Faithful. May I say a few words in my own defence?

Judge. Fellow! Fellow! You deserve to live no longer, but to be killed on the spot. Still, so that everybody may see how gentle we are with you, let us hear what you, vile renegade, have to say.

Faithful. First, then, in answer to what Mr. Envy has said. I never said anything but this: that whatever rule, or law, or custom, or people stands flat against the Word of God is directly opposed to Christianity. If I have spoken wrongly in this, show me my error, and I am ready here in front of you to take it back.

Second, as to Mr. Superstition and his charge against me. I said only this: that in the worship of God a divine faith is required; but there can be no divine faith without a divine revelation of the will of God. So whatever is forced into the worship of God that does not agree with divine revelation cannot be done by anything but a human faith, and that faith will be of no use towards eternal life.

Third, as to what Mr. Pickthank has said. I say — leaving aside his terms, such as that I am supposed to have abused people, and the like — that the prince of this town, together with the whole rabble of his attendants named by this gentleman, are fitter to exist in hell than in this town and country. And so, the Lord have mercy on me.

Then the Judge called to the jury, who had been standing by all this while to listen and observe: Gentlemen of the jury, you see this man, about whom so great an uproar has been made in this town. You have also heard what these worthy gentlemen have testified against him. And you have heard his reply and his confession. It now lies with you to hang him or spare his life; but I think it right to instruct you first in our law.

There was an Act made in the days of Pharaoh the Great, a servant of our prince, that to prevent those of a different religion from multiplying and growing too strong for him, their male children should be thrown into the river. There was also an Act made in the days of Nebuchadnezzar the Great, another of his servants, that whoever would not fall down and worship his golden image should be thrown into a fiery furnace. There was also an Act made in the days of Darius, that whoever, for a certain period, called on any god but him should be thrown into the lions' den.

Now this rebel has broken the substance of all these laws — not only in thought, which is not to be borne, but in word and deed as well; and that must necessarily be intolerable.

As for Pharaoh's law: that was made on a supposition, to prevent trouble, when no crime had yet appeared. But here is a crime that has appeared. As for the second and third, you see that he argues against our religion; and for the treason he has confessed, he deserves to die.

Then the jury went out. Their names were Mr. Blind-man, Mr. No-good, Mr. Malice, Mr. Love-lust, Mr. Live-loose, Mr. Heady, Mr. High-mind, Mr. Enmity, Mr. Liar, Mr. Cruelty, Mr. Hate-light and Mr. Implacable; and every one of them gave his private verdict against him among the rest, and afterwards they unanimously agreed to bring him in guilty before the Judge.

And first, among themselves, Mr. Blind-man the foreman said: I can see clearly that this man is a heretic. Then Mr. No-good said: Rid the earth of a fellow like that. Yes, said Mr. Malice, because I hate the very look of him. Then Mr. Love-lust said: I could never stand him. Nor could I, said Mr. Live-loose, because he was always condemning the way I live. Hang him, hang him, said Mr. Heady. A miserable little wretch, said Mr. High-mind. My heart rises against him, said Mr. Enmity. He is a rogue, said Mr. Liar. Hanging is too good for him, said Mr. Cruelty. Let us get him out of the way, said Mr. Hate-light. Then Mr. Implacable said: If I were given the whole world, I could not be reconciled to him; so let us bring him in guilty and sentence him to death at once.

And so they did. So he was immediately condemned — to be taken from the place where he was to the place he had come from, and there to be put to the cruellest death that could be devised.

So they brought him out, to deal with him according to their law. And first they whipped him, then they beat him with their fists, then they slashed his flesh with knives; after that they stoned him with stones, then jabbed him with their swords; and last of all they burned him to ashes at the stake. And so Faithful came to his end.

Now I saw that behind the crowd there stood a chariot and a pair of horses, waiting for Faithful. And as soon as his enemies had finished him off, he was taken up into it, and was carried straight up through the clouds with the sound of a trumpet, by the shortest way to the Celestial Gate.

But as for Christian, he had some respite, and was sent back to prison. So he stayed there for a while. But the one who overrules everything, holding the power of their rage in his own hand, so arranged things that Christian escaped them for the time being and went on his way. And as he went, he sang:

	Well, Faithful, you have faithfully professed
	before your Lord, and you shall be his guest
	and blessed with him, when faithless men, who prized
	their empty pleasures, cry out in the fires.
	Sing, Faithful, sing, and let your name survive;
	for though they killed you, you are still alive.

Hopeful, and Mr. By-ends

Now I saw in my dream that Christian did not go out alone. There was one whose name was Hopeful — made so by watching Christian and Faithful, in their words and their behaviour, through their sufferings at the Fair — who joined himself to Christian, and entering into a brotherly agreement with him, told him he would be his companion. So one man died to bear witness to the truth, and another rose out of his ashes to be Christian's companion on his pilgrimage. This Hopeful also told Christian that there were a good many more of the men at the Fair who would take their time and follow on after them.

So I saw that soon after they had got out of the Fair, they caught up with somebody walking ahead of them, whose name was By-ends. So they said to him: What part of the country are you from, sir? And how far are you going this way? He told them he came from the town of Fair-speech and was going to the Celestial City — but he did not tell them his name.

From Fair-speech! said Christian. Is there anything good living there?

By-ends. Yes, said By-ends, I hope so.

Christian. Tell me, sir — what may I call you? said Christian.

By-ends. I am a stranger to you and you to me. If you are going this way, I shall be glad of your company; if not, I shall have to be content.

Christian. This town of Fair-speech, said Christian — I have heard of it; and as I recall, they say it is a wealthy place.

By-ends. Yes, I can promise you it is. And I have a great many rich relations there.

Christian. Tell me — who are your relations there, if a man may be so bold?

By-ends. Nearly the whole town; and in particular my Lord Turnabout, my Lord Timeserver, my Lord Fair-speech — from whose ancestors the town first took its name — and Mr. Smooth-man, Mr. Facing-both-ways and Mr. Anything. And the parson of our parish, Mr. Two-tongues, was my mother's own brother on the father's side. And to tell you the truth, I have become a gentleman of good standing; and yet my great-grandfather was only a waterman, looking one way and rowing another — and I got most of my estate by the same trade.

Christian. Are you a married man?

By-ends. Yes, and my wife is a very virtuous woman, the daughter of a virtuous woman. She was my Lady Feigning's daughter, so she comes of a very honourable family, and has reached such a pitch of breeding that she knows how to conduct herself with everybody, prince and peasant alike. It is true that we differ a little in religion from the stricter sort, but only on two small points. First, we never struggle against wind and tide. Second, we are always at our most enthusiastic when religion goes about in his silver slippers; we love to walk with him in the street if the sun is shining and the people are applauding him.

Then Christian stepped a little aside to his companion Hopeful and said: It runs in my mind that this is one By-ends of Fair-speech; and if it is, we have as complete a scoundrel in our company as lives anywhere in these parts.

Then Hopeful said: Ask him. I should not have thought he would be ashamed of his name.

So Christian came up with him again and said: Sir, you talk as though you knew rather more than the rest of the world does; and if I am not mistaken, I think I can half guess who you are. Is your name not Mr. By-ends of Fair-speech?

By-ends. That is not my name. It is a nickname given me by certain people who cannot stand me; and I must be content to bear it as an insult, as other good men have borne theirs before me.

Christian. But did you never give people any reason to call you by that name?

By-ends. Never, never! The worst I ever did to give them the excuse for it was that I always had the luck to find my own judgment falling in with the way of the times, whatever it happened to be — and my chance was to profit by it. But if things come to me that way, let me count it a blessing; and let not the malicious load me with abuse on account of it.

Christian. I did think you were the man I had heard about; and to tell you what I think, I am afraid this name belongs to you rather more properly than you would like us to believe.

By-ends. Well, if you choose to imagine so, I cannot help it. You will find me a fair-minded companion, if you will still let me be your associate.

Christian. If you are going to come with us, you must go against wind and tide — which I gather is against your opinion. You must also acknowledge religion in his rags as well as in his silver slippers; and stand by him when he is in irons as well as when he walks the streets to applause.

By-ends. You must not impose on me, or lord it over my faith. Leave me my liberty, and let me come with you.

Christian. Not a step further, unless you will do exactly what I have just said, as we do.

Then By-ends said: I shall never desert my old principles, since they are harmless and profitable. If I may not go with you, I must do what I did before you caught up with me — go by myself, until somebody catches up with me who will be glad of my company.

Now I saw in my dream that Christian and Hopeful left him and kept their distance ahead of him. But one of them, looking back, saw three men following Mr. By-ends; and as they came up with him he made them a very low bow, and they paid him a compliment in return. The men's names were Mr. Hold-the-world, Mr. Money-love and Mr. Save-all — men Mr. By-ends had known before, because in their boyhood they had been schoolfellows together, taught by one Mr. Gripeman, a schoolmaster in Love-gain, which is a market town in the county of Coveting, in the north. This schoolmaster taught them the art of getting, whether by force, fraud, flattery, lying, or by putting on a show of religion; and these four gentlemen had picked up a great deal of their master's art, so that any one of them could have kept such a school himself.

Well, when they had greeted each other, as I said, Mr. Money-love said to Mr. By-ends: Who are those on the road ahead of us? — because Christian and Hopeful were still in sight.

By-ends. They are a couple of men from a far country who, in their own fashion, are going on pilgrimage.

Money-love. What a pity! Why did they not wait, so that we might have had the pleasure of their company? Because they, and we, and you too, sir, I hope, are all going on pilgrimage.

By-ends. So we are, indeed. But the men ahead of us are so rigid, and so much in love with their own notions, and think so little of anybody else's opinions, that let a man be as godly as you please, if he does not fall in with them on every point they push him clean out of their company.

Save-all. That is hard. But we do read of some who are righteous overmuch; and men like that are driven by their own rigidity to judge and condemn everybody but themselves. But tell me, please — what were the things you differed about, and how many?

By-ends. Why, in their headstrong way they conclude that it is a duty to rush on with their journey in all weathers, while I am for waiting for wind and tide. They are for risking everything for God at a single stroke; I am for taking every advantage to secure my life and my property. They are for holding on to their notions though everybody else is against them; I am for religion in whatever way, and as far as, the times and my own safety will allow. They are for religion when he is in rags and out of favour; I am for him when he walks in his golden slippers, in the sunshine, and with applause.

Mr. Hold-the-world. Yes — and you stay right there, good Mr. By-ends. Because for my part I can count a man nothing but a fool who, having the freedom to keep what he has, is unwise enough to lose it. Let us be as wise as serpents. It is best to make hay while the sun shines. You see how the bee lies still all winter and stirs herself only when she can have profit along with her pleasure. God sends sometimes rain and sometimes sunshine; if they are fools enough to go through the first, let us at least be content to take the fair weather along with us.

For my part, I like that religion best which will stand together with the security of God's good blessings to us. Because who that is governed by his reason could imagine, since God has given us the good things of this life, that he does not mean us to keep them for his sake? Abraham and Solomon grew rich in religion. And Job says that a good man shall pile up gold like dust. But he must not be the sort of man these two ahead of us are, if they are as you have described them.

Mr. Save-all. I think we are all agreed on this, and so there is no need for any more words about it.

Mr. Money-love. No, there is no need for more words about it indeed; because a man who believes neither Scripture nor reason — and you see we have both on our side — neither knows his own liberty nor looks after his own safety.

Mr. By-ends. My brothers, we are all going on pilgrimage, as you see. And to keep our minds better off bad things, allow me to put this question to you. Suppose a man — a minister, or a tradesman, or whatever he may be — should have an opportunity in front of him to get the good blessings of this life, but in such a way that he can only get at them by becoming, in appearance at least, unusually enthusiastic about some points of religion that he never bothered with before. May he not use this means to reach his end, and still be a thoroughly honest man?

Mr. Money-love. I see the bottom of your question; and with these gentlemen's permission I will try to shape an answer for you.

And first, to speak to your question as it concerns a minister. Suppose a minister, a worthy man, who holds only a very small living and has his eye on a greater one, far richer and fatter. And suppose he now has an opportunity of getting it, but only by being more studious, by preaching more often and more zealously, and — because the temper of the people requires it — by altering some of his principles. For my part, I see no reason why a man may not do this, provided he has a call to it. Yes, and a good deal more besides, and still be an honest man. And here is why.

First, his desire for a greater living is lawful — this cannot be denied — since Providence has set it in front of him. So he may get it if he can, and raise no question of conscience about it.

Second, his desire for that living makes him more studious, a more zealous preacher, and so on, and so makes him a better man; yes, and makes him improve his abilities better, which is according to the mind of God.

Third, as for his falling in with the temper of his people by giving up some of his principles in order to serve them, this shows: one, that he is of a self-denying temper; two, that he has a sweet and winning manner; and so, three, that he is all the fitter for the ministry.

Fourth, I conclude, then, that a minister who exchanges a small living for a large one should not be judged greedy for doing it, but rather — since his abilities and his industry are improved by it — should be counted a man who is following his calling and the opportunity put into his hand to do good.

And now to the second part of the question, which concerns the tradesman you mentioned. Suppose a man like that has only a poor business in the world, but by becoming religious he may improve his trade, perhaps get a rich wife, or better and more numerous customers for his shop. For my part I see no reason why this may not lawfully be done. And here is why.

First, to become religious is a virtue, by whatever means a man becomes so.

Second, it is not unlawful to get a rich wife, or more trade for my shop.

Third, besides, the man who gets these things by becoming religious gets what is good, from people who are good, by becoming good himself. So then here is a good wife, and good customers, and good profit, and all of them got by becoming religious, which is good. Therefore to become religious in order to get all these is a good and profitable plan.

This answer, made by Mr. Money-love to Mr. By-ends' question, was highly applauded by all of them; so they concluded, on the whole, that it was thoroughly wholesome and advantageous. And because they thought nobody was able to contradict it, and because Christian and Hopeful were still within calling distance, they jointly agreed to attack them with the question as soon as they caught up — all the more since the two had opposed Mr. By-ends a little earlier.

So they called after them, and Christian and Hopeful stopped and stood still until they came up. But they had settled as they went that it should not be Mr. By-ends but old Mr. Hold-the-world who put the question to them, because they supposed the answer given to him would be free of the last of the heat that had flared up between Mr. By-ends and the two of them at their parting a little while before.

So they came up to one another; and after a short greeting, Mr. Hold-the-world put the question to Christian and his companion, and told them to answer it if they could.

Christian. Then Christian said: Even a baby in religion could answer ten thousand questions like that. Because if it is unlawful to follow Christ for the sake of bread — as it is in the sixth chapter of John — how much more disgusting is it to make him and religion into a stalking-horse for getting and enjoying the world! Nor do we find anybody but heathens, hypocrites, devils and witches who are of this opinion.

First, heathens. When Hamor and Shechem had a mind to Jacob's daughter and his cattle, and saw that there was no way for them to get at them except by being circumcised, they said to their companions: if every male among us is circumcised, as they are, will not their cattle and their goods and every beast of theirs become ours? Their daughter and their cattle were what they were after, and their religion was the stalking-horse they used to get at them. Read the whole story.

Second, the hypocritical Pharisees were of this religion too. Long prayers were their pretence, but getting widows' houses was their intention; and greater damnation from God was their sentence.

Third, Judas the devil was of this religion. He was religious for the money-bag, so that he could get hold of what was in it; but he was lost, thrown away, the very son of destruction.

Fourth, Simon the sorcerer was of this religion too, because he wanted the Holy Spirit so that he could make money with it; and his sentence out of Peter's mouth was accordingly.

Fifth, I cannot get it out of my mind that the man who takes up religion for the sake of the world will throw religion away for the sake of the world. Because just as surely as Judas had the world in view when he became religious, so surely did he sell religion and his Master for it too.

So to answer this question in the affirmative, as I gather you have done, and to accept such an answer as sound, is heathenish, hypocritical and devilish alike; and your reward will be according to what you have done.

Then they stood staring at one another, but had nothing to answer Christian with. Hopeful also approved the soundness of Christian's answer; so there was a great silence among them. Mr. By-ends and his company faltered too and kept back, so that Christian and Hopeful could get ahead of them.

Then Christian said to his companion: If these men cannot stand before the judgment of men, what will they do with the judgment of God? And if they are struck dumb when they are dealt with by vessels of clay, what will they do when they are rebuked by the flames of a devouring fire?

Then Christian and Hopeful got ahead of them again, and went on until they came to a delightful plain called Ease, where they went along very contentedly. But that plain was narrow, so they were quickly over it.

Now on the far side of that plain there was a little hill called Lucre, and in that hill a silver mine, which some of those who had gone that way before had turned aside to look at, because of how unusual it was. But going too near the edge of the pit, they found the ground treacherous under them; it gave way, and they were killed. Some had also been maimed there, and could never to their dying day be themselves again.

Demas, and the Pillar of Salt

Then I saw in my dream that a little off the road, opposite the silver mine, stood Demas, looking every inch a gentleman, calling to passers-by to come and look. He said to Christian and his companion: Hey there! Turn aside this way, and I will show you something.

Christian. What is so worth seeing that it would justify turning us off our road?

Demas. Here is a silver mine, and some men digging in it for treasure. If you will come, then for a little trouble you may set yourselves up very handsomely.

Hopeful. Then Hopeful said: Let us go and look.

Christian. Not I, said Christian. I have heard of this place before now, and how many have been killed at it. And besides that, treasure is a trap to those who go looking for it, because it holds them back on their pilgrimage.

Then Christian called out to Demas, saying: Is the place not dangerous? Has it not held a good many people back on their pilgrimage?

Demas. Not very dangerous — except to the careless. (But he went red as he said it.)

Christian. Then Christian said to Hopeful: Let us not move a single step, but keep straight on our way.

Hopeful. I will guarantee you that when By-ends comes up, if he gets the same invitation we did, he will turn aside to have a look.

Christian. No doubt about it, because his principles lead him that way; and it is a hundred to one that he dies there.

Demas. Then Demas called again, saying: But will you not come over and look?

Christian. Then Christian answered him flatly, saying: Demas, you are an enemy to the right ways of the Lord of this road, and you have already been condemned for turning aside yourself by one of his Majesty's judges. Why are you trying to bring us in for the same condemnation? Besides, if we turn aside at all, our Lord the King will certainly hear about it, and will put us to shame there, where we would rather stand before him with confidence.

Demas called out again that he was one of their brotherhood too, and that if they would wait a little he would come and walk with them himself.

Christian. Then Christian said: What is your name? Is it not the same one I called you by?

Demas. Yes, my name is Demas. I am a son of Abraham.

Christian. I know you. Gehazi was your great-grandfather and Judas your father, and you have walked in their footsteps. This is nothing but a devilish trick you are playing. Your father was hanged as a traitor, and you deserve no better reward. You may be sure that when we come to the King, we shall give him word of this behaviour of yours.

So they went on their way.

By this time By-ends and his companions had come back into sight, and at the first signal they went straight over to Demas. Now whether they fell into the pit by leaning over the edge of it, or whether they went down to dig, or whether they were smothered at the bottom by the foul air that usually rises in such places, I am not certain. But this much I noticed: they were never seen on the road again.

Then Christian sang:

	By-ends and silver Demas both agree;
	one calls, the other runs, so he may be
	a sharer in his profit. Both of these
	settle for this world, and go no further on.

Now I saw that just on the other side of this plain, the Pilgrims came to a place where an old monument stood close by the roadside; and they were both troubled at the sight of it, because the shape of it was so strange. It seemed to them to be a woman transformed into a pillar. So they stood there looking at it, and looking again, and for some time could not tell what to make of it.

At last Hopeful noticed writing above its head, in an unfamiliar hand. But being no scholar, he called Christian over, who was the educated one, to see whether he could pick out the meaning. So Christian came, and after piecing the letters together for a little he found it was this: "Remember Lot's wife."

So he read it out to his companion; after which they both concluded that this was the pillar of salt that Lot's wife had been turned into, for looking back with a greedy heart as she was leaving Sodom for safety. And this sudden and astonishing sight gave them the occasion for this conversation.

Christian. Ah, my brother — this is a well-timed sight! It came just when we needed it, right after the invitation Demas gave us to come over and look at the Hill Lucre. If we had gone over as he wanted us to, and as you were inclining to do, my brother, then for all I know we would have been made like this woman ourselves — a spectacle for everybody who came after us to stare at.

Hopeful. I am sorry I was so foolish, and I am left wondering that I am not standing here now as Lot's wife is. Because what was the difference between her sin and mine? She only looked back — and I wanted to go over and see. Let grace be praised, and let me be ashamed that such a thing was ever in my heart at all.

Christian. Let us take note of what we see here, to help us in time to come. This woman escaped one judgment, since she did not die in the destruction of Sodom; and yet she was destroyed by another, as we can see — she is turned into a pillar of salt.

Hopeful. True. And she can serve us as both a warning and an example: a warning, that we should keep clear of her sin; and a sign of what judgment will overtake those who are not stopped by the warning. In the same way Korah, Dathan and Abiram, with the two hundred and fifty men who died in their sin, also became a sign or an example to others to beware.

But above all, one thing puzzles me: how Demas and his companions can stand over there so confidently, looking for the very treasure that this woman was turned into a pillar of salt merely for looking back after — because we do not read that she took so much as one step off the road. And all the more since the judgment that came on her made her an example within sight of where they are standing; they cannot help seeing her, if they would only lift their eyes.

Christian. It is a thing to wonder at, and it shows that their hearts have grown desperate in the matter. I cannot think who to compare them to so exactly as to people who pick pockets in the presence of the judge, or cut purses under the gallows. It is said of the men of Sodom that they were sinners exceedingly, because they were sinners before the Lord — that is, in his sight, and in spite of all the kindnesses he had shown them; because the land of Sodom was in those days like the garden of Eden. That is what provoked him all the more to jealousy, and made their punishment as hot as the fire of the Lord out of heaven could make it.

And it is entirely reasonable to conclude that people like this — sinning in full view, and in the teeth of the very examples set constantly in front of them to warn them off — must come in for the severest judgments there are.

Hopeful. You have certainly spoken the truth. But what a mercy it is that neither you, and above all not I, has been made into this example! This gives us reason to thank God, to fear him, and always to remember Lot's wife.

Doubting Castle and Giant Despair

Then I saw that they went on their way to a pleasant river, which King David called "the river of God" and John "the river of the water of life." Now their road ran right along the bank of the river, and here Christian and his companion walked with great delight. They drank the water of the river too, which was sweet, and put new life into their tired spirits. Besides that, on both banks of this river there were green trees bearing every kind of fruit, and the leaves of the trees were good for medicine. They were delighted with the fruit of these trees as well, and they ate the leaves to keep off the sickness and the other complaints that come to people who overheat their blood with travelling. On each side of the river there was also a meadow, beautifully set with lilies, and it was green all the year round.

In this meadow they lay down and slept, because here they could lie down in safety. When they woke, they gathered more fruit from the trees and drank more of the water of the river, and then lay down to sleep again. They did this for several days and nights. Then they sang:

	Look how these crystal waters slip and slide
	to comfort pilgrims by the highway side;
	the meadows green, besides their fragrant smell,
	set out a feast for them. And who can tell
	what pleasant fruit, what leaves these trees will yield,
	will sell up all he has to buy this field.

So when they were minded to go on — because they were not at their journey's end yet — they ate and drank and set off.

Now I saw in my dream that they had not travelled far before the river and the road parted company for a while; at which they were more than a little sorry, and yet they did not dare go off the road. Now the road away from the river was rough, and their feet were sore from all their travelling; "so the souls of the pilgrims were much discouraged because of the way." So as they went on, they kept wishing for a better road.

Now a little ahead of them, on the left-hand side of the road, there was a meadow with a stile going over into it, and that meadow is called By-path Meadow. Then Christian said to his companion: If this meadow runs alongside our road, let us go over into it. So he went to the stile to look — and there, on the other side of the fence, was a path running along beside the road.

Just what I wanted, said Christian. Here is the easier walking. Come along, good Hopeful, and let us go over.

Hopeful. But what if this path should lead us off the road?

Christian. That is not likely, said the other. Look — does it not run right alongside it?

So Hopeful, persuaded by his companion, went after him over the stile. Once they were over and onto the path, they found it very easy on their feet; and looking ahead of them, they spotted a man walking as they were, whose name was Vain-confidence. So they called after him and asked where that path led. He said, to the Celestial Gate.

Look, said Christian — did I not tell you? By this you can see we are right.

So they followed on, and he went ahead of them. But then night came on and it grew very dark, so that the two behind lost sight of the man in front.

So the man in front — Vain-confidence by name — not being able to see the way ahead of him, fell into a deep pit, which had been dug there on purpose by the Prince of those grounds to catch conceited fools with; and he was smashed to pieces by the fall.

Now Christian and his companion heard him fall. So they called out to ask what had happened, but there was nobody to answer; they only heard a groaning.

Hopeful. Then Hopeful said: Where are we now?

At that his companion fell silent, suspecting that he had led him off the road. And now it began to rain, and thunder and lighten most terribly, and the water rose fast.

Then Hopeful groaned to himself, saying: Oh, if only I had kept to my road!

Christian. Who could have thought that this path would lead us off the way?

Hopeful. I was afraid of it from the very first, and that is why I gave you that gentle warning. I would have spoken plainer, except that you are older than I am.

Christian. Good brother, do not take offence. I am sorry I have brought you off the road, and that I have put you into such immediate danger. Please, my brother, forgive me. I did not do it with any bad intention.

Hopeful. Take comfort, my brother, for I do forgive you; and I believe too that this will turn out for our good.

Christian. I am glad I have a merciful brother with me. But we must not stand about like this. Let us try to get back again.

Hopeful. But, good brother, let me go first.

Christian. No, if you please, let me go first — so that if there is any danger I may be the first into it, since it is by my doing that we are both off the road.

Hopeful. No, said Hopeful, you shall not go first; because your mind is troubled, and it may lead you off the road again.

Then, to encourage them, they heard a voice saying: "Set your heart towards the highway, the very road you came by; turn back again." But by this time the waters had risen a great deal, which made going back very dangerous. (And it occurred to me then that it is easier to go off the road when you are on it than to get back on when you are off it.) Still, they risked going back — but it was so dark, and the flood was so high, that going back they were within an inch of drowning nine or ten times.

Nor could they, for all the skill they had, get back to the stile that night. So at last, coming upon a little shelter, they sat down there until daybreak; but being worn out, they fell asleep.

Now there was, not far from where they lay, a castle called Doubting Castle, whose owner was Giant Despair; and it was in his grounds that they were now sleeping. So getting up early in the morning and walking up and down his fields, he caught Christian and Hopeful asleep on his land. Then in a grim, surly voice he told them to wake up, and asked them where they were from and what they were doing on his grounds. They told him they were pilgrims and that they had lost their way.

Then the Giant said: You have trespassed on me tonight, tramping over my grounds and lying down on them, and so you must come along with me.

So they were forced to go, because he was stronger than they were. They also had very little to say for themselves, because they knew they were in the wrong. So the Giant drove them ahead of him and put them in his castle, into a very dark dungeon, filthy and stinking to the spirits of these two men. And here they lay from Wednesday morning to Saturday night without a bite of bread or a drop of water, or any light, or anybody to ask how they were. So they were in a bad way here, and a long way from friends and acquaintances. And in this place Christian had a double sorrow, because it was through his own ill-considered advice that they had been brought into this trouble.

Now Giant Despair had a wife, and her name was Diffidence — which is to say Distrust. So when he had gone to bed, he told his wife what he had done: that he had taken a couple of prisoners and thrown them into his dungeon for trespassing on his grounds. Then he asked her what he had best do with them next. So she asked him what they were, where they had come from and where they were bound; and he told her. Then she advised him that when he got up in the morning he should beat them without any mercy at all.

So when he got up he fetched himself a heavy crabapple club, and went down into the dungeon to them; and there he first set about swearing at them as if they were dogs, although they had never given him a word of offence. Then he set on them and beat them terribly, and so thoroughly that they could not help themselves or even turn over on the floor. This done, he went off and left them there to grieve over their misery and mourn under their distress. So they spent that whole day in nothing but sighs and bitter lamentations.

The next night, talking with her husband about them again and learning that they were still alive, she advised him to urge them to make an end of themselves. So when morning came he went to them in the same surly way as before; and seeing that they were very sore from the beating he had given them the day before, he told them that since they were never likely to get out of that place, their best course would be to finish themselves off at once — with a knife, a rope or poison. Because why, said he, should you choose life, when it comes with so much bitterness in it?

But they begged him to let them go. At that he gave them an ugly look, and rushing at them would no doubt have finished them off himself — except that he fell into one of his fits, because in sunny weather he sometimes did, and lost the use of his hand for a while. So he went off and left them, as before, to think about what to do.

Then the prisoners talked it over between themselves, whether it was best to take his advice or not; and this is how the conversation began.

Christian. Brother, said Christian, what are we going to do? The life we are living now is miserable. For my part I do not know which is better, to live like this or to die out of hand. "My soul chooses strangling rather than life," and the grave would be easier for me than this dungeon. Shall we be governed by what the Giant says?

Hopeful. Our present condition is dreadful, certainly, and death would be far more welcome to me than staying here for ever. But even so, let us consider: the Lord of the country we are going to has said, You shall do no murder — not to another man's person; so how much more are we forbidden to take the Giant's advice and kill ourselves. Besides, a man who kills another can only commit murder on his body; but for a man to kill himself is to kill body and soul at one stroke.

And what is more, my brother — you talk about ease in the grave; but have you forgotten the hell that murderers certainly go to? Because "no murderer has eternal life."

And let us consider again that all the law is not in Giant Despair's hands. Others, as far as I can make out, have been taken by him just as we have, and have still escaped out of his hands. Who knows but that the God who made the world may cause Giant Despair to die? Or that at some point he may forget to lock us in? Or that he may shortly have another of his fits in front of us and lose the use of his limbs? And if that ever happens again, then for my part I am determined to summon up a man's courage and do everything I can to get out from under his hand. I was a fool not to have tried it before. But however that may be, my brother, let us be patient and put up with it a while. The time may come that will give us a happy release. Only let us not be our own murderers.

With these words Hopeful steadied his brother's mind for the moment; so they stayed together in the dark that day, in their sad and dismal condition.

Well, towards evening the Giant went down into the dungeon again to see whether his prisoners had taken his advice. But when he got there he found them alive — and truly, alive was all they were, because now, what with the lack of bread and water and the wounds they had taken when he beat them, they could do little more than breathe. But alive, as I say, he found them; at which he flew into a terrible rage and told them that since they had disobeyed his advice, it would go worse with them than if they had never been born.

At this they shook badly, and I think Christian fainted. But coming round a little, they took up their conversation again about the Giant's advice, and whether after all they had better take it. Now Christian again seemed to be for doing it, but Hopeful made his second reply, as follows.

Hopeful. My brother, said he — do you not remember how brave you have been before now? Apollyon could not crush you, nor could everything you heard and saw and felt in the Valley of the Shadow of Death. What hardship and terror and bewilderment you have already come through! And are you now nothing but fear?

You can see that I am in this dungeon with you, and I am a far weaker man by nature than you are. This Giant has wounded me as well as you, and has cut off the bread and water from my mouth too, and I am mourning in the dark alongside you. But let us practise a little more patience. Remember how you played the man at Vanity Fair, and were afraid neither of the chain, nor the cage, nor even of a bloody death. So let us — at the very least to avoid the shame that it does not become a Christian to be caught in — bear up patiently as well as we can.

Now night having come again, and the Giant and his wife being in bed, she asked him about the prisoners, and whether they had taken his advice. To which he answered: They are stubborn rogues; they would rather put up with every hardship than make an end of themselves.

Then she said: Take them into the castle yard tomorrow and show them the bones and skulls of the ones you have already finished off; and make them believe that before the week is out you will tear them to pieces as you did their fellows before them.

So when morning came, the Giant went to them again and took them into the castle yard, and showed them what his wife had told him to. These, said he, were pilgrims like you, once, and they trespassed on my grounds as you have done; and when I thought fit, I tore them to pieces, and within ten days I will do the same to you. Go on, get back down to your den. And with that he beat them the whole way there.

So they lay all through Saturday in a pitiful state, as before. Now when night came, and when Mrs. Diffidence and her husband the Giant had got to bed, they began to talk about their prisoners again; and the old Giant wondered aloud that he could bring them to an end neither by his blows nor by his advice. At which his wife answered: I am afraid, said she, that they are living in hope that somebody will come to rescue them, or that they have picklocks about them, and hope to escape by them.

And do you say so, my dear? said the Giant. Then I shall search them in the morning.

Well — on Saturday, about midnight, the prisoners began to pray, and kept on praying until almost daybreak.

Now a little before it was light, good Christian, like a man half astonished, burst out in this passionate speech: What a fool I am, said he, to lie in a stinking dungeon like this when I could just as well be walking free! I have a key in my coat called Promise, that will — I am certain of it — open any lock in Doubting Castle.

Then Hopeful said: That is good news, good brother. Pull it out and try it.

Then Christian pulled it out of his coat and began trying it on the dungeon door; and as he turned the key the bolt gave, and the door swung open easily, and Christian and Hopeful both came out. Then he went to the outer door that leads into the castle yard, and with his key opened that door too. After that he went to the iron gate, because that had to be opened as well; and that lock went damnably hard, but the key did open it. Then they shoved the gate open to make their escape quickly — but as it opened, that gate made such a creaking that it woke Giant Despair, who got up in a hurry to chase his prisoners and felt his limbs give way under him, because his fits had taken him again, so that he could not go after them at all.

Then they went on and came to the King's highway, and so were safe, because they were out of his jurisdiction.

Now when they had got back over the stile, they began to work out between themselves what they might do at that stile to stop those who came after them from falling into Giant Despair's hands. So they agreed to put up a pillar there, and to engrave on the side of it this sentence: "Over this stile is the way to Doubting Castle, which is kept by Giant Despair, who despises the King of the Celestial Country and seeks to destroy his holy pilgrims."

So a great many who came after read what was written there, and escaped the danger. When this was done, they sang as follows:

	Off the road we went, and there we found
	what it is to walk on forbidden ground.
	And let those who come after us take care,
	in case their carelessness should land them where
	we were — as trespassers, and prisoners there,
	whose castle's Doubting and whose name's Despair.

The Delectable Mountains

Then they went on until they came to the Delectable Mountains, which belong to the Lord of that hill we spoke of before. So they went up into the mountains to look at the gardens and orchards, the vineyards and the springs of water; and there they drank and washed themselves, and ate freely from the vineyards.

Now on the tops of these mountains there were shepherds feeding their flocks, and they were standing by the roadside. So the Pilgrims went over to them; and leaning on their staffs, as tired pilgrims usually do when they stop to talk with anybody along the road, they asked: Whose Delectable Mountains are these? And whose are the sheep feeding on them?

Shepherd. These mountains are Immanuel's Land, and they are within sight of his city; and the sheep are his too, and he laid down his life for them.

Christian. Is this the road to the Celestial City?

Shepherd. You are exactly on your road.

Christian. How far is it from here?

Shepherd. Too far for anybody except those who will really get there.

Christian. Is the road safe or dangerous?

Shepherd. Safe for those it is meant to be safe for; but offenders shall fall on it.

Christian. Is there anything here to relieve pilgrims who are tired and faint on the road?

Shepherd. The Lord of these mountains has charged us not to be "forgetful to entertain strangers." So everything good about the place is at your service.

I saw too in my dream that when the Shepherds realized they were travellers, they put questions to them as well, which they answered as they had elsewhere: Where have you come from? And how did you get onto the road? And by what means have you kept to it so long? — because only a few of those who set out to come here ever show their faces on these mountains. But when the Shepherds heard their answers and were pleased with them, they looked at them very kindly and said: Welcome to the Delectable Mountains.

The Shepherds, I say — whose names were Knowledge, Experience, Watchful and Sincere — took them by the hand and led them to their tents, and had them share whatever was ready at the time. They said, moreover: We would like you to stay here a while, to get to know us, and to enjoy yourselves further with all the good of these Delectable Mountains. So the Pilgrims said they were content to stay; and they went to their rest that night, because it was very late.

Then I saw in my dream that in the morning the Shepherds called Christian and Hopeful to come and walk with them on the mountains. So they went out with them and walked a while, with a pleasant view on every side. Then the Shepherds said to one another: Shall we show these Pilgrims some wonders?

So when they had decided to do it, they took them first to the top of a hill called Error, which was very steep on the far side, and told them to look down to the bottom. So Christian and Hopeful looked down, and saw at the bottom a number of men smashed to pieces by a fall they had taken from the top.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

The Shepherds answered: Have you not heard of those who were led into error by listening to Hymenaeus and Philetus, on the matter of the faith of the resurrection of the body?

They answered: Yes.

Then the Shepherds said: Those you see lying smashed to pieces at the bottom of this mountain are the men themselves; and they have been left unburied to this day, as you see, as an example to others, to take care how they climb too high or come too near the edge of this mountain.

Then I saw that they took them to the top of another mountain, whose name is Caution, and told them to look into the distance. And when they did, they made out what they took to be several men walking up and down among the tombs that stood there; and they could tell the men were blind, because they kept stumbling over the tombs and could not find their way out from among them.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

The Shepherds answered: Did you not see, a little below these mountains, a stile leading into a meadow on the left-hand side of the road?

They answered: Yes.

Then the Shepherds said: From that stile there runs a path that leads straight to Doubting Castle, which is kept by Giant Despair. And these men — pointing at the figures among the tombs — once came on pilgrimage as you are doing now, right up to that same stile. And because the proper road was rough at that spot, they chose to leave it and go into that meadow; and there they were taken by Giant Despair and thrown into Doubting Castle. And after they had been kept in the dungeon a while, he at last put out their eyes and led them out among those tombs, where he has left them to wander to this very day — so that the saying of the wise man might be fulfilled: "He who wanders out of the way of understanding shall remain in the congregation of the dead."

Then Christian and Hopeful looked at one another, with the tears bursting out of them, but said nothing at all to the Shepherds.

Then I saw in my dream that the Shepherds took them to another place, in a hollow, where there was a door in the side of a hill; and they opened the door and told them to look in. So they looked in, and saw that inside it was very dark and full of smoke. They also thought they could hear a rumbling noise there, like fire, and the cry of somebody being tormented; and they could smell the reek of brimstone.

Christian. Then Christian said: What does this mean?

The Shepherds told them: This is a side road to hell, a way that hypocrites go in by — people who sell their birthright, like Esau; who sell their master, like Judas; who blaspheme the Gospel, like Alexander; and who lie and dissemble, like Ananias and his wife Sapphira.

Then Hopeful said to the Shepherds: I gather that every one of these had the appearance of being on pilgrimage, just as we have now. Is that not so?

Shepherd. Yes — and kept it up a long time, too.

Hopeful. How far might they have got on their pilgrimage in their day, given that they were still thrown away so miserably?

Shepherd. Some further, and some not as far, as these mountains.

Then the Pilgrims said to one another: We shall need to cry out to the Strong One for strength.

Shepherd. Yes — and you will need to use it, when you have it, too.

By this time the Pilgrims wanted to go on, and the Shepherds wanted them to; so they walked on together towards the end of the mountains. Then the Shepherds said to one another: Let us show the Pilgrims the gates of the Celestial City from here, if they have the knack of looking through our telescope.

The Pilgrims gladly accepted the offer; so they took them to the top of a high hill called Clear, and gave them the glass to look through.

Then they tried to look; but the memory of that last thing the Shepherds had shown them made their hands shake, and because of that they could not hold the glass steady. Even so, they thought they saw something like the gate, and something of the glory of the place as well. Then they went away, and sang this song:

	So by the Shepherds secrets are revealed
	which from all other men are kept concealed.
	Come to the Shepherds, then, if you would see
	things deep, things hidden, things of mystery.

When they were about to leave, one of the Shepherds gave them a written note about the road. Another told them to beware of the Flatterer. The third told them to take care that they did not fall asleep on the Enchanted Ground. And the fourth wished them godspeed.

So I woke up from my dream.

And I slept, and dreamed again, and saw the same two Pilgrims going down the mountains along the highway towards the city. Now a little below these mountains, on the left, lies the country of Conceit; and out of that country there comes into the road the Pilgrims were walking on a little crooked lane. So here they met a very brisk young man who had come out of that country, whose name was Ignorance. So Christian asked him what parts he came from, and where he was going.

Ignorance. Sir, I was born in the country lying over there, a little to the left, and I am going to the Celestial City.

Christian. But how do you expect to get in at the gate? You may find some difficulty there.

Ignorance. Just as other good people do, said he.

Christian. But what do you have to show at that gate that would get it opened to you?

Ignorance. I know my Lord's will, and I have lived a good life. I pay every man what I owe him. I pray, I fast, I pay tithes, I give to the poor, and I have left my own country for the place I am going to.

Christian. But you did not come in at the little gate at the head of this road. You came in by that crooked lane; and so I am afraid that, whatever you may think of yourself, when the day of reckoning comes it will be laid to your charge that you are a thief and a robber, instead of your being let into the city.

Ignorance. Gentlemen, you are complete strangers to me and I do not know you. Be content to follow the religion of your country, and I will follow the religion of mine. I hope everything will turn out well. And as for this gate you talk about, everybody knows it is a long way off from our country. I cannot think that anybody in our parts so much as knows the road to it — nor do they need to care whether they do or not, since we have, as you see, a fine pleasant green lane that comes down from our country and joins the road by the shortest way.

When Christian saw that the man was "wise in his own conceit," he said to Hopeful in a whisper: "There is more hope of a fool than of him." And he added: "When a fool walks along the road, his wisdom fails him, and he tells everybody he meets that he is a fool." What — shall we talk with him further, or get ahead of him for now and leave him to think over what he has heard already, and then stop for him again later, and see whether we can do him any good by degrees?

Then Hopeful said:

	Let Ignorance a little while now muse
	on what's been said, and let him not refuse
	good counsel when it comes, lest he remain
	still ignorant of what's the greatest gain.
	God says that those who have no understanding,
	although he made them, he will not save.

Hopeful added: It is not good, I think, to tell him everything at once. Let us pass him by, if you like, and talk to him later, as much as he can take.

So they both went on, and Ignorance came along behind.

Now when they had got a little way past him, they came into a very dark lane, where they met a man whom seven devils had bound with seven strong ropes and were carrying back to the door they had seen in the side of the hill. Now good Christian began to tremble, and so did Hopeful his companion. Yet as the devils led the man away, Christian looked to see whether he knew him; and he thought it might be one Turn-away, who lived in the town of Apostasy. But he could not see his face properly, because he was hanging his head like a thief who has been caught. But once they were past, Hopeful looked after him and spotted a paper on his back with this written on it: "A loose-living believer, and a damnable apostate."

Then Christian said to his companion: Now I remember what I was told about something that happened to a good man near here. The man's name was Little-faith — a good man, and he lived in the town of Sincere. This was the thing.

At the entrance to this passage there comes down from Broad-way Gate a lane called Dead Man's Lane, so called because of the murders that are commonly done there. And this Little-faith, going on pilgrimage as we are now, happened to sit down there and fell asleep. Now at that very time there happened to come down the lane from Broad-way Gate three sturdy rogues, whose names were Faint-heart, Mistrust and Guilt — three brothers; and spotting Little-faith where he was, they came galloping up at speed.

Now the good man had just woken from his sleep and was getting up to go on with his journey. So they all came up to him and told him to stand, with threatening language. At this, Little-faith went as white as a cloud, and had the power neither to fight nor to run.

Then Faint-heart said: Hand over your purse. But when he was slow to do it, because he was unwilling to lose his money, Mistrust ran up to him and thrust his hand into his pocket and pulled out a bag of silver. Then Little-faith cried out: Thieves! Thieves! At which Guilt, with a great club he had in his hand, struck Little-faith on the head, and with that blow felled him flat to the ground, where he lay bleeding like a man who would bleed to death.

All this while the thieves stood by. But at last, hearing that somebody was on the road and afraid it might be one Great-grace, who lives in the city of Good-confidence, they took to their heels and left this good man to fend for himself.

Now after a while Little-faith came round, and getting up, managed to scramble on his way. That was the story.

Hopeful. But did they take everything he had?

Christian. No. They never went through the place where his jewels were, so he kept those. But I was told the good man was much distressed by his loss, because the thieves got most of his spending money. What they did not get, as I said, were the jewels; and he had a little odd money left as well, but hardly enough to get him to the end of his journey. In fact, if I was not misinformed, he was forced to beg as he went, to keep himself alive, since he was not allowed to sell his jewels. But beg and do what he could, he went — as we say — with many a hungry belly for most of the rest of the way.

Hopeful. But is it not a wonder they did not get his certificate off him, the one he was to be admitted by at the Celestial Gate?

Christian. It is a wonder. But they did not get it — though he did not keep it from them by any cleverness of his own; because being terrified by their coming on him, he had neither the power nor the presence of mind to hide anything. So it was more by good Providence than by any effort of his that they missed that good thing.

Hopeful. But it must have been a comfort to him that they did not get that jewel from him.

Christian. It might have been a great comfort to him, if he had made the use of it he should have. But those who told me the story said he made very little use of it all the rest of the way, and that because of the shock of having his money taken. In fact he forgot it for a great part of the rest of his journey; and besides, whenever it did come into his mind and he began to take comfort from it, fresh thoughts of his loss would come over him again, and those thoughts would swallow up everything else.

Hopeful. Poor man. That must have been a great grief to him.

Christian. Grief! Yes, a grief indeed. Would it not have been so to any of us, if we had been treated as he was — robbed and wounded too, and in a strange place, as he was? It is a wonder he did not die of grief, poor heart. I was told that he scattered nearly the whole rest of the way with nothing but mournful and bitter complaints, telling everybody who caught up with him, or whom he caught up with, where he had been robbed and how; who it was that did it, and what he had lost; how he was wounded, and how he barely escaped with his life.

Hopeful. But it is a wonder his need did not drive him to sell or pawn some of his jewels, so that he could have had something to relieve himself with on his journey.

Christian. You talk like a man who still has the eggshell on his head to this very day. What should he pawn them for, or who should he sell them to? In that whole country where he was robbed his jewels were valued at nothing; nor did he want the kind of relief that could have been got there. Besides, if his jewels had been missing at the gate of the Celestial City, he would have been shut out of an inheritance there — and he knew that perfectly well; and that would have been worse to him than the appearance and the villainy of ten thousand thieves.

Hopeful. Why are you so sharp with me, my brother? Esau sold his birthright, and for a bowl of stew at that, and that birthright was his greatest jewel. And if he did, why might Little-faith not have done the same?

Christian. Esau did sell his birthright, certainly, and so do many others, and by doing it shut themselves out of the chief blessing — as that wretch did too. But you must draw a distinction between Esau and Little-faith, and between their situations.

Esau's birthright was a symbol; Little-faith's jewels were not. Esau's belly was his god; Little-faith's belly was not. Esau's lack was in his bodily appetite; Little-faith's was not. Besides, Esau could see no further than satisfying his appetites: "Look, I am at the point of death," said he, "and what good will this birthright do me?" But Little-faith, although it was his lot to have only a little faith, was kept by that little faith from such excesses, and was made to see his jewels and value them too highly to sell them as Esau sold his birthright.

You do not read anywhere that Esau had faith — not so much as a little. So it is no wonder that where the flesh alone rules, as it will in a man who has no faith to resist it, he sells his birthright and his soul and everything else, and that to the devil of hell; because it is with such men as it is with the ass in heat, that cannot be turned aside. When their minds are set on their appetites, they will have them whatever they cost.

But Little-faith was of another temper. His mind was on divine things; his living was on things that were spiritual, and from above. So what purpose would there be for a man of that temper to sell his jewels — even if there had been anybody who would have bought them — in order to fill his mind with empty things? Will a man pay a penny to fill his belly with hay? Or could you persuade a turtledove to live on carrion like a crow? Though faithless men can pawn or mortgage or sell what they have for the sake of their appetites, and themselves outright into the bargain, still, those who have faith — saving faith, though only a little of it — cannot do so. That, my brother, is where you are mistaken.

Hopeful. I admit it. But your severe remark had almost made me angry.

Christian. Why, I only compared you to some of the brisker sort of birds, that will go running to and fro on untrodden paths with the shell still on their heads. But let that pass, and think about the matter we were discussing, and all will be well between you and me.

Hopeful. But, Christian — these three fellows, I am convinced in my heart, are nothing but a pack of cowards. Would they have run as they did, do you think, at the noise of one man coming along the road? Why did Little-faith not summon up more courage? It seems to me he could have stood one round with them, and given in when there was no help for it.

Christian. That they are cowards, many have said; but few have found it so when they were tried. As for great courage, Little-faith had none. And I can see, my brother, that if you had been the man concerned, you are only for one round and then giving in. And truly, since this is the height of your spirit now, while they are at a distance from us, if they were to appear to you as they did to him they might give you second thoughts.

But consider again: they are only hired thieves. They serve under the king of the bottomless pit, who will come to their aid himself if he has to, and his voice is like the roaring of a lion. I have been in the same fight myself as Little-faith was, and I found it a terrible thing. These three villains set on me; and when I began, like a Christian, to resist, they gave one call and in came their master. I would have given my life for a penny, as the saying goes — except that, as God would have it, I was clothed in tested armour. And yes, even so equipped, I found it hard work to acquit myself like a man. No man can tell what waits for us in that combat but the man who has been in the battle himself.

Hopeful. Well — but they ran, you see, when they only supposed that Great-grace was on the road.

Christian. True. They have often run, both they and their master, when Great-grace has merely appeared; and no wonder, because he is the King's Champion. But I imagine you will allow some difference between Little-faith and the King's Champion. Not all the King's subjects are his champions, nor can they, when they are tested, do the feats of war that he can. Is it reasonable to think that a little child could handle Goliath as David did? Or that there should be the strength of an ox in a wren? Some are strong, some are weak; some have great faith and some have little. This man was one of the weak ones, and so he got the worst of it.

Hopeful. I wish it had been Great-grace, for their sakes.

Christian. If it had been, he might have had his hands full. Because I must tell you that although Great-grace is excellent with his weapons, and can do well enough with them so long as he keeps them at sword's point, still, if they get inside his guard — even Faint-heart, or Mistrust, or the other one — it will go hard but they will tip his heels up. And when a man is down, you know, what can he do?

Anybody who looks closely at Great-grace's face will see scars and cuts there that easily prove what I am saying. Yes, and I once heard that he said, and that when he was in the fight, "We despaired even of life." How these sturdy rogues and their fellows made David groan and mourn and roar! And Heman and Hezekiah too, though they were champions in their day, were forced to bestir themselves when these attacked them — and even so, they had their coats soundly beaten by them. Peter once wanted to go and try what he could do; and though some say of him that he is the prince of the apostles, they handled him so that in the end they made him afraid of a mere serving girl.
