Besides, their king is at their whistle. He is never out of earshot; and if at any time they get the worst of it, he comes in to help them if he possibly can. And of him it is said: "The sword of the one who attacks him cannot hold, nor the spear, the dart, nor the coat of mail. He counts iron as straw, and bronze as rotten wood. The arrow cannot make him run; slingstones are turned to stubble against him. Darts are counted as stubble; he laughs at the shaking of a spear."

What can a man do in a case like this? It is true that if a man could have Job's horse at every turn, and had the skill and the courage to ride him, he might do remarkable things: "for his neck is clothed with thunder, and he will not be afraid of the grasshopper; the glory of his snorting is terrible; he paws in the valley and rejoices in his strength; he goes out to meet the armed men. He mocks at fear and is not frightened, and he does not turn back from the sword. The quiver rattles against him, and the glittering spear and the shield. He swallows the ground with fierceness and rage, and he will not believe it is the sound of the trumpet. Among the trumpets he says, Ha, ha! And he smells the battle far off, the thunder of the captains and the shouting."

But for foot-soldiers like you and me, let us never wish to meet an enemy, nor boast as though we could do better when we hear that others have been worn down, nor be pleased with the thought of our own manhood. Because men like that generally come off worst when they are tried. Look at Peter, whom I mentioned before. He would swagger — yes, he would; he would do better, so his empty mind prompted him to say, and stand up for his Master more than anybody else. But who was ever so beaten and so run down by these villains as he was?

So when we hear that such robberies are done on the King's highway, there are two things it is right for us to do.

First, to go out armed, and to be sure to take a shield with us. Because it was for want of a shield that the man who struck so hard at Leviathan could not make him yield; and indeed, if that is missing, he is not afraid of us at all. That is why the one with the knowledge has said, "Above all, taking the shield of faith, with which you shall be able to quench all the fiery darts of the wicked."

Second, it is good as well that we ask the King for an escort — yes, that he will go with us himself. This is what made David rejoice when he was in the Valley of the Shadow of Death; and Moses would rather have died where he stood than go one step without his God. My brother, if he will only come along with us, what need have we to be afraid of ten thousand who set themselves against us? But without him, the proud helpers "fall among the slain."

I for my part have been in the fight before now; and though, by the goodness of the one who is best, I am alive, as you see, still I cannot boast about my manhood. I shall be glad if I meet with no more shocks like that one — though I am afraid we are not out of all danger yet. However, since the lion and the bear have not devoured me so far, I hope God will deliver us from the next uncircumcised Philistine too.

Then Christian sang:

	Poor Little-faith! Have you been among the thieves?
	Were you robbed? Remember this, whoever believes
	and gets more faith, shall then a victor be
	over ten thousand — else scarce over three.

So they went on, and Ignorance followed. Then they went on until they came to a place where they saw a road come into their road, and it seemed to run just as straight as the one they were meant to take; and here they did not know which of the two to take, because both seemed to lie straight ahead. So they stood still to consider.

And as they were thinking about the road, a figure came to them, black of body but covered in a very light robe, and asked them why they were standing there. They answered that they were going to the Celestial City, but did not know which of these roads to take.

Follow me, said the figure — that is where I am going too.

So they followed him along the road that had just joined theirs, which by degrees turned, and turned them so far away from the city they wanted to reach that in a short time their faces were pointing away from it. And still they followed him. But before long, before they realized it, he had led them both inside the compass of a net, in which they were so tangled that they did not know what to do; and with that the white robe fell off the black figure's back. Then they saw where they were. So there they lay, crying for some time, because they could not get themselves out.

Christian. Then Christian said to his companion: Now I see that I have gone wrong. Did the Shepherds not tell us to beware of the flatterers? It is as the wise man says, and we have found it out today: "A man who flatters his neighbour spreads a net for his feet."

Hopeful. They also gave us a written note of directions about the road, to help us find it more surely; but we have forgotten to read that as well, and have not kept ourselves out of the paths of the destroyer. David was wiser than we are here, because he says, "Concerning the works of men, by the word of your lips I have kept myself from the paths of the destroyer."

So they lay there lamenting themselves in the net.

At last they spotted a Shining One coming towards them, with a whip of fine cord in his hand. When he had come to the place where they were, he asked them where they had come from and what they were doing there. They told him they were poor pilgrims going to Zion, but had been led off their road by a black figure dressed in white, who told us, they said, to follow him, because he was going there too.

Then the one with the whip said: It is Flatterer, a false apostle, who has transformed himself into an angel of light.

So he tore the net open and let the men out. Then he said to them: Follow me, and I will set you on your road again. So he led them back to the road they had left in order to follow the Flatterer.

Then he asked them: Where did you sleep last night? They said: With the Shepherds, on the Delectable Mountains. He asked them then whether they had not had a note of directions for the road from those Shepherds. They answered: Yes.

But did you, said he, when you came to a standstill, pull out your note and read it? They answered: No. He asked them why. They said they had forgotten.

He asked, further, whether the Shepherds had not told them to beware of the Flatterer. They answered: Yes — but we did not imagine, they said, that this fine-spoken man was he.

Then I saw in my dream that he ordered them to lie down; and when they did, he punished them severely, to teach them the good road they ought to walk in. And as he punished them he said, "As many as I love, I rebuke and chasten; be zealous, therefore, and repent."

When this was done, he told them to go on their way and pay good attention to the Shepherds' other directions. So they thanked him for all his kindness, and went quietly along the right road, singing:

	Come over here, you that walk along the way,
	and see how pilgrims fare who go astray!
	They are caught in an entangling net,
	because good counsel they too lightly did forget.
	True, they were rescued — but you see as well,
	they're whipped besides. Let that your warning be.

Now after a while they saw, a long way off, somebody coming quietly and alone along the highway to meet them. Then Christian said to his companion: There is a man over there with his back towards Zion, and he is coming to meet us.

Hopeful. I see him. Let us look out for ourselves now, in case he turns out to be a flatterer too.

So he came nearer and nearer, and at last came up to them. His name was Atheist, and he asked them where they were going.

Christian. We are going to Mount Zion.

Then Atheist burst into a great fit of laughter.

Christian. What is the meaning of your laughing?

Atheist. I am laughing to see what ignorant people you are, to take on such a wearisome journey — and all you are likely to get for your pains is the walking.

Christian. Why, man — do you think we shall not be received?

Atheist. Received! There is no such place as you are dreaming of anywhere in this world.

Christian. But there is in the world to come.

Atheist. When I was at home in my own country I heard what you are saying now; and from hearing it I went out to see, and I have been looking for this city these twenty years — and I find no more of it than I did the first day I set out.

Christian. We have both heard, and we believe, that there is such a place to be found.

Atheist. If I had not believed it when I was at home, I would not have come this far to look. But finding nothing — and I should have found it, if there had been such a place, because I have gone further to look for it than you have — I am going back again, and I mean to refresh myself with the things I threw away then, for the hope of something that I now see does not exist.

Christian. Then Christian said to Hopeful his companion: Is what this man says true?

Hopeful. Take care — he is one of the flatterers. Remember what it has already cost us once for listening to that kind of fellow. What, no Mount Zion? Did we not see the gate of the city from the Delectable Mountains? And besides, are we not to walk by faith now? Let us go on, said Hopeful, in case the man with the whip catches up with us again.

You should have been the one teaching me that lesson, and I will whisper it in your ear instead: "Stop, my son, listening to instruction that will cause you to stray from the words of knowledge." I say, my brother, stop listening to him, and let us "believe to the saving of the soul."

Christian. My brother, I did not put the question to you because I doubted the truth of what we believe myself, but to test you and draw out from you some fruit of the honesty of your heart. As for this man, I know he is blinded by the god of this world. Let us go on, you and I, knowing that we have belief in the truth, "and no lie is of the truth."

Hopeful. Now I rejoice in the hope of the glory of God.

So they turned away from the man; and he, laughing at them, went on his way.

The Enchanted Ground

Then I saw in my dream that they went on until they came into a certain country whose air naturally makes a person drowsy, if he comes into it as a stranger. And here Hopeful began to get very dull and heavy with sleep; so he said to Christian: I am beginning to get so drowsy now that I can hardly keep my eyes open. Let us lie down here and take a nap.

Christian. Not on any account, said the other — in case we fall asleep and never wake up again.

Hopeful. Why, my brother? Sleep is sweet to a working man. We might be refreshed if we took a nap.

Christian. Do you not remember that one of the Shepherds told us to beware of the Enchanted Ground? What he meant by that was that we should beware of sleeping. "So let us not sleep, as others do, but let us watch and be sober."

Hopeful. I admit I was in the wrong. And if I had been here alone, I would have run the risk of death by sleeping. I see the truth of what the wise man says: "Two are better than one." Your company has been a mercy to me all this way, and you shall have a good reward for your trouble.

Christian. Now then, said Christian, to keep off drowsiness in this place, let us get into some good conversation.

Hopeful. With all my heart, said the other.

Christian. Where shall we begin?

Hopeful. Where God began with us. But you begin, if you please.

Christian. First I will sing you this song:

	When saints grow sleepy, let them come this way
	and hear how these two pilgrims talk and pray.
	Yes, let them learn from them, by any means,
	to hold their drowsy, slumbering eyelids open.
	The fellowship of saints, if managed well,
	will keep them wide awake, in spite of hell.

Christian. Then Christian began, and said: I will ask you a question. How did you first come to think of doing what you are doing now?

Hopeful. Do you mean, how did I first come to look after the good of my soul?

Christian. Yes, that is my meaning.

Hopeful. I carried on a long while enjoying the things that were seen and sold at our fair — things which I believe now, if I had stayed with them, would have drowned me in ruin and destruction.

Christian. What things were they?

Hopeful. All the treasures and riches of the world. I also took a great deal of pleasure in wild living, revelling, drinking, swearing, lying, impurity, breaking the Sabbath, and anything else that tends to destroy the soul. But I found at last, by hearing and thinking about divine things — which in fact I heard from you, and from beloved Faithful, who was put to death for his faith and his good living at Vanity Fair — that "the end of these things is death," and that it is because of them that "the wrath of God comes upon the children of disobedience."

Christian. And did you fall under the power of that conviction straight away?

Hopeful. No. I was not willing at first to know the evil of sin, or the damnation that follows from committing it. When my mind first began to be shaken by the Word, I tried to shut my eyes against its light.

Christian. But what was the reason you behaved like that towards the first workings of God's blessed Spirit on you?

Hopeful. The reasons were these. First, I did not know this was the work of God in me. It never occurred to me that God begins the conversion of a sinner by waking him up about his sin. Second, sin was still very sweet to my flesh, and I was unwilling to leave it. Third, I could not see how to part with my old companions; their company and the things they did were too attractive to me. Fourth, the hours when these convictions were on me were such troublesome, heart-terrifying hours that I could not bear them — could not even bear the memory of them on my heart.

Christian. Then it seems you sometimes got rid of your trouble?

Hopeful. Yes, truly. But it would come back into my mind again, and then I would be as bad as before — worse, in fact.

Christian. And what was it that brought your sins back to mind?

Hopeful. A great many things. If I so much as met a good man in the street; or if I heard anybody reading in the Bible; or if my head started to ache; or if I was told that some of my neighbours were sick; or if I heard the bell tolling for somebody who had died; or if I thought about dying myself; or if I heard that somebody else had died suddenly — but above all, when I thought about myself, and that I must very soon come to judgment.

Christian. And could you at any time get the guilt of sin off you easily, when it came on you by any of these ways?

Hopeful. No, not I — because then they would take a faster hold on my conscience. And then, if I so much as thought about going back to sin, though my mind was set against it, it would be a double torment to me.

Christian. And what did you do then?

Hopeful. I thought I would have to try to mend my life; because otherwise, I thought, I am certain to be damned.

Christian. And did you try to mend it?

Hopeful. Yes. I ran not only from my sins but from sinful company as well, and took to religious duties — praying, reading, weeping over sin, telling my neighbours the truth, and so on. I did these things and a great many more, too many to go into here.

Christian. And did you think yourself in a good way then?

Hopeful. Yes, for a while. But at last my trouble came tumbling down on me again, and that right on top of all my reforming.

Christian. How did that come about, since you were reformed by then?

Hopeful. There were several things that brought it on me, especially sayings like these: "All our righteousness is like filthy rags." "By the works of the law shall no flesh be justified." "When you have done all those things, say, We are unprofitable" — and many more of the same sort.

From these I began to reason with myself like this: If all my righteousness is filthy rags; if by keeping the law nobody can be justified; and if, when we have done everything, we are still unprofitable, then it is nothing but folly to think of getting to heaven by the law.

I thought further along these lines: if a man runs up a hundred pounds of debt at the shopkeeper's, and after that pays for everything he takes, still, if the old debt stands there in the book uncrossed, the shopkeeper can sue him for it and have him thrown into prison until he pays it.

Christian. Well, and how did you apply that to yourself?

Hopeful. Why, I thought this to myself. By my sins I have run a long way into God's book, and my reforming now will not pay off that score. So under all my present improvements I still had to think: how am I to be freed from the damnation I have brought myself into danger of by everything I did before?

Christian. A very good application. But please go on.

Hopeful. Another thing that has troubled me, even since my recent improvements, is this: if I look closely at the best of what I do now, I still see sin — new sin — mixing itself in with the best of what I do. So that now I am forced to conclude that in spite of all my old foolish opinions of myself and of my religious duties, I have committed enough sin in a single duty to send me to hell, even if my earlier life had been faultless.

Christian. And what did you do then?

Hopeful. Do! I could not tell what to do, until I confided in Faithful — because he and I knew each other well. And he told me that unless I could get hold of the righteousness of a man who had never sinned, then neither my own righteousness nor all the righteousness in the world could save me.

Christian. And did you think he was telling the truth?

Hopeful. If he had told me that while I was pleased and satisfied with my own reforming, I would have called him a fool for his trouble. But now, since I can see my own weakness and the sin that sticks to my best performance, I have been forced round to his opinion.

Christian. But when he first suggested it to you, did you think there was such a man to be found — one of whom it could justly be said that he had never committed a sin?

Hopeful. I have to admit the words sounded strange at first; but after a little more talk and time in his company, I was fully convinced of it.

Christian. And did you ask him who this man was, and how you were to be justified by him?

Hopeful. Yes. And he told me it was the Lord Jesus, who lives at the right hand of the Most High. And this, he said, is how you must be justified by him: by trusting in what he did in his own person in the days of his flesh, and in what he suffered when he hung on the tree.

I asked him further how that man's righteousness could have the power to justify somebody else before God. And he told me that he was the mighty God, and did what he did, and died his death as well, not for himself but for me — and that his doings, and the worth of them, would be credited to me if I believed in him.

Christian. And what did you do then?

Hopeful. I raised my objections against believing, because I thought he was not willing to save me.

Christian. And what did Faithful say to that?

Hopeful. He told me to go to him and see. Then I said that would be presumption; but he said no, because I had been invited to come. Then he gave me a book of Jesus' own composing, to encourage me to come more freely; and he said of that book that every jot and tittle in it stood firmer than heaven and earth.

Then I asked him what I was to do when I came; and he told me I must beg on my knees, with all my heart and soul, for the Father to reveal him to me. Then I asked him further how I was to make my petition to him. And he said: Go, and you will find him on a mercy seat, where he sits all the year round to give pardon and forgiveness to those who come.

I told him I did not know what to say when I came. And he told me to say something to this effect: God be merciful to me, a sinner, and make me know and believe in Jesus Christ. Because I can see that if his righteousness had not existed, or if I have no faith in that righteousness, I am utterly cast away. Lord, I have heard that you are a merciful God, and that you have appointed that your Son Jesus Christ should be the Saviour of the world; and further, that you are willing to give him to such a poor sinner as I am — and I am a sinner indeed. Lord, take this opportunity, then, and magnify your grace in the salvation of my soul, through your Son Jesus Christ. Amen.

Christian. And did you do as you were told?

Hopeful. Yes — over and over and over.

Christian. And did the Father reveal his Son to you?

Hopeful. Not the first time, nor the second, nor the third, nor the fourth, nor the fifth — no, nor the sixth time either.

Christian. What did you do then?

Hopeful. What! Why, I could not tell what to do.

Christian. Did you not think of leaving off praying?

Hopeful. Yes — two hundred times over.

Christian. And what was the reason you did not?

Hopeful. I believed that what I had been told was true — that without the righteousness of this Christ, all the world could not save me. And so I thought to myself: if I leave off, I die; and I can only die at the throne of grace. And along with that, this came into my mind: "Though it delays, wait for it, because it will surely come; it will not delay." So I kept on praying until the Father showed me his Son.

Christian. And how was he revealed to you?

Hopeful. I did not see him with my bodily eyes, but with the eyes of my understanding; and this is how it happened. One day I was very sad — sadder, I think, than at any other time in my life — and this sadness came from a fresh sight of the size and the vileness of my sins. And as I was expecting nothing but hell and the everlasting damnation of my soul, suddenly, as I thought, I saw the Lord Jesus look down from heaven at me and say, "Believe on the Lord Jesus Christ, and you shall be saved."

But I answered: Lord, I am a great sinner — a very great sinner. And he answered, "My grace is sufficient for you."

Then I said: But, Lord, what is believing? And then I saw from that saying, "He that comes to me shall never hunger, and he that believes on me shall never thirst," that believing and coming were one and the same thing; and that whoever came — that is, whoever ran out in his heart and his affections after salvation by Christ — really did believe in Christ.

Then the tears came into my eyes, and I asked further: But, Lord, may such a great sinner as I am really be accepted by you and saved by you? And I heard him say, "And the one who comes to me I will in no way cast out."

Then I said: But how, Lord, must I think of you as I come to you, so that my faith may be placed on you rightly? Then he said, "Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners." "He is the end of the law for righteousness to everyone who believes." "He died for our sins and rose again for our justification." "He loved us and washed us from our sins in his own blood." "He is the mediator between God and us." "He lives for ever to intercede for us."

From all of which I gathered that I must look for righteousness in his person, and for the settling of my sins by his blood; and that what he did in obedience to his Father's law, and in submitting to its penalty, was not for himself but for whoever will accept it for his own salvation and be grateful for it. And now my heart was full of joy, my eyes full of tears, and my whole affection running over with love for the name and the people and the ways of Jesus Christ.

Christian. This was a revelation of Christ to your soul indeed. But tell me particularly what effect it had on your spirit.

Hopeful. It made me see that all the world, for all the righteousness in it, is in a state of condemnation. It made me see that God the Father, though he is just, can justly justify the sinner who comes to him. It made me deeply ashamed of the vileness of my former life, and overwhelmed me with the sense of my own ignorance; because no thought had ever come into my heart before that showed me the beauty of Jesus Christ like that. It made me love a holy life, and long to do something for the honour and glory of the name of the Lord Jesus. Yes — I thought that if I had a thousand gallons of blood in my body now, I could spill every drop of it for the sake of the Lord Jesus.

Then I saw in my dream that Hopeful looked back and saw Ignorance, whom they had left behind, coming after them. Look, he said to Christian, how far back that youngster is loitering.

Christian. Yes, yes, I see him. He has no use for our company.

Hopeful. But I should have thought it would have done him no harm to have kept pace with us so far.

Christian. That is true; but I warrant you he thinks otherwise.

Hopeful. I think he does. But even so, let us wait for him.

So they did. Then Christian said to him: Come on, man — why are you hanging back so far?

Ignorance. I take my pleasure in walking alone — a great deal more than in company, unless I happen to like it better.

Then Christian said quietly to Hopeful: Did I not tell you he had no use for our company? But even so, said he, come up, and let us talk the time away in this lonely place.

Then, turning his speech to Ignorance, he said: Come — how are you? How do things stand between God and your soul now?

Ignorance. Well, I hope. Because I am always full of good impulses that come into my mind and comfort me as I walk.

Christian. What good impulses? Tell us, please.

Ignorance. Why, I think about God and heaven.

Christian. So do the devils and the damned souls.

Ignorance. But I think about them and I want them.

Christian. So do many people who are never likely to get there. "The soul of the sluggard desires, and has nothing."

Ignorance. But I think about them, and I leave everything for them.

Christian. That I doubt. Because leaving everything is a hard business — harder than most people realize. But why, or on what grounds, are you persuaded that you have left everything for God and heaven?

Ignorance. My heart tells me so.

Christian. The wise man says, "He that trusts his own heart is a fool."

Ignorance. That is said about an evil heart, but mine is a good one.

Christian. But how do you prove that?

Ignorance. It comforts me with hopes of heaven.

Christian. That may be through its own deceitfulness; because a man's heart can supply him with comfort in hopes of a thing he has no ground at all to hope for.

Ignorance. But my heart and my life agree together, and so my hope is well grounded.

Christian. Who told you your heart and your life agree together?

Ignorance. My heart tells me so.

Christian. Ask my friend whether I am a thief! Your heart tells you so! Unless the Word of God bears witness in this matter, no other testimony is worth anything.

Ignorance. But is it not a good heart that has good thoughts? And is that not a good life that goes according to God's commandments?

Christian. Yes, that is a good heart that has good thoughts, and that is a good life that goes according to God's commandments. But it is one thing to have these, and quite another only to think you have them.

Ignorance. Tell me, then — what do you count as good thoughts, and a life according to God's commandments?

Christian. There are good thoughts of various kinds: some about ourselves, some about God, some about Christ, and some about other things.

Ignorance. What are good thoughts about ourselves?

Christian. Those that agree with the Word of God.

Ignorance. And when do our thoughts about ourselves agree with the Word of God?

Christian. When we pass the same judgment on ourselves that the Word passes. To explain myself: the Word of God says of people in their natural condition, "There is none righteous; there is none that does good." It also says that "every imagination of the heart of man is only evil, and that continually." And again, "The imagination of man's heart is evil from his youth." Now when we think this about ourselves, and feel it, then our thoughts are good ones, because they agree with the Word of God.

Ignorance. I will never believe that my heart is as bad as that.

Christian. Then you have never had one good thought about yourself in your life. But let me go on. As the Word passes judgment on our hearts, so it passes judgment on our ways; and when our thoughts about our hearts and our ways agree with the judgment the Word gives on both, then both are good, because they agree with it.

Ignorance. Explain what you mean.

Christian. Why, the Word of God says that man's ways are crooked ways — not good, but twisted. It says they are naturally off the good road, and that they have never known it. Now when a man thinks this about his own ways — and I mean when he thinks it with real feeling, and with humbling of heart — then he has good thoughts about his own ways, because his thoughts now agree with the judgment of the Word of God.

Ignorance. And what are good thoughts about God?

Christian. Just as I said about ourselves: when our thoughts about God agree with what the Word says about him. And that means when we think about his being and his attributes as the Word has taught us, which I cannot go into at length now. But to speak of him with reference to ourselves: we have right thoughts of God when we think that he knows us better than we know ourselves, and can see sin in us when and where we can see none in ourselves; when we think that he knows our innermost thoughts, and that our heart, with all its depths, is always open to his eyes; and also when we think that all our righteousness stinks in his nostrils, and that he cannot therefore bear to see us stand in front of him with any confidence, even in the very best of what we do.

Ignorance. Do you think I am such a fool as to think God can see no further than I can? Or that I would come to God on the strength of the best of what I do?

Christian. Why, how do you think about this, then?

Ignorance. Why, to be brief — I think I must believe in Christ for justification.

Christian. What! You think you must believe in Christ when you cannot see that you need him! You see neither the corruption you were born with nor the sins you have actually committed; you have such an opinion of yourself and of what you do as plainly shows you to be a man who never saw any necessity for Christ's personal righteousness to justify him before God. How, then, can you say, I believe in Christ?

Ignorance. I believe well enough for all that.

Christian. And how do you believe?

Ignorance. I believe that Christ died for sinners, and that I shall be justified before God from the curse through his gracious acceptance of my obedience to his law. Or to put it another way: Christ makes my religious duties acceptable to his Father by virtue of his merits, and so I shall be justified.

Christian. Let me answer this confession of your faith.

First, you believe with an imaginary faith; because this faith is nowhere described in the Word.

Second, you believe with a false faith; because it takes justification away from the personal righteousness of Christ and applies it to your own.

Third, this faith does not make Christ the justifier of your person, but only of your actions — and of your person for the sake of your actions, which is false.

Fourth, therefore this faith is deceitful, and will leave you under wrath on the day of God Almighty. Because true justifying faith puts the soul, feeling its lost condition under the law, to flying for refuge to Christ's righteousness — and that righteousness of his is not some act of grace by which he makes your obedience acceptable to God for justification, but his own personal obedience to the law, doing and suffering for us what the law required of us. That righteousness, I say, is what true faith accepts; and the soul, sheltered under the skirt of it and presented by it as spotless before God, is accepted and acquitted from condemnation.

Ignorance. What! Would you have us trust to what Christ has done in his own person, outside us altogether? This notion would loosen the reins on our appetites and let us live exactly as we please. Because what does it matter how we live, if we can be justified by Christ's personal righteousness from everything, as soon as we believe it?

Christian. Ignorance is your name, and as your name is, so are you — and this very answer of yours proves what I say. You are ignorant of what justifying righteousness is, and just as ignorant of how to secure your soul from the heavy wrath of God through faith in it. Yes, and you are ignorant too of the true effects of saving faith in this righteousness of Christ, which are to bend and win the heart over to God in Christ, to love his name, his Word, his ways and his people — and not what you ignorantly imagine.

Hopeful. Ask him whether he ever had Christ revealed to him from heaven.

Ignorance. What! You are a man for revelations, are you? I believe that everything you and all the rest of you say on that subject is nothing but the product of disordered brains.

Hopeful. Why, man! Christ is so hidden in God from the natural understanding of the flesh that no man can know him savingly unless God the Father reveals him.

Ignorance. That is your faith, but not mine; and yet mine is as good as yours, I have no doubt, even though I do not have as many fancies in my head as you do.

Christian. Allow me to put in a word. You ought not to speak so lightly about this matter. Because I will boldly state, exactly as my good companion has done, that no man can know Jesus Christ except by the revelation of the Father. Yes, and the faith too, by which the soul lays hold of Christ, must — if it is the right kind — be worked in us by the exceeding greatness of his mighty power; and the working of that faith, I can see, poor Ignorance, is a thing you know nothing about. Wake up, then; see your own wretchedness, and run to the Lord Jesus. And by his righteousness, which is the righteousness of God, since he himself is God, you will be delivered from condemnation.

Ignorance. You go so fast that I cannot keep up with you. You go on ahead; I must stay behind a while.

Then they said:

	Well, Ignorance, will you go on being a fool,
	and slight good counsel, ten times given you?
	And if you still refuse it, you will know
	before too long the evil of doing so.
	Remember, man, in time; stoop, do not fear.
	Good counsel taken well will save; so hear.
	But if you slight it still, then you will be
	the loser, Ignorance — I promise you.

Then Christian turned to his companion.

Christian. Well, come along, my good Hopeful. I can see that you and I are going to walk by ourselves again.

So I saw in my dream that they went on at a good pace ahead, and Ignorance came shambling after. Then Christian said to his companion: I feel very sorry for this poor man. It will certainly go badly with him in the end.

Hopeful. There are plenty in our town in the same condition — whole families, yes, whole streets of them, and pilgrims among them too. And if there are so many in our parts, how many do you think there must be in the place where he was born?

Christian. Indeed, the Word says, "He has blinded their eyes, so that they should not see." But now that we are on our own — what do you make of such men? Do you think they never have convictions of sin, and consequently fears that their situation is dangerous?

Hopeful. No — you answer that question yourself; you are the older man.

Christian. Then I say that sometimes, as I think, they may. But being naturally ignorant, they do not understand that convictions like that are for their good; and so they desperately try to stifle them, and go on presumptuously flattering themselves in the way their own hearts want to go.

Hopeful. I do believe, as you say, that fear does men a great deal of good, and helps to set them right at the beginning of their pilgrimage.

Christian. Without any doubt it does, if it is the right kind. Because the Word says so: "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom."

Hopeful. How would you describe right fear?

Christian. True or right fear can be recognized by three things.

First, by where it comes from: it is caused by saving convictions about sin.

Second, it drives the soul to take a fast hold of Christ for salvation.

Third, it produces and keeps up in the soul a great reverence for God, his Word and his ways — keeping it tender, and making it afraid to turn away from them, to the right hand or the left, to anything that might dishonour God, break its peace, grieve the Spirit or give the enemy something to sneer about.

Hopeful. Well said. I believe you have spoken the truth. Are we nearly past the Enchanted Ground now?

Christian. Why, are you tired of this conversation?

Hopeful. No, truly — I only wanted to know where we are.

Christian. We have not above two miles more to go on it. But let us come back to our subject. Now the ignorant do not know that convictions of the kind that put them in fear are for their good, and so they try to stifle them.

Hopeful. How do they try to stifle them?

Christian. First, they think those fears are worked in them by the devil, though in fact they are worked by God; and thinking so, they resist them as things that tend directly to their own ruin.

Second, they also think these fears tend to spoil their faith — when, poor men, alas for them, they have no faith at all. And so they harden their hearts against them.

Third, they take it for granted that they ought not to be afraid, and so, in spite of the fears, they grow presumptuously confident.

Fourth, they see that these fears tend to take away their pitiful old self-made holiness, and so they resist them with all their might.

Hopeful. I know something of this myself, because before I knew myself it was just so with me.

Christian. Well, let us leave our neighbour Ignorance to himself for now, and take up another useful question.

Hopeful. With all my heart — but you must still start it.

Christian. Well then: did you not know, about ten years ago, a man called Temporary in your parts, who was very forward in religion at the time?

Hopeful. Know him! Yes. He lived in Graceless, a town about two miles from Honesty, and he lived next door to a man called Turnback.

Christian. That is right; he lived under the same roof with him. Well, that man was much awakened once. I believe that then he had some sight of his sins and of the wages that were owing for them.

Hopeful. I am of your mind, because my house was not more than three miles from his, and he would often come to me, and with a great many tears. Truly I pitied the man, and was not altogether without hope for him. But one can see that not everybody who cries, Lord, Lord, is in earnest.

Christian. He told me once that he had decided to go on pilgrimage, as we are doing now. But all of a sudden he got to know a man called Save-self, and after that he became a stranger to me.

Hopeful. Now that we are talking about him, let us look a little into the reason for the sudden backsliding of him and others like him.

Christian. It may be very useful. But you begin.

Hopeful. Well then — in my judgment there are four reasons for it.

First, although the consciences of such men are woken up, their minds are not changed. So when the power of guilt wears off, the thing that provoked them to be religious stops, and they naturally turn back to their own course again. It is the same as we see with a dog that is sick from something it has eaten: as long as the sickness lasts it vomits and brings everything up — not that it does this out of any free choice, if we may speak of a dog having a choice, but because its stomach is upset. But now, when its sickness is over and its stomach eased, its appetite not being at all turned away from its vomit, it comes round and licks the whole lot up again. And so what is written is true: "The dog is turned to his own vomit again." So, I say, being hot for heaven only by virtue of the sense and fear of the torments of hell, then as their sense of hell and their fear of damnation chills and cools, so their desire for heaven and salvation cools too. And so it comes about that when their guilt and fear is gone, their desire for heaven and happiness dies, and they go back to their old course.

Second, another reason is that they have slavish fears that get the better of them. I am speaking now of the fears they have of other people, because "the fear of man brings a snare." So although they seem to be hot for heaven as long as the flames of hell are about their ears, still, when that terror has passed off a little, they fall to having second thoughts — namely, that it is a good thing to be prudent, and not to run the risk of losing everything for they know not what, or at the very least of bringing unavoidable and unnecessary trouble on themselves. And so they fall in with the world again.

Third, the shame that goes with religion lies in their way like a block. They are proud and haughty, and in their eyes religion is a low and contemptible thing. So when they have lost their sense of hell and of the wrath to come, they go back to their old course.

Fourth, guilt, and thinking about terrifying things, are painful to them. They do not like to see their misery before they get into it — though perhaps if they looked at it first, and if they could bring themselves to like the sight, it might make them run where the righteous run and are safe. But because, as I said before, they positively avoid all thought of guilt and terror, therefore once they are rid of their awakenings about the terrors and wrath of God, they gladly harden their hearts, and choose the sort of paths that will harden them more and more.

Christian. You are near the heart of it. Because the bottom of the whole thing is the want of a change in their mind and their will. And so they are just like a criminal standing in front of the judge: he quakes and trembles and seems to repent most heartily, but the bottom of it all is the fear of the rope, not any hatred of the offence. And that is obvious, because let this man have his freedom and he will still be a thief, and a rogue still — whereas if his mind had been changed he would be another man.

Hopeful. Now I have shown you the reasons for their going back; you show me the way it happens.

Christian. So I will, gladly.

First, they draw their thoughts off, as far as they can, from any remembrance of God, of death and of the judgment to come.

Second, then they cast off their private duties by degrees — private prayer, curbing their appetites, keeping watch, sorrow for sin, and the like.

Third, then they avoid the company of lively and warm Christians.

Fourth, after that, they grow cold towards public duty — hearing, reading, godly conversation and so on.

Fifth, then they begin to pick holes, as we say, in the coats of some of the godly; and they do it devilishly, so that they may have some plausible excuse for throwing religion behind their backs on account of some weakness they have spotted in them.

Sixth, then they begin to attach themselves to, and keep company with, worldly, loose and dissolute men.

Seventh, then they give way to worldly and dissolute talk in private; and they are delighted if they can see such things in anybody who is reckoned honest, so that they may do it all the more boldly on their example.

Eighth, after this they begin to play with small sins openly.

Ninth, and then, being hardened, they show themselves for what they are. And so, launched out again into the gulf of misery, unless a miracle of grace prevents it, they perish everlastingly in their own self-deceptions.

The Land of Beulah and the River

Now I saw in my dream that by this time the Pilgrims had got over the Enchanted Ground and were coming into the country of Beulah, whose air was very sweet and pleasant. The road ran straight through it, and they took their ease there for a while. Here they heard the singing of birds all day long, and saw the flowers coming up out of the earth every day, and heard the voice of the turtledove in the land. In this country the sun shines night and day; and so this was well beyond the Valley of the Shadow of Death, and out of Giant Despair's reach as well — from this place they could not so much as see Doubting Castle.

Here they were within sight of the city they were going to, and here too some of its citizens came out to meet them; because the Shining Ones walk about freely in this land, since it is on the borders of heaven. In this land the contract between the bride and the bridegroom was renewed as well. Yes, here, "as the bridegroom rejoices over the bride, so did their God rejoice over them." Here they had no lack of grain or wine; because in this place they met with an abundance of everything they had been looking for through their whole pilgrimage.

Here they heard voices out of the city — loud voices, saying, "Say to the daughter of Zion, Look, your salvation is coming! Look, his reward is with him!" Here all the inhabitants of the country called them "The holy people," "The redeemed of the Lord," "Sought out."

Now as they walked in this land they had more joy than they had had in the parts further away from the kingdom they were bound for; and drawing near to the city, they got a still clearer view of it. It was built of pearls and precious stones, and its street was paved with gold; so that between the natural glory of the city and the reflection of the sunlight off it, Christian fell sick with longing. Hopeful also had a fit or two of the same illness. So here they lay by for a while, crying out because of the pangs of it, "If you find my Beloved, tell him that I am sick with love."

But being a little strengthened, and better able to bear their sickness, they walked on and came nearer and nearer, to where there were orchards, vineyards and gardens whose gates opened onto the highway. Now as they came up to these places, there stood the gardener in the road; and the Pilgrims said to him: Whose fine vineyards and gardens are these?

He answered: They are the King's, and they are planted here for his own delight, and for the refreshment of pilgrims as well.

So the gardener took them into the vineyards and told them to refresh themselves with the good things there. He also showed them the King's walks and the shady arbours where he liked to be; and here they stayed, and slept.

Now I saw in my dream that they talked more in their sleep this time than they had in the whole of their journey; and while I was puzzling over it, the gardener said to me: Why are you puzzling over the matter? It is the nature of the fruit of the grapes in these vineyards to go down so sweetly that it makes the lips of sleeping men speak.

So I saw that when they woke they set themselves to go up to the city. But as I said, the reflection of the sun off the city — because "the city was pure gold" — was so extremely glorious that they still could not look at it with the naked eye, but only through an instrument made for the purpose. So I saw that as they went on, two men met them, in clothing that shone like gold; and their faces shone like the light.

These men asked the Pilgrims where they had come from, and they told them. They also asked where they had lodged, and what difficulties and dangers, what comforts and pleasures they had met with along the way; and they told them.

Then the men who had met them said: You have only two difficulties left to meet, and then you are in the city.

So Christian and his companion asked the men to come along with them; and they said they would. But, they said, you must gain it by your own faith.

So I saw in my dream that they went on together until they came in sight of the gate.

Now I further saw that between them and the gate there was a river — but there was no bridge to cross by, and the river was very deep. So at the sight of this river the Pilgrims were badly shaken. But the men who were with them said: You must go through it, or you cannot reach the gate.

Then the Pilgrims began to ask whether there was no other way to the gate. To which they answered: Yes; but nobody except two — Enoch and Elijah — has been allowed to walk that path since the foundation of the world, nor will be, until the last trumpet sounds.

Then the Pilgrims, and Christian in particular, began to sink in their minds, and looked this way and that; but they could find no way at all by which they might get round the river. Then they asked the men whether the water was the same depth all the way across. They said no — and yet they could not help them there; because, they said, you will find it deeper or shallower according to how you believe in the King of the place.

So they addressed themselves to the water; and going in, Christian began to sink, and crying out to his good friend Hopeful he said: I am sinking in deep water; the waves are going over my head, all his waves are going over me!

Then the other said: Take heart, my brother. I can feel the bottom, and it is solid.

Then Christian said: Ah, my friend — "the sorrows of death have closed round me"; I shall never see the land that flows with milk and honey. And with that, a great darkness and horror came down on Christian, so that he could not see in front of him. Here too he largely lost his senses, so that he could neither remember nor talk coherently about any of the sweet refreshments he had met with on his pilgrimage. Everything he said only showed that he had a horror in his mind, and fears in his heart that he was going to die in that river and never get in at the gate.

Here also, as those standing by could tell, he was full of troubled thoughts about the sins he had committed, both since he became a pilgrim and before it. It was noticed too that he was troubled by apparitions of hobgoblins and evil spirits, because every now and then he would let as much slip in words. So Hopeful had all he could do here to keep his brother's head above water. Sometimes he would go right under, and then after a while come up again half dead.

Hopeful would try to comfort him too, saying: Brother, I can see the gate, and men standing by to receive us. But Christian would answer: It is you — it is you they are waiting for. You have been Hopeful ever since I knew you.

And so have you, said Hopeful to Christian.

Ah, brother, said he — surely if I were right with him, he would rise up now and help me. But because of my sins he has brought me into the trap and left me here.

Then Hopeful said: My brother, you have quite forgotten the text where it is said of the wicked, "There are no pangs in their death; their strength is firm. They are not in trouble like other men, nor plagued like other men." These troubles and distresses you are going through in these waters are no sign that God has abandoned you. They are sent to test you — to see whether you will call to mind what you have already received of his goodness, and live on him in your distress.

Then I saw in my dream that Christian was lost in thought for a while. And Hopeful added this word to him: Take heart — Jesus Christ makes you whole.

And with that Christian burst out in a loud voice: Oh, I see him again! And he tells me, "When you pass through the waters, I will be with you; and through the rivers, they shall not overflow you."

Then they both took courage, and after that the enemy was as still as a stone until they had got across. Christian soon found ground to stand on, and it turned out that the rest of the river was shallow. And so they got over.

Now on the bank of the river on the other side they saw the two shining men again, who were waiting there for them; so having come out of the river, they greeted them, saying: We are ministering spirits, sent out to serve those who shall be heirs of salvation.

So they went along towards the gate. Now you must note that the city stood on a mighty hill, but the Pilgrims went up that hill easily, because they had these two men to lead them up by the arms. They had also left their mortal clothing behind them in the river; because although they went in wearing it, they came out without it. So they went up with great lightness and speed, even though the foundation the city was built on was higher than the clouds. They went up through the regions of the air, talking sweetly as they went, and comforted because they had got over the river safely and had such glorious companions attending them.

The talk they had with the Shining Ones was about the glory of the place; and they told them that its beauty and glory were beyond describing. There, they said, is "Mount Zion, the heavenly Jerusalem, the innumerable company of angels, and the spirits of just men made perfect." You are going now, they said, to the paradise of God, where you shall see the tree of life and eat its never-fading fruit; and when you get there you shall be given white robes, and your walking and talking shall be with the King every day, through all the days of eternity.

There you shall not see again the things you saw when you were in the lower region on earth — sorrow, sickness, affliction and death — "for the former things have passed away." You are going now to Abraham, to Isaac and Jacob, and to the prophets: men whom God has taken away from the evil to come, and who are now resting on their beds, each one walking in his righteousness.

Then the men asked: What must we do in the holy place?

To which it was answered: There you must receive the comfort of all your labour, and have joy for all your sorrow. You must reap what you have sown — the fruit of all your prayers and tears and sufferings for the King along the way. In that place you must wear crowns of gold, and enjoy the perpetual sight and vision of the Holy One, because "there you shall see him as he is." There you shall serve him continually with praise, with shouting and thanksgiving — the one you longed to serve in the world, though with great difficulty, because of the weakness of your flesh.

There your eyes shall be delighted with the sight, and your ears with hearing the pleasant voice of the Mighty One. There you shall enjoy your friends again, who have gone there before you; and there you shall joyfully welcome every one who follows you into the holy place after you. There too you shall be clothed with glory and majesty, and given a retinue fit to ride out with the King of glory. When he comes with the sound of a trumpet in the clouds, as on the wings of the wind, you shall come with him; and when he sits on the throne of judgment, you shall sit beside him. Yes, and when he passes sentence on all the workers of wickedness, whether they are angels or men, you also shall have a voice in that judgment, because they were his enemies and yours. And when he returns to the city again, you shall go too, with the sound of the trumpet, and be with him for ever.

Now while they were drawing on towards the gate like this, a company of the heavenly host came out to meet them; and the other two Shining Ones said to them: These are the men who loved our Lord when they were in the world, and who left everything for his holy name. He has sent us to fetch them, and we have brought them this far on the journey they longed for, so that they may go in and look their Redeemer in the face with joy.

Then the heavenly host gave a great shout, saying, "Blessed are those who are called to the marriage supper of the Lamb."

At this time too there came out to meet them several of the King's trumpeters, dressed in white and shining clothes, who with loud and melodious sounds made the very heavens echo. These trumpeters greeted Christian and his companion with ten thousand welcomes from the world; and they did it with shouting, and with the sound of the trumpet.

This done, they surrounded them on every side. Some went in front, some behind, some on the right hand and some on the left, as though to guard them through the upper regions, sounding continually as they went, with melodious noise and high notes; so that the very sight of it, to anybody who could have seen it, was as if heaven itself had come down to meet them. So they walked on together; and as they walked, every now and then these trumpeters, with joyful sound, would mix their music with looks and gestures to keep showing Christian and his brother how welcome they were in their company, and how gladly they had come out to meet them.

And now these two men were, as it were, in heaven before they came to it, being swallowed up with the sight of angels and with the sound of their melodious notes. Here too they had the city itself in view, and they thought they could hear all the bells in it ringing to welcome them in. But above all, there were the warm and joyful thoughts they had about living there themselves, with such company, and that for ever and ever. Oh, what tongue or pen could ever put their glorious joy into words!

And so they came up to the gate.

Now when they had come up to the gate, there was written over it in letters of gold: "Blessed are those who do his commandments, that they may have a right to the tree of life, and may enter in through the gates into the city."

Then I saw in my dream that the Shining Men told them to call at the gate; and when they did, some looked down over the top of it — Enoch, Moses and Elijah among them — and were told: These pilgrims have come from the City of Destruction, out of the love they have for the King of this place. And then the pilgrims each handed in his certificate, the one they had been given at the beginning. So these were carried in to the King, who, when he had read them, said: Where are the men?

To which it was answered: They are standing outside the gate.

Then the King commanded that the gate be opened. "That the righteous nation," he said, "which keeps the truth, may enter in."

Now I saw in my dream that these two men went in at the gate; and as they entered, they were transfigured, and clothes were put on them that shone like gold. There were people who met them with harps and crowns and gave them to them — the harps to praise with, and the crowns as a token of honour. Then I heard in my dream that all the bells in the city rang again for joy, and that it was said to them, "Enter into the joy of your Lord." And I heard the men themselves singing with a loud voice, saying, "Blessing and honour and glory and power be to him that sits on the throne, and to the Lamb, for ever and ever."

Now just as the gates were opened to let the men in, I looked in after them; and the City shone like the sun. The streets were paved with gold, and in them walked a great many men with crowns on their heads, palms in their hands, and golden harps to sing praises with. There were some among them who had wings, and they answered one another without stopping, saying, "Holy, holy, holy is the Lord." And after that they shut the gates — which, when I had seen it, made me wish myself among them.

Now while I was gazing at all this, I turned my head to look back, and saw Ignorance come up to the riverside. But he got over quickly, and without half the difficulty the other two men had met with; because there happened to be in that place at the time a ferryman called Vain-hope, who helped him over with his boat. So he, like the others I had seen, went up the hill to come to the gate — only he came alone, and nobody met him with the least word of encouragement.

When he had come up to the gate, he looked up at the writing above it and began to knock, supposing that he would be let in at once. But he was asked by the men who looked over the top of the gate: Where have you come from, and what do you want?

He answered: I have eaten and drunk in the presence of the King, and he has taught in our streets.

Then they asked him for his certificate, so that they could go in and show it to the King. So he fumbled inside his coat for one, and found none.

Then they said: Have you none at all? But the man never answered a word.

So they told the King; but he would not come down to see him. Instead he ordered the two Shining Ones who had brought Christian and Hopeful to the City to go out and take Ignorance, and bind him hand and foot, and carry him away. So they took him up and carried him through the air to the door I had seen in the side of the hill, and put him in there.

Then I saw that there was a way to hell, even from the gates of Heaven, as well as from the City of Destruction!

So I awoke, and behold it was a dream.

The Conclusion

	Now, Reader, I have told my dream to you.
	See whether you can interpret it to me,
	or to yourself, or to your neighbour — but take care
	of misinterpreting; because that, instead
	of doing good, will only do you harm.
	Misinterpret, and evil follows on.
	Take care as well that you are not extreme
	in playing with the outside of my dream,
	and do not let my figures and comparisons
	set you off laughing, or set you quarrelling.
	Leave that to boys and fools; but as for you,
	see the substance of my matter through.
	Draw back the curtains, look behind my veil,
	turn up my metaphors, and do not fail:
	there, if you look for them, you'll find such things
	as will be helpful to an honest mind.
	Whatever dross you find there, be so bold
	as to throw it away — but do preserve the gold.
	And what if my gold should be wrapped up in ore?
	Nobody throws the apple out for the core.
	But if you cast the whole lot away as vain,
	I do not know but it will make me dream again.

Part Two: Christiana

The Author's Way of Sending Forth His Second Part

	Go now, my little book, to every place
	where my first Pilgrim has but shown his face.
	Call at their door. If any say, Who's there?
	then answer them: Christiana is here.
	If they invite you in, then in you go
	with all your boys; and then, as you well know,
	tell them who these are, and where they came from —
	perhaps they'll know them by their looks, or name.
	But if they don't, then ask them yet again
	whether they didn't once take in and entertain
	one Christian, a Pilgrim. If they say
	they did, and were delighted with his way,
	then let them know that these are his relations:
	yes, this is his own wife, and these his children.
	Tell them they have left their house and home
	and turned Pilgrims, seeking a world to come;
	that they have met with hardships on the road,
	that trouble finds them out by night and day;
	that they have trodden on snakes, fought with devils,
	and have come out on top of a good many evils.
	Yes, tell them of the others too, who have
	out of sheer love of pilgrimage been brave
	defenders of that road, and how they still
	refuse this world, to do their Father's will.
	Go, tell them also of the choice good things
	that pilgrimage to the Pilgrim brings.
	Let them be told as well how dear they are
	to their own King, and how they're in his care;
	what fine houses he is providing for them,
	though they meet rough winds and swelling tides;
	and what a splendid calm they'll have at last
	who hold to their Lord, and to his ways, hold fast.
	Perhaps with heart and hand they will embrace
	you, as they did my firstborn, and will grace
	you and your fellows with such welcome and such fare
	as shows they are lovers of Pilgrims everywhere.

Objection 1

	But what if they will not believe of me
	that I am truly yours? Because there are some
	who counterfeit the Pilgrim and his name,
	and by disguise try to seem the very same,
	and by that means have worked their way into
	the hands and houses of I don't know who.

Answer

	It's true. Some have lately, to counterfeit
	my Pilgrim, put my title on their own;
	and others have stitched half my name and title
	onto their book, to help the thing sell better.
	But by their features they declare themselves
	not mine at all, whoever else's they are.
	If you meet any such, your only way
	in front of them all is to say out your say
	in your own native language, which no man
	uses now, and nobody can easily fake.
	If, after all that, they still have doubts of you,
	and think that you, like gypsies, wander round
	up to no good, to dirty the whole country,
	or that you're out to cheat good honest people
	with things you've no warrant for — then send for me,
	and I will testify that you are pilgrims.
	Yes, I will testify that only you
	are my Pilgrims; and that alone will do.

Objection 2

	But what if I should ask about him at the door
	of those who wish him damned in life and limb?
	What shall I do, when at a house like that
	I ask for Pilgrims, and they only rage the more?

Answer

	Don't frighten yourself, my book. Such bogeymen
	are nothing but the ground of groundless fears.
	My Pilgrim's book has travelled sea and land,
	and yet I never once could understand
	that it was slighted, or turned out of doors
	by any kingdom, whether rich or poor.
	In France and Flanders, where men kill each other,
	my Pilgrim is esteemed a friend, a brother.
	In Holland too, they say, as I am told,
	my Pilgrim is with some worth more than gold.
	Highlanders and wild Irish can agree
	that my Pilgrim should be on familiar terms with them.
	In New England he is so far advanced,
	receives there such a loving countenance,
	as to be trimmed, new-clothed and decked with gems
	so that he can show off his features and his limbs.
	And more than that: so handsomely he walks
	that thousands sing about him daily, and talk.
	And if you come nearer home, it will appear
	my Pilgrim knows no ground for shame or fear.
	City and country both will take him in
	with, Welcome, Pilgrim; yes, and they can't refrain
	from smiling, if my Pilgrim's only by,
	or shows his head in any company.
	Fine gentlemen will hug my Pilgrim and love him,
	value him greatly — yes, and rate him above
	things of much greater bulk; and with delight
	say, My lark's leg is better than a kite.
	Young ladies, and young gentlewomen too,
	show no small kindness to my Pilgrim.
	Their cabinets, their bosoms and their hearts
	my Pilgrim has, because to them he imparts
	his pretty riddles in such wholesome strains
	as yield them double profit for the pains
	of reading. Yes, I think I may be bold
	to say some prize him far above their gold.
	The very children walking in the street,
	if they should chance my holy Pilgrim meet,
	will greet him well, will wish him well, and say
	he is the finest young fellow of the day.
	Those who have never seen him still admire
	what they have heard of him, and much desire
	to have his company, and hear him tell
	those pilgrim stories that he knows so well.
	Yes, some who did not love him at the first,
	but called him fool and simpleton, say they must,
	now that they've seen and heard him, praise him now,
	and send him on to those they love.
	So then, my Second Part, you needn't be
	afraid to show your head. None can hurt you
	who wish well to the one that went before,
	because you follow after with a second store
	of things as good, as rich, as profitable
	for young, for old, for shaky and for stable.

Objection 3

	But there are some who say, He laughs too loud;
	and some who say, His head is in a cloud.
	Some say his words and stories are so dark
	they cannot tell, by them, how to find his mark.

Answer

	One may, I think, say this: both his laughs and cries
	can well be guessed at by his watery eyes.
	Some things are of a nature that will make
	a man's fancy chuckle while his heart's aching.
	When Jacob saw his Rachel with the sheep,
	he did at the same moment kiss and weep.
	And where some say a cloud is in his head —
	that only shows how wisdom is covered over
	with its own mantles, and set there to stir the mind
	to a search after what it would gladly find.
	Things that seem hidden away in obscure words
	only allure the godly mind the more
	to study what such sayings should contain
	that speak to us in such a clouded strain.
	I also know a dark comparison
	will press itself upon the fancy harder,
	and stick faster in the heart and head,
	than things not borrowed from a simile.
	So then, my book, let no discouragement
	hinder your travels. Look — you are being sent
	to friends, not enemies; to friends who will make room
	for you, your Pilgrims, and embrace your words.
	Besides, whatever my first Pilgrim left concealed,
	you, my brave second Pilgrim, have revealed.
	What Christian left locked up, and went his way,
	sweet Christiana opens with her key.

Objection 4

	But some don't like the method of your first;
	they count it romance, and throw it away as dust.
	If I should meet with such, what should I say?
	Must I slight them as they slight me, or not?

Answer

	My Christiana, if you meet with such,
	by all means, in the most loving way, greet them.
	Do not return them abuse for abuse;
	but if they frown, then please — smile back at them.
	Perhaps it's just their nature, or some bad report
	that's made them scorn you so, or answer sharply.
	Some love no cheese, some love no fish, and some
	love neither their friends nor their own house and home.
	Some shy at pork, think nothing of a chicken,
	and love a fowl no better than a cuckoo or an owl.
	Leave such, my Christiana, to their choice,
	and look for those who will rejoice to find you.
	By no means force yourself; but humbly go
	and present yourself to them in your Pilgrim's dress.
	Go, then, my little book, and show to all
	who take you in and bid you welcome
	what you shall keep shut up and hid from the rest;
	and wish that what you show them may be blessed
	to them for good, and make them choose to be
	Pilgrims far better than either you or me.
	Go then, I say, and tell all men who you are.
	Say: I am Christiana, and my part
	is now, with my four sons, to tell you what
	it is for people to take a Pilgrim's lot.
	Go also, tell them who and what they are
	that now go on pilgrimage with you.
	Say: Here's my neighbour Mercy; she is one
	who has walked a Pilgrim with me a long time.
	Come, see her in her single life, and learn
	between the idle and the Pilgrim to discern.
	Yes, let young women learn from her to prize
	the world that is to come, in any case.
	When young girls with light steps follow God,
	and leave old doting sinners to his rod,
	it's like those days in which the young ones cried
	Hosanna! — while the old ones stood and jeered.
	Next, tell them of old Honest, whom you found
	with his white hair, treading the Pilgrim's ground.
	Yes, tell them how plain-hearted this man was,
	how, following his good Lord, he bore his cross.
	Perhaps with some grey head this may prevail
	to fall in love with Christ and mourn his sin.
	Tell them also how Master Fearing went
	on pilgrimage, and how the time he spent
	in loneliness, with all his fears and cries —
	and how at last he won the joyful prize.
	He was a good man, though much down in spirit;
	he is a good man, and does life inherit.
	Tell them of Master Feeble-mind as well,
	who would not go in front, but still behind.
	Show them how near he came to being killed,
	and how one Great-heart got his life back for him.
	This man was true of heart, though weak in grace;
	you could read true godliness in his face.
	Then tell them of Master Ready-to-halt,
	a man on crutches, but with little fault.
	Tell them how Master Feeble-mind and he
	loved one another, and in opinion much agreed.
	And let all know that though weakness was their lot,
	still, sometimes one could sing and one could dance.
	Do not forget Master Valiant-for-truth,
	that man of courage, though a very youth.
	Tell everybody that his spirit was so stout
	no man could ever once make him turn about;
	and how Great-heart and he could not hold back,
	but pulled down Doubting Castle, and killed Despair.
	Do not pass over Master Despondency,
	nor Much-afraid, his daughter, though they lie
	under such heavy cloaks as make them look
	(to some) as if their God had quite forsaken them.
	They went softly, but they went sure; and at the end
	they found the Lord of Pilgrims was their friend.
	When you have told the world of all these things,
	then turn about, my book, and touch these strings,
	which, if but touched, will make such music
	as makes a cripple dance and a giant quake.
	These riddles lying folded in your breast,
	set out and open freely; and for the rest
	of your more mysterious lines, let them remain
	for those whose nimble wits shall win them.
	Now may this little book a blessing be
	to those who love this little book and me;
	and may the man who buys it have no cause to say
	his money was lost, or thrown away.
	Yes, may this Second Pilgrim yield such fruit
	as suits each good Pilgrim's fancy;
	and may it persuade some who go astray
	to turn their feet and hearts to the right way.
	This is the hearty prayer of

The Author,

John Bunyan

Christiana's Summons

	"I have used similitudes."

Courteous Companions,

Some time ago it was a pleasure to me, and a profit to you, to tell you the dream I had about Christian the Pilgrim, and about his dangerous journey towards the Celestial Country. I told you then as well what I saw concerning his wife and children, and how unwilling they were to go with him on pilgrimage — so unwilling that he was forced to set out without them, because he dared not risk the destruction he was afraid would come of staying with them in the City of Destruction. So, as I showed you then, he left them and went.

Now it has happened, through the sheer press of business, that I have been much hindered and held back from my usual travels into the parts he came from, and so could not, until now, get an opportunity to make any further inquiry after those he left behind, so that I could give you an account of them. But having had some business that way lately, I went down there again. And having taken lodgings in a wood about a mile from the place, I dreamed again as I slept.

And in my dream, an elderly gentleman came past where I lay; and because he was going part of the way I was travelling, it seemed to me that I got up and went along with him. So as we walked, as travellers usually do, we seemed to fall into conversation; and our talk happened to turn on Christian and his travels. This is how I began with the old man.

Sir, said I, what town is that down there, lying on the left-hand side of our road?

Then Mr. Sagacity said — for that was his name — It is the City of Destruction: a populous place, but full of a very ill-natured and idle sort of people.

I thought that was the city, said I. I went through that town myself once, and so I know this report you give of it is true.

Sagacity. Too true. I wish I could speak the truth and say something better about the people who live there.

Well, sir, said I, then I see you are a well-meaning man, and one who takes pleasure in hearing and telling what is good. Tell me — did you never hear what happened some time ago to a man in this town whose name was Christian, who went on pilgrimage up towards the higher country?

Sagacity. Hear of him! Yes — and I heard too of the harassment, the troubles, the wars, the captivities, the cries and groans and frights and fears that he met with on his journey. Besides which, I have to tell you, the whole country rings with him. There are hardly any houses that have heard of him and what he did that have not gone looking for the record of his pilgrimage and got hold of it. Yes, I think I may say that that hazardous journey of his has won a great many well-wishers to his way. Because although while he was here he was a fool in everybody's mouth, now that he is gone he is highly praised by all of them. It is said he lives splendidly where he is; and many who are determined never to run his risks still find their mouths watering at his gains.

They may well think, said I — if they think anything true at all — that he lives well where he is; because he now lives at and in the Fountain of Life, and has what he has without labour or sorrow, since there is no grief mixed in with it there. But tell me — what do the people say about him?

Sagacity. Say! The people say strange things about him. Some say that he now walks in white; that he has a chain of gold about his neck; that he has a crown of gold set with pearls on his head. Others say that the Shining Ones who showed themselves to him now and then on his journey have become his companions, and that he is on as easy terms with them where he is as one neighbour is with another here.

Besides that, it is confidently stated about him that the King of the place where he is has already given him a very rich and pleasant house at court; and that every day he eats and drinks and walks and talks with him, and receives the smiles and favour of the one who is Judge of all there.

What is more, some expect that his Prince, the Lord of that country, will shortly be coming into these parts, and will want to know the reason, if they can give any, why his neighbours thought so little of him and held him up to so much ridicule when they found he meant to be a pilgrim. Because they say that he stands so high now in his Prince's affections, and that his Sovereign is so much concerned about the insults thrown at Christian when he became a pilgrim, that he will regard them all as done to himself. And no wonder — because it was for love of his Prince that Christian took the risks he did.

I must say, said I, I am glad of it. I am glad for the poor man's sake, that he now has rest from his labour, and that he now reaps the harvest of his tears with joy, and that he has got beyond the gunshot of his enemies and out of the reach of those who hate him. I am glad too that a rumour of these things is going round in this country. Who can tell but it may have some good effect on some of those left behind?

But please, sir, while it is fresh in my mind — do you hear anything of his wife and children? Poor hearts. I have been wondering what has become of them.

Sagacity. Who — Christiana and her sons? They are likely to do as well as Christian himself did. Because although they all played the fool at first, and could not be persuaded by Christian's tears or his pleading, still, second thoughts have worked wonderfully in them; so they have packed up and gone after him too.

Better and better, said I. But what — wife and children and all?

Sagacity. It is true. I can give you an account of the matter, because I was on the spot at the time and knew the whole affair thoroughly.

Then, said I, a man may report it for a fact?

Sagacity. You need not be afraid to state it — I mean that they have all gone on pilgrimage, both the good woman and her four boys. And since we are, as I gather, going a considerable way together, I will give you an account of the whole thing.

This Christiana — for that was her name from the day she and her children took up a pilgrim's life — after her husband had gone over the river, and she could hear nothing more of him, began to have her thoughts working in her mind. First, because she had lost her husband, and because the loving bond of that relationship was utterly broken between them. Because you know, he said to me, nature can do no less than fill the living with many a heavy thought at the memory of losing those they loved. This, then, over her husband, cost her many a tear.

But that was not all. Christiana also began to consider whether her own unbecoming behaviour towards her husband was not one reason she never saw him again, and why he was taken from her the way he was. And on top of this there came into her mind, in swarms, all her unkind and unnatural and ungodly conduct towards her dear friend — which clogged her conscience and loaded her down with guilt.
