She was, moreover, badly broken by calling to mind her husband's restless groans, his salt tears, and his lamenting over himself; and how she had hardened her heart against all his pleading and his loving attempts to persuade her and her sons to go with him. Yes — there was not a thing Christian either said to her or did in front of her the whole time his burden hung on his back that did not come back on her like a flash of lightning and tear her heart open. Above all, that bitter cry of his, "What shall I do to be saved?" rang in her ears most miserably.

Then she said to her children: Sons, we are all ruined. I have sinned your father away, and he is gone. He wanted us with him, but I would not go myself. And I have kept you back from life as well.

At that the boys all burst into tears and cried out to go after their father.

Oh, said Christiana, if it had only been our lot to go with him! Then things would have gone well with us, beyond anything they are likely to do now. Because although I used foolishly to imagine, about your father's troubles, that they came out of some foolish notion he had got, or that he was overrun with melancholy, still, now I cannot get it out of my mind that they came from something else altogether: that the Light of light was given him, and that by its help, as I can see now, he has escaped the traps of death.

Then they all wept again, and cried out: Oh, cursed be the day!

The next night Christiana had a dream. She saw, as it were, a broad sheet of parchment opened out in front of her, on which the sum of all her ways was written down; and the record, as she thought, looked very black against her. Then she cried out aloud in her sleep, "Lord, have mercy on me, a sinner!" And the little children heard her.

After that, she thought she saw two very evil-looking creatures standing by her bedside, saying: What are we going to do with this woman? Because she cries out for mercy waking and sleeping. If she is allowed to go on as she has begun, we shall lose her as we lost her husband. So one way or another we must find a way to take her mind off what is to come hereafter; otherwise nothing in the world can stop her becoming a pilgrim.

Now she woke up in a great sweat, and shaking; but after a while she fell asleep again. And then she thought she saw Christian her husband in a place of bliss, among a great many immortals, with a harp in his hand, standing and playing on it before One who sat on a throne with a rainbow round his head. And she saw him bow his head, with his face down to the paved floor under the Prince's feet, saying: I thank my Lord and King with all my heart for bringing me into this place. Then a company of those standing round about shouted, and played on their harps; but no living person could tell what they said, except Christian and his companions.

The next morning, when she was up and had prayed to God and talked with her children a while, somebody knocked hard at the door. So she called out, saying: If you come in God's name, come in.

So he said, Amen, and opened the door, and greeted her with "Peace be to this house." And when he had done that, he said: Christiana, do you know why I have come?

Then she went red and began to tremble, and her heart began to grow warm with wanting to know where he had come from and what his errand to her was. So he said to her: My name is Secret; I live among those who are on high. It is spoken of, where I live, as if you had a wish to come there. And there is a report too that you are aware of the wrong you did your husband in hardening your heart against his way, and in keeping these children of yours in ignorance.

Christiana, the Merciful One has sent me to tell you that he is a God ready to forgive, and that he takes delight in multiplying his pardons for offences. He also wants you to know that he invites you to come into his presence, to his table, and that he will feed you with the richest of his house, and with the inheritance of Jacob your father.

There is Christian your husband — that was — with legions more of his companions, for ever looking at that face which gives life to all who look at it; and every one of them will be glad when they hear the sound of your feet crossing your Father's threshold.

Christiana was deeply humbled at this, and bowed her head to the ground; and the Visitor went on and said: Christiana, here is a letter for you as well, which I have brought from your husband's King.

So she took it and opened it — and it smelled like the finest perfume, and it was written in letters of gold. The contents of the letter were that the King would have her do as Christian her husband had done, because that was the way to come to his city and to live in his presence with joy for ever.

At this the good woman was quite overcome. So she cried out to her visitor: Sir, will you take me and my children with you, so that we too may go and worship this King?

Then the visitor said: Christiana, the bitter comes before the sweet. You must enter this Celestial City by way of troubles, as the man who went before you did. So my advice is that you do what Christian your husband did. Go to that little gate over there, across the plain, because that stands at the head of the road you must go up; and I wish you all speed.

I advise you as well to put this letter inside your coat, and to read it over to yourself and to your children until you have it by heart; because it is one of the songs you must sing while you are in this house of your pilgrimage. And you must hand it in at the gate at the far end.

Now I saw in my dream that this old gentleman, as he told me this story, seemed to be greatly moved by it himself. He went on, and said:

So Christiana called her sons together and began to speak to them like this. My sons, I have lately been, as you may have noticed, under a great deal of distress of soul about the death of your father — not because I have any doubt at all about his happiness, since I am satisfied now that he is well. I have also been much troubled by thoughts about my own condition and yours, which I really do believe is by nature a wretched one. My behaviour to your father in his distress lies heavy on my conscience too, because I hardened my own heart and yours against him, and refused to go with him on pilgrimage.

The thought of these things would kill me outright now, if it were not for a dream I had last night, and for the encouragement this stranger has given me this morning. Come, my children — let us pack up and be off to the gate that leads to the Celestial Country, so that we may see your father, and be with him and his companions in peace, according to the laws of that land.

Then her children burst into tears for joy, that their mother's heart was so turned. So their visitor said goodbye to them, and they began to get ready to set out on their journey.

But while they were on the point of going, two of the women who were Christiana's neighbours came up to the house and knocked at her door. To whom she said, as before: If you come in God's name, come in.

At this the women were taken aback, because they were not used to hearing that kind of language, or noticing it fall from Christiana's lips. Still, they came in — and there they found the good woman getting ready to leave her house.

So they began, and said: Neighbour, tell us, please — what do you mean by this?

Mrs. Timorous and Mercy

Christiana answered the elder of them, whose name was Mrs. Timorous: I am getting ready for a journey. (This Timorous was the daughter of the man who met Christian on the Hill Difficulty and wanted him to turn back for fear of the lions.)

Timorous. What journey, may I ask?

Christiana. To go after my good husband. And with that she burst into tears.

Timorous. I hope not, good neighbour. For your poor children's sake, do not throw yourself away in such an unwomanly fashion.

Christiana. No — my children are going with me. Not one of them is willing to stay behind.

Timorous. I wonder with all my heart what, or who, has put you into this frame of mind.

Christiana. Oh, neighbour — if you knew only as much as I do, I have no doubt you would come with me.

Timorous. Tell me, please: what new knowledge have you got hold of that turns your mind so against your friends, and tempts you to go off nobody knows where?

Christiana. Then Christiana answered: I have been sorely afflicted since my husband left me, and especially since he went over the river. But what troubles me most is my churlish behaviour towards him when he was in his distress. And besides that, I am now as he was then: nothing will do for me but going on pilgrimage.

I dreamed last night that I saw him. Oh, that my soul were with him! He lives in the presence of the King of that country; he sits and eats with him at his table; he has become a companion of immortals, and has been given a house to live in that makes the best palaces on earth look to me, by comparison, like a rubbish heap. The Prince of the place has sent for me too, with the promise of a welcome if I will come to him. His messenger was here just now, and has brought me a letter inviting me.

And with that she pulled out her letter and read it, and said to them: Now what have you to say to this?

Timorous. Oh, the madness that has taken hold of you and your husband, to run yourselves into such difficulties! You have heard, I am sure, what your husband met with almost at the very first step he took on the road — as our neighbour Obstinate can still testify, because he went along with him; yes, and Pliable too, until they, like sensible men, were afraid to go any further. We heard as well how he met with the lions, and Apollyon, and the Shadow of Death, and a great many other things. Nor should you forget the danger he met with at Vanity Fair; because if he, a man, was so hard put to it, what can you do, being only a poor woman?

Consider too that these four sweet children are yours, your own flesh and bone. So even if you should be rash enough to throw yourself away, still, for the sake of your own children, stay at home.

But Christiana said to her: Do not tempt me, neighbour. I have a price put into my hand now to buy something with, and I should be the greatest fool alive if I had no heart to seize the chance. And as for all these troubles you tell me I am likely to meet on the road — so far from being any discouragement to me, they show me I am in the right. The bitter must come before the sweet, and that is what will make the sweet the sweeter. So, since you did not come to my house in God's name, as I said, please go away and stop disturbing me.

Then Timorous turned abusive as well, and said to her companion: Come along, neighbour Mercy, let us leave her to her own devices, since she despises our advice and our company.

But Mercy hung back, and could not fall in with her neighbour so readily; and that for two reasons. First, her heart went out to Christiana. So she said to herself: If my neighbour must go, I will go a little way with her and help her on. Second, her heart was moved about her own soul, because what Christiana had said had taken some hold on her mind. So she said to herself again: I will have some more talk with this Christiana; and if I find truth and life in what she says, then I and my whole heart will go along with her.

So Mercy began to answer her neighbour Timorous like this.

Mercy. Neighbour, I did come along with you to see Christiana this morning; and since she is, as you see, taking her last farewell of her country, I think I will walk a little way with her this sunny morning and help her on her road. (But she did not tell her the second reason; she kept that to herself.)

Timorous. Well, I see you have a mind to go off and play the fool too. But take warning while there is time, and be sensible. While we are out of danger, we are out of it; but once we are in, we are in.

So Mrs. Timorous went back to her house, and Christiana set off on her journey. But when Timorous had got home, she sent for some of her neighbours — Mrs. Bat's-eyes, Mrs. Inconsiderate, Mrs. Light-mind and Mrs. Know-nothing. And when they had come to her house, she set about telling them the story of Christiana and her intended journey. This is how she began her tale.

Timorous. Neighbours, having had little to do this morning, I went to pay Christiana a visit; and when I came to the door I knocked, as you know is our custom. And she answered: If you come in God's name, come in. So in I went, thinking everything was as usual. But when I got in, I found her getting ready to leave the town — she and her children too. So I asked her what she meant by it. And she told me, in short, that she had now made up her mind to go on pilgrimage, as her husband did. She told me too about a dream she had had, and how the King of the country where her husband is had sent her a letter inviting her to come there.

Then Mrs. Know-nothing said: What — do you think she will really go?

Timorous. Yes, go she will, whatever comes of it. And this is how I know it. The thing that was my great argument for persuading her to stay at home — namely the troubles she is likely to meet on the road — is one of her great arguments for going. Because she told me in so many words, "The bitter goes before the sweet." Yes, and just because it does, it makes the sweet the sweeter.

Mrs. Bat's-eyes. Oh, the blind, foolish woman! said she. Will she not take warning from her husband's sufferings? For my part I am sure that if he were back here again, he would settle for a whole skin and never run so many risks for nothing.

Mrs. Inconsiderate answered as well, saying: Away with such fanciful fools out of the town! A good riddance, I say, as far as she is concerned. If she stayed where she lives and kept this frame of mind, who could live quietly next to her? Because she will either be gloomy or unneighbourly, or she will talk about the sort of things no sensible person can put up with. So for my part I shall never be sorry she has gone. Let her go, and let somebody better come into her place. It has never been a good world since these whimsical fools started living in it.

Then Mrs. Light-mind added this: Come, put that kind of talk away. I was at Madam Wanton's yesterday, where we were as merry as could be. Because who do you think was there but myself, and Mrs. Love-the-flesh, and three or four more, with Mr. Lechery, Mrs. Filth and some others. So we had music there, and dancing, and whatever else was called for to fill out the pleasure. And I must say my lady herself is an admirably well-bred gentlewoman, and Mr. Lechery is as fine a fellow as you could wish.

By this time Christiana had got out on her road, and Mercy was walking with her. So as they went, her children being with them too, Christiana began to talk.

And, Mercy, said Christiana, I take it as an unexpected kindness that you should set foot outside your door with me, to keep me company a little way on my road.

Mercy. Then young Mercy said, for she was still young: If I thought there would be any purpose in going with you, I would never go near that town again.

Christiana. Well, Mercy, said Christiana, throw in your lot with me. I know perfectly well what the end of our pilgrimage will be. My husband is where he would not give up being for all the gold in the Spanish mines. And you will not be turned away either, even though you come only on my invitation. The King who has sent for me and my children is one who delights in mercy. Besides — if you like, I will take you into my service, and you shall come along with me as my servant; and yet we will have everything in common between us. Only come along with me.

Mercy. But how am I to be sure that I shall be received too? If I had that hope from somebody who could tell me, I would make no difficulty at all, but would go, helped by the one who can help — however wearisome the road might be.

Christiana. Well, kind Mercy, I will tell you what to do. Come with me as far as the little gate, and there I will make further inquiries for you; and if you do not meet with any encouragement there, then I shall be content for you to go back to your own place. And I will pay you for the kindness you are showing me and my children in coming with us on our road as you are doing.

Mercy. Then I will go there, and take whatever follows; and may the Lord grant that my lot falls there just as the King of Heaven shall have his heart set on me.

Christiana was glad at heart then, not only because she had a companion, but also because she had prevailed on this poor girl to fall in love with her own salvation. So they went on together — and Mercy began to cry.

Then Christiana said: Why is my sister crying?

Mercy. Ah, said she, who could help grieving, if they really considered what a state and condition my poor relations are still in, back in that sinful town of ours? And what makes my grief heavier is that they have nobody to teach them, and nobody to tell them what is coming.

Christiana. Compassion becomes a pilgrim. And you are doing for your friends what my good Christian did for me when he left me. He mourned because I would not listen to him or take any notice of him. But his Lord and ours gathered up his tears afterwards and put them into his bottle; and now both you and I, and these sweet children of mine, are reaping the fruit and the benefit of them.

I hope, Mercy, that these tears of yours will not be lost either; because the truth has said that "those who sow in tears shall reap in joy," singing. And "he who goes out weeping, carrying precious seed, shall doubtless come back rejoicing, bringing his sheaves with him."

Then Mercy said:

	Let the Most Blessed be my guide,
	if it should be his will,
	up to his gate, into his fold,
	up to his holy hill.
	And let him never let me swerve
	or turn myself aside
	from his free grace and holy ways,
	whatever may betide.
	And let him gather in my own,
	the ones I've left behind;
	Lord, make them pray to be your own
	with all their heart and mind.

The Slough and the Gate

Now my old friend went on, and said: But when Christiana came up to the Slough of Despond, she stopped short; because, she said, this is the place where my dear husband was nearly smothered in the mud. She noticed too that in spite of the King's order to make this place good for pilgrims, it was rather worse than it had been before.

So I asked whether that was true. Yes, said the old gentleman, only too true. Because there are a great many who claim to be the King's workmen, and who say they are for mending the King's highway, who bring dirt and dung instead of stones, and so spoil it instead of mending it.

So here Christiana and her boys came to a halt. But Mercy said: Come, let us risk it — only let us be careful. Then they watched the stepping stones closely and managed to get across, staggering as they went. Even so, Christiana was nearly in, and that more than once or twice. And no sooner had they got over than they thought they heard words saying to them, "Blessed is she who believed; for there shall be a fulfilment of the things that were told her from the Lord."

Then they went on again; and Mercy said to Christiana: If I had as good a ground as you have for hoping for a kind reception at the little gate, I think no Slough of Despond would put me off.

Well, said the other, you know your own sore and I know mine; and, good friend, we shall all have trouble enough before we come to the end of our journey.

Because can it be imagined that people who mean to reach such splendid glories as we do, and who are so envied that happiness as we are, will not meet with every fear and fright, every trouble and affliction that those who hate us can possibly attack us with?

And now Mr. Sagacity left me to dream out my dream by myself.

So it seemed to me that I saw Christiana and Mercy and the boys all go up to the gate; and when they had got there they had a short discussion about how they ought to manage their calling at the gate, and what should be said to the one who opened it. So it was settled that since Christiana was the eldest, she should be the one to knock for admission, and that she should speak to whoever opened, on behalf of the rest.

So Christiana began to knock; and, as her poor husband had done, she knocked, and knocked again. But instead of anyone answering, they all thought they heard something like a dog come barking at them — a dog, and a big one at that. And this frightened the women and the children, and for a while they did not dare knock any more, in case the mastiff should fly at them.

So now they were greatly tossed about in their minds, and did not know what to do. They dared not knock, for fear of the dog; and they dared not go back, in case the Keeper of the gate should catch sight of them going and be offended with them. At last they thought of knocking again, and knocked harder than they had the first time.

Then the Keeper of the gate said: Who is there? At which the dog stopped barking, and he opened to them.

Then Christiana bowed low and said: Do not let our Lord be offended with his servants, because we have knocked at his princely gate.

Then the Keeper said: Where have you come from, and what is it you want?

Christiana answered: We have come from the place Christian came from, and on the same errand as he did — namely, to be graciously admitted, if it should please you, in at this gate onto the road that leads to the Celestial City. And I answer further, my Lord, that I am Christiana, who was once the wife of Christian, and who is now got above.

At this the Keeper of the gate marvelled, saying: What! Has she become a pilgrim now, who only a little while ago detested that life?

Then she bowed her head and said: Yes — and so have these sweet children of mine.

Then he took her by the hand and let her in, and said as well, "Suffer the little children to come to me." And with that he shut the gate. This done, he called up to a trumpeter above the gate to welcome Christiana with shouting and the sound of the trumpet, for joy. So the trumpeter obeyed, and sounded, and filled the air with his melodious notes.

Now all this time poor Mercy stood outside, trembling and crying for fear that she had been refused. But once Christiana had got admission for herself and her boys, she began to plead for Mercy.

Christiana. And she said: My Lord, I have a companion of mine still standing outside, who has come here on the same account as I have. She is very cast down in her mind, because she comes, as she thinks, without being sent for — whereas I was sent for to come, by my husband's King.

Now Mercy began to get very impatient, because every minute felt as long to her as an hour. So she cut Christiana's pleading short by knocking at the gate herself. And she knocked so loud that she made Christiana jump.

Then the Keeper of the gate said: Who is there? And Christiana said: It is my friend.

So he opened the gate and looked out — and there was Mercy fallen down outside in a faint, because she had fainted away, afraid that no gate would ever be opened to her.

Then he took her by the hand and said: Young woman, I tell you to get up.

Oh sir, said she, I am faint; there is hardly any life left in me.

But he answered: Somebody once said, "When my soul fainted within me, I remembered the Lord; and my prayer came in to you, into your holy temple." Do not be afraid, but stand on your feet, and tell me why you have come.

Mercy. I have come to something I was never invited to, as my friend Christiana was. Hers was an invitation from the King, and mine was only from her. So I am afraid I am presuming.

Keeper. Did she ask you to come with her to this place?

Mercy. Yes; and, as my Lord sees, I have come. And if there is any grace or forgiveness of sins to spare, I beg that I, your poor servant, may have a share in it.

Then he took her by the hand again and led her gently in, and said: I pray for all those who believe in me, by whatever means they come to me.

Then he said to those standing by: Fetch something and give it to Mercy to smell, to stop her fainting. So they fetched her a bundle of myrrh, and after a while she came round.

And now Christiana and her boys and Mercy had been received by the Lord at the head of the road, and spoken to kindly by him. Then they said to him further: We are sorry for our sins, and we beg our Lord's pardon, and to be told what we must do next.

I grant pardon, he said, by word and by deed: by word, in the promise of forgiveness; by deed, in the way I obtained it. Take the first from my lips with a kiss, and the other as it shall be revealed to you.

Now I saw in my dream that he spoke a great many good words to them, which gladdened them greatly. He also took them up to the top of the gate and showed them by what deed they had been saved, and told them at the same time that they would have that sight again, for their comfort, as they went along the road.

So he left them a while in a summer parlour below, where they got into conversation among themselves; and Christiana began: Oh Lord, how glad I am that we have got in here!

Mercy. Well you may be. But of all of us I have most reason to jump for joy.

Christiana. There was one moment, standing at the gate, when I thought — because I had knocked and nobody answered — that all our effort had been wasted; and especially when that ugly cur set up such a heavy barking at us.

Mercy. But my worse fear came after I had seen you taken into his favour and myself left outside. Now, I thought, what is written has come true: "Two women shall be grinding together; the one shall be taken and the other left." I had all I could do to keep from crying out, I am finished!

And I was afraid to knock any more. But when I looked up at what was written over the gate, I took courage. And I thought too that I must either knock again or die. So I knocked — but I cannot tell you how, because my spirit was struggling then between life and death.

Christiana. You cannot tell how you knocked? I am sure your knocking was so desperate that the very sound of it made me jump. I thought I had never heard such knocking in my life. I thought you meant to come in by main force, or take the kingdom by storm.

Mercy. But to be in my situation — who that was in it could have done otherwise? You saw that the door was shut on me, and that there was a most savage dog about the place. Who, I say, that was as fainthearted as I was, would not have knocked with all their might? But tell me, please — what did my Lord say about my rudeness? Was he not angry with me?

Christiana. When he heard your thundering, he gave the most wonderfully innocent smile. I believe what you did pleased him well enough, because he gave no sign of anything else. But I do wonder in my heart why he keeps such a dog. If I had known about that beforehand, I am afraid I would not have had the courage to venture here like this. But now that we are in, we are in, and I am glad of it with all my heart.

Mercy. If you like, I will ask him next time he comes down why he keeps such a filthy cur in his yard. I hope he will not take it badly.

Yes, do, said the children — and persuade him to hang it; because we are afraid it will bite us when we go on from here.

So at last he came down to them again; and Mercy fell face down on the ground before him and worshipped, and said: Let my Lord accept the sacrifice of praise that I offer him now with the fruit of my lips.

So he said to her, "Peace be to you; stand up." But she stayed on her face and said, "You are righteous, Lord, when I plead with you; and yet let me talk with you about your judgments." Why do you keep such a savage dog in your yard, at the sight of which women and children like us are ready to run away from your gate in fear?

He answered and said: That dog has another owner, and he is kept shut up on another man's ground; only my pilgrims hear him barking. He belongs to the castle you can see over there in the distance, though he can come right up to the walls of this place. He has frightened many an honest pilgrim out of a worse way into a better one, by the loudness of his roaring.

The man who owns him certainly does not keep him out of any goodwill to me or mine, but with the intention of keeping pilgrims from coming to me, and of making them afraid to knock at this gate for entry. Sometimes he has broken out too, and has savaged some that I loved. But I bear all of this patiently at present. I also give my pilgrims timely help, so that they are not handed over to his power, to have done to them what his doggish nature would prompt.

But come — you whom I have bought: I dare say that if you had known about him beforehand, you would not have been afraid of a dog anyway.

Beggars who go from door to door will run the risk of the bawling and barking and even the biting of a dog, rather than lose the chance of a penny. And shall a dog — a dog in another man's yard, a dog whose barking I turn to my pilgrims' profit — keep anybody from coming to me? I deliver them from the lions, and their darling from the power of the dog.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: I confess my ignorance. I spoke about what I did not understand. I acknowledge that you do all things well.

Christiana. Then Christiana began to talk about their journey, and to ask about the road. So he fed them, and washed their feet, and set them on the way of his own steps, exactly as he had dealt with her husband before.

So I saw in my dream that they walked on their way, and had very pleasant weather for it. Then Christiana began to sing:

	Blessed be the day when I began
	to be a pilgrim too;
	and blessed be that man as well
	who moved me to it.
	It's true it was a long while first
	before I sought to live for ever;
	but now I run as fast as I can —
	better late than never.
	Our tears to joy, our fears to faith
	are turned, as we can see;
	so that our start, as someone says,
	shows what our end will be.

Now there was, on the other side of the wall that fenced in the road Christiana and her companions had to go up, a garden; and that garden belonged to the man who owned the barking dog mentioned earlier. And some of the fruit trees growing in that garden pushed their branches out over the wall; and being ripe, whoever found them would gather them and often eat them, to their own harm.

So Christiana's boys, as boys will, being taken with the trees and the fruit hanging on them, pulled the branches down and began to eat. Their mother told them off for doing it, but the boys carried on anyway.

Well, said she, my sons, you are doing wrong, because that fruit is not ours. But she did not know that the trees belonged to the enemy; I assure you that if she had, she would have been ready to die of fright.

But that passed off, and they went on their way. Now by the time they had got about two bowshots from the place that let them onto the road, they saw two very evil-looking creatures coming down quickly to meet them. At that, Christiana and Mercy her friend covered themselves with their veils and kept walking; the children went on ahead. And so at last they all met.

Then the two who had come down to meet them came right up to the women, as though they meant to embrace them. But Christiana said: Stand back, or go quietly past us, as you ought.

Yet these two, like men who were deaf, took no notice of what Christiana said, but began to lay hands on them. At that Christiana grew furious and kicked at them. Mercy too did everything she could to fight them off. Christiana said to them again: Stand back and be off with you. We have no money to lose, being pilgrims, as you can see — and pilgrims who live on the charity of our friends at that.

Ill-favoured one. Then one of the two men said: We are not attacking you for money. We have come out to tell you that if you will only grant one small request we shall ask, we will make women of you for ever.

Christiana. Now Christiana, guessing what they meant, answered: We will not listen to what you ask, or consider it, or give way to it. We are in a hurry and cannot stop. Our business is a business of life and death.

So she and her companion made another attempt to get past them; but the men blocked their way.

Ill-favoured one. And they said: We mean no harm to your lives. It is another thing we want.

Christiana. Ah, said Christiana — you want us body and soul, because I know that is what you have come for. But we will die on the spot rather than let ourselves be brought into traps that would put our well-being hereafter at risk.

And with that they both shrieked out and cried, Murder! Murder! — and so put themselves under the protection of the laws that exist for the protection of women. But the men still came at them, meaning to have their way. So the women cried out again.

Now since they were, as I said, not far from the gate they had come in at, their voices carried from where they were as far as the gate. So some of the household came out; and knowing it was Christiana's voice, they hurried to her rescue. But by the time they had got in sight of them, the women were in a violent struggle, with the children standing crying alongside.

Then the man who had come to their rescue called out to the ruffians, saying: What is this you are doing? Would you make my Lord's people break his law? He also tried to seize them, but they escaped over the wall into the garden of the man who owned the great dog; and so the dog became their protector.

Then this Reliever came up to the women and asked them how they were. So they answered: Thanks be to your Prince, well enough. Only we have had a bad fright. And we thank you as well for coming in to help us, because otherwise we would have been overpowered.

Reliever. So after a few more words this Reliever said: I wondered very much, when you were received at the gate up there, that knowing as you did that you were only weak women, you did not petition the Lord there for an escort. Then you might have avoided these troubles and dangers, because he would have granted you one.

Christiana. Ah, said Christiana, we were so taken up with the blessing right in front of us that dangers still to come went clean out of our minds. Besides — who would have thought that so near to the King's palace there would be such wicked creatures lying in wait? It really would have been well for us if we had asked our Lord for an escort. But since our Lord knew it would be for our good, I wonder that he did not send one along with us anyway.

Reliever. It is not always necessary to grant what is not asked for, in case by granting it the thing comes to be thought little of. But when the lack of something is actually felt, then in the eyes of the person who feels it, it comes to be valued at its proper worth, and consequently it will be used properly afterwards. If my Lord had granted you an escort, you would not have lamented your oversight in failing to ask for one, as now you have good reason to do. So all things work for good, and tend to make you more careful.

Christiana. Shall we go back to my Lord and confess our foolishness and ask for one?

Reliever. I will present him with your confession of your foolishness. You need not go back; because everywhere you come you will find nothing lacking. In every one of my Lord's lodgings, which he has prepared for the reception of his pilgrims, there is enough to equip them against attacks of any kind. But as I said, "He will be asked by them to do it for them." And a thing that is not worth asking for is a poor thing indeed.

When he had said this he went back to his place, and the Pilgrims went on their way.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: What a sudden setback this is! I reckoned we were past all danger now, and that we should never see any more sorrow.

Christiana. Your innocence, my sister, said Christiana to Mercy, may excuse you a good deal. But as for me, my fault is all the greater, because I saw this danger before I ever came out of doors, and still made no provision against it where provision could have been had. So I am much to blame.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: How did you know about it before you left home? Explain this riddle to me.

Christiana. Why, I will tell you. Before I set foot outside the door, one night as I lay in my bed I had a dream about this very thing. Because I thought I saw two men, as like these two as anything in the world, standing at the foot of my bed and plotting how they might stop my salvation. I will tell you their very words. They said — this was when I was in my troubles — What shall we do with this woman? Because she cries out, waking and sleeping, for forgiveness. If she is allowed to go on as she has begun, we shall lose her as we lost her husband. That, you see, might have made me take care, and provide for it while provision could be had.

Mercy. Well, said Mercy, just as this neglect has given us the occasion to see our own shortcomings, so our Lord has taken the occasion by it to show the riches of his grace. Because he has, as we can see, followed us with kindness we never asked for, and has delivered us out of the hands of men who were stronger than we are, purely out of his own good pleasure.

The House of the Interpreter

So now, when they had talked away a little more time, they came near to a house that stood beside the road — a house that had been built for the relief of pilgrims, as you will find more fully told in the First Part of these Records of the Pilgrim's Progress. So they came on towards the house, the House of the Interpreter; and when they got to the door they heard a great deal of talk going on inside. So they listened, and heard, as they thought, Christiana mentioned by name.

Because you have to understand that news of her and her children going on pilgrimage had run on ahead of her. And this was all the more pleasing to the household because they had heard she was Christian's wife — that woman who had been so unwilling, not long since, even to hear of going on pilgrimage. So they stood still and listened to the good people inside praising her, who little thought she was standing at the door.

At last Christiana knocked, as she had done at the gate before. And when she had knocked, a young woman named Innocent came to the door and opened it and looked out — and there were two women standing there.

Innocent. Then the young woman said to them: Who is it you would like to speak to here?

Christiana. Christiana answered: We understand that this is a privileged place for those who have become pilgrims; and we standing at this door are such. So we ask that we may have a share in what we have come here for; because the day, as you see, is nearly over, and we are unwilling to go any further tonight.

Innocent. Tell me, please, what may I call your name, so that I can tell my Lord inside?

Christiana. My name is Christiana. I was the wife of that pilgrim who travelled this way some years ago, and these are his four children. This young woman is my companion, and is going on pilgrimage too.

Innocent. Then Innocent ran in — for that was her name — and said to those inside: Can you imagine who is at the door? Christiana is there, and her children, and her companion, all waiting to be received here.

Then they leapt for joy, and went and told their Master. So he came to the door, and looking at her, he said: Are you that Christiana whom Christian, the good man, left behind him when he took up a pilgrim's life?

Christiana. I am that woman who was so hard-hearted as to think nothing of my husband's troubles, and who left him to go on his journey alone. And these are his four children. But now I have come too, because I am convinced that no road is the right one but this.

Interpreter. Then what is written of the man who said to his son is fulfilled: "Go and work in my vineyard today. He answered and said, I will not — but afterwards he repented, and went."

Christiana. Then Christiana said: So be it, amen. God make it a true saying about me, and grant that I may be found by him at the last in peace, without spot and blameless.

Interpreter. But why are you standing here at the door? Come in, daughter of Abraham. We were talking about you just now, because word came to us before you did that you had become a pilgrim. Come in, children; come in, young woman.

So he took them all into the house.

So when they were inside, they were told to sit down and rest. And when they had done that, those who looked after the Pilgrims in the house came into the room to see them. And one smiled, and another smiled, and they all smiled, for joy that Christiana had become a pilgrim. They looked at the boys too, and stroked them over the faces with their hands, as a sign of how gladly they received them. They behaved lovingly to Mercy as well, and welcomed them all into their Master's house.

After a while, since supper was not ready, the Interpreter took them into his rooms of emblems and showed them what Christian, Christiana's husband, had seen some time before. So here they saw the man in the cage, and the man and his dream, and the man who cut his way through his enemies, and the picture of the greatest of them all, together with the rest of the things that had been so useful to Christian then.

This done, and after Christiana and her company had digested it a little, the Interpreter took them aside again and brought them first into a room where there was a man who could look nowhere but downwards, with a muckrake in his hand. There was somebody standing over his head as well with a heavenly crown in his hand, offering him that crown for his muckrake; but the man neither looked up nor took any notice, but went on raking together the straws and the little sticks and the dust of the floor.

Then Christiana said: I am fairly sure I know something of what this means. This is a picture of a man of this world, is it not, good sir?

Interpreter. You have said the right thing, said he. And his muckrake shows his earthbound mind. And as for seeing him pay more attention to raking up straws and sticks and the dust of the floor than to the one who calls him from above with the heavenly crown in his hand — that is to show that heaven is nothing but a fable to some people, and that the things down here are counted the only solid things there are. Now as for its also being shown you that the man could look nowhere but downwards, that is to let you know that earthly things, when they have real power over people's minds, carry their hearts clean away from God.

Christiana. Then Christiana said: Oh, deliver me from this muckrake!

Interpreter. That prayer, said the Interpreter, has lain by so long it is nearly rusty. "Give me not riches" is hardly the prayer of one man in ten thousand. Straws and sticks and dust are the great things most people are after now.

At that Mercy and Christiana wept, and said: It is only too true.

When the Interpreter had shown them this, he took them into the very best room in the house — a very fine room it was. So he told them to look round and see whether they could find anything useful there. So they looked round and round; and there was nothing at all to be seen except a very large spider on the wall — and they overlooked that.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: Sir, I see nothing. But Christiana held her tongue.

Interpreter. But, said the Interpreter, look again. So she looked again, and said: There is nothing here but an ugly spider, hanging by her hands on the wall.

Then he said: Is there only one spider in all this spacious room?

Then the tears came into Christiana's eyes, because she was a woman quick to understand; and she said: Yes, Lord, there is more than one here. Yes — and spiders whose venom is far more destructive than what is in that one.

The Interpreter then looked at her kindly and said: You have spoken the truth. This made Mercy blush, and made the boys cover their faces, because they were all beginning to understand the riddle now.

Then the Interpreter said again: "The spider takes hold with her hands," as you see, "and is in kings' palaces." And why is that written down, except to show you that however full of the venom of sin you may be, still, by the hand of faith you may lay hold of, and live in, the best room that belongs to the King's house above?

Christiana. I did think, said Christiana, of something along those lines; but I could not work it all out. I thought we were like spiders, and that we looked like ugly creatures whatever fine room we were in. But that from this spider — this venomous, ill-favoured creature — we were to learn how to exercise faith, that never came into my mind. And yet she has taken hold with her hands, as I can see, and lives in the best room in the house. God has made nothing in vain.

Then they all seemed glad, but there were tears in their eyes; and they looked at one another, and bowed to the Interpreter.

He then took them into another room where there was a hen with chickens, and told them to watch for a while. So one of the chicks went to the trough to drink; and every time she drank she lifted up her head and her eyes towards heaven.

See, said he, what this little chick does; and learn from her to acknowledge where your mercies come from, by looking up as you receive them.

Now again, said he, watch and look. So they paid attention, and saw that the hen had four different ways of dealing with her chicks. First, she had an everyday call, which she used all day long. Second, she had a special call, which she used only sometimes. Third, she had a brooding note. And fourth, she had a cry of alarm.

Now, said he, compare this hen to your King, and these chicks to those who obey him. Because, answering to her, he has his own methods that he uses towards his people. By his everyday call he gives nothing; by his special call he always has something to give; he has a brooding voice for those who are under his wing; and he has a cry of alarm to give warning when he sees the enemy coming. I chose, my dear ones, to bring you into the room where such things are, because you are women, and these are things you will follow easily.

Christiana. And sir, said Christiana, please let us see some more.

So he took them into the slaughterhouse, where a butcher was killing a sheep; and the sheep was quiet, and took her death patiently. Then the Interpreter said: You must learn from this sheep to suffer, and to bear wrongs without grumbling and complaining. Look how quietly she takes her death, and how without any protest she lets her skin be pulled over her ears. Your King calls you his sheep.

After this he led them into his garden, where there was a great variety of flowers; and he said: Do you see all these?

So Christiana said, Yes.

Then he said again: See how the flowers differ in height, and in quality, and in colour, and in scent, and in usefulness; and some are better than others. And where the gardener has planted them, there they stand, and do not quarrel with one another.

Then he took them into his field, which he had sown with wheat and corn; but when they looked, the tops had all been cut off and only the straw was left. He said again: This ground was manured and ploughed and sown. But what shall we do with the crop?

Then Christiana said: Burn some, and make muck of the rest.

Then the Interpreter said again: Fruit, you see, is the thing you are looking for; and for want of it you condemn the crop to the fire and to be trodden underfoot. Take care that you do not condemn yourselves in the same way.

Then, as they were coming in from outside, they spotted a little robin with a large spider in his beak. So the Interpreter said: Look at this.

So they looked, and Mercy wondered at it; but Christiana said: What a disgrace this is to such a pretty little bird as the robin — a bird above most others, that likes to keep up a sort of friendship with people. I had always thought they lived on breadcrumbs or on other such harmless things. I think worse of him than I did.

The Interpreter then answered: This robin is an emblem, and a very apt one for a certain kind of believer. To look at, they are, like this robin, pretty in their song, their colour and their bearing. They also seem to have a very great love for believers who are sincere, and above everything to want to keep company with them, as if they could live on the good man's crumbs. They claim as well that this is why they frequent the houses of the godly and the appointed services of the Lord. But when they are on their own, like the robin, they can catch and gobble up spiders; they can change their diet, drink wickedness and swallow sin down like water.

So when they had come back into the house — because supper was still not ready — Christiana asked again whether the Interpreter would either show or tell them some other useful things. So the Interpreter began, and said:

The fatter the sow, the more she wants the mud. The fatter the ox, the more friskily he goes to the slaughter. And the healthier and stronger a man is, the more prone he is to evil.

There is a desire in women to go neat and fine; and it is a fitting thing to be adorned with what is of great value in God's sight.

It is easier to sit up one or two nights than to sit up a whole year together. So it is easier to make a good start at professing faith than to hold out to the end as one ought.

Every ship's master, in a storm, will willingly throw overboard whatever is of the smallest value in the vessel. But who would throw the best out first? Nobody but the man who does not fear God.

One leak will sink a ship, and one sin will destroy a sinner.

The man who forgets his friend is ungrateful to him; but the man who forgets his Saviour is merciless to himself.

The man who lives in sin and expects happiness afterwards is like a man who sows weeds and expects to fill his barn with wheat or barley.

If a man wants to live well, let him fetch his last day up close to him, and make it his constant companion.

Whispering, and the changing of people's minds, prove that sin is in the world.

If the world, which God thinks little of, is reckoned by men to be of such value — what is heaven, which God commends?

If this life, which comes with so many troubles, is one we are so unwilling to let go of, what must the life above be?

Everybody will loudly praise the goodness of men. But who is there who is properly moved by the goodness of God?

We seldom sit down to a meal without eating and leaving something. In the same way there is more merit and righteousness in Jesus Christ than the whole world has any need of.

When the Interpreter had finished, he took them out into his garden again and brought them to a tree whose inside was all rotten and gone, and yet it was still growing and had leaves on it.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: What does this mean?

This tree, said he, whose outside is fine and whose inside is rotten, is what a great many people in the garden of God may be compared to: people who speak up loudly for God with their mouths, but who in fact will do nothing for him; whose leaves are fine, but whose hearts are good for nothing but to be tinder for the devil's tinderbox.

Now supper was ready, the table laid, and everything set out on the board; so they sat down and ate, when somebody had given thanks. And the Interpreter usually entertained those who lodged with him with music at meals; so the musicians played. There was also somebody who sang, and he had a very fine voice. His song was this:

	The Lord alone is my support,
	the one who gives me feed;
	how can I then want anything
	that I might stand in need?

When the song and the music were finished, the Interpreter asked Christiana what it was that first moved her to take up a pilgrim's life.

Christiana answered: First, the loss of my husband came into my mind, which grieved me deeply — but all that was only natural affection. Then, after that, my husband's troubles and his pilgrimage came into my mind, and also how churlishly I had behaved towards him over it. So guilt took hold of my mind, and would have drawn me into the pit; except that just at the right moment I had a dream about my husband's well-being, and a letter sent me by the King of the country where my husband lives, telling me to come to him. The dream and the letter together worked on my mind so much that they forced me onto this road.

Interpreter. But did you meet with no opposition before you left the house?

Christiana. Yes — a neighbour of mine, one Mrs. Timorous. She was related to the man who tried to persuade my husband to turn back for fear of the lions. She called me every kind of fool over what she called my intended desperate adventure, and urged everything she could to put me off it: the hardship and the troubles my husband met with on the road. But I got over all of that fairly well.

But a dream I had about two evil-looking creatures, who I thought were plotting how to make me come to grief on my journey — that has troubled me a great deal. Yes, it still runs in my mind and makes me afraid of everybody I meet, in case they should meet me to do me some harm and turn me off the road.

Indeed, I may tell my Lord, though I would not have everybody know it, that between here and the gate we came in by, we were both so badly attacked that we were made to cry out, Murder! And the two who made that attack on us were like the two I saw in my dream.

Then the Interpreter said: Your beginning is good; your latter end shall greatly increase.

So he turned to Mercy and said to her: And what moved you to come here, my dear?

Then Mercy blushed and trembled, and for a while stayed silent.

Interpreter. Then he said: Do not be afraid — only believe, and speak your mind.

Mercy. So she began, and said: Truly, sir, it is my lack of experience that makes me want to keep quiet; and that is what fills me with fears of falling short at the end. I cannot tell of visions and dreams as my friend Christiana can; nor do I know what it is to mourn over having refused the advice of good relations.

Interpreter. What was it, then, dear heart, that prevailed with you to do what you have done?

Mercy. Why — when our friend here was packing up to leave our town, another woman and I happened to go and see her. So we knocked at the door and went in. And when we were inside and saw what she was doing, we asked her what she meant by it. She said she had been sent for to go to her husband; and then she went on and told us how she had seen him in a dream, living in a wonderful place among immortals, wearing a crown, playing on a harp, eating and drinking at his Prince's table and singing praises to him for bringing him there.

Now while she was telling us these things, I thought my heart burned inside me. And I said in my heart: if this is true, I will leave my father and my mother and the land where I was born, and I will go along with Christiana if she will have me. So I asked her further whether these things were true, and whether she would let me go with her; because I could see now that there was no living any longer in our town without the danger of ruin.

And yet I came away with a heavy heart — not because I was unwilling to come away, but because so many of my relations were left behind. And I have come with all the longing of my heart, and I will go, if I may, along with Christiana, to her husband and his King.

Interpreter. Your setting out is good, because you have given credit to the truth. You are a Ruth, who out of the love she had for Naomi and for the Lord her God left father and mother and the land of her birth, to come out and go with a people she had never known before. "The Lord repay your work, and may a full reward be given you by the Lord God of Israel, under whose wings you have come to trust."

Now supper was over, and the beds were made ready. The women were put to sleep on their own, and the boys by themselves. And when Mercy was in bed she could not sleep for joy, because now her doubts about falling short at the last were further away from her than they had ever been. So she lay there blessing and praising God, who had shown her such favour.

In the morning they rose with the sun and got themselves ready to leave; but the Interpreter wanted them to wait a while, because, he said, you must go from here in proper order.

Then he said to the young woman who had first opened to them: Take them into the garden to the bath, and wash them there, and make them clean of the dirt they have picked up travelling.

Then Innocent took them and brought them into the garden to the bath. So she told them they must wash and be clean there, because that was what her Master wanted the women who called at his house on their way to do. Then they went in and washed — they and the boys and all — and they came out of that bath not only sweet and clean, but with new life and strength in their limbs. So when they came back in, they looked a great deal better than they had when they went out to wash.

When they had come back from the bath in the garden, the Interpreter took them and looked at them and said to them: Fair as the moon.

Then he called for the seal that is used to seal those who have been washed in his bath. So the seal was brought, and he set his mark on them, so that they might be recognized in the places they still had to go. Now the seal was the substance and sum of the Passover that the children of Israel ate when they came out of the land of Egypt; and the mark was set between their eyes. This seal added greatly to their beauty, because it was an ornament to their faces. It added to their dignity too, and made their faces more like the faces of angels.

Then the Interpreter said again to the young woman who was waiting on these women: Go into the vestry and fetch out clothes for these people.

So she went and fetched out white clothing and laid it down in front of him; and he told them to put it on. It was fine linen, white and clean.

When the women had been dressed like this, they seemed almost frightening to one another; because each could not see on herself the glory that she could see on the other. So now they began to think each other better than themselves. "You are lovelier than I am," said one; and "you are more beautiful than I am," said the other. The children too stood amazed at the state they had all been brought into.

Great-heart, and the Cross

The Interpreter then called for one of his manservants, a man called Great-heart, and told him to take sword and helmet and shield. And take these daughters of mine, he said, and escort them to the house called Beautiful, where they will rest next.

So Great-heart took up his weapons and went ahead of them; and the Interpreter said, God speed. Those who belonged to the household sent them off as well with many a good wish. So they went on their way, and sang:

	This place has been our second stage;
	here we have heard and seen
	those good things which, from age to age,
	have hidden from others been.
	The dunghill-raker, spider, hen,
	the chicken too, to me
	have taught a lesson; let me then
	conformed to it be.
	The butcher, garden and the field,
	the robin and his bait,
	and the rotten tree as well all yield
	me argument of weight
	to move me now to watch and pray,
	to strive to be sincere,
	to take my cross up day by day
	and serve the Lord with fear.

Now I saw in my dream that they went on, with Great-heart walking in front of them; and so they came to the place where Christian's burden fell off his back and tumbled into a tomb. Here they paused, and here too they blessed God.

Now, said Christiana, it comes into my mind what was said to us at the gate — that we should have pardon by word and by deed: by word, that is, by the promise; and by deed, that is, in the way it was obtained. What the promise is, I know something about. But what it is to have pardon by deed, or in the way it was obtained — Mr. Great-heart, I imagine you know; so if you would, let us hear you speak about it.

Great-heart. Pardon by the deed done is pardon obtained by somebody, for somebody else who needs it — not obtained by the person pardoned, but, as another puts it, in the way in which I have obtained it. So then, to speak to the question more fully: the pardon that you and Mercy and these boys have received was obtained by another — namely by the one who let you in at the gate. And he obtained it in a double way: he has performed a righteousness to cover you, and spilt blood to wash you in.

Christiana. But if he parts with his righteousness to us, what will he have left for himself?

Great-heart. He has more righteousness than you need, or than he needs himself.

Christiana. Please show me how that can be.

Great-heart. With all my heart. But first I must say this by way of preface: the one we are about to speak of has no equal anywhere. He has two natures in one Person, plainly to be distinguished but impossible to divide. A righteousness belongs to each of these natures, and each righteousness is essential to that nature; so that you could as easily wipe out the nature itself as separate its justice or righteousness from it. Of these righteousnesses, then, we are not made partakers — they are not put on us so that we might be made just and live by them.

Besides these, there is a righteousness this Person has as the two natures are joined in one. And this is neither the righteousness of the Godhead as distinct from the manhood, nor the righteousness of the manhood as distinct from the Godhead, but a righteousness that consists in the union of both natures. It may properly be called the righteousness essential to his being fitted by God for the office of mediator, which he was to be entrusted with.

If he parts with the first righteousness, he parts with his Godhead. If he parts with the second, he parts with the purity of his manhood. If he parts with this third, he parts with the perfection that fits him for the office of mediation.

So he has another righteousness besides, which consists in performance, or obedience, to a revealed will. And that is the one he puts on sinners, and by which their sins are covered. Which is why he says, "As by one man's disobedience many were made sinners, so by the obedience of one shall many be made righteous."

Christiana. But are the other righteousnesses of no use at all to us?

Great-heart. Yes, they are. Because although they are essential to his natures and his office, and so cannot be handed over to anybody else, it is by virtue of them that the righteousness which justifies is effective for that purpose. The righteousness of his Godhead gives power to his obedience. The righteousness of his manhood gives his obedience the capacity to justify. And the righteousness that consists in the union of the two natures in his office gives that righteousness the authority to do the work it is appointed for.

So then, here is a righteousness that Christ as God has no need of, because he is God without it. Here is a righteousness that Christ as man has no need of to make him so, because he is perfect man without it. And again, here is a righteousness that Christ as God-and-man has no need of, because he is perfectly that without it.

Here, then, is a righteousness that Christ — as God, as man, as God-and-man — has no need of with reference to himself, and which he can therefore spare: a justifying righteousness that he does not need for himself, and therefore gives away. Which is why it is called "the gift of righteousness."

And this righteousness, since Christ Jesus the Lord has put himself under the law, must be given away. Because the law does not only bind the man who is under it "to do justly," but to practise charity. So he must, and by the law he ought, if he has two coats, to give one to the man who has none. Now our Lord does indeed have two coats — one for himself and one to spare; and so he freely gives one to those who have none.

And that, Christiana and Mercy and the rest of you here, is how your pardon comes by deed, or by another man's work. Your Lord Christ is the one who has done the work, and has given away what he worked for to the next poor beggar he meets.

But again: for pardon by deed, something has to be paid to God as a price, as well as something being prepared to cover us with. Sin has handed us over to the just curse of a righteous law; and from that curse we must be justified by way of redemption, a price being paid for the harm we have done. And this is by the blood of your Lord, who came and stood in your place and instead of you, and died your death for your offences. So he has ransomed you from your offences by blood, and covered your polluted and misshapen souls with righteousness. And for the sake of that, God passes you by, and will not harm you when he comes to judge the world.

Christiana. This is splendid. Now I see there was something to be learned from our being pardoned by word and by deed. Good Mercy, let us work at keeping this in mind; and my children, remember it too. But, sir — was this not the thing that made my good Christian's burden fall off his shoulders, and made him give three leaps for joy?

Great-heart. Yes. It was believing this that cut the cords which could not be cut by any other means; and it was to give him a proof of the power of it that he was allowed to carry his burden all the way to the Cross.

Christiana. I thought so. Because although my heart was light and joyful before, it is ten times lighter and more joyful now. And I am convinced, from what I have felt — and I have felt only a little of it as yet — that if the most heavily burdened man in the world were standing here and could see and believe as I do now, it would make his heart merrier and lighter still.

Great-heart. There is not only comfort in this, and the easing of a burden, when we see and think about these things; there is also a deep affection born in us by it. Because who could once think that pardon comes not only by promise but in this way, and not be moved by the way and the means of his own redemption — and so by the Man who has worked it out for him?

Christiana. True. It seems to me it makes my heart bleed to think that he should bleed for me. Oh, you loving One! Oh, you blessed One! You deserve to have me; you have bought me; you deserve to have all of me; you have paid ten thousand times more for me than I am worth!

No wonder this made the tears stand in my husband's eyes, and made him trudge along so nimbly. I am sure he wished me with him — and, wretch that I was, I let him go all alone.

Oh, Mercy, if only your father and mother were here! Yes, and Mrs. Timorous too. In fact I wish with all my heart that Madam Wanton were here as well. Surely, surely their hearts would be moved. Neither the fear of the one nor the strong appetites of the other could persuade them to go home again and refuse to become good pilgrims.

Great-heart. You are speaking now in the warmth of your feelings. Will it always be like this with you, do you think? Besides, this is not given to everybody who saw your Jesus bleed. There were people standing by who saw the blood run from his heart to the ground, and who were so far from this that instead of grieving they laughed at him, and instead of becoming his disciples hardened their hearts against him. So everything you have, my daughters, you have by a particular impression made in you by God as you contemplate what I have been telling you. Remember what you were told: that the hen gives her chicks no food by her everyday call. So what you have, you have by a special grace.

Now I saw further in my dream that they went on until they came to the place where Simple and Sloth and Presumption were lying asleep when Christian went past on pilgrimage — and there they were, hanged in irons a little way off on the other side.

Mercy. Then Mercy said to their guide and escort: Who are those three men? And what have they been hanged there for?

Great-heart. These three men were men of very bad character. They had no wish to be pilgrims themselves, and they hindered anybody else they could. They were all for laziness and folly themselves, and anybody they could talk round they made the same; and along with it they taught them to take for granted that they would come out all right in the end. They were asleep when Christian went by; and now, when you go by, they are hanged.

Mercy. But could they really persuade anybody to their way of thinking?

Great-heart. Yes. They turned several people off the road. There was Slow-pace, whom they persuaded to do as they did. They also succeeded with one Short-wind, and one No-heart, and one Linger-after-lust, and one Sleepyhead, and with a young woman called Dull, so that they turned off the road and became like them.

Besides that, they put about a bad report of your Lord, persuading others that he was a slave-driver. They also put about a bad report of the good land, saying it was not half as good as some people claimed. They began running his servants down too, and counting even the best of them meddlers, nuisances and busybodies. And further, they could call the bread of God husks, and the comforts of his children fancies, and the travelling and labour of pilgrims a waste of time.

Christiana. No, said Christiana — if they were like that, they shall never be mourned by me. They have only what they deserve. And I think it is a good thing that they hang so near the highway, so that others may see them and take warning. But would it not have been well if their crimes had been engraved on some plate of iron or brass and left here, on the very spot where they did their mischief, as a warning to other bad men?

Great-heart. So they are — as you may see for yourself, if you will step over to the wall.

Mercy. No, no — let them hang, and let their names rot, and let their crimes live for ever against them. I think it a great favour that they were hanged before we came here. Who knows what they might otherwise have done to poor women like us?

Then she turned it into a song, saying:

	Now then, you three — hang there, and be a sign
	to all who shall against the truth combine.
	And let the man who comes behind fear this end
	if to the pilgrims he is not a friend.
	And you, my soul, of all such men beware
	who to holiness the opposers are.

The Hill Difficulty and the Lions

So they went on until they came to the foot of the Hill Difficulty, where again their good friend Mr. Great-heart took the chance to tell them what happened there when Christian himself came past. So he took them first to the spring.

Look, said he, this is the spring Christian drank from before he went up this hill. It was clear and good then; but now it is dirty, from the feet of people who do not want pilgrims quenching their thirst here.

Mercy. At which Mercy said: Why so spiteful, I wonder?

But, said their guide, it will do well enough if you take it up and put it into a vessel that is clean and sound; because then the dirt will sink to the bottom and the water will come out of its own accord clearer. So Christiana and her companions had to do exactly that. They scooped it up and put it into an earthenware pot, and let it stand until the dirt had settled to the bottom, and then they drank it.

Next he showed them the two side roads at the foot of the hill, where Formalist and Hypocrisy lost themselves. And, said he, these are dangerous paths. Two men were thrown away here when Christian came by. And although, as you see, these ways have since been blocked off with chains and posts and a ditch, there are still people who would rather risk them than take the trouble to go up this hill.

Christiana. "The way of transgressors is hard." It is a wonder they can get into those ways at all without breaking their necks.

Great-heart. They will risk it. Yes — and if at any time one of the King's servants happens to see them and calls out to them and tells them they are on the wrong road, and warns them of the danger, they will answer him back abusively and say, "As for the word you have spoken to us in the name of the Lord, we will not listen to you; we will certainly do whatever comes out of our own mouths." And more than that: if you look a little further, you will see these ways have been made warning enough not only by the posts and the ditch and the chain but by being hedged up as well — and still they choose to go there.

Christiana. They are lazy. They do not like taking trouble; an uphill road is unpleasant to them. So what is written is fulfilled in them: "The way of the slothful man is like a hedge of thorns." Yes — they would rather walk into a trap than go up this hill and along the rest of this road to the city.

Then they set off and began to go up the hill, and up the hill they went. But before they got to the top Christiana began to pant, and said: I dare say this is a hill that makes you breathe. No wonder that people who love their comfort more than their souls choose themselves a smoother road.

Then Mercy said: I must sit down. And the smallest of the children began to cry.

Come, come, said Great-heart, do not sit down here — because a little further up is the Prince's shelter. Then he took the little boy by the hand and led him up to it.

When they had got to the shelter they were very glad to sit down, because they were all in a great sweat. Then Mercy said: How sweet rest is to people who have been working! And how good the Prince of pilgrims is, to provide resting places like this for them! I have heard a great deal about this shelter, but I never saw it before. But here let us take care not to fall asleep; because I have heard that that cost poor Christian dear.

Then Mr. Great-heart said to the little ones: Come, my fine boys, how are you? What do you think of going on pilgrimage now?

Sir, said the smallest, I was nearly beaten out of heart. But thank you for lending me a hand when I needed it. And I remember now what my mother has told me: that the way to heaven is like going up a ladder, and the way to hell is like going down a hill. But I would rather go up the ladder to life than down the hill to death.

Then Mercy said: But the proverb is that going downhill is easy.

But James said — for that was his name — The day is coming when, in my opinion, going downhill will be the hardest thing of all.

That is a good boy, said his master; you have given her the right answer. Then Mercy smiled, but the little boy went red.

Christiana. Come, said Christiana, will you eat something, just to sweeten your mouths while you sit here and rest your legs? Because I have a piece of pomegranate here that Mr. Interpreter put into my hand just as I was coming out of his door. He gave me a piece of honeycomb too, and a little bottle of cordial.

I thought he gave you something, said Mercy, because he called you aside.

Yes, so he did, said the other. But, said Christiana, it shall be as I said it would be when we first came from home: you shall have a share in every good thing I have, because you so willingly became my companion.

Then she shared it out and they ate — Mercy and the boys together. And Christiana said to Mr. Great-heart: Sir, will you have some as we are doing?

But he answered: You are going on pilgrimage, and I shall be going back shortly. Much good may what you have do you. At home I eat the same thing every day.

Now when they had eaten and drunk and chatted a little longer, their guide said to them: The day is wearing away; if you think it best, let us get ready to go.

So they got up to go, and the little boys went in front. But Christiana forgot to take her bottle of cordial with her, so she sent her little boy back to fetch it.

Then Mercy said: I think this is a losing place. Christian lost his scroll here, and here Christiana has left her bottle behind. Sir, what is the reason for it?

So their guide answered and said: The reason is sleep, or forgetfulness. Some sleep when they should be keeping awake, and some forget when they should be remembering. And this is exactly why, at the resting places, pilgrims often come off losers in one thing or another. Pilgrims ought to keep watch, and to remember what they have already received, precisely when they are enjoying themselves most; and for want of doing that, their rejoicing often ends in tears and their sunshine in a cloud. Witness Christian's own story in this place.

When they had come to the spot where Mistrust and Timorous met Christian to persuade him to turn back for fear of the lions, they saw what looked like a scaffold; and in front of it, towards the road, a broad plate with a verse written on it, and underneath the reason for that scaffold being put up in that place. The verse was this:

	Let the man who sees this scaffold take good heed
	unto his heart and tongue;
	for if he does not, here he'll come to speed
	as some did long ago.

And the words underneath the verse were: "This scaffold was built for the punishment of those who, through Timorousness or Mistrust, are afraid to go further on pilgrimage. On this scaffold both Mistrust and Timorous were burned through the tongue with a hot iron, for trying to hinder Christian on his journey."

Then Mercy said: This is very like what the Beloved says: "What shall be given to you, or what shall be done to you, you false tongue? Sharp arrows of the mighty, with coals of juniper."

So they went on until they came within sight of the lions. Now Mr. Great-heart was a strong man, so he was not afraid of a lion. But when they came up to the place where the lions were, the boys who had been walking in front were glad to shrink back behind, because they were afraid of the lions. So they stepped back and went behind.

At this their guide smiled and said: How now, my boys — do you like to go in front when no danger is coming, and like to come behind as soon as the lions appear?

Now as they went up, Mr. Great-heart drew his sword, meaning to force a way through for the Pilgrims in spite of the lions. Then somebody appeared who had apparently taken it on himself to reinforce the lions; and he said to the Pilgrims' guide: What is the reason for your coming here? Now the name of that man was Grim, or Bloody-man, because of the pilgrims he had killed; and he was of the race of the giants.

Great-heart. Then the Pilgrims' guide said: These women and children are going on pilgrimage. This is the road they have to take, and take it they shall, in spite of you and your lions.

Grim. This is not their road, and they shall not go on it. I have come out to stop them, and I will back the lions to that end.

Now, to tell the truth, on account of the fierceness of the lions and the grim bearing of the man who backed them, this road had lately gone very little used, and was almost all overgrown with grass.

Christiana. Then Christiana said: Though the highways have gone unused until now, and though travellers have been made in the past to walk by side paths, it must not be so now that I have risen. Now "I am risen a mother in Israel."

Grim. Then he swore by the lions that it should be so, and told them to turn aside, because they should have no passage there.

Great-heart. But their guide went straight at Grim, and laid into him so hard with his sword that he forced him back.

Grim. Then the man who was trying to back the lions said: Will you kill me on my own ground?

Great-heart. It is the King's highway we are on, and it is on his road that you have placed your lions. But these women and these children, weak as they are, shall hold on their way in spite of your lions.
