And with that he gave him another downright blow and brought him to his knees. With that blow he also broke his helmet, and with the next he cut off an arm. Then the giant roared so hideously that his voice frightened the women — and yet they were glad to see him sprawling on the ground.

Now the lions were chained, so of themselves they could do nothing. So when old Grim, who had meant to back them, was dead, Mr. Great-heart said to the Pilgrims: Come on now and follow me, and no harm shall come to you from the lions.

So on they went — but the women trembled as they passed them, and the boys looked as if they would die of it. Still, they all got past without any further harm.

Now they were within sight of the Porter's Lodge, and they soon came up to it; but they made all the more haste after this to get there, because it is dangerous travelling in that place at night.

So when they had come to the gate, the guide knocked, and the Porter called out: Who is there? But as soon as the guide had said, It is I, he knew his voice and came down — because the guide had often been there before, escorting pilgrims.

When he had come down he opened the gate; and seeing the guide standing right in front of it, but not seeing the women, because they were behind him, he said: How now, Mr. Great-heart, what is your business here so late at night?

I have brought some pilgrims here, said he, who by my Lord's command are to lodge here. I would have been here some time ago if I had not been opposed by the giant who used to back the lions; but after a long and wearing fight with him I have cut him off, and have brought the Pilgrims here safely.

Porter. Will you not come in and stay till morning?

Great-heart. No, I shall go back to my Lord tonight.

Christiana. Oh, sir, I do not know how to be willing for you to leave us in the middle of our pilgrimage. You have been so faithful and so loving to us; you have fought so bravely for us; you have been so warm-hearted in advising us. I shall never forget your kindness to us.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: Oh, if only we could have your company to the end of our journey! How can poor women like us hold out on a road as full of trouble as this one, without a friend and a defender?

James. Then James, the youngest of the boys, said: Please, sir, let yourself be persuaded to come with us and help us — because we are so weak, and the road is so dangerous.

Great-heart. I am at my Lord's command. If he assigns me to be your guide the whole way, I will gladly attend on you. But here you slipped up at the start: when he told me to come this far with you, you should have begged him for me to go the whole way with you, and he would have granted your request. As things are, though, I must leave. And so — good Christiana, Mercy, and my fine children — goodbye.

Then the Porter, Mr. Watchful, asked Christiana what country she came from and who her family were; and she said: I have come from the City of Destruction. I am a widow, and my husband is dead. His name was Christian, the Pilgrim.

What! said the Porter — was he your husband?

Yes, said she; and these are his children. And this, she said, pointing to Mercy, is one of my townswomen.

Then the Porter rang his bell, as he is accustomed to do at such times, and one of the young women came to the door, whose name was Humble-mind. And the Porter said to her: Go and tell them inside that Christiana, the wife of Christian, and her children, have come here on pilgrimage.

So she went in and told them. But oh, what a noise of gladness there was inside, when the young woman had barely got the words out of her mouth! So they came hurrying to the Porter — because Christiana was still standing at the door. Then some of the most serious among them said to her: Come in, Christiana; come in, wife of that good man; come in, blessed woman; come in, and all who are with you.

So she went in, and her children and her companions followed her. Now when they had gone in, they were taken into a very large room and told to sit down; so they sat down, and the heads of the house were called to see the guests and welcome them. Then they came in, and understanding who they were, greeted them each with a kiss and said: Welcome, you vessels of the grace of God; welcome to us, your friends.

The House Beautiful Again

Now because it was getting late, and because the Pilgrims were tired from their journey and faint as well from the sight of the fight and the terrible lions, they asked to get ready for bed as soon as they could.

No, said the household — refresh yourselves first with a bite of food. Because they had prepared a lamb for them, with the sauce that traditionally goes with it; the Porter had heard beforehand that they were coming and had told those inside.

So when they had eaten and finished their prayers with a psalm, they asked to go to their rest. But, said Christiana, if we may be so bold as to choose, let us have the room that was my husband's when he was here.

So they took them up there, and they all slept in one room. When they were settled, Christiana and Mercy fell into conversation about suitable things.

Christiana. I little thought once, when my husband went on pilgrimage, that I should ever follow him.

Mercy. And you thought as little of lying in his bed, and resting in his room, as you are doing now.

Christiana. And far less did I ever think of seeing his face with any comfort, or of worshipping the Lord the King alongside him. And yet now I believe I shall.

Mercy. Listen! Do you not hear a noise?

Christiana. Yes. I believe it is a sound of music, for joy that we are here.

Mercy. Wonderful! Music in the house, music in the heart, and music in heaven too, for joy that we are here.

So they talked a while, and then went to sleep. And in the morning, when they woke, Christiana said to Mercy:

Christiana. What was it that made you laugh in your sleep last night? I suppose you were dreaming.

Mercy. So I was, and a sweet dream it was. But are you sure I laughed?

Christiana. Yes, you laughed heartily. But please, Mercy, tell me your dream.

Mercy. I dreamed that I was sitting all alone in a lonely place, lamenting the hardness of my heart. Now I had not been sitting there long when it seemed a great many people gathered round to look at me and hear what I was saying. So they listened, and I went on lamenting the hardness of my heart. At this some of them laughed at me, and some called me a fool, and some began to push me about.

At that I thought I looked up and saw somebody coming towards me with wings. So he came straight to me and said, Mercy, what is the matter with you? And when he had heard my complaint, he said, "Peace be to you." He also wiped my eyes with his handkerchief and dressed me in silver and gold. He put a chain round my neck and earrings in my ears and a beautiful crown on my head. Then he took me by the hand and said, Mercy, come after me.

So he went up and I followed, until we came to a golden gate. Then he knocked; and when those inside had opened, the man went in and I followed him up to a throne, on which one sat; and he said to me, Welcome, daughter. The place looked bright and glittering, like the stars — or rather like the sun. And I thought I saw your husband there.

So I woke up from my dream. But did I really laugh?

Christiana. Laugh! Yes, and well you might, to see yourself so well off. Because you must allow me to tell you that I believe it was a good dream, and that just as you have begun to find the first part of it true, so you will find the second part true at last. "God speaks once, yes twice, and yet man does not perceive it: in a dream, in a vision of the night, when deep sleep falls on men, in their slumbering on the bed." We do not need to lie awake in bed to talk with God. He can visit us while we sleep, and make us hear his voice then. Our heart often wakes while we sleep, and God can speak to it, by words or by proverbs or by signs and comparisons, quite as well as if we were awake.

Mercy. Well, I am glad of my dream; because I hope before long to see it come true, and to be made to laugh again.

Christiana. I think it is high time we got up and found out what we are to do.

Mercy. Please, if they invite us to stay a while, let us gladly accept the offer. I am all the more willing to stay a while here so as to get better acquainted with these young women. It seems to me that Prudence and Piety and Charity have very fine, serious faces.

Christiana. We shall see what they do.

So when they were up and dressed, they came down; and they asked each other how they had slept, and whether it had been comfortable.

Mercy. Very good, said Mercy. It was one of the best nights' lodging I ever had in my life.

Then Prudence and Piety said: If you can be persuaded to stay here a while, you shall have whatever the house can provide.

Charity. Yes — and with a very good will, said Charity.

So they agreed, and stayed there about a month or more, and did each other a great deal of good. And because Prudence wanted to see how Christiana had brought her children up, she asked her permission to test them. So Christiana freely consented. Then she began with the youngest, whose name was James.

Prudence. And she said: Come, James — can you tell me who made you?

James. God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit.

Prudence. Good boy. And can you tell me who saves you?

James. God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit.

Prudence. Still a good boy. But how does God the Father save you?

James. By his grace.

Prudence. How does God the Son save you?

James. By his righteousness, his death, his blood and his life.

Prudence. And how does God the Holy Spirit save you?

James. By enlightening me, by making me new, and by keeping me.

Then Prudence said to Christiana: You are to be praised for bringing your children up like this. I suppose I need not ask the others these questions, since the youngest of them can answer them so well. So now I will turn to the next youngest.

Prudence. Then she said: Come, Joseph — for that was his name — will you let me question you?

Joseph. With all my heart.

Prudence. What is man?

Joseph. A creature with reason, made so by God, as my brother said.

Prudence. What is implied by the word "saved"?

Joseph. That man, by sin, has brought himself into a state of captivity and misery.

Prudence. What is implied by his being saved by the Trinity?

Joseph. That sin is so great and mighty a tyrant that nobody but God can pull us out of its grip; and that God is so good and loving to man as actually to pull him out of that miserable state.

Prudence. What is God's purpose in saving poor men?

Joseph. The glory of his name, and of his grace and justice; and the everlasting happiness of his creature.

Prudence. Who are the ones who must be saved?

Joseph. Those who accept his salvation.

Prudence. Good boy, Joseph. Your mother has taught you well, and you have listened to what she has said to you.

Then Prudence said to Samuel, who was the second eldest:

Prudence. Come, Samuel — are you willing for me to question you as well?

Samuel. Yes, indeed, if you please.

Prudence. What is heaven?

Samuel. A place and a state most blessed, because God lives there.

Prudence. What is hell?

Samuel. A place and a state most wretched, because it is the dwelling place of sin, and the devil, and death.

Prudence. Why would you want to go to heaven?

Samuel. So that I may see God and serve him without ever getting tired; so that I may see Christ and love him everlastingly; and so that I may have that fullness of the Holy Spirit in me that I cannot possibly enjoy here.

Prudence. A very good boy too, and one who has learned well.

Then she turned to the eldest, whose name was Matthew, and said to him: Come, Matthew, shall I question you as well?

Matthew. With a very good will.

Prudence. Then I ask you: was there ever anything that existed before God?

Matthew. No — because God is eternal; nor is there anything besides himself that existed before the beginning of the first day. "For in six days the Lord made heaven and earth, the sea, and everything in them."

Prudence. What do you think of the Bible?

Matthew. It is the holy Word of God.

Prudence. Is there nothing written in it but what you understand?

Matthew. Yes — a great deal.

Prudence. What do you do when you come to places in it that you do not understand?

Matthew. I consider that God is wiser than I am. And I pray that he will be pleased to let me know everything in it that he knows will be for my good.

Prudence. What do you believe about the resurrection of the dead?

Matthew. I believe they shall rise, the same as were buried — the same in their nature, though not in their decay. And I believe this on two grounds: first, because God has promised it; and second, because he is able to do it.

Then Prudence said to the boys: You must go on listening to your mother, because she can teach you more. You must also pay careful attention to any good talk you hear from others, because it is for your sakes that they say good things. Observe as well, and carefully, what the heavens and the earth teach you; but above all be much occupied in meditating on that Book which was the cause of your father's becoming a pilgrim. I for my part, my children, will teach you what I can while you are here, and shall be glad if you will ask me questions that tend to godly building up.

Now by the time these Pilgrims had been at this place a week, Mercy had a visitor who claimed to have some goodwill towards her; and his name was Mr. Brisk — a man of some breeding, who made a show of religion, but a man who stuck very close to the world. So he came once or twice, or more, to Mercy, and offered her his love.

Now Mercy had a lovely face, and was all the more attractive for it. And her disposition was always to be busy at something; because when she had nothing to do for herself she would be making stockings and clothes for other people, and would give them away to those who needed them. And Mr. Brisk, not knowing where or how she disposed of what she made, seemed greatly taken with her, because he found she was never idle. I will warrant her a good housewife, he said to himself.

Mercy then mentioned the business to the young women of the house, and asked them about him, because they knew him better than she did. So they told her that he was a very busy young man who made a show of religion, but who was, they were afraid, a stranger to the power of what was good.

In that case, said Mercy, I will not look at him again. Because I mean never to have anything hung round my soul to drag on it.

Prudence then replied that there was no need to give him any great discouragement; her carrying on as she had begun, working for the poor, would soon cool his ardour.

So the next time he came, he found her at her old work, making things for the poor. Then he said: What — always at it?

Yes, said she — either for myself or for other people.

And how much can you earn in a day? said he.

I do these things, said she, "so that I may be rich in good works, laying up in store a good foundation for the time to come, so that I may lay hold on eternal life."

Why, tell me — what do you do with them? said he.

Clothe the naked, said she.

At that his face fell. So he gave up coming to see her; and when he was asked the reason why, he said that Mercy was a pretty girl, but troubled with awkward notions.

When he had left her, Prudence said: Did I not tell you Mr. Brisk would soon give you up? Yes — and he will spread a bad report of you as well. Because for all his pretence of religion and his apparent love for Mercy, still, Mercy and he are of such different tempers that I do not believe they will ever come together.

Mercy. I might have had husbands before now, though I never mentioned it to anybody. But they were the sort who did not like my character, though none of them ever found fault with my person. So they and I could not agree.

Prudence. Mercy in our day is little valued, except as a word. As for the practice of it, which is what your character sets out, there are very few who can put up with it.

Mercy. Well, said Mercy, if nobody will have me, I will die unmarried; or else my character shall be a husband to me. Because I cannot change my nature; and to have somebody who runs across me in this, I am resolved never to allow as long as I live. I had a sister called Bountiful who married one of these boors; and he and she could never agree. But because my sister was determined to go on as she had begun — that is, to show kindness to the poor — her husband first denounced her publicly at the market cross, and then turned her out of doors.

Prudence. And yet he was a professing believer, I dare say.

Mercy. Yes, the sort of believer he was. And the world is full of that sort now; but I will have none of them.

Now Matthew, Christiana's eldest son, fell ill; and his illness was severe, because he was in great pain in the stomach, so that at times he was doubled up as though he were being pulled double. There lived not far from there one Mr. Skill, an old and well-regarded physician. So Christiana asked for him, and they sent for him, and he came.

When he had come into the room and observed the boy a little, he concluded that he was suffering from severe cramps. Then he said to his mother: What has Matthew been eating lately?

Eating? said Christiana. Nothing but what is wholesome.

The physician answered: This boy has been meddling with something that is lying undigested in his stomach, and that will not shift without help. And I tell you, he must be purged or he will die.

Samuel. Then Samuel said: Mother, mother — what was it my brother gathered up and ate as soon as we had come from the gate at the head of this road? You remember there was an orchard on the left hand, on the other side of the wall, and some of the trees hung over the wall, and my brother pulled the branches down and ate.

Christiana. True, my child, said Christiana. He did take some and eat it. Naughty boy that he was, I told him off, and still he would eat it.

Mr. Skill. I knew he had eaten something that was not wholesome food; and that food — that fruit — is the most harmful of the lot. It is the fruit of Beelzebub's orchard. I am astonished nobody warned you about it. A great many have died of it.

Christiana. Then Christiana began to cry, and said: Oh, the naughty boy! And oh, the careless mother! What am I to do for my son?

Mr. Skill. Come, do not be too downcast. The boy may be well again; but he must be purged, and made to be sick.

Christiana. Please, sir, use the very utmost of your skill on him, whatever it costs.

Mr. Skill. No — I hope I shall be reasonable.

So he made him a purge; but it was too weak. It was said to be made of the blood of a goat, the ashes of a heifer, and some juice of hyssop. When Mr. Skill saw that this purge was too weak, he made him one that would do the job: it was made *ex carne et sanguine Christi* — out of the flesh and blood of Christ. (You know physicians give their patients strange medicines.) And it was made up into pills with a promise or two, and a proportionate quantity of salt. Now he was to take them three at a time, on an empty stomach, in half a quarter of a pint of the tears of repentance.

When this medicine had been prepared and brought to the boy, he was unwilling to take it, though he was torn with the cramps as if he were being pulled to pieces.

Come, come, said the physician, you must take it.

It turns my stomach, said the boy.

I must have you take it, said his mother.

I shall be sick and bring it up again, said the boy.

Please, sir, said Christiana to Mr. Skill, how does it taste?

It has no bad taste, said the doctor. And with that she touched one of the pills with the tip of her tongue.

Oh, Matthew, said she, this medicine is sweeter than honey. If you love your mother, if you love your brothers, if you love Mercy, if you love your own life — take it.

So with much difficulty, after a short prayer for God's blessing on it, he took it, and it worked kindly with him. It made him purge; it made him sleep and rest quietly; it put him into a good warmth and a breaking sweat, and it rid him entirely of his cramps. So in a short time he got up and walked about with a stick, and would go from room to room and talk with Prudence and Piety and Charity about his illness and how he was cured.

So when the boy was well, Christiana asked Mr. Skill: Sir, what will satisfy you for your trouble and your care of my child?

And he said: You must pay the Master of the College of Physicians, according to the rules laid down for such cases.

Christiana. But, sir, said she, what else is this pill good for?

Mr. Skill. It is a universal pill. It is good against all the diseases that Pilgrims are liable to; and when it is properly prepared it will keep good for ever.

Christiana. Please, sir, make me up twelve boxes of them; because if I can get these, I will never take any other medicine.

Mr. Skill. These pills are good for preventing diseases as well as for curing them once you are ill. Yes, and I dare say it and stand by it: that if a man will only use this medicine as he should, it will make him live for ever. But, good Christiana, you must give these pills no other way than I have prescribed; because if you do, they will do no good.

So he gave Christiana medicine for herself and her boys and for Mercy, and told Matthew to take care how he ate any more green plums; and he kissed them and went on his way.

You were told earlier that Prudence had told the boys that whenever they liked they should ask her questions that might do them good, and that she would say something to them.

Matthew. Then Matthew, who had been ill, asked her why medicine is mostly bitter to our tongues.

Prudence. To show how unwelcome the Word of God, and its effects, are to a worldly heart.

Matthew. Why does medicine, if it does us good, purge us and make us sick?

Prudence. To show that the Word, when it works effectively, cleans out the heart and mind. Because look — what the one does to the body, the other does to the soul.

Matthew. What should we learn from seeing the flame of our fire go upwards, and from seeing the beams and sweet influences of the sun strike downwards?

Prudence. From the going up of the fire we are taught to rise to heaven by hot and eager desires. And from the sun sending its heat and beams and sweet influences downwards, we are taught that the Saviour of the world, though he is high, reaches down with his grace and love to us below.

Matthew. Where do the clouds get their water?

Prudence. Out of the sea.

Matthew. What may we learn from that?

Prudence. That ministers should fetch their teaching from God.

Matthew. Why do they empty themselves out on the earth?

Prudence. To show that ministers should give out to the world what they know of God.

Matthew. Why is the rainbow caused by the sun?

Prudence. To show that the covenant of God's grace is confirmed to us in Christ.

Matthew. Why do the springs come to us from the sea, through the earth?

Prudence. To show that the grace of God comes to us through the body of Christ.

Matthew. Why do some of the springs rise on the tops of high hills?

Prudence. To show that the spirit of grace will spring up in some who are great and powerful, as well as in many who are poor and lowly.

Matthew. Why does the fire take hold on the candlewick?

Prudence. To show that unless grace catches light on the heart, there will be no true light of life in us.

Matthew. Why are the wick and the tallow and everything used up to keep the candle burning?

Prudence. To show that body and soul and everything should be at the service of, and should spend themselves to keep in good condition, that grace of God which is in us.

Matthew. Why does the pelican pierce her own breast with her beak?

Prudence. To feed her young with her own blood, and by that to show that Christ the blessed one so loves his young, his people, as to save them from death with his blood.

Matthew. What may one learn from hearing the cock crow?

Prudence. Learn to remember Peter's sin and Peter's repentance. The cock's crowing also shows that day is coming on; so let the crowing of the cock put you in mind of that last and terrible day of judgment.

Now about this time their month was up; so they let those of the house know that it was time for them to be up and going. Then Joseph said to his mother: It would be a good idea if you did not forget to send to Mr. Interpreter's house, to ask him to grant that Mr. Great-heart may be sent to us, so that he can be our escort for the rest of the way.

Good boy, said she — I had almost forgotten.

So she drew up a petition and asked Mr. Watchful, the Porter, to send it by some suitable man to her good friend Mr. Interpreter. And when it had come and he had seen the contents of the petition, he said to the messenger: Go and tell them I will send him.

When the household where Christiana was saw that they meant to go on, they called the whole house together to give thanks to their King for sending them such profitable guests. And when that was done they said to Christiana: And shall we not show you something, as it is our custom to do with pilgrims, that you may think about when you are on the road?

So they took Christiana and her children and Mercy into the inner room and showed them one of the apples that Eve ate, and that she also gave to her husband, and for the eating of which they were both turned out of Paradise; and asked her what she thought it was.

Then Christiana said: It is food, or poison — I do not know which.

So they explained the matter to her, and she held up her hands in amazement.

Then they took her to a place and showed her Jacob's ladder. Now at that moment there were some angels going up it. So Christiana looked and looked to see the angels going up, and so did the rest of the company. Then they were about to go on to another place to show them something else, but James said to his mother: Please ask them to stay here a little longer, because this is a wonderful sight.

So they turned back, and stood feeding their eyes on such a lovely view.

After this they took them to a place where a golden anchor hung up; and they told Christiana to take it down, because, they said, you shall have it with you — you absolutely must have it, so that you may lay hold on what is within the veil and stand steady, in case you meet with stormy weather. So they were glad of it.

Then they took them to the mountain where Abraham our father offered up his son Isaac, and showed them the altar, the wood, the fire and the knife; because they are still to be seen there to this very day. When they had seen it, they lifted up their hands and blessed themselves and said: Oh, what a man Abraham was, for love of his Master and denial of himself!

After they had shown them all these things, Prudence took them into the dining room, where there stood a fine pair of virginals; and she played on them, and turned everything she had shown them into this excellent song:

	Eve's apple we have showed to you —
	of that one, be aware;
	you have seen Jacob's ladder too,
	and the angels going there.
	An anchor you've received as well;
	but let not these suffice
	until, with Abraham, you have given
	your best as sacrifice.

Now about this time somebody knocked at the door; so the Porter opened, and there was Mr. Great-heart. And when he had come in, what joy there was! Because it all came fresh into their minds again, how only a little while ago he had killed old Grim Bloody-man the giant and delivered them from the lions.

Then Mr. Great-heart said to Christiana and to Mercy: My Lord has sent each of you a bottle of wine, and some parched corn as well, together with a couple of pomegranates. He has also sent the boys some figs and raisins, to refresh you on your road.

Then they set themselves to their journey, and Prudence and Piety went along with them. When they came to the gate, Christiana asked the Porter whether anybody had gone by lately. He said no — only one, some time ago, who also told me that there had lately been a great robbery on the King's highway further on. But, he said, the thieves have been caught, and will shortly be tried for their lives.

Then Christiana and Mercy were afraid; but Matthew said: Mother, fear nothing, as long as Mr. Great-heart is going with us and is our escort.

Then Christiana said to the Porter: Sir, I am very much obliged to you for all the kindnesses you have shown me since I came here, and for being so loving and kind to my children. I do not know how to repay your kindness; so please, as a token of my respect for you, accept this small offering.

So she put a gold coin into his hand; and he made her a low bow and said: Let your clothing be always white, and let your head lack no oil. Let Mercy live and not die, and let her works not be few.

And to the boys he said: Run from the appetites of youth, and follow after godliness in the company of those who are serious and wise; so you will put gladness into your mother's heart, and win the praise of everybody who is sober-minded.

So they thanked the Porter and left.

Now I saw in my dream that they went on until they had come to the brow of the hill; where Piety, suddenly remembering, cried out: Oh dear — I have forgotten what I meant to give Christiana and her companions. I will go back and fetch it.

So she ran and fetched it. And while she was gone, Christiana thought she heard, in a grove a little way off on the right hand, the most exquisite melodious note, with words something like these:

	Through all my life your favour is
	so freely shown to me,
	that in your house for evermore
	my dwelling-place shall be.

And still listening, she thought she heard another answer it, saying:

	And why? The Lord our God is good,
	his mercy is for ever sure;
	his truth at all times firmly stood,
	and shall from age to age endure.

So Christiana asked Prudence what it was that made those exquisite notes.

They are, said she, our country birds. They only sing these notes now and then, except in the spring, when the flowers come out and the sun shines warm; and then you may hear them all day long. I often go out to listen to them, said she; and we often keep them tame in the house too. They are very fine company for us when we are low, and they make the woods and groves and lonely places into places you want to be.

By this time Piety had come back; so she said to Christiana: Look here — I have brought you an outline of all the things you have seen at our house, which you can look at when you find yourself forgetting, and so call those things back to mind for your building up and your comfort.

The Valley of Humiliation

Now they began to go down the hill into the Valley of Humiliation. It was a steep hill and the path was slippery; but they were very careful, so they got down well enough.

When they were down in the Valley, Piety said to Christiana: This is the place where Christian your husband met with the foul fiend Apollyon, and where they had that dreadful fight. You cannot but have heard about it. But take courage — as long as you have Mr. Great-heart here to be your guide and escort, we hope you will fare the better for it.

So when these two had committed the Pilgrims to their guide's care, he went on ahead and they followed after.

Great-heart. Then Mr. Great-heart said: We need not be so afraid of this Valley, because there is nothing here to hurt us unless we bring it on ourselves. It is true that Christian met Apollyon here, and had a hard fight with him; but that fight was the fruit of the slips he made coming down the hill. Because those who slip up there must expect fights down here. And that is how this Valley has got such a bad name. Because the ordinary people, when they hear that some frightening thing has happened to somebody in a certain place, take the view that the place is haunted by some foul fiend or evil spirit — when in fact it is on account of their own doing that such things happen to them there.

This Valley of Humiliation is in itself as fruitful a place as any the crow flies over. And I am convinced that if we could hit on it, we might find something hereabouts that would give us an account of why Christian was so hard beset in this place.

Then James said to his mother: Look, over there is a pillar, and it seems to have something written on it. Let us go and see what it is.

So they went, and found written there: "Let Christian's slips, before he came here, and the battles he met with in this place, be a warning to those who come after."

There, said their guide — did I not tell you there was something round here that would give a hint as to why Christian was so hard beset in this place?

Then turning to Christiana, he said: This is no disgrace to Christian, any more than to a great many others whose luck and lot were the same as his; because it is easier going up this hill than down it, and that can be said of very few hills anywhere in the world. But we will leave the good man; he is at rest, and he had a splendid victory over his enemy. May the one who lives above grant that we fare no worse than he did, when we come to be tested.

But let us come back to this Valley of Humiliation. It is the best and most useful piece of ground in all these parts. It is rich soil, and, as you see, consists mostly of meadow; and if a man came here in the summer, as we are doing now, and knew nothing about it beforehand, and took pleasure in what he could see, he would find plenty here to delight him. Look how green this Valley is, and how beautiful with lilies. I have known many working men, too, who have made good estates in this Valley of Humiliation — "because God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble" — because it really is very fruitful soil, and brings forth by handfuls.

Some have even wished that the nearest way to their Father's house ran through here, so that they would be troubled no more with hills or mountains to climb. But the way is the way, and there is an end of it.

Now as they were going along and talking, they spotted a boy minding his father's sheep. The boy was in very poor clothes, but had a fresh and handsome face; and as he sat there by himself, he sang.

Listen, said Mr. Great-heart, to what the shepherd's boy is saying. So they listened, and he said:

	He that is down needs fear no fall;
	he that is low, no pride;
	he that is humble ever shall
	have God to be his guide.
	I am content with what I have,
	little be it or much;
	and, Lord, contentment still I crave,
	because you save all such.
	Fullness to such is a burden
	as go on pilgrimage;
	here little, and hereafter bliss,
	is best from age to age.

Then the guide said: Do you hear him? I will venture to say that this boy lives a merrier life, and carries more of that herb called heart's-ease in his chest, than the man dressed in silk and velvet. But let us go on with what we were saying.

In this Valley our Lord used to have his country house. He loved to be here. He loved to walk in these meadows too, because he found the air pleasant. Besides which, here a man is free from the noise and the rushing about of this life. Every other place is full of noise and confusion; only the Valley of Humiliation is empty and quiet. Here a man will not be hindered and held back in his thinking, as he is apt to be elsewhere. This is a Valley that nobody walks in but those who love a pilgrim's life. And although Christian had the bad luck to meet Apollyon here and to have a brisk encounter with him, still, I have to tell you that in earlier times men have met angels here, have found pearls here, and have found the words of life in this place.

Did I say our Lord had his country house here in the old days, and that he loved to walk here? I will add this: that in this place, and to the people who live and go about these grounds, he has left a yearly income, to be faithfully paid them at set seasons, for their keep along the way and for their further encouragement to go on with their pilgrimage.

Samuel. Now as they went on, Samuel said to Mr. Great-heart: Sir, I gather it was in this Valley that my father and Apollyon had their battle. But whereabouts was the fight? Because I can see this Valley is a large one.

Great-heart. Your father had that battle with Apollyon at a place ahead of us, in a narrow passage just beyond Forgetful Green. And that place really is the most dangerous spot in all these parts. Because if at any time pilgrims run into a hard knock, it is when they forget what favours they have received and how little they deserve them. This is the place where others have been hard pressed as well. But more about the place when we come to it; because I am fairly sure that to this day there is either some sign of the battle left, or some monument to testify that such a battle was fought there.

Mercy. Then Mercy said: I think I am as well in this Valley as I have been anywhere else in the whole of our journey. It seems to me the place suits my spirit. I love being in places like this, where there is no rattling of coaches or rumbling of wheels. It seems to me that here one may, without much disturbance, think about what one is, and where one came from, and what one has done, and what the King has called one to. Here one may think, and break at heart, and melt in one's spirit, until one's eyes become like "the fishpools of Heshbon." Those who go rightly through this Valley of Weeping make it a well, and the rain that God sends down from heaven on those who are here fills the pools too. This is the Valley out of which the King will give his people their vineyards; and those who go through it shall sing, as Christian did, for all that he met with Apollyon.

Great-heart. It is true, said their guide. I have gone through this Valley many a time, and I was never better than when I was here. I have also been an escort to a number of pilgrims, and they have said the same. "To this man will I look," says the King, "even to him who is poor and of a contrite spirit, and trembles at my Word."

Now they came to the place where the battle we spoke of was fought. Then the guide said to Christiana, her children and Mercy: This is the place. On this ground Christian stood, and up there came Apollyon against him. And look — did I not tell you? Here is some of your husband's blood on these stones to this day. See too how here and there you can still make out on the ground some of the splinters of Apollyon's broken darts. See how they beat the ground with their feet as they fought, each holding his position against the other; and how with their glancing blows they split the very stones to pieces.

Christian really did play the man here, and showed himself as tough as Hercules himself would have been if he had been standing there. When Apollyon was beaten, he made his retreat into the next Valley, which is called the Valley of the Shadow of Death, and which we shall come to shortly.

Look — over there stands a monument as well, with this battle engraved on it, and Christian's victory, to his fame through all the ages.

So, because it stood right by the roadside in front of them, they stepped over to it and read the writing, which word for word was this:

	Close by here a battle was fought,
	most strange, and yet most true;
	Christian and Apollyon sought
	each other to subdue.
	The man so bravely played the man,
	he made the fiend to fly;
	of which a monument I stand,
	the same to testify.

When they had gone past this place, they came to the borders of the Shadow of Death. And this Valley was longer than the other, and a place most strangely haunted with evil things, as a great many can testify. But these women and children got through it the better, because they had daylight, and because Mr. Great-heart was their escort.

When they had entered this Valley, they thought they heard a groaning, like the groaning of dead men — a very great groaning. They thought too that they could hear words of lamentation spoken, as of people in the last extremes of torment. These things made the boys quake, and the women looked pale and drawn; but their guide told them to take comfort.

So they went on a little further, and they thought they felt the ground begin to shake under them, as if there were some hollow place there. They heard a kind of hissing too, like snakes — but nothing had appeared as yet.

Then the boys said: Are we not at the end of this dismal place yet?

But again the guide told them to take courage, and to watch their feet — in case, he said, you get caught in some trap.

Now James began to feel sick; but I think the cause of it was fear. So his mother gave him some of that glass of cordial that had been given her at the Interpreter's house, and three of the pills that Mr. Skill had prepared, and the boy began to revive.

So they went on, until they came to about the middle of the Valley; and then Christiana said: I think I see something over there on the road ahead of us — a thing of such a shape as I have never seen.

Joseph. Then Joseph said: Mother, what is it?

An ugly thing, child; an ugly thing, said she.

But, mother, what is it like? said he.

It is like I cannot tell you what, said she. And by now it was only a little way off; and then she said: It is close.

Well, well, said Mr. Great-heart. Let those who are most afraid keep close to me.

So the fiend came on, and their escort went to meet it; but just as it reached him, it vanished from all their sight. Then they remembered what had been said some time ago: "Resist the devil, and he will flee from you."

So they went on, feeling a little refreshed. But they had not gone far before Mercy, looking behind her, saw something she thought most like a lion; and it came after them at a great padding pace, and it had a hollow roaring voice; and at every roar it gave, it made the whole Valley echo, and made their hearts ache — all except the heart of their guide.

So it came up; and Mr. Great-heart went behind and put all the Pilgrims in front of him. The lion came on fast too, and Mr. Great-heart set himself to give it battle. But when it saw that resistance was going to be made, it also drew back, and came no further.

Then they went on again, with their escort walking ahead of them, until they came to a place where a pit had been dug the whole width of the road; and before they could get ready to cross it, a great mist and darkness came down on them, so that they could not see.

Then the Pilgrims said: Oh dear — now what are we to do?

But their guide answered: Do not be afraid. Stand still, and see what end will be put to this as well.

So they waited there, because their path was blocked. And they thought too that they could hear the noise and rushing of their enemies more clearly, and that the fire and the smoke of the pit were much easier to make out.

Then Christiana said to Mercy: Now I see what my poor husband went through. I have heard a great deal about this place, but I was never here until now. Poor man — he went through here all alone in the night; he had night almost the whole way. And these fiends were busy about him too, as if they meant to tear him to pieces. Many have spoken of it, but nobody can tell what the Valley of the Shadow of Death means until they are in it themselves. "The heart knows its own bitterness, and a stranger does not share its joy." To be here is a fearful thing.

Great-heart. This is like doing business in great waters, or going down into the deep; this is like being in the heart of the sea, and going down to the roots of the mountains. Now it feels as though the earth with its bars were closed round us for ever. But let those who walk in darkness and have no light trust in the name of the Lord and lean on their God.

For my part, as I have told you already, I have often gone through this Valley, and have been far harder pressed than I am now — and yet you can see I am alive. I would not boast, because I am not my own saviour; but I trust we shall have a good deliverance. Come, let us pray for light to the one who can lighten our darkness, and who can rebuke not only these but all the Satans in hell.

So they cried out and prayed; and God sent light and deliverance, so that now there was nothing blocking their way — not even where they had just been stopped by a pit.

Still, they were not through the Valley yet; so they went on. And now there were great stinks and loathsome smells, greatly to their disgust.

Then Mercy said to Christiana: There is not such pleasant being here as at the gate, or at the Interpreter's, or at the house where we slept last night.

Oh, but, said one of the boys, it is not so bad to go through here as it would be to live here always. And for all I know, one reason we have to go this way to the house prepared for us is so that our home may be all the sweeter to us.

Well said, Samuel, said the guide — you have spoken now like a man.

Why, if ever I get out of here again, said the boy, I think I shall value light and a good road better than ever I did in all my life.

Then the guide said: We shall be out before long.

So on they went; and Joseph said: Can we not see the end of this Valley yet?

Then the guide said: Watch your feet, because you will be among the traps in a moment.

So they watched their feet and went on; but they were much troubled by the traps. Now when they had got in among them, they spotted a man thrown into the ditch on the left-hand side, with his flesh all ripped and torn.

Then the guide said: That is one Heedless, who was going this way. He has lain there a great while. There was one Take-heed with him when he was caught and killed, but he escaped their hands. You cannot imagine how many are killed hereabouts — and yet men are foolhardy enough to set out lightly on pilgrimage, and to come without a guide. Poor Christian! It was a wonder he escaped here. But he was loved by his God; and he had a good heart of his own, too, or he could never have done it.

Now they were coming towards the end of the road; and just where Christian had seen the cave when he went past, out came a giant named Maul. This Maul used to ruin young pilgrims with clever false arguments; and he called Great-heart by name and said to him: How many times have you been forbidden to do these things?

Then Mr. Great-heart said: What things?

What things! said the giant. You know what things. But I will put an end to your trade.

But tell me, said Mr. Great-heart — before we set to, let us understand what we have to fight about.

Now the women and children stood trembling, and did not know what to do.

Said the giant: You rob the country, and you rob it with the worst kind of theft.

Those are only generalities, said Mr. Great-heart. Come to particulars, man.

Then the giant said: You practise the trade of a kidnapper. You gather up women and children and carry them off into a strange country, to the weakening of my master's kingdom.

But now Great-heart replied: I am a servant of the God of heaven. My business is to persuade sinners to repent. I am commanded to do my best to turn men, women and children "from darkness to light, and from the power of Satan to God." And if that is really the ground of your quarrel, then let us set to as soon as you like.

Then the giant came on, and Mr. Great-heart went to meet him; and as he went he drew his sword, but the giant had a club. So without any more ado they set to; and at the first blow the giant knocked Mr. Great-heart down onto one knee. At that the women and children cried out. So Mr. Great-heart recovered himself and laid about him in a thoroughly vigorous way, and gave the giant a wound in the arm.

He fought like this for the space of an hour, at such a pitch of heat that the breath came out of the giant's nostrils the way steam comes out of a boiling cauldron.

Then they sat down to rest; but Mr. Great-heart took himself to prayer, while the women and children did nothing but sigh and cry the whole time the battle lasted.

When they had rested and got their breath back, they both set to it again; and Mr. Great-heart with a full blow brought the giant down to the ground.

No, hold on — let me get up, said the giant. So Mr. Great-heart fairly let him get to his feet.

So at it they went again, and the giant came within very little of smashing Mr. Great-heart's skull altogether with his club.

Mr. Great-heart, seeing that, ran at him in the full heat of his spirit and pierced him under the fifth rib; and with that the giant began to weaken and could hold his club up no longer. Then Mr. Great-heart followed up his blow and struck the giant's head from his shoulders.

Then the women and children rejoiced, and Mr. Great-heart praised God too for the deliverance he had worked. When this was done, they put up a pillar among them and fastened the giant's head on it, and wrote underneath in letters that passers-by could read:

	He that wore this head was one
	who did the pilgrims misuse;
	he stopped their way, he spared none,
	but did them all abuse —
	until I, Great-heart, rose to be
	the pilgrims' guide, and stood
	to oppose him, who was he
	that was their enemy.

The Valley of the Shadow, and Old Honest

Now I saw that they went to the rise a little further on, which had been thrown up as a lookout for pilgrims — the place from which Christian first caught sight of his brother Faithful. So here they sat down and rested, and here too they ate and drank and made merry, because they had been delivered from such a dangerous enemy.

As they sat there eating, Christiana asked the guide whether he had taken any hurt in the battle.

Then Mr. Great-heart said: No, apart from a little on my flesh. And even that is so far from doing me any harm that it is proof at present of my love for my Master and for you, and by grace it will be a means of increasing my reward at the end.

Christiana. But were you not afraid, good sir, when you saw him come out with his club?

Great-heart. It is my duty, said he, to distrust my own ability, so that I may rely on the one who is stronger than all.

Christiana. But what did you think when he knocked you down to the ground at the first blow?

Great-heart. Why, I thought, said he, that my Master himself was served the same way — and yet it was he who conquered in the end.

Matthew. Whatever the rest of you think, I think God has been wonderfully good to us, both in bringing us out of this Valley and in delivering us out of this enemy's hands. For my part, I see no reason why we should distrust our God any more, since he has now, and in such a place as this, given us such proof of his love.

Then they got up and went on. Now a little ahead of them stood an oak tree; and under it, when they came to it, they found an old pilgrim fast asleep. They knew he was a pilgrim by his clothes, and his staff, and his belt.

So the guide, Mr. Great-heart, woke him; and the old gentleman, as he opened his eyes, cried out: What is the matter? Who are you? And what is your business here?

Great-heart. Come, man, do not be so hot. There is nobody here but friends.

Still, the old man got up and stood on his guard, and would know from them who they were. Then the guide said: My name is Great-heart. I am the guide of these Pilgrims, who are going to the Celestial Country.

Honest. Then Mr. Honest said: I beg your pardon. I was afraid you were of that company who some time ago robbed Little-faith of his money. But now that I look about me better, I can see you are honester people than that.

Great-heart. Why — what would you have done, or what could you have done, to help yourself, if we really had been of that company?

Honest. Done! Why, I would have fought as long as there was breath in me. And if I had, I am sure you could never have had the better of me; because a Christian can never be overcome unless he gives in of his own accord.

Great-heart. Well said, father Honest, said the guide. Because by this I know you are a bird of the right feather, since you have spoken the truth.

Honest. And by this I know as well that you understand what true pilgrimage is; because everybody else thinks we are the easiest of all people to overcome.

Great-heart. Well, now that we have met so happily, let me ask your name, and the name of the place you came from.

Honest. My name I cannot tell you. But I came from the town of Stupidity; it lies about four degrees beyond the City of Destruction.

Great-heart. Oh — are you from that country? I think I can half guess who you are. Your name is Old Honesty, is it not?

So the old gentleman blushed and said: Not Honesty in the abstract; Honest is my name. And I hope my nature may agree with what I am called.

Honest. But, sir, said the old gentleman, how could you guess that I was that sort of man, given that I came from such a place?

Great-heart. I had heard of you before, from my Master; because he knows everything that is done on the earth. But I have often wondered that anybody should come out of your town, because it is worse than the City of Destruction itself.

Honest. Yes — we lie further from the sun, and so we are colder and duller. But if a man were standing in a mountain of ice, still, if the Sun of Righteousness rises on him, his frozen heart will feel a thaw. And that is how it has been with me.

Great-heart. I believe it, father Honest, I believe it; because I know the thing is true.

Then the old gentleman greeted all the Pilgrims with a holy kiss of love, and asked them their names, and how they had got on since they set out on their pilgrimage.

Christiana. Then Christiana said: My name I suppose you have heard. Good Christian was my husband, and these four are his children.

But you cannot imagine how the old gentleman was struck when she told him who she was. He skipped, he smiled, he blessed them with a thousand good wishes, saying:

Honest. I have heard a great deal about your husband, and about his travels and the wars he went through in his day. Let it be said for your comfort: your husband's name rings all over these parts of the world. His faith, his courage, his endurance and his sincerity through it all have made his name famous.

Then he turned to the boys and asked them their names, which they told him. And then he said to them: Matthew, be like Matthew the tax-collector — not in his vice but in his virtue. Samuel, said he, be like Samuel the prophet, a man of faith and prayer. Joseph, said he, be like Joseph in Potiphar's house: chaste, and a man who runs from temptation. And James, be like James the Just, and like James the brother of our Lord.

Then they told him about Mercy, and how she had left her town and her family to come along with Christiana and her sons. At which the honest old man said: Mercy is your name; by mercy you shall be held up, and carried through all the difficulties that will come at you on your road, until you come to the place where you shall look the Fountain of Mercy in the face with comfort.

All this while the guide, Mr. Great-heart, was very much pleased, and smiled at his companion.

Now as they walked along together, the guide asked the old gentleman whether he had known a certain Mr. Fearing, who came on pilgrimage from his part of the country.

Honest. Yes, very well, said he. He was a man who had the root of the matter in him — but he was one of the most troublesome pilgrims I ever met with in all my days.

Great-heart. I can see you knew him, because you have given a very accurate description of him.

Honest. Knew him! I was a great companion of his. I was with him nearly all the time. When he first began to think about what was coming on us hereafter, I was with him.

Great-heart. I was his guide from my Master's house to the gates of the Celestial City.

Honest. Then you knew he was a troublesome one.

Great-heart. I did; but I could put up with it well enough, because men of my calling are often entrusted with escorting people like him.

Honest. Well then, let us hear a little about him, and how he conducted himself under your escort.

Great-heart. Why — he was always afraid he would fall short of the place he so much wanted to get to. Everything frightened him that he heard anybody mention that had the least appearance of opposition in it. I hear that he lay roaring at the Slough of Despond for about a month together; and he did not dare venture across, even though he saw several go over ahead of him, and many of them offered him a hand. But he would not go back either. He said he would die if he never reached the Celestial City — and yet he was cast down by every difficulty and tripped over every straw that anybody threw in his path.

Well, after he had lain at the Slough of Despond a great while, as I say, one sunny morning — I do not know how — he ventured it, and so got over. But once he was over he could hardly believe it. He had, I think, a Slough of Despond in his own mind, a slough he carried about with him everywhere; or he could never have been as he was.

So he came up to the gate — you know the one I mean, that stands at the head of this road — and there again he stood a good while before he would risk knocking. When the gate was opened he would draw back and give way to others, and say he was not worthy. Because for all that he had got to the gate ahead of some of them, still, a good many of them went in before him. There the poor man would stand, shaking and shrinking. I dare say it would have made anybody's heart ache to see him. And still he would not go back.

At last he took the hammer that hung by the gate in his hand and gave one or two small taps. Then One opened to him — but he shrank back as before. The one who opened stepped out after him and said: Trembling man, what do you want? At that he fell down to the ground. The one who spoke to him was astonished to see him so faint. So he said to him: Peace be to you. Get up, because I have set the door open for you. Come in, because you are blessed. With that he got up and went in trembling; and once he was in, he was ashamed to show his face.

Well, after he had been entertained there a while, as you know is the custom, he was told to go on his way, and told the road he should take. So on he came, until he came to our house. But he behaved at my Master the Interpreter's door exactly as he had done at the gate. He lay about outside in the cold a good while before he would risk calling — and yet he would not go back, and the nights were long and cold then.

Indeed, he had a note of his necessity in his coat, addressed to my Master, asking him to receive him and grant him the comfort of his house, and to allow him a strong and valiant escort as well, because he was such a chicken-hearted man himself. And even so, he was afraid to call at the door. So he lay about the place until, poor man, he was nearly starved. Yes — such was his low state that although he saw several others get in by knocking, he was still afraid to risk it.

At last, I think, I looked out of the window and noticed a man going up and down outside the door; so I went out to him and asked who he was. But, poor man, the tears stood in his eyes, so I understood what he wanted. So I went in and told them in the house, and we put the matter to our Lord. So he sent me out again to beg him to come in; but I can tell you I had hard work to manage it.

At last he came in. And I will say this for my Lord: he treated him wonderfully lovingly. There were only a few good pieces on the table, but some of them were laid on his plate. Then he presented his note, and my Lord looked at it and said his request should be granted. So when he had been there a good while, he seemed to get a little heart, and to be a little more comfortable. Because my Master, you must understand, is a man of very tender feeling, and especially towards those who are afraid; so he treated him in whatever way would most encourage him.

Well — when he had seen the things of the place and was ready to take his journey to the city, my Lord, as he had done for Christian before, gave him a bottle of cordial and some good things to eat. So we set off, and I walked ahead of him; but the man was a man of very few words. He only sighed aloud.

When we came to the place where the three fellows were hanged, he said he was afraid that would be his end too. He only seemed glad when he saw the Cross and the Tomb. There, I admit, he wanted to stay a little and look; and for a while afterwards he seemed a little cheerful.

When we came to the Hill Difficulty he made no fuss about that, and he was not much afraid of the lions either; because you have to understand that his trouble was not about that kind of thing. His fear was about whether he would be accepted at the end.

I got him into the House Beautiful, I think, before he was willing to go. And once he was in I introduced him to the young women of the place; but he was ashamed to make much of himself in company. He wanted very much to be alone — and yet he always loved good conversation, and would often get behind the screen to listen to it. He also loved seeing ancient things, and turning them over in his mind. He told me afterwards that he loved being in those two houses he had last come from — the gate, and the Interpreter's — but that he had not dared to be so bold as to ask.

And when we left the House Beautiful and he went down the hill into the Valley of Humiliation, he went down as well as ever I saw a man do in my life; because he did not care how low he was, so long as he might be happy at the end. Yes — I think there was a kind of sympathy between that valley and him, because I never saw him better in his whole pilgrimage than when he was in that valley.

Here he would lie down and hug the ground and kiss the very flowers that grew in the valley. He would be up now every morning at daybreak, walking and tracing to and fro about the place.

But when he came to the entrance of the Valley of the Shadow of Death, I thought I was going to lose my man. Not because he had any inclination to go back — he always hated that — but because he was ready to die of fright. Oh, the hobgoblins will have me! The hobgoblins will have me! he cried; and I could not get him off it. He made such a noise, such an outcry here, that if they had only heard him it would have been enough to encourage them to come and fall on us.

But this I noticed particularly: that this valley was as quiet the whole time he was going through it as I have ever known it, before or since. I suppose these enemies here had a special check laid on them from our Lord, and an order not to interfere until Mr. Fearing was past.

Vanity Town

It would take too long to tell you everything, so we will only mention a passage or two more. When he came to Vanity Fair, I thought he would have fought every man at the fair. I was afraid the two of us would be knocked on the head there, he was so hot against their follies. On the Enchanted Ground, too, he was very wide awake. But when he came to the river, where there was no bridge, then he was in a bad way again. Now, now, he said, he would be drowned for ever, and would never see with any comfort that Face he had come so many miles to look on.

And here I noticed something very remarkable: the water of that river was lower at that moment than I have ever seen it in my life. So he went over at last, hardly getting his feet wet. When he was going up to the gate, I began to take my leave of him and to wish him a good reception up there. And he said: I shall have it, I shall. Then we parted, and I never saw him again.

Honest. Then it seems he was well at last.

Great-heart. Yes, yes. I never had any doubt about him. He was a man of a choice spirit; only he was always kept very low, and that made his life so much of a burden to himself and so troublesome to others. He was, above most men, sensitive about sin. He was so afraid of doing others an injury that he would often deny himself things that were perfectly lawful, because he did not want to give offence.

Honest. But what should be the reason that such a good man should be so much in the dark all his days?

Great-heart. There are two sorts of reason for it. One is that the wise God will have it so: some must pipe and some must weep. Now Mr. Fearing was one who played the bass line. He and his fellows sound the sackbut, whose notes are more mournful than the notes of other instruments — though some say that the bass is the foundation of music. And for my part, I care nothing at all for a profession of faith that does not begin in heaviness of mind. The first string a musician usually touches is the bass, when he means to put everything in tune. God also plays this string first, when he is tuning the soul for himself. Only here was Mr. Fearing's imperfection: he could play no other music but this, until towards the very end.

I make bold to talk in this figurative way, to sharpen the wits of young readers; and because in the book of Revelation the saved are compared to a company of musicians who play on their trumpets and harps and sing their songs before the throne.

Honest. He was a very zealous man, as anyone can see from the account you have given of him. Difficulties, lions, Vanity Fair — he was not afraid of any of them. It was only sin and death and hell that were a terror to him, because he had some doubts about his own share in that celestial country.

Great-heart. You are right. Those were the things that troubled him; and they arose, as you have rightly observed, from a weakness in his mind on that particular point, not from any weakness of spirit as to the practical part of a pilgrim's life. I honestly believe that, as the proverb goes, he could have bitten a firebrand in two if it had stood in his way; but the things that weighed him down are things no man has ever yet shaken off easily.

Christiana. Then Christiana said: This account of Mr. Fearing has done me good. I thought nobody had been like me; but I can see there was some likeness between this good man and me. Only we differed in two things. His troubles were so great that they broke out; mine I kept inside. And his lay so heavy on him that they stopped him from knocking at the houses provided for pilgrims to stay in — whereas my trouble was always the sort that made me knock the louder.

Mercy. If I may speak my heart too, I have to say that something of him has been in me as well; because I have always been more afraid of the lake, and of losing my place in Paradise, than I have been of losing anything else. Oh, I thought, if only I may have the happiness of a home there — that is enough, even if I part with the whole world to win it.

Matthew. Then Matthew said: Fear was one thing that made me think I was a long way from having in me what goes along with salvation. But if it was like that with such a good man as he was, why should it not turn out well for me too?

James. No fears, no grace, said James. Although there is not always grace where there is a fear of hell, still, there is certainly no grace where there is no fear of God.

Great-heart. Well said, James; you have hit the mark. Because the fear of God is the beginning of wisdom; and certainly those who lack the beginning have neither middle nor end. But we will end our talk about Mr. Fearing here, after we have sent this farewell after him:

	Well, Master Fearing, you did fear
	your God, and were afraid
	of doing anything, while here,
	by which you'd be betrayed.
	And did you fear the lake and pit?
	Would others did so too!
	Because as for those who want your wit,
	they their own selves undo.

Now I saw that they still went on talking; because after Mr. Great-heart had finished with Mr. Fearing, Mr. Honest began to tell them about another man, whose name was Mr. Self-will.

He claimed to be a pilgrim, said Mr. Honest, but I am convinced he never came in at the gate that stands at the head of the road.

Great-heart. Did you ever have any conversation with him about it?

Honest. Yes, more than once or twice. But he would always be like himself — self-willed. He cared nothing for people, or for argument, or for example either. Whatever his own mind prompted him to do, that he would do, and nothing could get him to do anything else.

Great-heart. Tell me — what principles did he hold? I suppose you can say.

Honest. He held that a man might follow the vices of the pilgrims as well as their virtues, and that if he did both he would certainly be saved.

Great-heart. What! If he had said it is possible for the heart to be guilty of the vices as well as to share in the virtues of pilgrims, he could not have been much blamed; because in fact we are not absolutely exempted from any vice, except on condition that we watch and struggle. But that, I gather, is not the point. If I understand you rightly, you mean that he was of the opinion that it was allowable to be like that.

Honest. Yes, yes, that is what I mean; and that is what he believed and practised.

Great-heart. But what grounds did he have for saying it?

Honest. Why, he said he had the Scripture for his warrant.

Great-heart. Please, Mr. Honest, give us a few particulars.

Honest. So I will. He said that having to do with other men's wives had been practised by David, God's beloved, and that therefore he could do it. He said that having more than one woman was a thing Solomon practised, and therefore he could do it. He said that Sarah and the godly midwives of Egypt lied, and so did Rahab who was saved, and therefore he could do it. He said the disciples went at their Master's bidding and took away the owner's donkey, and therefore he could do the same. He said that Jacob got his father's inheritance by trickery and pretence, and therefore he could do the same.

Great-heart. Utterly base! And you are sure he held this opinion?

Honest. I have heard him argue for it, bring Scripture for it, bring reasons for it, and so on.

Great-heart. An opinion that ought not to be allowed the smallest room anywhere in the world.

Honest. You must understand me correctly. He did not say that any man might do this — but that those who had the virtues of the men who did such things might do the same.

Great-heart. But what could be falser than that conclusion? Because this amounts to saying that because good men in the past have sinned out of weakness, therefore he has permission to do it deliberately. Or as though, because a child was blown over by a gust of wind, or stumbled on a stone and fell and dirtied itself in the mud, therefore he might deliberately lie down and wallow in it like a pig. Who could have thought anybody could be so far blinded by the power of his appetites? But what is written must be true: they "stumble at the Word, being disobedient — to which also they were appointed."

His supposition that people who give themselves up to godly men's vices may have those men's virtues is a delusion just as strong as the other. It is exactly as if a dog were to say, I have, or may have, the qualities of the child, because I lick up its filth. Eating up the sins of God's people is no sign of a man possessed of their virtues. Nor can I believe that anybody holding this opinion has, at that moment, either faith or love in him. But I know you have made strong objections to him. Tell me — what can he say for himself?

Honest. Why, he says that to do this as a matter of stated opinion seems a great deal more honest than to do it while holding the opposite opinion.

Great-heart. A very wicked answer. Because although letting the reins loose on our appetites while our opinions are against such things is bad, still, to sin and then argue for permission to do it is worse. The first trips up onlookers by accident; the second argues them into the trap.

Honest. There are many of this man's mind who have not got this man's mouth; and that is what makes going on pilgrimage as little regarded as it is.

Great-heart. You have spoken the truth, and it is to be lamented. But the man who fears the King of Paradise will come out of it all.

Christiana. There are strange opinions in the world. I knew one man who said it was time enough to repent when they came to die.

Great-heart. Such people are not very wise. That man would have been very unwilling, if he had been given a week to run twenty miles in for his life, to put that journey off to the last hour of the week.

Honest. You are right. And yet most of those who count themselves pilgrims really do just that. I am, as you see, an old man, and I have been a traveller on this road many a day, and I have taken notice of a great many things.

I have seen some who set out as though they would drive the whole world in front of them, and who yet within a few days died as those people died in the wilderness, and so never got a sight of the promised land.

I have seen some who promised nothing at all when they first set out to be pilgrims — men you would have thought could not last a day — who have yet turned out very good pilgrims.

I have seen some who ran hurrying forward and who then, after a little while, ran just as fast back again.

I have seen some who spoke very well of a pilgrim's life at first, and who after a while spoke just as much against it. I have heard some, when they first set out for Paradise, say positively that there is such a place — who, when they had almost got there, came back again and said there is none.

I have heard some boast about what they would do in case they were opposed, who at a mere false alarm have run for it, abandoning faith, the pilgrim's road and everything else.

Now, as they were going along like this, somebody came running to meet them and said: Gentlemen — and you of the weaker sort — if you value your lives, look out for yourselves, because the robbers are up ahead of you.

Great-heart. Then Mr. Great-heart said: They are the three who attacked Little-faith before now. Well, said he, we are ready for them.

So they went on their way. And they looked at every turn to see whether they would meet the villains; but whether they had heard about Mr. Great-heart, or whether they had other game in view, they never came up to the Pilgrims.

The House of Gaius

Christiana then said she wished there were an inn for herself and her children, because they were tired. So Mr. Honest said: There is one a little way ahead of us, where a very honourable disciple lives, one Gaius.

So they all agreed to turn in there, and all the more readily because the old gentleman gave him such a good report. So when they came to the door they went in without knocking, because people do not usually knock at the door of an inn. Then they called for the master of the house, and he came to them. So they asked whether they might sleep there that night.

Gaius. Yes, gentlemen, if you are honest men — because my house is for nobody but pilgrims.

Then Christiana and Mercy and the boys were all the more pleased, because the innkeeper was a lover of pilgrims. So they asked for rooms, and he showed them one for Christiana and her children and Mercy, and another for Mr. Great-heart and the old gentleman.

Great-heart. Then Mr. Great-heart said: Good Gaius, what have you got for supper? Because these pilgrims have come a long way today and are tired.

Gaius. It is late, said Gaius, so we cannot conveniently go out for food. But you shall be welcome to whatever we have, if that will content you.

Great-heart. We shall be content with whatever you have in the house; because I have tested you before, and you are never without what is needed.

Then he went down and spoke to the cook, whose name was Taste-that-which-is-good, to get supper ready for so many pilgrims. When this was done he came up again, saying: Come, my good friends, you are welcome to me, and I am glad I have a house to entertain you in. And while supper is getting ready, if you like, let us entertain one another with some good conversation.

So they all said: Agreed.

Gaius. Then Gaius said: Whose wife is this older woman? And whose daughter is this young one?

Great-heart. The woman is the wife of one Christian, a Pilgrim of former days, and these are his four children. The young woman is an acquaintance of hers, whom she has persuaded to come with her on pilgrimage. The boys all take after their father and are eager to walk in his footsteps. In fact, if they only see a place where the old Pilgrim lay down, or the print of his foot, it gives their hearts joy, and they want to lie or tread in the same spot.

Gaius. Then Gaius said: Is this Christian's wife? And are these Christian's children? I knew your husband's father, yes, and his father's father as well. There have been a great many good men of this stock; their ancestors first lived at Antioch. Christian's forebears — I suppose you have heard your husband speak of them — were very worthy men. Beyond anybody I know, they showed themselves men of great virtue and courage for the Lord of the Pilgrims, for his ways, and for those who loved him.

I have heard of many of your husband's relations who stood up under every trial for the sake of the truth. Stephen, who was one of the first of the family your husband sprang from, was knocked on the head with stones. James, another of that line, was killed with the edge of the sword. To say nothing of Paul and Peter, men of the old family your husband came from, there was Ignatius, who was thrown to the lions; Romanus, whose flesh was cut piece by piece off his bones; and Polycarp, who played the man in the fire. There was the one who was hung up in a basket in the sun for the wasps to eat, and the one they put into a sack and threw into the sea to drown. It would be completely impossible to count up all of that family who have suffered injury and death for the love of a pilgrim's life.

And I cannot help being glad to see that your husband has left four such boys behind him. I hope they will keep up their father's name and walk in their father's footsteps and come to their father's end.

Great-heart. Indeed, sir, they are promising lads. They seem to choose their father's ways with all their hearts.

Gaius. That is exactly what I said. So Christian's family is likely to spread out over the face of the earth still, and to be numerous on it. So let Christiana look out some young women for her sons to be betrothed to, so that their father's name and the house of his forebears may never be forgotten in the world.

Honest. It would be a pity if this family were to die out and be extinct.

Gaius. Fall it cannot; but be diminished it may. Let Christiana take my advice, though, and that is the way to keep it up.

And, Christiana, said the innkeeper, I am glad to see you and your friend Mercy here together — a lovely pair. And if I may advise you, take Mercy into a nearer relationship with you. If she is willing, let her be given to Matthew, your eldest son. That is the way to keep you a posterity on the earth.

So this match was agreed on, and in due course they were married; but more of that later.

Gaius went on, and said: I will now speak on behalf of women, to take away their reproach. Because just as death and the curse came into the world by a woman, so did life and health: "God sent forth his Son, made of a woman." Yes, and to show how much those who came after detested the mother's act, the women of the Old Testament longed for children, in the hope that this or that woman might turn out to be the mother of the Saviour of the world.
