I will say again that when the Saviour had come, women rejoiced in him before either man or angel did. I do not read that any man ever gave Christ so much as a coin; but the women followed him and supported him out of what they had. It was a woman who washed his feet with her tears, and a woman who anointed his body for burial. It was women who wept when he was going to the Cross, and women who followed him from the Cross, and who sat by his tomb when he was buried. It was women who were first with him on the morning of his resurrection, and women who first brought his disciples the news that he was risen from the dead. Women, then, are highly favoured, and show by all this that they share with us in the grace of life.

Now the cook sent up to say that supper was nearly ready, and sent somebody to lay the cloth and the plates, and to set the salt and the bread in order.

Then Matthew said: The sight of this cloth, and of this forerunner of the supper, gives me a keener appetite for my food than I had before.

Gaius. Then let everything in this life that ministers teaching to you give you a keener desire to sit at the supper of the great King in his kingdom. Because all the preaching and the books and the services here are only the laying of the plates and the setting of the salt on the table, compared with the feast our Lord will make for us when we come to his house.

So supper came up. And first a shoulder and a breast of meat were set on the table in front of them, to show that they must begin their meal with prayer and praise to God: the shoulder that David lifted his heart up to God with, and the breast where his heart lay, which he used to lean against his harp with when he played. These two dishes were very fresh and good, and they all ate heartily of them.

The next thing they brought up was a bottle of wine, red as blood. So Gaius said to them: Drink freely — this is the juice of the true vine, that gladdens the heart of God and of man. So they drank and were merry.

The next was a dish of milk with bread crumbled into it; but Gaius said: Let the boys have that, so that they may grow by it.

Then in due course they brought up a dish of butter and honey. Then Gaius said: Eat freely of this; because this is good for cheering up and strengthening your judgment and your understanding. This was our Lord's dish when he was a child: "Butter and honey shall he eat, that he may know to refuse the evil and choose the good."

Then they brought up a dish of apples, and they were very good-tasting fruit. Then Matthew said: May we eat apples, since apples were what the serpent tricked our first mother with?

Then Gaius said:

	Apples they were, with which we were beguiled;
	yet sin, not apples, has our souls defiled.
	Apples forbidden, eaten, taint the blood;
	to eat them when commanded does us good.
	Drink of his flagons then, you church, his dove,
	and eat his apples, you who are sick of love.

Then Matthew said: I raised the objection because a while ago I was made ill by eating fruit.

Gaius. Forbidden fruit will make you ill; but not what our Lord has allowed.

While they were talking like this, another dish was set in front of them, and it was a dish of nuts. Then some at the table said: Nuts spoil tender teeth, and especially children's teeth. Which when Gaius heard, he said:

	Hard texts are nuts — I will not call them cheats —
	whose shells keep back their kernels from the eaters.
	Open the shells, then, and you'll have the meat;
	they're brought here for you to crack and eat.

Then they were very merry, and sat at the table a long time talking about many things. Then the old gentleman said: My good landlord, while we are cracking your nuts, will you please open this riddle for us?

	There was a man, though some men called him mad:
	the more he threw away, the more he had.

Then they all listened carefully, wondering what good Gaius would say. So he sat still for a while, and then answered:

	The man who gives his goods away to the poor
	shall have as much again, and ten times more.

Then Joseph said: I must say, sir, I did not think you would find it out.

Oh, said Gaius, I have been trained up in this way a long while. Nothing teaches like experience. I have learned from my Lord to be kind, and I have found by experience that I have gained by it. "There is a man who scatters, and yet increases; and there is a man who withholds more than he ought, and it leads him to poverty." "There is a man who makes himself rich and yet has nothing; and there is a man who makes himself poor and yet has great riches."

Then Samuel whispered to Christiana his mother, and said: Mother, this is a very good man's house. Let us stay here a good while, and let my brother Matthew be married to Mercy here before we go any further.

Which Gaius the host overheard, and said: With a very good will, my child.

So they stayed there more than a month, and Mercy was given to Matthew as his wife.

While they stayed here, Mercy, as her custom was, would be making coats and clothes to give away to the poor, by which she brought a very good name on the Pilgrims.

But to come back to our story. After supper the lads asked for a bed, because they were worn out with travelling. So Gaius called for somebody to show them their room; but Mercy said: I will put them to bed. So she took them up to bed, and they slept well.

But the rest sat up all night, because Gaius and they were such congenial company that they could not tell how to part. Then after a great deal of talk about their Lord, and themselves, and their journey, old Mr. Honest — the one who had put the riddle to Gaius — began to nod off.

Then Great-heart said: What, sir, are you getting drowsy? Come, wake yourself up; here is a riddle for you.

Then Mr. Honest said: Let us hear it.

Then Mr. Great-heart said:

	He that will kill must first be overcome;
	who would live abroad must first die at home.

Ha, said Mr. Honest, that is a hard one — hard to explain and harder to practise. But come, landlord, said he, I will leave my part to you, if you like. You explain it, and I will hear what you say.

No, said Gaius; it was put to you, and you are expected to answer it.

Then the old gentleman said:

	He first by grace must conquered be
	who would his sin put by;
	and whoever lives and would convince me
	must to himself first die.

That is right, said Gaius. Good doctrine and experience both teach this. Because, first, until grace shows itself and overcomes the soul with its glory, the soul has no heart at all to oppose sin. Besides — if sin is Satan's rope, by which the soul lies tied up, how could it put up any resistance before it is loosed from that weakness?

Second, nobody who knows anything of either reason or grace will believe that a man can be a living monument of grace while he is a slave to his own corruptions.

And now that it comes into my mind, I will tell you a story worth hearing. There were two men who went on pilgrimage: the one began when he was young, the other when he was old. The young man had strong corruptions to grapple with; the old man's had decayed along with the decay of his nature. The young man walked his road as steadily as the old one did, and was every bit as bright as he was. Now which of the two had his graces shining clearest, given that both seemed alike?

Honest. The young man's, without a doubt. Because whatever holds out against the greatest opposition gives the best proof that it is the strongest — especially when it also keeps pace with something that meets nothing like half as much opposition, as old age certainly does not.

Besides, I have noticed that old men have congratulated themselves on this mistake: taking the decay of their nature for a gracious victory over their corruptions, and so being apt to fool themselves. Old men who have grace really are best able to give advice to the young, because they have seen most of the emptiness of things. But even so, when an old man and a young one set out together, the young one has the advantage in showing the clearest evidence of a work of grace in him — even though the old man's corruptions are naturally the weaker.

So they sat talking until daybreak. Now when the household was up, Christiana asked her son James to read a chapter; so he read the fifty-third of Isaiah. When he had finished, Mr. Honest asked why the Saviour is said to come "out of a dry ground," and also that "he had no form or comeliness in him."

Great-heart. Then Mr. Great-heart said: To the first I answer that it is because the church of the Jews, which Christ came from, had by then lost nearly all the sap and spirit of religion. To the second I say that the words are spoken in the character of unbelievers — who, because they lack the eye that can see into our Prince's heart, therefore judge him by the plainness of his outside. Just like people who do not know that precious stones are covered over with a rough crust, and who, when they have found one, throw it away again because they have no idea what they have found, as men throw away a common stone.

Mr. Feeble-mind and Mr. Ready-to-halt

Well, said Gaius, now that you are here, and since I know Mr. Great-heart is good with his weapons, if you like we will walk out into the fields when we have refreshed ourselves, and see whether we can do any good. About a mile from here there is one Slay-good, a giant who does a great deal of harm on the King's highway in these parts; and I know roughly where his haunt is. He is master of a number of thieves. It would be a good thing if we could clear these parts of him.

So they agreed and went — Mr. Great-heart with his sword and helmet and shield, and the rest with spears and staffs. When they came to the place where he was, they found him with one Feeble-mind in his hands, whom his servants had brought to him, having caught him on the road. Now the giant was going through his pockets, meaning after that to pick his bones, because he was of the flesh-eating kind.

Well, as soon as he saw Mr. Great-heart and his friends at the mouth of his cave with their weapons, he demanded to know what they wanted.

Great-heart. We want you. Because we have come to avenge the quarrel of the many pilgrims you have killed after dragging them off the King's highway. So come out of your cave.

So he armed himself and came out; and to battle they went, and fought for more than an hour, and then stood still to get their breath.

Slay-good. Then the giant said: Why are you here on my ground?

Great-heart. To avenge the blood of pilgrims, as I told you before.

So they set to it again, and the giant made Mr. Great-heart give ground; but he came up again, and with a great heart he let fly so stoutly at the giant's head and sides that he made him drop his weapon out of his hand. So he struck him and killed him, and cut off his head and brought it away to the inn. He also took Feeble-mind the pilgrim and brought him back with him to his lodgings.

When they had got home, they showed the head to the household, and then set it up, as they had done with others before, as a warning to anybody who might try to do the same afterwards.

Then they asked Mr. Feeble-mind how he had fallen into his hands.

Feeble-mind. Then the poor man said: I am a sickly man, as you see. And because death used to knock at my door about once a day, I thought I should never be well at home; so I took up a pilgrim's life, and have travelled here from the town of Uncertain, where I and my father were born.

I am a man with no strength at all, either of body or of mind. But I would, if I could, spend my life on the pilgrim's road, even though I can only crawl along it. When I came to the gate at the head of the road, the Lord of that place received me freely. He raised no objection to my sickly looks or to my feeble mind, but gave me the things that were necessary for my journey and told me to hope to the end.

When I came to the house of the Interpreter I met with a great deal of kindness there; and because the Hill Difficulty was judged too hard for me, I was carried up it by one of his servants. Indeed, I have found a great deal of relief from pilgrims — though none of them were willing to go as slowly as I am forced to go. Still, as they came on, they told me to take heart, and said that it was their Lord's will that comfort should be given to the feeble-minded; and then they went on at their own pace.

When I had come up to Assault Lane, this giant met me and told me to prepare for a fight. But alas — feeble creature that I am, I had more need of a cordial. So he came up and took me.

I fancied he would not kill me. And when he had got me into his den, since I had not gone with him willingly, I believed I should come out alive again; because I have heard that no pilgrim who is taken captive by violent hands is, by the laws of Providence, to die at the enemy's hand, so long as he keeps his heart whole towards his Master. Robbed I expected to be, and robbed I certainly have been; but I have escaped with my life, as you see. And for that I thank my King as the author of it, and you as the means.

I expect other hard knocks as well. But this I have settled on: to run when I can, to walk when I cannot run, and to crawl when I cannot walk. As for the main point, I thank the one who loves me, I am fixed. My road is in front of me; my mind is beyond the river that has no bridge — even though I am, as you see, only of a feeble mind.

Honest. Then old Mr. Honest said: Were you not once acquainted with a certain Mr. Fearing, a pilgrim?

Feeble-mind. Acquainted with him! Yes. He came from the town of Stupidity, which lies four degrees to the north of the City of Destruction, and as far from where I was born. And yet we knew each other well — because in fact he was my uncle, my father's brother. He and I have been much of the same temper. He was a little shorter than I am, but we were much of the same complexion.

Honest. I can see you knew him; and I am inclined to believe you were related, too, because you have his pale look, a cast of the eye like his, and your way of speaking is much the same.

Feeble-mind. Most people who have known us both have said so. And besides, whatever I have read in him I have mostly found in myself too.

Gaius. Come, sir, said good Gaius, take heart. You are welcome to me and to my house; call freely for whatever you have a mind to, and whatever you want my servants to do for you they will do gladly.

Then Mr. Feeble-mind said: This is unexpected kindness — like the sun shining out of a very dark cloud. Did Giant Slay-good intend me this favour when he stopped me and decided to let me go no further? Did he intend that after he had gone through my pockets I should go to Gaius, my host? And yet that is how it has turned out.

Now just as Mr. Feeble-mind and Gaius were talking like this, somebody came running and called at the door, and told them that about a mile and a half off, one Mr. Not-right, a pilgrim, had been struck dead on the spot by a thunderbolt.

Feeble-mind. Oh dear, said Mr. Feeble-mind — is he killed? He caught up with me a few days before I got as far as here, and wanted to be my companion. He was with me too when Slay-good the giant took me; but he was quick on his feet and got away. And it seems he escaped in order to die, and I was taken in order to live.

	What seems to seek to kill a man outright
	often delivers from the saddest plight.
	That very providence whose face is death
	often to the lowly bequeaths life.
	I was taken, he escaped and fled;
	crossed hands give death to him and life to me.

Now about this time Matthew and Mercy were married. Gaius also gave his daughter Phoebe to James, Matthew's brother, as his wife. After which they still stayed more than ten days at Gaius's house, spending their time and the seasons as pilgrims usually do.

When they were to leave, Gaius made them a feast, and they ate and drank and were merry. Now the hour came when they had to go; so Mr. Great-heart called for the bill. But Gaius told him that at his house it was not the custom for pilgrims to pay for their entertainment. He kept them by the year, but looked for his payment from the good Samaritan, who had promised him that on his return he would faithfully repay him whatever expense he had been put to on their account.

Then Mr. Great-heart said to him:

Great-heart. "Beloved, you do faithfully whatever you do for the brothers and for strangers, who have borne witness to your love before the church; and if you go on to help them forward on their journey in a godly way, you will do well."

Then Gaius took his leave of them all, and of his children, and particularly of Mr. Feeble-mind. He also gave him something to drink on the road.

Now Mr. Feeble-mind, as they were going out of the door, looked as though he meant to hang back. Which when Mr. Great-heart noticed, he said: Come, Mr. Feeble-mind, please come along with us. I will be your escort, and you shall fare as the rest do.

Feeble-mind. Alas — I want a companion who suits me. You are all strong and hearty, but I, as you see, am weak. So I would rather come along behind, in case, on account of my many weaknesses, I should be a burden both to myself and to you.

I am, as I said, a man of a weak and feeble mind, and I shall be offended and weakened by things that others can take in their stride. I shall not like any laughing; I shall not like any bright clothes; I shall not like any pointless questions. In fact I am such a weak man that I shall be offended by things other people are perfectly free to do. I do not yet know the whole truth; I am a very ignorant Christian man. Sometimes, if I hear other people rejoicing in the Lord, it troubles me, because I cannot do the same. It is with me as it is with a weak man among the strong, or a sick man among the healthy, or a lamp that is despised — "he that is ready to slip with his feet is like a lamp despised in the thoughts of the man who is at ease." So I do not know what to do.

Great-heart. But, brother, said Mr. Great-heart, I have a commission to "comfort the feeble-minded" and to "support the weak." You must certainly come along with us. We will wait for you; we will lend you our help; we will deny ourselves some things, both in opinion and in practice, for your sake; we will not get into doubtful arguments in front of you; we will be made all things to you, rather than have you left behind.

Now all this while they were standing at Gaius's door. And as they were in the middle of this conversation, Mr. Ready-to-halt came past with his crutches in his hand — and he was going on pilgrimage too.

Feeble-mind. Then Mr. Feeble-mind said to him: Man, how did you come here? I was just complaining that I had no companion to suit me — and you are exactly what I wished for. Welcome, welcome, good Mr. Ready-to-halt. I hope you and I may be some help to each other.

Ready-to-halt. I shall be glad of your company, said the other; and good Mr. Feeble-mind, rather than we should part, now that we have met so happily, I will lend you one of my crutches.

Feeble-mind. No, said he — and though I thank you for your good will, I am not inclined to limp before I am lame. All the same, I think that when the occasion arises it might help me against a dog.

Ready-to-halt. If either I or my crutches can do you any service, we are both at your command, good Mr. Feeble-mind.

So they went on: Mr. Great-heart and Mr. Honest in front, Christiana and her children next, and Mr. Feeble-mind and Mr. Ready-to-halt bringing up the rear with his crutches.

Then Mr. Honest said:

Honest. Please, sir, now that we are on the road, tell us some profitable things about people who have gone on pilgrimage before us.

Great-heart. Gladly. I suppose you have heard how Christian in the old days met Apollyon in the Valley of Humiliation, and also what hard work he had getting through the Valley of the Shadow of Death. And I think you cannot but have heard how Faithful was put to it by Madam Wanton, and by Adam the First, and by one Discontent, and by Shame — four as deceitful villains as a man can meet with on the road.

Honest. Yes, I have heard all this. But indeed, good Faithful was hardest pressed by Shame; he was a tireless one.

Great-heart. Yes — because, as the Pilgrim rightly said, of all men he had the wrong name.

Honest. But tell me, sir — where was it that Christian and Faithful met Talkative? He was another remarkable one.

Great-heart. He was a confident fool; and yet a great many follow his ways.

Honest. He came near to taking Faithful in.

Great-heart. Yes; but Christian quickly put him in the way of finding him out.

So they went on until they came to the place where Evangelist met Christian and Faithful and prophesied to them about what would happen to them at Vanity Fair.

Great-heart. Then their guide said: It was about here that Christian and Faithful met Evangelist, who prophesied to them what troubles they would meet at Vanity Fair.

Honest. Did they? I dare say that was a hard chapter he read them then.

Great-heart. It was. But he gave them encouragement along with it. But why do we talk of them? They were a pair of lion-like men; they had set their faces like flint. Do you not remember how undaunted they were when they stood in front of the judge?

Honest. Well, Faithful suffered bravely.

Great-heart. So he did — and brave things came of it, because Hopeful and some others, as the story tells us, were converted by his death.

Honest. Well, but do go on; you are well acquainted with these things.

Great-heart. Of everything Christian met with after he had passed through Vanity Fair, one By-ends was the greatest rogue.

Honest. By-ends! Who was he?

Great-heart. A thoroughgoing rogue — an outright hypocrite. A man who would be religious whichever way the world went, but so cunning that he made sure he would neither lose nor suffer by it. He had his own brand of religion for every fresh occasion, and his wife was as good at it as he was. He would turn and change from one opinion to another — yes, and argue for doing it, too. But so far as I could learn, he came to a bad end with his by-ends; nor did I ever hear that any of his children were thought anything of by anybody who truly feared God.

Now by this time they had come within sight of the town of Vanity, where Vanity Fair is held. So when they saw they were so close to the town, they discussed with one another how they should get through it; and some said one thing and some another. At last Mr. Great-heart said: I have, as you know, often escorted pilgrims through this town. Now I am acquainted with one Mr. Mnason, a Cypriot by nation, an old disciple, at whose house we may lodge. If you think it a good idea, said he, we will turn in there.

Agreed, said old Honest. Agreed, said Christiana. Agreed, said Mr. Feeble-mind. And so they all said.

Now you must understand it was evening by the time they got to the edge of the town. But Mr. Great-heart knew the way to the old man's house. So they came there, and he called at the door; and the old man inside knew his voice the moment he heard it. So he opened up, and they all came in.

Then Mnason their host said: How far have you come today?

So they said: From the house of our friend Gaius.

I promise you, said he, you have come a good stretch. You may well be tired. Sit down.

So they sat down.

Great-heart. Then their guide said: Come, what cheer, gentlemen? I dare say you are welcome to my friend.

Mnason. I do bid you welcome, said Mr. Mnason. And whatever you need, only say so, and we will do what we can to get it for you.

Honest. Our great need a while ago was shelter and good company; and now I hope we have both.

Mnason. As for shelter, you can see what it is. As for good company, that will show itself in the testing.

Great-heart. Well, said Mr. Great-heart, will you take the Pilgrims up to their rooms?

Mnason. I will, said Mr. Mnason.

So he took them to their various places, and also showed them a very fine dining room where they could sit and eat supper together until it was time to go to bed.

Now when they were settled in their places, and had cheered up a little after their journey, Mr. Honest asked his landlord whether there were any good people in the town.

Mnason. We have a few — and indeed they are only a few, compared with the others.

Honest. But how are we to see some of them? Because the sight of good men, to people who are going on pilgrimage, is like the appearing of the moon and the stars to men sailing at sea.

Then Mr. Mnason stamped his foot, and his daughter Grace came up. So he said to her: Grace, go and tell my friends Mr. Contrite, Mr. Holy-man, Mr. Love-saint, Mr. Dare-not-lie and Mr. Penitent that I have a friend or two at my house who would like to see them this evening.

So Grace went to call them, and they came; and after greetings had been made, they sat down together at the table.

Then Mr. Mnason their landlord said: Neighbours, I have, as you see, a company of strangers come to my house. They are Pilgrims; they have come from far away, and they are going to Mount Zion. But who, said he, do you think this is? — pointing with his finger at Christiana. This is Christiana, the wife of Christian, that famous Pilgrim who, along with his brother Faithful, was so shamefully treated in our town.

At that they stood amazed, saying: We little thought we should see Christiana, when Grace came to call us. So this is a very comforting surprise.

Then they asked after her well-being, and whether these young men were her husband's sons. And when she had told them they were, they said: May the King whom you love and serve make you like your father, and bring you where he is, in peace.

Honest. Then Mr. Honest, when they were all sitting down, asked Mr. Contrite and the rest what state their town was in at present.

Contrite. You may be sure we are in a rush at fair time. It is hard to keep our hearts and spirits in any good order when we are in such a cluttered state. A man who lives in a place like this, and has to deal with the people we deal with, needs a reminder to warn him to take care every moment of the day.

Honest. But how are your neighbours as regards leaving people in peace?

Contrite. They are a great deal more moderate now than they used to be. You know how Christian and Faithful were treated in our town. But lately, I say, they have been far more moderate. I think the blood of Faithful lies heavy on them to this day; because since they burned him, they have been ashamed to burn anybody else. In those days we were afraid to walk in the streets, but now we can show our heads. Then the name of a believer was hateful; now, especially in some parts of our town — because you know our town is a large one — religion is counted respectable.

Then Mr. Contrite said to them: Tell us, how does it go with you on your pilgrimage? How is the country disposed towards you?

Honest. It happens to us as it happens to travellers: sometimes our road is clean, sometimes foul; sometimes uphill, sometimes down. We are seldom sure of anything. The wind is not always at our backs, nor is everybody we meet on the road a friend. We have met with some notable knocks already, and what still lies ahead we do not know. But for the most part we find it true, what has been said from of old: that a good man must expect trouble.

Contrite. You speak of knocks. What knocks have you met with?

Honest. Ask Mr. Great-heart, our guide; he can give the best account of that.

Great-heart. We have been set upon three or four times already. First, Christiana and her children were set upon by two ruffians, who they were afraid would take their lives. We have been set upon by Giant Bloody-man, Giant Maul and Giant Slay-good — though in fact with the last we did the setting-upon rather than he.

And this is how it happened. After we had been some time at the house of "Gaius, my host, and host of the whole church," we had a mind one day to take our weapons with us and go and see whether we could light on any of the enemies of pilgrims; because we had heard there was a notable one thereabouts. Now Gaius knew his haunt better than I did, because he lived nearby. So we looked and looked, until at last we made out the mouth of his cave. Then we were glad, and plucked up our spirits.

So we came up to his den; and when we got there, he had dragged this poor man, Mr. Feeble-mind, into his net by main force, and was about to make an end of him. But when he saw us — supposing, as we thought, that he had another catch — he left the poor man in his hole and came out. So we fell to it hard, and he laid about him vigorously; but in the end he was brought down to the ground, and his head cut off and set up by the roadside, as a warning to any who should practise such wickedness afterwards. And that I am telling you the truth, here is the man himself to confirm it, who was like a lamb taken out of the lion's mouth.

Feeble-mind. Then Mr. Feeble-mind said: I found this true, both to my cost and to my comfort. To my cost, when he was threatening to pick my bones every moment; and to my comfort, when I saw Mr. Great-heart and his friends coming up so close with their weapons to rescue me.

Holy-man. Then Mr. Holy-man said: There are two things that people who go on pilgrimage need to have: courage, and an unstained life. If they have no courage, they can never hold on their way; and if their lives are loose, they will make the very name of a Pilgrim stink.

Love-saint. Then Mr. Love-saint said: I hope this warning is not needed among you. But truly, there are many on the road who show themselves strangers to pilgrimage, rather than strangers and pilgrims on the earth.

Dare-not-lie. Then Mr. Dare-not-lie said: It is true. They have neither the pilgrim's need nor the pilgrim's courage. They do not walk straight, but all crooked; one shoe turns inward and the other outward, and their stockings hang out behind; a rag here and a tear there, to the discredit of their Lord.

Penitent. These things, said Mr. Penitent, are what they ought to be troubled about. Nor are the pilgrims likely to have the favour they want put on them and on their pilgrimage until the road is cleared of such spots and blemishes.

So they sat talking and passing the time until supper was set on the table; and they went to it and refreshed their weary bodies, and then went to bed.

Now they stayed at this fair a great while, at the house of this Mr. Mnason; who in due course gave his daughter Grace to Samuel, Christiana's son, as his wife, and his daughter Martha to Joseph.

The time they stayed here was, as I say, a long one, because things were not now as they had been in former days. So the Pilgrims got to know many of the good people of the town, and did them whatever service they could. Mercy, as her custom was, worked hard for the poor; so their bellies and their backs blessed her, and she was an ornament there to what she professed. And to say the truth for Grace and Phoebe and Martha, they were all of a very good nature and did a great deal of good where they were. They were all of them very fruitful too; so that Christian's name, as was said before, was likely to live on in the world.

While they were staying here, a monster came out of the woods and killed a great many of the people of the town. It would carry away their children too, and teach them to suckle its cubs. Now no man in the town dared so much as face this monster; they all fled when they heard the noise of it coming.

The monster was like no beast on earth. Its body was like a dragon's, and it had seven heads and ten horns. It made great havoc among the children, and yet it was governed by a woman. This monster set out terms to men; and those men who loved their lives more than their souls accepted those terms. So they came under it.

Now this Mr. Great-heart, together with those who had come to visit the pilgrims at Mr. Mnason's house, entered into an agreement to go and engage this beast, in the hope that they might deliver the people of this town out of the paws and mouth of such a devouring serpent.

So Mr. Great-heart, Mr. Contrite, Mr. Holy-man, Mr. Dare-not-lie and Mr. Penitent went out with their weapons to meet it. Now the monster at first was very rampant, and looked on these enemies with great contempt; but they belaboured it so, being sturdy men at arms, that they made it retreat; and so they came home again to Mr. Mnason's house.

The monster, you must understand, had its own particular seasons for coming out and making its attempts on the children of the townspeople. And these valiant men watched for it in those seasons and kept up a constant assault on it, so much so that in time it became not only wounded but lame; and it has not made the havoc among the townsmen's children that it used to. And some genuinely believe that this beast will die of its wounds.

So this made Mr. Great-heart and his companions famous in this town, so that many of the people who lacked any real discernment nevertheless had a reverent regard and respect for them. And it was on this account that these pilgrims came to no great harm here. It is true there were some of the baser sort who could see no further than a mole and understand no more than an animal; these had no reverence for these men, and took no notice of their courage or their exploits.

Well — the time came when the Pilgrims had to go on their way; so they got ready for their journey. They sent for their friends; they talked with them; they set aside some time to commit each other to their Prince's protection. There were others too who brought them such things as they had that were suitable for the weak and for the strong, for the women and for the men, and so loaded them up with what was needed.

Then they set off on their road; and their friends going with them as far as was convenient, they committed each other again to their King's protection, and parted.

So those who were of the Pilgrims' company went on, with Mr. Great-heart walking ahead of them. Now the women and children being weak, they were forced to go at whatever pace they could bear; and by this means Mr. Ready-to-halt and Mr. Feeble-mind had more company in their own condition.

When they had left the townspeople, and their friends had said goodbye, they soon came to the place where Faithful was put to death. So there they stopped, and thanked the one who had enabled him to bear his cross so well — and all the more because they now found they had a benefit from such a manly suffering as his was.

So after this they went on a good way further, talking about Christian and Faithful, and about how Hopeful joined himself to Christian after Faithful was dead.

Now they had come up to the Hill Lucre, where the silver mine was that took Demas off his pilgrimage, and into which, as some think, By-ends fell and perished; so they thought about that. But when they came to the old monument standing opposite the Hill Lucre — the pillar of salt that stands within sight of Sodom and its stinking lake — they wondered, as Christian had before them, that men of such knowledge and ripeness of wit as those men were should be so blinded as to turn aside here. Only they considered again that human nature is not much affected by the harm others have come to — especially if the thing they are looking at has some power of attraction over a foolish eye.

By-path Meadow Revisited

Now I saw that they went on until they came to the river that lies on this side of the Delectable Mountains — the river where the fine trees grow on both banks, and whose leaves, if taken inwardly, are good against overeating; where the meadows are green all the year round, and where they could lie down safely.

Along this riverside, in the meadow, there were shelters and folds for sheep, and a house built for the feeding and bringing up of those lambs, the babies of the women who go on pilgrimage. And there was one here who was entrusted with them: one who could feel compassion, and who could gather these lambs in his arm and carry them in his bosom, and who could gently lead those who were with young.

Now to this man's care Christiana urged her four daughters to commit their little ones, so that beside these waters they might be housed and sheltered and helped and fed, and so that none of them should be missing in time to come. If any of them stray or get lost, this man will bring them back again. He will also bind up whatever was broken, and will strengthen those who are sick. Here they will never lack food and drink and clothing; here they will be kept safe from thieves and robbers — because this man will die before a single one of those committed to his care shall be lost.

Besides which, they will certainly have good upbringing and correction here, and will be taught to walk in the right paths; and that, as you know, is a favour of no small value. And here too, as you see, are lovely waters, pleasant meadows, delicate flowers, and a variety of trees bearing wholesome fruit — not fruit like the kind Matthew ate, that hung over the wall out of Beelzebub's garden, but fruit that produces health where there is none, and keeps it up and increases it where there is.

So they were content to commit their little ones to him. And another thing that encouraged them to do it was that all of this was to be at the King's expense, so that it was like a hospital for young children and orphans.

Now they went on; and when they had come to By-path Meadow, to the stile Christian climbed over with his companion Hopeful when they were caught by Giant Despair and put into Doubting Castle, they sat down and discussed what would be best to do. That is: now that they were so strong, and had a man like Mr. Great-heart for their escort, whether they had not better make an attempt on the Giant, demolish his castle, and set free any pilgrims who might be in it, before they went any further.

So one said one thing and another said the opposite. One questioned whether it was lawful to go onto unconsecrated ground; another said they might, provided their purpose was good. But Mr. Great-heart said: Although that last assertion cannot be universally true, still, I have a command to resist sin, to overcome evil, and to fight the good fight of faith. And tell me — who should I fight that good fight with, if not with Giant Despair? So I mean to attempt the taking of his life and the demolition of Doubting Castle.

Then he said: Who will come with me?

Then old Honest said: I will.

And so will we, said Christiana's four sons, Matthew, Samuel, James and Joseph; because they were young men and strong.

So they left the women on the road, and with them Mr. Feeble-mind and Mr. Ready-to-halt with his crutches, to be their guard until they came back; because in that place, close as Giant Despair lived, so long as they kept to the road a little child could have led them.

So Mr. Great-heart, old Honest and the four young men went up to Doubting Castle to look for Giant Despair. When they came to the castle gate, they knocked for admission with an extraordinary racket. At which the old Giant came to the gate, with Diffidence his wife following.

Then he said: Who is it, and what is he, that is bold enough to come and disturb Giant Despair like this?

Mr. Great-heart replied: It is I, Great-heart, one of the escorts who bring the King of the Celestial Country's pilgrims to their place. And I demand that you open your gates and let me in. Get ready to fight as well, because I have come to take off your head and demolish Doubting Castle.

Now Giant Despair, being a giant, thought no man could beat him; and again he thought, since I have conquered angels before now, shall Great-heart make me afraid? So he armed himself and came out. He had a steel cap on his head and a breastplate of fire strapped to him, and he came out in iron shoes with a great club in his hand.

Then these six men closed on him and set on him from front and behind. And when Diffidence the giantess came up to help him, old Mr. Honest cut her down at a single blow. Then they fought for their lives, and Giant Despair was brought down to the ground — but he was very unwilling to die. He struggled hard, and had, as they say, as many lives as a cat. But Great-heart was the death of him, because he did not leave off until he had cut his head from his shoulders.

Then they set about demolishing Doubting Castle, which as you can imagine was easily enough done, now that Giant Despair was dead. They were seven days destroying it; and inside it they found, of pilgrims, one Mr. Despondency, almost starved to death, and one Much-afraid, his daughter. These two they brought out alive. But it would have astonished you to see the dead bodies lying here and there in the castle yard, and how full of dead men's bones the dungeon was.

When Mr. Great-heart and his companions had carried out this exploit, they took Mr. Despondency and his daughter Much-afraid under their protection; because they were honest people, for all that they had been prisoners in Doubting Castle to that tyrant Giant Despair.

So, as I say, they took the Giant's head with them — his body they had buried under a heap of stones — and came down to the road and to their companions, and showed them what they had done.

Now when Feeble-mind and Ready-to-halt saw that it really was the head of Giant Despair, they were tremendously cheerful and merry. Now Christiana, when there was need of it, could play the viol, and her daughter Mercy the lute. So, since they were all in such a merry mood, she played them a piece, and Ready-to-halt would dance. So he took Despondency's daughter, Much-afraid, by the hand, and off they went dancing in the road. It is true that he could not dance without one crutch in his hand; but I promise you he footed it well. And the girl deserved credit too, because she answered the music handsomely.

As for Mr. Despondency, the music did not do much for him; he was for eating rather than dancing, since he was very nearly starved. So Christiana gave him some out of her bottle of cordial for immediate relief, and then got him something to eat; and in a little while the old gentleman came round, and began to revive nicely.

The Fall of Doubting Castle

Now I saw in my dream that when all this was finished, Mr. Great-heart took the head of Giant Despair and set it on a pole beside the highway, directly opposite the pillar Christian had put up to warn pilgrims who came after him not to go onto his grounds.

	Though Doubting Castle has been demolished,
	and Giant Despair has lost his head,
	sin can rebuild the Castle, make it stand,
	and make Despair the Giant live again.

Then underneath, on a marble stone, he wrote these verses:

	This is the head of him whose name alone
	in former times did pilgrims terrify.
	His Castle's down; and Diffidence, his wife,
	brave Master Great-heart has bereft of life.
	Despondency, and his daughter Much-afraid —
	for them as well Great-heart the man has played.
	Whoever doubts it, if he'll cast his eye
	up here, may all his scruples satisfy.
	This head also, when doubting cripples dance,
	shows that from all their fears they have deliverance.

When these men had shown themselves so bravely against Doubting Castle, and had killed Giant Despair, they went forward; and went on until they came to the Delectable Mountains, where Christian and Hopeful refreshed themselves with all the good things of the place. They also got to know the shepherds there, who welcomed them to the Delectable Mountains as they had welcomed Christian before.

Now the Shepherds, seeing such a large party following Mr. Great-heart — for they knew him well — said to him: Good sir, you have a fine company here. Tell us, where did you find them all?

Then Mr. Great-heart replied:

	First, here is Christiana and her train,
	her sons and her sons' wives, who like the Wain
	keep by the pole, and steer by compass, so
	from sin to grace — or they'd not be here.
	Next, here's old Honest come on pilgrimage;
	Ready-to-halt as well, who, I'll engage,
	is true of heart; and so is Feeble-mind,
	who was not willing to be left behind.
	Despondency, good man, is coming after,
	and so is Much-afraid as well, his daughter.
	May we have lodging here — or must we still
	go further on? Let us know what to trust.

Then the Shepherds said: This is a comforting company. You are welcome to us, because we have comfort for the feeble as much as for the strong. Our Prince keeps an eye on what is done to the least of these; so infirmity must be no bar to our hospitality.

So they took them to the palace door, and then said to them: Come in, Mr. Feeble-mind; come in, Mr. Ready-to-halt; come in, Mr. Despondency, and Mrs. Much-afraid, his daughter. These, Mr. Great-heart, said the Shepherds to the guide, we call in by name, because they are the most liable to hang back. But as for you and the rest who are strong, we leave you to your usual freedom.

Then Mr. Great-heart said: Today I can see that grace shines in your faces, and that you really are my Lord's Shepherds; because you have not shoved these sick ones aside with side or shoulder, but have rather strewn their way into the palace with flowers, as you should.

So the feeble and the weak went in, and Mr. Great-heart and the rest followed after. When they were seated too, the Shepherds said to those of the weaker sort: What would you like? Because, they said, everything here must be managed for the support of the weak as well as for the warning of the unruly.

So they made them a feast of things easy to digest, and pleasant to the taste, and nourishing; and when they had had it, they went to their rest, each one to his own proper place.

When morning came — because the mountains were high and the day was clear, and because it was the Shepherds' custom to show Pilgrims some rarities before they left — after they were ready and had refreshed themselves, the Shepherds took them out into the fields and showed them first what they had shown Christian before. Then they took them to some new places.

The first was Mount Marvel, where they looked and saw a man in the distance tumbling the hills about with words. Then they asked the Shepherds what that should mean. So they told them that the man was a son of one Great-grace, whom you read about in the First Part of the Records of the Pilgrim's Progress; and that he is set there to teach pilgrims how to believe down, or tumble out of their way, whatever difficulties they meet with, by faith.

Then Mr. Great-heart said: I know him. He is a man above many.

Then they took them to another place, called Mount Innocent; and there they saw a man dressed all in white, and two men, Prejudice and Ill-will, constantly throwing dirt at him. Now, whatever dirt they threw at him would fall off again in a little while, and his clothes would look as clean as if no dirt had ever been thrown at them.

Then the Pilgrims said: What does this mean?

The Shepherds answered: This man's name is Godly-man, and this clothing is to show the innocence of his life. Now those who throw dirt at him are people who hate his doing good. But as you can see the dirt will not stick on his clothes, so it will be with anybody who truly lives innocently in the world. Whoever they are who would like to make such men dirty, they are all labouring in vain; because God, once a little time has gone by, will cause their innocence to break out like the light, and their righteousness like the noonday.

Then they took them to Mount Charity, where they showed them a man with a bundle of cloth lying in front of him, out of which he was cutting coats and clothes for the poor standing round him — and yet his bundle, or roll, of cloth was never any the smaller.

Then they said: What is this meant to be?

This is, said the Shepherds, to show you that the man who has a heart to give away what he works for to the poor shall never lack anything. He who waters shall himself be watered. And the cake the widow gave the Prophet did not leave her any the less in her barrel.

They also took them to a place where they saw one Fool and one Want-wit washing an Ethiopian, with the intention of making him white; but the more they washed him, the blacker he was.

Then they asked the Shepherds what that should mean. So they told them, saying: So it will be with the vile person. All the means used to get such a man a good name will in the end only make him more detestable. So it was with the Pharisees, and so it will be with all hypocrites.

Then Mercy, Matthew's wife, said to Christiana her mother: Mother, if it could be arranged, I would like to see the hole in the hill — the one commonly called the byway to hell.

So her mother mentioned it to the Shepherds. Then they went to the door. It was in the side of a hill, and they opened it and told Mercy to listen for a while. So she listened, and heard one saying: Cursed be my father, for holding my feet back from the way of peace and life. And another said: Oh, if only I had been torn to pieces before I had saved my life and lost my soul! And another said: If I could live again, how I would deny myself, rather than come to this place!

Then it was as though the very earth groaned and shook under the young woman's feet for fear. So she went white, and came away trembling, saying: Blessed be the man and the woman who are delivered from this place.

Now when the Shepherds had shown them all these things, they took them back to the palace and entertained them with what the house could provide. But Mercy, being young and pregnant, longed for something she saw there, and was ashamed to ask for it. Her mother-in-law then asked her what was wrong with her, because she looked unwell.

Then Mercy said: There is a looking-glass hanging up in the dining room that I cannot get out of my mind. If I do not have it, I think I shall lose the child.

Then her mother said: I will mention what you want to the Shepherds, and they will not refuse you.

But she said: I am ashamed for these men to know that I had such a longing.

No, my daughter, said she — it is no shame but a virtue to long for a thing like that.

So Mercy said: Then, mother, if you would, ask the Shepherds whether they are willing to sell it.

Now the glass was one in a thousand. Looked at one way, it would show a person his own features exactly; and turn it another way, and it would show the very face and likeness of the Prince of Pilgrims himself. Yes — I have talked with people who can tell, and they have said that they have seen the very crown of thorns on his head by looking in that glass, and have seen in it too the holes in his hands and his feet and his side. Indeed, there is such an excellence in that glass that it will show him to a person however they have a mind to see him: living or dead, on earth or in heaven, in his humiliation or in his exaltation, coming to suffer or coming to reign.

So Christiana went to the Shepherds privately — now the names of the Shepherds are Knowledge, Experience, Watchful and Sincere — and said to them: One of my daughters is pregnant, and I think she is longing for something she has seen in this house; and she thinks she will lose the child if you refuse her.

Experience. Call her, call her. She shall certainly have whatever we can help her to.

So they called her and said to her: Mercy, what is this thing you would like to have?

Then she blushed, and said: The great glass hanging up in the dining room.

So Sincere ran and fetched it, and it was given her with a glad heart. Then she bowed her head and gave thanks, and said: By this I know that I have found favour in your eyes.

They also gave the other young women whatever they wanted, and gave their husbands great praise for having joined with Mr. Great-heart in killing Giant Despair and demolishing Doubting Castle.

The Shepherds put a bracelet round Christiana's neck, and did the same for her four daughters; and they put earrings in their ears and jewels on their foreheads.

When they had a mind to go, they let them go in peace — but they did not give them the particular warnings that had been given to Christian and his companion before. The reason was that these had Great-heart for their guide, who was a man well acquainted with the way, and so could give them their warnings at a better moment: that is, just when the danger was coming near.

The warnings Christian and his companion had been given by the Shepherds they had also lost by the time they needed to put them into practice. So here was the advantage this company had over the other.

From here they went on singing, and said:

	See how fittingly the stages are set out
	for the relief of those who pilgrims are!
	And how they take us in without a check,
	who make the other life our mark and home!
	Whatever novelties they have they give us,
	so that we Pilgrims may live joyful lives;
	they lay such things upon us, too, as show
	that we are Pilgrims, wherever we may go.

When they had left the Shepherds, they soon came to the place where Christian met one Turn-away, who lived in the town of Apostasy. So Mr. Great-heart their guide reminded them of him, saying: This is the place where Christian met one Turn-away, who carried the record of his rebellion on his back. And this I have to say about the man: he would listen to no advice; once he had fallen, no persuasion could stop him.

When he came to the place where the Cross and the Tomb are, he met somebody who told him to look at them; but he ground his teeth and stamped, and said he was determined to go back to his own town. Before he came to the gate, he met Evangelist, who offered to lay hands on him and turn him back into the road. But this Turn-away resisted him; and after treating him with great contempt, he got away over the wall and so escaped his hand.

Then they went on. And just at the place where Little-faith was robbed before, there stood a man with his sword drawn and his face all bloody.

Then Mr. Great-heart said: Who are you?

The man answered, saying: My name is Valiant-for-truth. I am a pilgrim, and I am going to the Celestial City. Now, as I was on my way, three men set on me and put these three things to me. First, whether I would become one of them. Second, or go back where I came from. Third, or die on the spot.

To the first I answered that I had been an honest man a long while, and so it could not be expected that I should now throw in my lot with thieves. Then they demanded what I had to say to the second; so I told them that if I had not found the place I came from thoroughly uncomfortable I would never have left it at all, but that finding it entirely unsuitable and quite unprofitable for me, I had left it for this road. Then they asked me what I said to the third; and I told them that my life cost me far too dear for me to give it away lightly. Besides, said I, you have no business putting things to my choice like this; so if you interfere, it is at your own peril.

Then these three — Wild-head, Inconsiderate and Pragmatic — drew on me, and I drew on them.

So we set to, one against three, for more than three hours. They have left some of the marks of their valour on me, as you see, and have carried off some of mine as well. They have only just gone. I suppose they may, as the saying goes, have heard your horses coming, and so took to their heels.

Great-heart. But those were great odds — three against one.

Valiant-for-truth. It is true; but few or many are nothing to a man who has the truth on his side. "Though an army should camp against me," said one, "my heart shall not fear; though war should rise against me, in this will I be confident." Besides, he says, I have read in certain records that one man has fought an army. And how many did Samson kill with the jawbone of a donkey?

Great-heart. Then the guide said: Why did you not shout out, so that somebody might have come to your help?

Valiant-for-truth. So I did — to my King, who I knew could hear, and could give invisible help; and that was enough for me.

Great-heart. Then Great-heart said to Mr. Valiant-for-truth: You have conducted yourself worthily. Let me see your sword.

So he showed it him. And when he had taken it in his hand and looked at it a while, he said: Ha! This is a genuine Jerusalem blade.

Valiant-for-truth. It is. Let a man have one of these blades, with a hand to wield it and the skill to use it, and he may take on an angel with it. He need not fear it will fail him, if he only knows how to lay on. Its edges will never go blunt. It will cut flesh and bones, and soul and spirit, and everything.

Great-heart. But you fought a long while. I wonder you were not tired out.

Valiant-for-truth. I fought until my sword stuck to my hand; and when the two were joined together, as though a sword had grown out of my arm, and when the blood ran through my fingers — then I fought with the most courage of all.

Great-heart. You have done well. You have "resisted to the point of blood, striving against sin." You shall stay with us, and come in and go out with us; because we are your companions.

Then they took him and washed his wounds, and gave him what they had to refresh him; and so they went on together. Now, as they went along — because Mr. Great-heart was delighted with him, since he greatly loved anybody he found to be a fighting man, and because there were feeble and weak ones in his company — he questioned him about a good many things. First, what country he came from.

Valiant-for-truth. I am from Dark-land; because I was born there, and my father and mother are there still.

Great-heart. Dark-land, said the guide — does that not lie on the same coast as the City of Destruction?

Valiant-for-truth. Yes, it does. Now what made me come on pilgrimage was this. One Mr. Tell-true came into our parts, and he told everybody what Christian had done, who went from the City of Destruction: namely, how he had left his wife and children and taken up a pilgrim's life. It was also confidently reported how he had killed a serpent that came out to stop him on his journey, and how he got through to where he meant to go. It was also told what a welcome he had at all his Lord's lodgings, and especially when he came to the gates of the Celestial City — because there, said the man, he was received with the sound of a trumpet, by a company of Shining Ones. He told too how all the bells in the city rang for joy at his reception, and what golden clothes he was dressed in, along with a great many other things I will not go into now.

In a word, that man told the story of Christian and his travels in such a way that my heart fell into a burning hurry to be off after him. Neither father nor mother could hold me. So I got away from them, and I have come this far on my road.

Great-heart. You came in at the gate, did you not?

Valiant-for-truth. Yes, yes — because the same man told us as well that it would all come to nothing if we did not begin this road by entering at the gate.

Great-heart. Do you see, said the guide to Christiana — your husband's pilgrimage, and what he has gained by it, is spread abroad far and near.

Valiant-for-truth. Why — is this Christian's wife?

Great-heart. Yes, it is. And these are her four sons.

Valiant-for-truth. What — and going on pilgrimage too?

Great-heart. Yes, truly; they are following after him.

Valiant-for-truth. It gladdens my heart. Good man! How joyful he will be when he sees the very people who would not go with him coming in after him at the gates of the City!

Great-heart. Without a doubt it will be a comfort to him; because next to the joy of seeing himself there, it will be a joy to meet his wife and children there.

Valiant-for-truth. But now that you are on that subject, let me hear your opinion about it. Some people raise the question whether we shall know one another when we are there.

Great-heart. Do they think they will know themselves then, or that they will rejoice to see themselves in that bliss? And if they think they will know and do these things, why not know others, and rejoice in their welfare too?

And again: since our relations are our second selves, though that condition will be dissolved there, still, why may we not reasonably conclude that we shall be gladder to see them there than to see that they are missing?

Valiant-for-truth. Well, I can see where you stand on this. Have you any more things to ask me about my setting out on pilgrimage?

Great-heart. Yes. Were your father and mother willing for you to become a pilgrim?

Valiant-for-truth. Oh no. They used every means imaginable to persuade me to stay at home.

Great-heart. What could they say against it?

Valiant-for-truth. They said it was an idle life; and that if I were not naturally inclined to sloth and laziness, I would never approve of a pilgrim's way of living.

Great-heart. And what else did they say?

Valiant-for-truth. Why, they told me it was a dangerous road — indeed that the most dangerous road in the world, they said, is the one the pilgrims go.

Great-heart. Did they show how this road is so dangerous?

Valiant-for-truth. Yes, and in a great many particulars.

Great-heart. Name some of them.

Valiant-for-truth. They told me about the Slough of Despond, where Christian was very nearly smothered. They told me there were archers standing ready in Beelzebub's Castle to shoot anybody who knocked at the little gate to get in. They told me too about the wood and the dark mountains, about the Hill Difficulty, about the lions, and about the three giants, Bloody-man, Maul and Slay-good. They said as well that there was a foul fiend haunting the Valley of Humiliation, and that Christian was nearly killed by him. Besides which, they said, you must go over the Valley of the Shadow of Death, where the hobgoblins are, where the light is darkness, and where the road is full of snares and pits and traps. They told me about Giant Despair as well, and Doubting Castle, and the disaster the Pilgrims met with there. Further, they said I must go over the Enchanted Ground, which was dangerous. And that after all this I should find a river with no bridge over it, and that that river lay between me and the Celestial Country.

Great-heart. And was that all?

Valiant-for-truth. No. They also told me that this road was full of deceivers, and of people lying in wait there to turn good men off the path.

Great-heart. But how did they make that out?

Valiant-for-truth. They told me that Mr. Worldly-Wiseman lay in wait there to deceive people. They said too that Formalist and Hypocrisy were constantly on the road. They also said that By-ends, or Talkative, or Demas would very likely pick me up; that the Flatterer would catch me in his net; or that, like raw green Ignorance, I would presume to go on to the gate, from which he was always sent back to the hole in the side of the hill, and made to go the byway to hell.

Great-heart. I must say that was enough to discourage anybody. But did they stop there?

Valiant-for-truth. No — wait. They also told me about a great many who had tried that road in the old days, and had gone a long way down it to see whether they could find any of that glory there that so many had talked so much about from time to time; and how they came back again and called themselves fools for ever setting a foot out of doors on that path — to the satisfaction of the whole country. And they named several who had done so: Obstinate and Pliable, Mistrust and Timorous, Turn-away and old Atheist, and several more, some of whom, they said, had gone a long way to see whether they could find anything — and not one of them had got as much advantage out of going as would weigh a feather.

Great-heart. Did they say anything more to discourage you?

Valiant-for-truth. Yes. They told me about a Mr. Fearing who was a pilgrim, and how he found this road so lonely that he never had a comfortable hour on it. Also that Mr. Despondency had nearly been starved on it. Yes — and also, which I had almost forgotten, that Christian himself, about whom there has been such a noise, after all his ventures for a heavenly crown, was certainly drowned in the Black River and never went a foot further, however much it was hushed up.

Great-heart. And did none of this discourage you?

Valiant-for-truth. No. It all seemed like so much nothing to me.

Great-heart. How did that come about?

Valiant-for-truth. Why, I still believed what Mr. Tell-true had said, and that carried me past the lot of it.

Great-heart. Then this was your victory — your faith.

Valiant-for-truth. It was. I believed, by the grace of God, and so I came out, and got onto the road, and fought everything that set itself against me; and by believing I have come this far.

	Who would true valour see,
	let him come hither;
	one here will constant be,
	come wind, come weather.
	There's no discouragement
	shall make him once relent
	his first avowed intent
	to be a pilgrim.
	Whoever beset him round
	with dismal stories
	do but themselves confound;
	his strength the more is.
	No lion can him fright,
	he'll with a giant fight,
	but he will have a right
	to be a pilgrim.
	Hobgoblin nor foul fiend
	can daunt his spirit;
	he knows he at the end
	shall life inherit.
	Then fancies fly away,
	he'll fear not what men say;
	he'll labour night and day
	to be a pilgrim.

The Enchanted Ground

By this time they had got to the Enchanted Ground, where the air naturally makes a person drowsy. That place was all overgrown with briars and thorns, except here and there where there was an Enchanted Arbour; and if a man sits down in one of those, or falls asleep in one, it is an open question, some say, whether he will ever get up or wake again in this world.

So over this forest they went, one and all; and Mr. Great-heart went in front, since he was the guide, and Mr. Valiant-for-truth came behind as a rearguard, for fear that some fiend or dragon or giant or thief might fall on their rear and do them harm. They went on here with every man's sword drawn in his hand, because they knew it was a dangerous place. They also encouraged one another as well as they could. Mr. Great-heart ordered Feeble-mind to come along right behind him, and Mr. Despondency was kept under Mr. Valiant's eye.

Now they had not gone far before a great mist and darkness came down on them all, so that for a long while they could hardly see one another. So they were forced for some time to feel for each other with words, because they were certainly not walking by sight.

But anybody can imagine that this was poor going for the best of them, and how much worse for the women and children, who were tender in both feet and heart. Yet so it was that, by the encouraging words of the man leading in front and the man bringing them up behind, they managed a fairly good job of struggling along.

The road here was very wearisome too, with all the mud and the sliminess. Nor was there on all this ground so much as one inn or eating house where the feebler ones could refresh themselves. So there was grunting here, and puffing, and sighing. One of them tumbles over a bush, another sticks fast in the mud, and some of the children lost their shoes in the mire. One cries out, I am down! and another, Hey — where are you? and a third, The bushes have got such a hold of me, I do not think I can get away from them.

Then they came to an arbour, warm and promising a great deal of refreshment to the Pilgrims; because it was finely worked overhead, decorated with greenery, and furnished with benches and seats. It also had a soft couch in it, where the weary could lie back. All things considered, you may imagine this was tempting, because the Pilgrims were already beginning to be worn down by the badness of the road. But there was not one of them who so much as suggested stopping there.

Indeed, so far as I could see, they paid such constant attention to their guide's advice, and he told them so faithfully about dangers, and about the nature of dangers, at the very moment they were in them, that usually when they were closest to them they were the most heartened, and the most ready to encourage one another to deny the flesh. This arbour was called The Slothful's Friend, put there on purpose to lure some of the pilgrims, if it could, into taking their rest there when they were tired.

Then I saw in my dream that they went on across this lonely ground until they came to a place where a man is likely to lose his way. Now, although in daylight their guide could tell well enough how to avoid the roads that led the wrong way, in the dark he was brought to a stop. But he had in his pocket a map of all the roads leading to and from the Celestial City. So he struck a light — because he never goes without his tinderbox either — and took a look at his book, or map, which told him to be careful at this place to take the right-hand road.

And if he had not been careful to look at his map here, in all probability they would all have been smothered in the mud; because just a little ahead of them, and at the end of the cleanest-looking road at that, there was a pit, nobody knows how deep, full of nothing but mud, made there on purpose to destroy the Pilgrims.

Then I thought to myself: who that goes on pilgrimage would not want one of these maps about him, so that he can look at it when he is brought to a stand, and see which way he has to take?

So they went on across the Enchanted Ground until they came to where there was another arbour, built beside the highway. And in that arbour two men were lying, whose names were Heedless and Too-bold. These two had got this far on pilgrimage; but here, being worn out with their journey, they had sat down to rest and fallen fast asleep.

When the Pilgrims saw them, they stood still and shook their heads, because they knew the sleepers were in a pitiful state. Then they discussed what to do — whether to go on and leave them sleeping, or to go over to them and try to wake them. So they decided to go to them and wake them if they could, but with this precaution: to take care that they did not sit down themselves, or accept the comfort the arbour was offering.

So they went in and spoke to the men and called them each by name, because the guide, it seems, knew them. But there was no voice and no answer. Then the guide shook them and did everything he could to rouse them.

Then one of them said: I will pay you when I get my money. At which the guide shook his head.

I will fight as long as I can hold my sword in my hand, said the other. At which one of the children laughed.

Christiana. Then Christiana said: What does this mean?

The guide said: They are talking in their sleep. If you struck them, or beat them, or did anything else to them, they would answer you in the same way. Or, as one man said long ago, when the waves of the sea were beating on him and he slept like a man on a ship's mast: "When shall I awake? I will seek it yet again."

You know that when men talk in their sleep they say anything at all, and their words are governed neither by faith nor by reason. There is an incoherence in their words now, just as there was before between their going on pilgrimage and their sitting down here.

And this is the mischief of it: when heedless people go on pilgrimage, it is twenty to one that they get served like this. Because this Enchanted Ground is one of the last refuges the enemy of pilgrims has. That is why it is placed, as you see, almost at the end of the road, so that it stands against us with all the more advantage. Because the enemy thinks: when will these fools be so eager to sit down as when they are tired? And when are they so likely to be tired as when they are almost at the end of their journey?

That is why, I say, the Enchanted Ground is placed so close to the Land of Beulah, and so near the end of their race. So let pilgrims look out for themselves, in case it happens to them as it has happened to these, who as you can see have fallen asleep, and whom nobody can wake.

Then the Pilgrims wanted, trembling, to go forward; only they asked their guide to strike a light, so that they might go the rest of their road by the help of a lantern. So he struck a light, and they went by the help of it through the rest of this stretch, though the darkness was very great.

But the children began to be badly tired out, and they cried out to the one who loves pilgrims to make their road more comfortable. So by the time they had gone a little further, a wind got up that drove the fog away, and the air became clearer.

They were still a long way from being off the Enchanted Ground; only now they could see one another better, and see the road they were to walk on.

Mr. Stand-fast and Madam Bubble

Now when they were almost at the end of this ground, they became aware that a little way ahead of them there was the solemn sound of somebody in great distress. So they went on and looked ahead; and there, as they thought, they saw a man on his knees, with his hands and his eyes lifted up, speaking, so it seemed to them, earnestly to One who was above. They came nearer, but could not make out what he said. So they went quietly until he had finished.

When he had finished, he got up and began to run towards the Celestial City. Then Mr. Great-heart called after him, saying: Hello there, friend! Let us have your company, if you are going, as I suppose you are, to the Celestial City.

So the man stopped, and they came up to him. But as soon as Mr. Honest saw him he said: I know this man.

Then Mr. Valiant-for-truth said: Tell us, who is he?

He is one, said he, who comes from near where I lived. His name is Stand-fast, and he is certainly a thoroughly good pilgrim.

So they came up to one another; and Stand-fast said at once to old Honest: Hello, father Honest — are you here?

Yes, said he, that I am, as surely as you are.

I am very glad, said Mr. Stand-fast, to have found you on this road.

And I am just as glad, said the other, to have caught sight of you on your knees.

Then Mr. Stand-fast blushed and said: But did you see me?

Yes, I did, said the other, and my heart was glad at the sight.

Why — what did you think? said Stand-fast.

Think! said old Honest. What should I think? I thought we had an honest man on the road, and that we should have his company before long.

If you thought no worse than that, said Stand-fast, how happy I am. But if I am not what I ought to be, I must bear it alone.

That is true, said the other; but your fear only confirms me further that things are right between the Prince of Pilgrims and your soul; because it is said, "Blessed is the man who fears always."

Valiant-for-truth. Well, but brother, tell us please — what was it that brought you to your knees just now? Was it that some special mercies had laid an obligation on you, or what?

Stand-fast. Why — we are, as you see, on the Enchanted Ground. And as I was coming along I was thinking to myself what a dangerous stretch of road this place is, and how many people who had come even this far on pilgrimage had been stopped here and destroyed. I was thinking too about the kind of death this place destroys men with. Those who die here do not die of any violent illness. The death such men die is not painful to them; because the man who goes off in a sleep begins that journey with desire and pleasure. Yes — men like that consent to the sickness that is killing them.

Honest. Then Mr. Honest interrupted him and said: Did you see the two men asleep in the arbour?

Stand-fast. Yes, yes — I saw Heedless and Too-bold there; and for all I know they will lie there until they rot. But let me go on with my tale.

As I was thinking these things over, as I say, there was one in very pleasant clothes, but old, who presented herself to me and offered me three things: her body, her purse and her bed. Now the truth is I was both tired and sleepy; and I am also as poor as an owlet, which perhaps the witch knew.

Well, I repulsed her once and twice, but she brushed my refusals aside and smiled. Then I began to get angry, but that did not matter to her in the least. Then she made her offers again, and said that if I would be guided by her she would make me great and happy; because, she said, I am the mistress of the world, and men are made happy by me.

Then I asked her name, and she told me it was Madam Bubble. This put me further off from her than ever; but she still came after me with her enticements. Then I took myself to my knees, as you saw, and with my hands lifted up and with crying out, I prayed to the one who has said he would help. So, just as you came up, the gentlewoman went off. Then I went on giving thanks for this great deliverance of mine; because I truly believe she meant no good, but was rather looking to bring me to a stop on my journey.

Honest. Without a doubt her designs were bad. But wait — now that you speak of her, I think I have either seen her or read some account of her.

Stand-fast. Perhaps you have done both.

Honest. Madam Bubble! Is she not a tall, handsome woman, with something of a dark complexion?

Stand-fast. Right — you have hit it. She is exactly that.

Honest. Does she not speak very smoothly, and give you a smile at the end of a sentence?

Stand-fast. You have hit it again, because those are her very mannerisms.

Honest. Does she not wear a great purse at her side, and is not her hand often inside it, fingering her money, as though that were her heart's delight?

Stand-fast. That is just it. If she had been standing here all this while, you could not have set her out in front of me more fully, or described her features better.

Honest. Then the man who drew her picture was a good artist, and the man who wrote about her told the truth.

Great-heart. This woman is a witch, and it is by the power of her sorceries that this ground is enchanted. Whoever lays his head down in her lap might as well lay it down on the block with the axe hanging over it; and whoever lets his eyes rest on her beauty is counted an enemy of God. This is the woman who keeps all the enemies of pilgrims in their splendour. Yes — this is the woman who has bought many a man off from a pilgrim's life.

She is a great gossip. She and her daughters are always at one pilgrim's heels or another, first praising and then recommending the excellences of this life. She is a bold and shameless creature; she will talk to any man. She always laughs poor pilgrims to scorn, but praises the rich to the skies. If there is somebody clever at making money in a place, she will speak well of him from house to house. She loves banqueting and feasting enormously; she is always at one loaded table or another. She has given it out in some places that she is a goddess, and so some people worship her. She has her own times and public places for cheating; and she will say, and swear to it, that nobody can show a good to compare with hers. She promises to live with your children's children, if only they will love her and make much of her. She will throw gold out of her purse like dust, in certain places and to certain people. She loves to be sought after, and spoken well of, and to lie in men's arms. She is never tired of advertising her wares, and she loves best the men who think best of her. She will promise some men crowns and kingdoms if only they will take her advice — and yet she has brought many a man to the gallows, and ten thousand times more to hell.

Stand-fast. Oh, said Stand-fast, what a mercy it is that I resisted! Because where might she have dragged me to?
